INTRODUCTION 

The information contained in this manual is for the education and guidance of employees of the Atchison, Topeka and Santa Fe Railway Company whose duties require that they be familiar with the operation of diesel electric locomotives and their appurtenances. 

All employees concerned in the operation of diesel electric locomotives will be required to familiarize themselves with this manual in order that efficiency in operation will be secured, and delays eliminated. 

In addition to the information contained in this book, employees will be given instruction by Assistant Supervisors of Diesel Engines, and others, and will, at such time as they may be required by the Railway Company, be called for an examination as to their knowledge of the proper operation of diesel locomotives. 

Any employee whose work indicates an apparent absence of the required knowledge may be required to pass an examination at any time following such indications. The results of examination will be embodied in the service records. 

An employee who repeatedly fails to pass satisfactory examinations or to handle the diesel equipment in a satisfactory manner, will be required to familiarize himself with the subject in order that he may render satisfactory service. 

Employees who pass the examination satisfactorily will, from time to time, be called for further examination, in order that the highest degree of efficiency in service may be maintained at all times. 969049

DIESEL ENGINES-GENERAL PART I-SECTION 1

CHARACTERISTICS OF ENGINE CYCLES 

Diesel engines are designed to operate on the two or four stroke cycle principle. A cycle may be defined as a series of events through which a machine must pass to complete a definite end. In a diesel engine, a cycle entails (1) introduction of air charge; (2) compression (3) injection of fuel and combustion, (4) expansion (5) exhaust of burned gases. 

Four Cycle Engines: 

If an engine requires four strokes of the piston to complete the cycle, it is termed a four-cycle engine. During exhaust and intake strokes, the piston functions as an air pump. A complete revolution of the crankshaft is equal to two strokes of the piston. A stroke of the piston is defined as the movement of the piston from bottom dead center to top dead center, or from top dead center to bottom dead center. 

Two-Cycle Engine: 

If an engine requires two strokes of the piston to complete the cycle, it is termed a two-cycle engine. In this engine a charge of fuel is burned on every down stroke of the piston and an external supply of air under pressure is used to force exhaust gases out of the cylinder.

FUEL INJECTION SYSTEM 

The diesel engines use the solid injection system which consists of a pump and a spray injector valve. The purpose of the pump is to measure off a definite quantity of fuel on each downward stroke of the injector plunger. The quantity of fuel forced from the injector by the plunger depends upon the position of the main engine throttle. The fuel is forced under very high pressure into a very finely atomized spray. Immediately when the pressure is relieved the needle valve spring returns the needle valve to its seat shutting off the flow of fuel to the cylinder so that no further injection will occour until the next stroke of the injection pump.

The types of solid injection systems are used-one type has the fuel pump and spray injector valve built separately and the other type known as the unit injector has them built together. 

Pump and Nozzle Type Injection: 

In the pump and nozzle type injection system the pump is separate from the spray valve, the pump being located usually along the side of the engine, one pump for each cylinder and actuated by a camshaft from the engine. The pump consists of a

helical milled plunger similar to the unit system which meters the fuel and delivers it thru a heavy steel pipe to the spray or injector valve. The plunger is rotated by a gear and rack connected to the governor which varies the amount of fuel according to the power demand. The spray or injector valve is located in the cylinder or cylinder head and as the charge of fuel is forced by the pump the valve lifts, and sprays the fuel into the cylinder thru several small holes in the tip.

Unit Type Injector: 

This type of injector is used on all locomotives manu factured by the Electro-Motive Division of General Motors 

Corp. In the unit injector the forcing pump and the spray valves are combined into one unit. This unit is placed in the center of the cylinder head and is operated by a rocker arm which is in turn driven by the camshaft. The upper portion consists of the forcing pump which is fitted into the barrel very closely to prevent the fuel from escaping by the plunger. 

Upon the plunger is a milled helical groove which as it passes the ports in the barrel, forces a given quantity of fuel out the bottom of the barrel into the spray tip which is screwed on the lower end of the injector unit. The plunger is rotated by a gear and rack which is coupled to the governor linkage and as the plunger is rotated the quantity of fuel trapped underneath the plunger is varied according to the power demand on the engine. The spray tip consists of a small needle valve held to its seat by a spring. As the pressure of the fuel overcomes the spring, the needle valve opens and allows the fuel to be sprayed into the cylinder thru 6.small holes which break it up into small particles. The needle valve spring is set to raise at approximately 2300 pounds per square inch so that sufficient over pressure is present to overcome any pressure in the cylinder. 

Scavenging Blowers:

Two-cycle engines employ a blower to supply scavenging air to the cylinders. This air under a pressure of 3 to 5 p.s.i. (approximate) helps to remove burned gases thru the exhaust valves and to cool internal parts of the engine. 

A breather connection from the intake side of both blowers to the engine crankcase provides crankcase ventilation. An airoil separator in this line prevents oil mist from being drawn into the blower.

The blowers are driven from the engine crankshaft thru a gear train.

Turbocharger: 

The turbocharger consists of an exhaust gas operated turbine, driving a centrifugal blower. They are mounted on a common shaft.

The exhaust turbine is designed to handle a large volume of exhaust gas. All exhaust gas from the engine goes thru

Differences between 567 and 567A engines

The following items are incorporated in the 567A Model Engine and are not included in the 567 engine. 

1. The oil pan is of larger capacity, classifying the engine as "wet sump". A "dip stick" is provided to show oil level. 

2. The oil pan screen is located in a rectangular box on the accessory end of the engine. 

3. The lubricating oil relief valve is located inside the accessory drive gear housing instead of outside. 

4. The camshafts rotate in opposite directions, that is, inward. 

5. The blowers are mounted closely together and their quill shafts are on the outer sides. 

6. The oil separator is a single round unit instead of the former duplex type. 

7. The crankcase breather has been omitted. 

Differences between 16-567A and 16-567B Engines 1. The lube oil suction strainer housing has been added to the "B" engine at the accessory end, opposite the scavenging oil pump. This has two screens in parallel for the main bearing and piston cooling pump and one large mesh screen for the scavenging pump. This housing also provides overflow from the oil cooler to sump and has an opening for adding oil. 

2. Fuel oil sight glasses and relief valve have been added to the sintered bronze filter assembly on front of engine. 

3. The auxiliary generator drive assembly has been added.. 

This has an aluminum housing bolted on to the camshaft drive housing with the drive shaft driven by the upper idler gear. 

The housing supports the oil separator. 

4. The governor used with the 16-567B engine has the electro-hydraulic control which replaces the electro-pneumatic control previously used. The new governor incorporates as built in features, the electro-hydraulic governor control, the load regulator pilot valve, the low lubricating oil pressure and high lubricating oil suction shutdown mechanism.

Electro-Motive Model 567 Series Engines 

The Model 567 Series engines consist of the 12-567, 12-567A, 12-567B, 16-567,16-567A, 16-567AF and 16-567B engines. 

These engines are all the same in basic design. 

The 567 Series engine are "V" type, two cycle, fully scavenging, solid injection engines built by the Electro Mo tive Divn. of General Motors Corporation. 

The 12 cylinder 567 series engines develop 1000 and 

1200 brake horsepower at 800 RPM. 

The 16 cylinder 567,567A and 567AF engines develop 1350 brake horsepower at 800 RPM. 

The 16 cylinder 567B engines develop 1500 brake horsepower at 800 RPM.

The 567 series engines are similar in construction and the cylinders are arranged in two banks, set 450 apart, with the two opposing cylinders connected to the same throw of the crankshaft on a common bearing.

The two ends of the engine are known as the GENERATOR end and ACCESSORY end. The right side of the engine is determined by looking from the Generator end toward the Accessory end. 

Cylinder numbering begins with 1 on the Right side at the Accessory end running consecutively down the right side to the Generator end and continuing at the Accessory end down the left side.

CRANKCASE & OIL PAN The crankcase is a fabricated steel structure which includes the air box and surrounds the crankshaft. Hand hole covers along the side of the crankcase permit inspection of the bearings. In the center of the crankcase on the left side of the engine is a bayonet gauge having "High", "Low" and "Running level" marks to measure the oil in the oil pan. 

(See Plate 200-2 Cross-section of Model 567 Series Engine). 

CYLINDER HEADS 

Cylinder heads are made of alloy cast iron and are clamped in place in the counterbored, steel head retainers in the top deck of the crankcase. An indexing plate on the head fits over a dowel in the top deck to correctly locate the head. (See Plate 200-3).

OVERSPEED TRIP 

The overspeed trip mechanism is provided to stop the injection of fuel into the combustion chambers when the engine speed becomes excessive. 

When the engine speed reaches 880 RPM, the overspeed trip will shut the engine down. At 880 RPM, the fly weights

PLATE 200-3 TOP VIEW OF CYLINDER HEADS

overcome the spring tension and move out striking the trip pawls. This pushes the trip pawl out of the notch in the reset lever and the spring loaded linkage rotates the pawl camshaft on each bank of the engine. The cams on the pawl camshaft engage the rocker arm pawl of the rocker arms holding them clear of the camshaft thus stopping fuel injection immediately. 

The overspeed trip is manually reset by means of a shaft which projects from the cover on the overspeed trip housing, and must be turned in a counter clockwise direction until the trip pawl snaps into the notch in the reset lever. (Plate 200-4). 

The engine oil system consists of 3 parts: 

1. Lubricating oil pump, oil switch and relief valve; 

2. Piston cooling oil pump and "p" pipes; 

3. Scavenging oil pump and oil separator.

No relief valve is provided on the piston cooling oil system. The pressure varies according to engine speed and temperature and the viscosity of the oil. 

The lubricating oil pump is connected to the oil supply tank by a suction line. A spring loaded relief valve, mounted on the pump drive housing thru which the oil flows to the main bearing manifold. The spring pressure is adjusted so that oil enters the main bearing manifold at the required pressure at all times. Surplus oil is by-passed into the pump drive housing. (Plate-200-5).

The scavenging pump draws oil out of the sump in the pan and pumps oil thru the oil filters and cooler back to the tank.

Overflowing the tank baffle, the lube oil fills the suction strainer housing. The excess oil not required by these pumps flows back to the oil pan.

The pump located above the crankshaft is the pressure pump and serves two purposes. The outer portion supplies the piston cooling oil while the inner portion supplies oil for lubricating the entire engine. 

The oil separator is mounted between the blowers.

Air is drawn thru the oil separator from the blower intake. A metal screen in the separator, separates any oil from the vapor which is taken from the crankcase and drains the solid oil back to the crankcase.

The lubricating oil system hold approximately 110 gallons on the 12 cylinder engine and 180 gallons on the 16 cylinder engine.

cylinder heads and liners, the water manifolds in the engine, the water pumps and the cooling radiators. 

The water pump draws the water from the tank and discharges it into the lower part of the cylinder liner. It passes up thru the cylinder head thru the outlet manifold to the radiators and then thru the oil cooler back to the tank. 

The cooling system on a 12 cylinder engine holds 190 gallons of water and the 16 cylinder engine holds 245 gallons of water. (See Plate 200-2).

The water pumps are mounted on the front of the engine on all 567 series engines.

FUEL SYSTEM 

Fuel oil is drawn from the tank through a filter to the fuel pump and then is forced thru additional filters to the fuel manifold after passing thru the injectors where a portion is used, the fuel flows to a cross manifold which conveys it back to the fuel tank thru a 5 pound relief valve and sight glass.

Do not attempt to stop an engine by shutting off the fuel pump as damage to the injectors may result.

SINTERED BRONZE FILTERS 

This filter is mounted on the front end of the engine. 

The two sintered bronze filters are connected by a three-way valve so that flow of fuel thru the filter can be controlled by the position of the valve handle. When the handle is moved to the left, fuel flows thru the right hand element. Fuel flows thru left hand element when the handle is moved to the right.

There are two sight glasses mounted on top of the filter element (On 567B Engines). The one nearest the engine shows the fuel returning from the engine fuel manifold. The one away from the engine is the 60 pound sight glass because it receives fuel oil by-passed thru the relief valve in the event the sintered bronze fuel filter elements are dirty and the fuel pump discharge pressure reaches approximately 60 p.s.i. (Plate 200-6).

GOVERNORS AND ENGINE SPEED CONTROL 

The governor is a device for controlling the speed of the engine as determined by the position of the main throttle. The governor is mounted on the front (accessory) end of the engine on the governor drive housing which contains a 90° bevel gear drive. The bevel gear is driven from a large spur gear on the accessory drive gear train by means of a serrated shaft. The governor drive is lubricated by means of drilled passages in the housing.

The governor speed is controlled by direct connection between the speed adjusting shaft on the governor and the throttle handle, by an electro-pneumatic device, or by an electro-hydraulic device. The woodward governors are used. (Plate 200-7).

The speed control mechanism in reality only applies a predetermined amount of pressure to the speeder spring. This linkage is actuated by electro-pneumatic or electro-hydraulic valves. The 567B engines use the electro-hydraulic control. 

However, the following throttle sequence chart is the same in either case.

Engine Speed Chart (See Plate 200-7)

PILOT VALVE, INJECTION RACKS AND LINKAGE 

The pilot valve is a device for controlling the flow of oil to the vane motor of the load regulator. The pilot valve in electro-hydraulic governor is embodied in the governor housing. In the electro-pneumatic governor the pilot valve is located in front of and adjacent to the governor. In each case the operation of the pilot valve depends on the action of the governor. The pilot valve, by means of linkages connecting it to the governor speeder shaft and the power piston, causes the load regulator to adjust the generator loading to any engine speed (Throttle setting).

The movement of the governor power piston is transmitted thru lever and link connections to the injector control shaft on each cylinder bank. The link between each injector control rack lever and the layshaft is adjustable for setting the injector control rack.

GENERAL DESCRIPTION 

The Winton 900 H. P. model 12-201A diesel is a two cycle "V" type, 12 cylinder, solid injection engine developing 900 brake horsepower at 750 revolutions per minute. The cylinders are arranged in two banks of six cylinders each, set 60 degrees apart with two opposing cylinders connected to the same throw on the crankshaft.

The two ends of the engine are known as GENERATOR end and ACCESSORY or BLOWER end. The right side of the engine is determined by standing at ACCESSORY or BLOWER end and looking towards GENERATOR end. Cylinder numbering begins with 1 on the left side at the ACCESSORY or BLOWER end, runs consecutively down the left-hand side to the GENERATOR end, then continues from the ACCESSORY or BLOWER end down the right hand side.

CRANKCASE The crankcase is an entirely welded steel frame and serves as the backbone of the engine as well as a reservoir for the scavenging air from the blower. As the pistons pass the cylinder liner ports on their downward stroke, air from the air box rushes into the liner forcing the exhaust gases out around the exhaust valves. This air in the air box is supplied at approximately 3 to 5 pounds pressure. Inspection covers on top of the crankcase between the banks open into the air box. These covers permit inspection of pistons and liners. Inspection covers on each side of the crankcase permit entry to the main and connecting rod bearings for inspection and work on the bearings. FLYWHEEL The flywheel is mounted at the rear, or generator end, of the engine and the generator coupling and generator cooling fan are bolted to the flywheel.

The lubricating oil serves two purposes:

to keep the metal

parts from coming in contact with each other, and to cool. 

Oil naturally absorbs heat and this heat is carried by the scavenging oil and is dissipated in the oil cooler. The lubricating oil system has two separate functions: the scavening system takes the oil from the engine and returns it through the oil cooler and the filters to the oil reservoir. The pressure system takes the oil from the oil reservoir and supplies it under pressure to the engine. The engine is lubricated by a force feed system to all main bearings and rocker assemblies on the cylinder head. The cylinder walls are lubricated by the oil spray thrown out from the lower end of the connecting rod. The connecting rods are hollow bored through which oil is forces from the crankshaft to an oil spray tip on the top end under the piston which helps cool the piston. The crankcase is kept nearly dry of oil at all times, this being known as a dry sump type of engine. The lubricating oil system holds approximately 110 gallons of lubricating oil. 

With hot oil the oil pressure should normally register 10 to 15 pounds at idling speed and approx. 25 pounds at full speed of 750 RPM.

OIL SEPARATOR 

A passage below the blower carries fumes from the crankcase to the blower intake. In this line is located the oil separator which removes any oil which might be carried over. 

The oil separator is a fine mesh screen filter which condenses the oil. This oil is piped back to the blower sump.

COOLING SYSTEM 

Water is drawn through the centrifugal pump and is forced out two headers, one on each side of the engine, each header having six connections through water nipples to the cylinder liners. After entering the liners the water passes upward cooling the cylinder and into the cylinder head around the exhaust valves and exhaust stack elbows. It then leaves the engine passing into a header which is coupled to the radiators where it gives up its heat and is returned to the engine. The cooling system holds approximately 200 gallons of water.

CAMSHAFT DRIVE 

The camshaft is driven by a double width roller chain. 

The automatic chain take-up is in the "loose" side of the chain. This provides take-up of any slack which developes in the chain. 

The camshaft, with its integral cams, consists of two sections and are supported in split type bearing shells. 

The bearings are steel backed and babbit faced. Two cylinders fire together every 600 of flywheel rotation.

OVERSPEED TRIP 

The overspeed governor is located on the right bank of cylinders and driven by gears from the camshaft. It operates in conjunction with the main governor and causes the main governor to stop injection of fuel when the engine speed reaches 825 R.P.M. It has a reset trip which must be reset by hand before any fuel can be injected and engine started. 

The overspeed governor may also be used to stop the engine by pulling up the overspeed trip manually.

GOVERNOR WOODWARD SI 

The Woodward SI governor is used and is driven from the front of the engine through a 90 degrees gear drive. It is a centrifugal hydraulic type allowing very close speed regulations. The main throttle is not connected to the injector linkage. The work of moving the injector racks is preformed by the hydraulic piston in the governor known as the servo-motor. 

FUEL OIL SYSTEM 

Fuel is drawn by suction from the fuel tank going through a purolator filter before it reaches the pump. A rotary type pump is used which forces the fuel through filters to the engine. At the engine the fuel enters a manifold on each bank of cylinders and then passes through a strainer to the injectors. After passing through the injectors where a portion is used the fuel flows to a return line which conveys it back to the tank.

Do not stop the engine by shutting off the fuel pump as damage to the injectors may result.

GENERAL DESCRIPTION 

The Winton, model 8-201A diesel is a two-cycle, 8 cylinder, solid injection, verticle type engine with 8 cylinders in a line, developing 600 brake horsepower at 750 revolutions per minute.

The two ends of the engine are known as the blower or accesory end (front) and flywheel or generator end (rear). 

Cylinders are numbered from blower end starting with cylinder 

No.1 and run consecutively to cylinder No. 8 at generator end. The right and left sides of the engine are determined by standing at the blower end and looking toward the generator end. (Plate 400-2). 

HARMONIC BALANCER 

The harmonic balancer is located on the blower end of the crankshaft between the crankcase and the blower. It consists of a two-piece hub, laminated springs and a rim and acts to dampen torsional vibration inherent in crankshafts. 

The lubricating oil serves two purposes: to keep the metal parts from coming in contact with each other, and to cool. 

Oil naturally absorbs heat and this heat is carried by the scavenging oil and is dissipated in the oil cooler. The lubricating oil system has two separate functions; the scavenging system takes the oil from the engine and returns it through the oil cooler and the filters to the oil reservoir. The pressure system takes the oil from the oil reservoir and supplies it under pressure to the engine. 

The engine is lubricated by a force feed system to all main bearings and rocker assemblies on the cylinder head. 

The cylinder walls are lubricated by the oil spray thrown out from the lower end of the connecting rods. The connecting rods are hollow-bored through which oil is forced from the crankshaft to an oil spray tip on the top end under the piston which helps cool the piston. The crankcase is kept nearly dry of oil at all times, this being known as a dry sump engine. The lubricating oil system holds approximately 

60 gallons of lubricating oil. With hot oil the oil pressure should normally register 15 to 18 pounds at idling speed

and approximately 30 lbs. at full speed of 750 R.P.M. If lubricating oil pressure drops below 15 pounds per square inch with engine at full throttle shut engine down, and do not operate until condition is corrected. 

OIL SEPARATOR 

A passage below the blower carries fumes from the crankcase to the blower intake. In this line is located the oil separator which removes any oil which might be carried over. The oil separator is a fine mesh screen which condenses the oil. This oil is piped back to the blower sump.

COOLING SYSTEM 

Water is drawn through the centrifugal pump and is forced into the water inlet manifold on the left side of the engine. 

From the manifold water is delivered through individual sleeves to the bottom of each cylinder liner. It then passes upward cooling the cylinder and into the cylinder head around the exhaust valves and exhaust stack elbow. It then leaves the engine passing into an outlet manifold which is coupled to the radiators where the excess heat is removed and the water is returned to the engine. The cooling system holds approximately 150 gallons of water. 

GOVERNOR 

WOODWARD SI 

The speed of the engine is controlled by a Woodward SI governor, which is connected to the fuel injector racks by necessary linkage. The governor is driven from the front end of the camshaft through a 900 bevel gear. The governor is a centrifugal hydraulic type allowing very close speed regulation. The main throttle which is connected directly to the speed adjusting lever of the governor is not connected to the injector linkage. The work of moving the injector racks is performed by the hydraulic piston in the governor known as the servo-motor. 

FUEL OIL SYSTEM 

Fuel is drawn by suction from the fuel tank going through a filter before it reaches the pump. A rotary type pump is used which forced the fuel through filters to the engine. At the engine the fuel enters a manifold and then is forced through a strainer to each injector. After passing through the injectors where a portion is used the fuel flows to a return line which conveys it back to the tank. Do not stop the engine by shutting off the fuel pump as damage to the injectors may result.

Firing Order 

The cylinders are arranged vertically in a straight line. Crank throws are spaced 120 degrees apart, having separate crankpin for each cylinder. The generator end or flywheel end of the engine is the rear, the other the front. 

Cylinders are numbered starting with No. 1 at the front of the engine and are numbered consecutively toward the generator with No. 6 next to the generator. The "air" side is the right side of the engine looking from the generator end. The camshaft is located on the "air" side and the fuel pumps on the "exhaust" side. (Plate 600-2).

The flywheel is mounted at the generator end of the engine and the generator coupling and cooling fan are bolted to the flywheel.

CRANKCASE The crankcase is a steel structure which supports the engine block and surrounds the crankshaft. Removable covers are provided along the side of the crankcase for inspection of the bearings. In the crankcase on the exhaust side is a bayonet gauge with "high" and "low" marks to measure oil in the crankcase. Oil level should be maintained within 1/2 inch of the high mark. The oil pan is located below the crankcase and is a part of this structure. The oil pan sump holds the main supply of engine lubricating oil. 

OVERSPEED TRIP MECHANISM 

The overspeed trip mechanism stops the injection of fuel into the combustion chambers when the engine exceed 10% of its normal rated speed.

The overspeed trip device consists of two spring-loaded weights which fly out when centrifugal force becomes great enough to overcome the spring tension. When this point is reached the weights move outward and hit a trigger releasing the cutout shaft which shifts endwise allowing the cutout

PLATE 600-5 EXHAUST SIDE-1000 HORSE POWER ENGINE EXHAUST SIDE 1000 HORSEPOWER ENGINE L. GOVERNOR 2.MECHANICAL CONTROL 3. FUEL PUMP CONTROLS 4. GEAR TRAIN 5. WATER PUMP 7. FUEL PUMP DRIVE 8. FUEL OIL FILTER 6. OVERSPEED STOP 9. SAFETY DEVICE IO. LUBRICATING OIL PUMP II. TURBOCHARGER

plungers to stop fuel injection. (Plate 600-3). 

To reset the overspeed safety device, pull out on the resetting handle located on the front of the fuel pump drive casing at the governor end, until the trigger inside the casing latches with the end of the pin. When properly set, the handle on the governor end of the fuel pump drive casing will be in a vertical position. 

The cutout buttons are now reset by pulling out the buttons at each pump until the trip clears the finger on the rack, and then turn the button to the right and engage the trip pin in the first notch cut in the rack. When set in this position, the dog on the cutout plunger button will be crosswise to the slot cut in the cover. Restart the engine in the usual manner. 

The lubricating oil serves two purposes: to keep the metal parts from coming in contact with each other and to cool. The system holds 120 gallons and the oil supply is contained in the crankcase sump, making this a "wet sump" type of engine. The oil pump is located at the front end of the engine and is driven from the crankshaft. The rotors in the pump are steel gears closely fitted within the pump housing. 

The pump removes the oil from the sump and forces it through the filtering and oil cooling system to the lubricating oil header inside the engine. From the header, oil is distributed to the main crankshaft bearings and the hollow push rods. 

From the main bearings oil flows through the hollow drilled crankshaft, lubricating the connecting rod bearings and piston pins. Part of the oil is used to cool the pistons. The oil which passed up through the hollow push rods travels into rocker arms lubricating valve rocker assemblies.

All the oil drains back into the crankcase, then it is picked up by the pressure pump and forced through the system repeating the cycle. A 35-pound pressure relief valve is located between the oil cooling radiators and the engine to regulate the lubricating oil pressure to the desired limits. 

Locomotives 50 and 50A have EMD oil coolers in place of the oil radiators.

COOLING SYSTEM 

The engine cooling water is circulated by a centrifugal water pump, fitted with a single inlet shrouded impeller. 

Water flows by gravity from the bottom of the cooling radiators into the water pump. The pump forces water into the block (around the cylinder liners) and upward into the cylinder head water passages, then into a common outlet header. From the outlet header the water is carried through branch lines to the radiators. After passing through the

A water expansion tank is connected between the water outlet header and the return pipe to the pump. A vent pipe for the system is connected to the expansion tank and extends through the roof. 

FUEL OIL SYSTEM 

The Pump and Nozzle type injection system is employed on these engines. (See Part 1, Section 1).

Fuel oil is drawn by the motor-driven auxiliary pump from the main tank under the locomotive, through the check valve, the emergency trip and duplex strainer to the intake side of the pump. It is forced by the pump through the filtering unit located on the side of the diesel engine, then to the fuel oil intake header mounted on the fuel injection pumps. On the other end of the manifold is a line to return excess fuel to the tank. The manifold or header feeds the fuel pump plunger which forces the oil under high pressure to the nozzles.

There is 35-pound regulating valve in the return line which acts to regulate the fuel oil pressure in the manifold.

GENERAL DESCRIPTION The De La Vergne 8 cylinder model VO diesel is a 4 cycle, solid injection, vertical engine with 8 cylinders in line and develops 1000 horsepower at 625 R.P.M.

The generator end of the engine is the front and the pump end is the rear. Cylinders are numbered starting at the drive end with No. 1 and numbered consecutively to No. 8 at the pump end. The right side of the engine is determined looking from the generator end. The fuel pumps are on the right side. 

The flywheel is mounted on the generator end and the generator coupling and cooling fan are bolted to the flywheel. 

ENGINE BED PLATE 

The half-round pan beneath the engine frame is of welded steel construction and holds the crankcase lubricating oil. 

The pan is connected to the flange at the bottom of the engine frame by bolts. The oil level gauge rod and filler pipe are located at the right rear corner of the oil pan, by No. 8 cylinder. (Plate 700-2).

GOVERNOR DRIVE AND OVERSPEED TRIP 

The governor is driven from the camshaft through a 90° gear drive. 

The overspeed trip is mounted adjacent to the governor and is gear driven from the engine camshaft. This device consists of a spring loaded weight which is normally against its inner stop. When the centrifugal force of the weight becomes greater than the spring tension (690 R. P.M. engine speed) the weight moves outward to release a lever which turns, moving the injector pump control racks to stop position, shutting the engine down. (Plate 700-3).

The oil supply is held within the engine base oil pan and an oil filler connection and oil level gauge rod is located on the left rear corner of the engine near the floor at No. 8 cylinder. The system contain 105 gallons. 

The displacement type oil pump is located at the rear end of the engine and driven by a roller chain from the crankshaft. From the sump the oil is circulated through a suction strainer into the pump. Leaving the pump, part of the oil passes through a radiator and a metal edge type strainer then into the engine and part of the oil goes through a lubricating oil filter into the crankcase. The oil is supplied to the camshaft chain drive, pump chain drive, valve rocker arms, push rods, main bearings and connecting rod bearings. The cams on the camshaft, rollers, rocker arm guides and fuel pumps are splash lubricated with oil in the camshaft trough. The camshaft and connecting rod bearings are supplied from a manifold with small connecting supply pipes to each main bearing housing. 

All the oil returns from the engine oil system to the supply sump by gravity.

A relief valve between the radiators and engine maintains the lubricating oil pressure between 35 and 45 lbs.

COOLING SYSTEM 

The engine cooling water is circulated by a centrifugal pump fitted with a single inlet shrouded impeller. The pump is driven by a chain from the crankshaft on the rear of the engine. Water is drawn from the bottom of the radiators into the pump and forced into the inlet manifold which runs into the block and around the cylinder liners. The water moves from the block into the cylinder heads then from the top of the heads into the outlet water manifold and returns to the top of the radiators. An expansion tank is located at the top of the radiators and connecting pipes from the intake and outlet manifolds run into this tank. An overflow pipe and a vent pipe from the vent box on the engine outlet manifold is connected to this tank.

The entire system should be full of water at all times and a water glass to determine the water level is located in the cab. This gauge gives a water level reading only when the valve on the pipe leading to the water glass is open just below the water glass. If the water goes below the safe water level mark on the gauge the engine should be shut down and not worked until system is refilled. The cooling system is also equipped with a gauge for indicating the temperature of the cooling water. 

FUEL OIL SYSTEM 

Fuel oil is drawn by a motor driven pump from the main

It is forced through the filtering unit into a pipe which supplies each injector unit. The excess oil to the header and the leakage from the pumps is returned to the tank through a pipe with 25 lb. relief valve. The relief valve puts back pressure against the injectors. The injection system is of the mechanical type with fuel pumps, located above camshaft at each cylinder and nozzles in each cylinder head.

ENGINE SHUT DOWN DEVICE 

This device consists of a lubricating oil operated electric switch, an electric solenoid operated double acting fuel oil valve and a piston operated shut down unit which is connected to the engine fuel injection pump control shaft. 

During normal operation the lubricating oil switch is held in the closed position by lubricating oil pressure, thus permitting the electric current to energize the solenoid, which keeps the fuel valve open and allows fuel to flow to the piston operated shut down unit. This allows free travel of the fuel racks. 

If the lubricating oil pressure drops below 18 pounds the solenoid is de-energized which releases fuel oil pressure from the shut down piston and the spring behind the shut down piston forces the engine fuel injection pump control shaft to the shut down position which stops the engine. 

A torsion spring located at the governor end of the control shaft permits the shaft to turn to the stop position independently of the governor.

The engine must not be operated when oil pressure is less than 18 pounds.

During the starting period, when there is no pressure in the lubricating oil header to close the lubricating oil switch, the solenoid circuit is closed by means of an auxiliary switch connected to the starting button in the cab. 

After engine is turning over and the starting button is released the auxiliary switch is also opened. Sufficient pressure has then been built up in the lubricating oil system to keep the lubricating oil switch closed.

Before starting the engine the motor drive fuel supply pump must be in operation so that fuel under pressure is available to release the piston shut down unit, otherwise the fuel injection pump control shaft will remain in stop position and the engine will not start.

GENERAL DESCRIPTION 

The Caterpillar D-17000 diesel engine is of the fourcycle 8-cylinder V type construction using solid injection. 

The cylinders are arranged in two banks, each bank consisting of four cylinders, two of which are cast integrally in one block. The banks are set 45 degrees apart with the two operating cylinders connected to the same throw of the crankshaft. 

The flywheel end of the engine is the rear end of the engine and the fan end is the front end. The left side of the engine, determined by looking from the front end, contains the compression release lever and lubricating oil pump. 

Firing order of the engine is 1-8-5-4-7-2-3-6, which is determined in the following manner. Viewed from the front or fan end of the engine the cylinders are from front to back left side 1-3-5-7, right side 2-4-6-8. (Plate 800-2 and 800-3).

OIL PAN 

The oil pan is a structure of cast steel, bolted to the bottom of the crankcase and provided with two sumps. The upper sump acts as a settling basin to trap some of the foreign matter before it overflows into the lower or main engine sump. Each sump has a drain and the lower sump is provided with a screen to keep dirt, lint and other particles which may have passed the upper sump from entering the scavenging pumps. 

THE  

The lubricating oil serves two purposes, to keep the metal parts from coming in contact with each other and to cool. Oil naturally absorbs heat and this heat is dissipated in the oil cooler just before it enters the engine. The "Triplex Lubricating System" as it has been termed is a full flow filter system employing the pressure and scavenging pumps, an oil reservoir tank, Michiana single element filter, three single element metallic filters and an oil cooler. The crankcase oil pan is designed with two oil sumps to protect

The pressure pump secures its oil from the oil reservoir and pumps it thru the oil cooler and metallic filters before delivering it to the engine. 

The scavenging pump, having volume capacity greater than the pressure pump, removes the oil from the crankcase sump and pumps it continuously thru the Michiana filter and the reservoir. A return overflow line returns the excess oil not needed by the pressure pump back to the crankcase sump. The lubricating oil system holds 35 gallons, Engine oil pressure is automatically maintained at 30 to 35 lbs. by means of a bypass valve in the upper oil pan fitting inside the crankcase on the right side of the engine. 

COOLING SYSTEM 

The capacity of the cooling system is 40 gallons. 

Water is drawn from the bottom of the radiators by the pumps and delivered into the bottom of the cylinder blocks. 

Here it surrounds the cylinder liners and flows upward into the cylinder heads and out of the return water manifold. 

From the return water manifold it flows through a thermostat set at 1600 and then into the top of the radiators. 

FUEL OIL SYSTEM 

The fuel transfer pump picks up fuel from the locomotive fuel tank and forces it through absorbent type filters mounted on the left side of the engine, which remove dirt and other foreign particles. From there the fuel is supplied to the fuel pump manifold for the individual fuel pumps. The fuel injector pumps meter and force the fuel through the fuel injection lines to the fuel injector valves in the cylinder head. A return line from the transfer pump to the supply tank takes care of the fuel in excess of that needed to run the engine. 

To prime the fuel system use hand priming pump located on lower right front corner of engine and follow instructions un der Fuel Oil System in Part 2, Sec. 13. 

COMPRESSION RELEASE LEVER 

A compression release lever is provided to relieve the cylinder compression so that the engine may be cranked more easily before starting.

This lever turns a shaft running along the upper part of the cylinder block, just below the cylinder head joint. This

GENERAL DESCRIPTION 

This two cycle engine is of the opposed piston type. Air from a blower is introduced into the cylinder and compressed between the two pistons which work vertically toward each other in each cylinder. A charge of fuel under pressure is sprayed into the cylinder by the two injection nozzles after the air has been compressed. Ignition is accomplished by the heat of compression. 

The upper and lower pistons drive separate crankshafts which are interconnected by a vertical drive gear with flexible coupling. 

Fresh air is admitted to the cylinder, and exhaust gases are expelled by the pistons uncovering and covering the inlet and exhaust ports near the upper and lower ends of the cylinder. The vertical drive connection of the two crankshafts is made with the lower crankshaft advanced in operating position ahead of the upper crankshaft. Illustration shows the lower crankshaft 120 past outer dead center and the upper crankshaft on outer dead center. The difference is this crankshaft setting is called the "LOWER CRANK LEAD.

The combustion space is formed between recessed heads of the two pistons as the crankshafts approach inner dead center. 

When the upper crank is approximately 60 before inner dead center and the lower crank is 60 past inner dead center, the two pistons are closest together in operating position and the point midway between them is called the "Combustion Dead Center". As the pistons approach the inner dead center, fuel is injected into the combustion space. The injection starts approximately 140 before the lower crankshaft reaches inner dead center.

Observing Plate 900-1 it can be seen that when the upper piston reaches its inner dead center in the compression stroke, the lower piston has completed 120 (the total crank lead) of its power stroke. This causes the lower piston to receive a full engine load, the greater part of the expansion work with the result that about 72% of the total power is

delivered to the lower crankshaft. The remaining power is delivered to the upper crankshaft where it is partially absorbed in driving the scavenging blower. This leaves a small amount of power to be transmitted through the vertical gear drive to the lower crankshaft which is connected to the final drive. 

Oil Separator: 

The upper and lower crankshaft compartments are vented by means of a pipe connected to the suction side of the blow- In the vertical drive compartment this vent line passes through an oil separator, where a copper ribbon screen prevents oil from being carried into the blower with air from the crankcase. er. 

Fuel System: 

The fuel system consists of the supply and injection systems. The positive displacement supply pump draws fuel from the tank and delivers it through the strainer-filter to the engine inlet. The capacity of this pump is such that more fuel is pumped into the header than is needed by the injection pumps with the result that a pressure of about 15 pounds is built up and maintained in the header. A relief valve built into the header regulates the pressure allowing the excess fuel to return to the tank. The fuel oil header consists of a pipe which is connected to each injection pump. 

Fuel from the strainer-filter is circulated through the headers and returned to the tanks by the supply pump. Fuel required enters the injection pump and the excess returns to the the tank. The capacity of the supply pump is such that a sufficient velocity is obtained in the injection pump headers to insure replacement of fuel as it is used by the individual injection pumps.

Fuel Injection System: 

The 90 16 The fuel injection system consists of two injection_pumps two nozzles and the neccesary tubes for each cylinder. pumps and nozzles are enclosed in the cylinder block and are located symmetrically with one pump and nozzle on each side ofǝbi every cylinder. (See Plate 900-2).

Each injection pump received fuel oil from the supply sysyl tem at the fuel inlet port. A measured quantity of fuel is compressed and delivered through the injection tubes to the nozzle. The injection pump control rod actuates the control racks of the pumps to regulate the amount of fuel to be delive ered. 

The fuel control rods on each side of the engine are cor 91 nected by suitable linkage to the governor at the control enclo of the engine. The travel of the control rod in the full fue direction is limited so that the engine cannot be overloaded [I 60.

PLATE 900-2 FUEL INJECTION SYSTEM 

Camshafts- 

Fuel Oil

The fuel control racks of the injection pumps admit the proper amount of fuel to the nozzle. The quantity remaining of the measured amount is returned to the supply header and return header.

The injection pumps are inverted and operated through tappet assemblies by individual cams on the camshaft. The camshafts are located in the upper crankshaft compartment of the cylinder block. They are driven by a silent chain from the upper crankshaft at the control end of the engine. 

A drip pan under each injection pump collects any fuel oil or lubricating oil draining from the injection pump body. The injection nozzle compartments are drained by tubes which are interconnected to the tubes from the drip pans. 

Drain oil collected in these tubes is returned to the leakage tank.

The injection pumps receive fuel at low pressure, measure it in correct amounts for injection, build up a high pressure, and deliver the fuel to the injection nozzles. 

On the down stroke of the injection pump plunger, fuel enters the injection nozzle and enters the chamber at the end of the needle. The fuel under pressure, acting against the face of the needle at the valve seat and at the shoulder will lift the needle from its seat. 

This action is counteracted by the spring thru the push rod. The fuel pressure will exceed the spring pressure and fuel will pass thru the (.020") holes in the nozzle tip and be injected into the combustion space of the cylinder in a fine spray.

Lubricating System: (See Plate 900-3) 

The lubricating oil pump is mounted on the control end of the engine, and is driven thru gears and a flexible coupling by the lower crankshaft.

The pump draws oil from the oil pan and forces it thru the filters and coolers. Leaving the cooler the oil is piped to the engine, where it enters the lower lubricating oil header and flows thru the lower header toward the blower end. 

There, a vertical pipe carries the oil to the upper header. 

Thru supply pipes from both lower and upper headers, oil is forced to each main bearing and thence, thru tubes swedged into the crankshaft to each crankpin bearing. From each crankpin bearing, oil passes thru the drilled passage in the connecting rod to the piston pin bearings and to the piston oil cooling pockets. 

The surfaces between the thrust bearing shells and the crankshaft flanges are lubricated thru openings in the thrust bearing shells.

The cooling oil from each lower piston is discharged thru the piston cooling oil outlet into the oil pan. Oil from each upper piston is discharged thru the piston cooling outlet into the compartment around the upper ends of the cylinders. This oil can then drain either toward the blower end or the control end, and down to the oil pan. 

The two camshafts receive lubrication from the upper oil header. The camshafts are hollow, and small openings at each bearing allow oil to reach the bearing surfaces. Lubricating oil for the timing chain is supplied by an opening in the end of each camshaft and by the excess oil from the number one main bearing. The oil spray from the timing chain provides adequate lubrication for the bearings of the idler sprockets, the control mechanisms and the drive gears and bearings of the governor, water, fuel and lubrication oil pumps.

The drive bushings of the flexible pump drive (on the control end of the lower crankshaft) receive lubrication thru an opening in the lower crankshaft from the control end main bearing.

Oil from the upper engine compartment enters the injection pump and lubricates the tappet assembly. The excess oil is drained thru leads to a return header which conducts the oil to the control end compartment. 

The blower drive gears are lubricated by sprays of oil from special nozzles located on each side of the center line of the engine. These nozzles are part of the oil piping connecting the lower and upper headers. 

The vertical drive gears and pinions are lubricated by a spray of oil from nozzles which are connected to the upper and lower oil headers by means of tubes. 

Having performed its various functions, lubricating oil not used drips down into the oil pan below the lower crankshaft. It is then ready to repeat the cycle set up by the suction of the lubricating oil pump. Minimum lubricating oil pressure is 15 pounds per square inch at full throttle and 8 pounds at idle.

COOLING SYSTEM 

Entering the engine thru an inlet in each exhaust nozzle, the water moves thru passages which surround the exhaust nozzles, and on into the exhaust manifold water passages extending the full length of the engine. The exhaust passages from the cylinder liners and the lower part of the liner are also cooled by the water travel thru the exhaust belts. The water enters from the exhaust manifolds at openings at the lower side of the belts and returns to the manifolds at openings at the top. 

The water rises from the exhaust manifolds to pass thru an inlet elbow on each side of each cylinder. These elbows carry the water to the space between the cylinder liner and its jacket.

Cast-In ribs on the cylinder liner direct the water upward to cool the liner. Water passages also lead to the water jackets on the injection nozzle and cylinder relief valves to cool these units.

Upon reaching the top of the liner jacket, the water passes out of the cylinder space thru an outlet pipe which leads to the water header.

This header, rectangular in cross-section extends along the opposite side of the cylinder block from the control mechanism. Its outlet flange is at the control end of the engine. 

Exhaust System: 

The exhaust system conducts the gases from the engine combustion ports to the atmosphere. (See Plate 900-4). The hot gases are forced out of the cylinder liner when the lower pistons uncover the exhaust ports. They enter individual exhaust ports. They enter individual exhaust belts connected to the exhaust manifolds, which are mounted in the cylinder block on each side of the engine. The gases pass on thru the two exhaust nozzles at the control end of the engine to the exhaust pipe muffler and on to the atmosphere. 

GOVERNORS 

The 6 cylinder engine uses the basic SI Woodward governor with some modifications. These include a carbonstat pilot valve added to side of power cylinder, shut down mechanism added to top, carbonstat pilot valve adjusting plunger is added to link end of piston rod and speed stop arms are added to speed adjusting screw.

Movement of the speed control shaft is controlled by the actuator thru the governor speed control rod. The segment on the control shaft in turn moves the speeder plug to change compression of the speeder spring which controls engine speed.

The function of the air operated shut-down device is to give a means of shutting down the engine from the cab. The shut-down cylinder and by-pass valves built into the top of the power cylinder are controlled by the "Rotair" valve on the control panel. In shutting engine down, air is admitted on top of power cylinder, forcing power piston down and shutting off fuel supply to nozzles.

ALCO 16 CYLINDER MODEL 244B ENGINE 

The Model 244B 12 and 16 cylinder Diesel engine is a "V" type with 450 angle between banks. The engine is single acting, turbo-supercharged, of four stroke cycle having an open combustion chamber. An individual fuel injection pump and injector nozzle is provided for each cylinder.

Each cylinder requires two engine revolutions or four strokes of the piston to complete one working cycle. The four strokes are for intake, compression, power and exhaust. 

The engine has an all welded steel frame. Full pressure lubrication of all parts is provided. A closed cooling system; the water flows successively thru the engine, the radiators and the lubricating oil cooler and is circulated by the engine-driven water pump. Lubricating oil is cooled by water in the heat exchanger and water by fan cooled radiators. Thermostatically controlled radiator shutters and fan automatically maintain desired engine temperature. 

Pistons are numbered from the front of the engine down each cylinder bank. The generator end of the engine is the rear end. OIL PAN The main base which forms the oil pan is of welded construction. The main frame holds the upper halves of the main bearings and is bolted to the main base forming the crankcase and oil pan. The oil pan is the reservoir for lubricating oil the engine. is to limit the engine R.P.M. to a predetermined speed. This is done by an off-center weight, adjustable to the desired overspeed condition, which moves outward from centrifugal force, engaging the overspeed switch causing the engine governor to go to the no fuel position. The overspeed mechanism and the engine tachometer generators are on the same shaft and assembly. (Plate 1000-3)

FUEL SYSTEM 

The engine is equipped with separate injector pumps and injector nozzles.

The fuel is drawn from the main supply thru the suction filters to the fuel pump. From the discharge side of the fuel pump, fuel goes first thru another filter to the right bank header, which feeds right bank injection pumps. It crosses over at the generator end to the left bank. The pressure regulating valve at the end of the left bank header, regulates pressure in the headers to 35 pounds per sq. inch. The fuel pressure regulator on the discharge side of the pump is set at 75 p.s.i. for protection of the pump. Both pressure regulating valves return fuel to the main supply tank. (Plate 1000-4)

The lube oil system has a capacity of 230 gallons. Since the oil in the system is stored in the engine base, it is classified as a wet sump engine. The oil pump draws oil from the engine sump and discharges it past the pressure regulating valve to the lube oil filter. From the lube oil filter the oil flows thru the cooler to the strainer. From the strainer the oil flows to the engine. Before the oil enters the main header a portion is piped off to lubricate the valve lever mechanism, camshaft bearings, gear trains and turbosupercharger bearings. The oil pressure gage and oil pressure switches 1 and 2 are located in a line off the far end of the main header. Individual jumper pipes conduct the oil to each main bearing. A drilled passage in the crankshaft allows oil to flow to the adjacent connecting rod bearing where it lubricates the bearing, piston pin and bushing. Oil flows from the piston pin thru holes in the piston head cooling the piston. (Plate 1000-5)

A Bayonet gage is located on the right side of the engine for checking oil level. Lube oil pressure should be approximately 35 to 50 p.s.i. in Run 8. Engine should not be operated with lube oil pressure below 22 p.s.i.

COOLING SYSTEM 

Cooling water is circulated by a centrifugal pump and flows from the pump to right and left bank headers of the engine. On coming out of the engine the water is collected in

PLATE 1000-3 OVERSPEED CONTROL

the outlet headers and conveyed thru the cooling radiators thru the lube oil cooler and back to the water pump. 

The inlet headers are connected to each cylinder by individual water jumpers. It circulates thru the cylinder water jacket into the cylinder head. From the cylinder head the water flows into the outlet header. The cooling water system holds 330 gallons.

The high temperature switch is located in the outlet water header. The Grad-U-Stat is located between the radiators and oil cooler. The Grad-U-Stat controls, both the Eddy Current Clutch to the cooling fans and the radiator shutters. 

Water temperature should run from 140 to 180 degrees. F.

TURBOCHARGER The turbocharger consists of a centrifugal blower driven by an exhaust gas turbine. Both the blower and turbine are mounted on a common rotor shaft. The turbocharger is lubricated by engine oil pressure. Air to the turbocharger passes thru an air filter silencer into the turbocharger where it is compressed and supplied to the Diesel engine intake manifolds at a pressure of approximately 17 p.s.i. at full load.

GENERAL DESCRIPTION 

The Baldwin Diesel engine is a four stroke cycle engine built as a 6 cylinder normally aspirated engine and as a 6 or 8 cylinder engine of the supercharged model. It has separate fuel injection pumps and injectors for each cylinder. pumps are engine driven with exception of the fuel oil supply pump which is motor driven.

All The turbocharged engine permits a higher horsepower than a normally aspirated engine. The energy of the exhaust gas is used to drive a gas turbine which is directly coupled to a centrifugal blower. This blower supplies air to the engine cylinders under pressure of from 5-1/2 to 7 p.s.i. This air helps to scavenge hot gases from the cylinder and increases the amount of air in the cylinder which permits burning of larger quantities of fuel, thus a higher horsepower rating is obtained. 

OVERSPEED CONTROL 

The overspeed stop is a device, which functions to shutdown the engine in the event the engine should exceed the maximum safe speed. It is driven by bevel gears from the

09004 

1. GOVERNOR ACTUATOR 2. GOVERNOR LINKAGE 3. GOVERNOR 

4. OVERSPEED STOP 

5. FUEL CONTROL SHAFT 6. FUEL INJECTION PUMP PLATE 1100-2 OVERSPEED CONTROL

camshaft at twice engine speed and is located directly below the engine governor. The overspeed stop is set to trip at an engine speed approximately 10% higher than rated maximum speed. 

An off-center weight balanced by spring is set to trip the overspeed mechanism. This action, operating thru the fuel injector control shaft moves the injector pump racks to the stop position. To reset the overspeed stop, insert a 1/2 inch bar or a screwdriver into the housing to engage the hole in the tripping lever, Pry downward until the trip mechanism is reengaged. (Plate 1100-2).

FUEL SYSTEM 

The engine is equipped with separate fuel injectors and fuel injection pumps. (See Part 1, Sec. 1).

The fuel oil is pumped from the supply tank by means of a motor driven centrifugal supply pump. A strainer is located on the suction side of the pump and a cartridge type filter is located in the pressure line to the injection pumps. 

An emergency cut-off valve is located in the fuel line to the pump and a 35 pound relief valve is located on the pressure side of the fuel pumps. Another relief valve located on the injection pump supply header is set at 25 pounds. The fuel oil supply header furnishes fuel oil to all injection pumps. 

A drain header collects any fuel oil leakage and returns fuel to the supply tank. There is a protective shut-down system operated by fuel oil pressure and controlled by lube oil pressure and engine temperature. This device will automatically shut the engine down in the event of low oil pressure or high engine temperature thru its connection with the injection pump control shaft. (Plate 1100-3).

A positive displacement gear pump, chain-driven from the crankshaft at the pump end of the engine draws lube oil from the engine base and circulates it thru the system. The oil passes thru a suction strainer before entering the pump. 

After leaving the pump, the oil passes thru a full flow filter, a metal edged strainer and a heat exchanger before entering the engine distributing system. 

The engine distributing system supplies oil under pressure to all engine bearings, camshaft and pump chain drives, valve rocker arms, push rods and for piston cooling. The cams, rollers and guides for the push rods and fuel pumps are splash lubricated from oil contained in the camshaft trough. 

The push rod roller guides are lubricated by oil draining down along the push rods from the valve rocker arms. The oil in the camshaft trough is replenished continuously by engine oil pressure and is kept at a constant level by means of an overflow to the engine crankcase. 

The engine base is provided with an oil filler connection and an oil level gage rod. The oil level should be

COOLING SYSTEM 

The cooling water system is of the closed type. A centrifugal pump, chain driven from the engine crankshaft, circulates the water thru the engine jackets and by means of the water jumpers thru the cylinder head. From the cylinder head the water is collected by the water outlet header and carried to the cooling radiators. From the cooling radiators the cooling water flows thru the lube oil heat exchanger and back to the engine. The system is equipped with an expansion tank from which make-up water is furnished. TURBOCHARGER The Elliott Buchi turbocharger is a self contained unit comprising a centrifugal blower and gas turbine mounted on a common shaft. The exhaust gas turbine utilizes the energy in the exhaust gases to drive the centrifugal blower. The blower furnishes all air required by the engine at low pressure thru the air intake manifold. 

The turbocharger is lubricated by engine lube oil supplied thru an independent filter. The lube oil pressure at the turbo is regulated to 30 pounds and should not be allowed to go below 20 pounds.

The The turbocharger is cooled by engine cooling water. water temperature rise should not exceed 300 F. thru the turbo. When shutting down the engine, it should be operated at idle for approximately 10 minutes in order to dissipate as much heat as possible. ECT
