Santa Fe GENERAL 

ALL LOCOMOTIVES PART II-SECTION 1

GENERAL DESCRIPTION 

A diesel-electric locomotive consists of a car to haul and house the equipment which includes; diesel engines, generators, traction motors, control apparatus and a bank of storage batteries. Some locomotives include water tanks and steam generators. The diesel engines turn the generators which makes electric current. This current operates the traction motors, geared directly to the driving axles which finally turn the wheels. Operation of the traction motors, generators and diesel engines is coordinated by a set of controls, some are automatic and others must be operated manually. When the throttle is opened the controls make the diesel engine run at the speed required to turn the generators fast enough to produce the electricity required by the traction motors. 

In freight service a locomotive is needed to move heavy trains, negotiate steep grades and operate mostly at slow to medium speeds. Freight locomotives are designed with a very high traction effort for starting and continuous operation at slow speeds.

In passenger service where high speed with lighter trains is demanded, the locomotives are designed for a greater maximum speed which results in the continuous rating of the locomotive being higher. 

In switch service where short moves at slow speed of either large or small cuts of cars is preferred, small and large switchers are used depending on the service required. 

Starting the diesel engines is accomplished by pressing the engine start button. This switch energizes the starting contactors, closing the circuit from the batteries to the main generator. The main generator then operating as a motor, cranks the engine.

The flow of current through the generator is always in the same direction. Reversing the locomotive is accomplished by reversing the direction of circuit in the traction motor fields. 

The electrical cabinets house the high and low voltage electrical control equipment. Extreme caution should be exercised and the unit should be isolated when the locomotive is operating before making inspection of this cabinet and doors should be closed during normal operation. 

On road locomotives the traction motors are numbered starting from the control end of the unit and the wheels are numbered starting from the end of the unit marked "F". 

On switching locomotives the traction motors and wheels are numbered starting from the front end of the locomotive.

Electrical Terms: 

The following are standard "terms" used in connection with the electrical equipment:

BATTERY 

AMMETER 

BATTERY 

AMMETER 

BATTERY VOLTMETER 

A volt is a unit of electrical pressure. The higher the pressure (voltage) of a battery the more current will force through a wire, coil, lamp, or other device.

LOAD The ampere is a unit of the amount of current in α circuit. 

Current flows from a source on one wire and flows back on another.

LOAD 

Resistance (measured in ohms) is the "holding back" quality of a circuit. A circuit of low resistance will allow a large current to flow and vice versa.

RESISTANCE BATTERY 

When objects are connected so that the same current flows through one after the other, they are in series. The current is the same anywhere in the circuit.

BATTERY 

MOTOR 

ARMATURE MOTOR FIELD RESISTOR 

When objects are connected across a source So that the current in each is independent of the other, they are in parallel. The current from the source is the total of the currents flowing through all objects across the line. 

If a device is connected in parallel with some equipment, thus bypassing a portion of the current which would otherwise flow through that equipment, it is in shunt. 

BATTERY

Proper Method of Handling Throttle and Control on Switching Locomotives to Avoid Rough Handling of Equipment: 

1. When preparing to couple into car, open throttle just enough to close throttle control switch. Handling of throttle in this manner will result not only in small amount of current being supplied to traction motors, but will permit locomotive to move slowly in either forward or backward motion. 

Just before couplers contact close throttle to permit locomotive to drift to coupling. 

2. When taking slack, open throttle until control switch is closed and leave throttle in that position to avoid delivering excessive power resulting in rough handling of equipment. 

3. When air is used the trainline should be fully charged and also must be positive brakes are fully released before attempting to move equipment. 

4. Avoid operating diesel locomotives with brakes set and throttle open as this handling will result in overheating and consequent damage to the traction motors. 

5. Throttle on diesel locomotives must be opened or closed gradually so that power will not be applied or shut off suddendly. Rapid opening or closing of throttle will result in rough handling. 

6. When locomotive is at rest and brakes set, the throttle must be completely closed, otherwise generator will put electric current through traction motors causing serious damage.

PRECAUTIONS BEFORE STARTING ENGINE 

1. Check position of all valves; (a) Drain valves in cooling, lubricating oil system and air reservoirs. (b) Steam valves in cooling system and steamline if equipped. 

2. Check fuel supply 

3. Check water supply in sight-glass on cooling watertank making sure water-glass cocks are open. 

4. Check lubricating oil supply. (a) Engine crankcase sump or oil tank. (b) Engine governor (c) Air compressor (d) 900 Fan drives (on engines so equipped). 

OPERATION OF ENGINE START SWITCH 

The start switch should be closed firmly and if the engine does not rotate immediately, the start switch should be released and cause investigated. If the engine rotates but does not fire, the start switch should be released after 10 seconds

and cause investigated. If the engine fires immediately start switch should be held closed until engine comes up to idle speed or oil pressure builds up to the minimum allowable oil pressure. 

BEFORE OPERATING LOCOMOTIVE 

1. Make certain main reservoir air pressure is up to 130 p.s.i. 

2. Lube oil pressure should be at specified pressure. 

3. Engine cooling water temperature should be up to 125 degrees F. before the locomotive is worked whenever possible to do so. 

4. On locomotives equipped with hand brakes, be sure the brake is fully released and check to see that the air brakes apply and release.

TRANSITION 

The transition of locomotives refers to the traction motor power circuits and the changing of the traction motor connections either manually or automatically to obtain the desired locomotive tractive effort and speed within the voltage operating limit of the main generator. 

Automatic transition is controlled by main generator voltage and amperage or by locomotive speed.

OPERATION OF REVERSE LEVER 

The reverse lever must not be moved from "Forward to Reverse" or "Reverse to Forward" while the locomotive is in motion. (Except in cases of extreme emergency.)

When leaving locomotive, the reverse lever should be placed in NEUTRAL position and removed from the control stand on those with detachable reverse levers. This should be done while the control switch is closed. 

In connection with the operation of the reverse lever, it should be noted that, as there is no mechanical connection between the reverse lever and the reverser drum in the voltage cabinet, therefore, in most cases, the reverser drum does not assume the position corresponding to that of the reverse lever until the throttle is advanced to the Number 1 position. In the event, the locomotive backs onto a train and gravity or another locomotive starts the train, the reverser drum will be in the wrong position until the throttle is momentarily advanced to the Number 1 position to allow the reverser to operate.

MOVEMENT THROUGH WATER 

Under absolutely no circumstances should the locomotive be operated or towed through water deep enough to touch the bottom of the traction motor frame. 

It is absolutely necessary that all traction motor

blowers are operating to protect against excessive moisture from entering the traction motors. 

The following locomotives must not be operated or towed through water above top of rail greater than the depth and not in excess of the speed shown:

Type of Locomotive 

Passenger 

Freight General Purpose 

Yard 

44 Ton Yard

Maximum Depth above Top of Rail Maximum Speed (Inches) (MPH) 55555 35552 

OPERATING OVER RAILROAD CROSSINGS 1. Switch Locomotives 

Before arriving at a railraod crossing, partly close the throttle to reduce traction motor current as much as practicable, in order to minimize arcing under the brushes and flashovers at the traction motor commutators as the wheels jar over the crossing. 

2. Road Locomotives 

The throttle must always be reduced until all trucks have passed over the crossing. If running with the throttle above the 5th notch, reduce to the 5th notch. If running with the throttle in 4th or 5th notch, reduce throttle one notch.

TO PUMP UP MAIN RESERVOIR AIR PRESSURE 

When the locomotive has been coupled to train and it is desired to pump air rapidly, the following procedure should be followed: 

1. Place reverse lever in neutral or off position. 

2. Open generator field switch. 

3. Place transition lever (if used) in No.1 position. 

4. Open throttle to increase engine and air compressor speed not to exceed Run 4 position. On switch locomotives do not exceed 1/2 throttle position.

SPEED CONTROL 

Road locomotives are equipped with a speed control switch or speed governor which will automatically set the air brakes and operate the pneumatic control switch (PC) if the train speed exceeds it's limitations. 

When the train speed reaches the maximum allowable speed, a warning whistle will sound and in 5 seconds an automatic brake application will be initiated. Immediately after the application is initiated, place the automatic brake valve in

lap position which will limit the application to a full service reduction. As soon as the speed has been reduced below the setting of the speed control, the brakes may be released in the usual manner. When the application is initiated the pneumatic control switch will kick out. Following the release, move the throttle to idle position and reset the pneumatic control switch by pulling the button out on those so equipped. 

The throttle may then be advanced in the usual manner. 

CHANGING OPERATING ENDS ON DOUBLE END LOCOMOTIVES 

When it is necessary to operate from the opposite control station, the throttle must be in the "idle" position, the transition lever (if used) in the "off" position and the reverse lever in "off" position and removed from the control stand (if possible) in the cab from which you are operating. 

The engine brakes should be applied and then follow the instructions as outlined below in the order named: 

1. Locomotives equipped with 8 EL Brake equipment. 

a. Make a full service reduction with the automatic brake valve and place brake valve in lap position; close brake valve cut-out cock and move brake valve to running position and apply locking pin. b. Move independent brake valve to lap position and apply locking pin. c. Shut off control and generator field switches. Leave fuel pump switch on until fuel pump switch is closed on opposite end, making sure that "PC" switch is reset. Then open fuel pump switch on end to be cutout. Lock control push buttons out on locomotives so equipped. d. Proceed to opposite cab, closing control switch and such other switches as necessary. e. Remove locking pin from independent brake valve and move handle to application position. f. Place automatic brake valve in lap position, open brake valve cut-out cock and when line 10 pressure is restored, place automatic brake valve in running position. 

2. Locomotives equipped with 24 RL brake equipment. 

a. Make a full service reduction with the automatic brake valve and place brake valve in lap position; close brake valve cut-out cock and move brake valve to running position and apply locking pin. b. Place independent brake valve in running position and remove handle, and move Rotair valve to either passenger lap or freight lap position according to service used.

c. Shut off control and generator field switches. 

Leave fuel pump switch on until fuel pump switch is closed on opposite end, making sure that "PC" switch is reset, then open fuel pump switch on end to be cut-out. Lock control push buttons out on locomotives so equipped. d. Proceed to opposite cab, closing control switch and such other switches as necessary. e. Insert the independent brake valve handle, place

Rotair valve in passenger or freight position, depending on service used and move independent brake valve to application position. f. Place automatic brake valve in lap position, open brake valve cut-out cock and when line 10 pressure is restored, place automatic brake valve in running position. 

3. Locomotives equipped with 6 BL or 6 SL Brake Equipment. 

a. Make a full service reduction with the automatic brake valve and place brake valve in lap position and remove handle. Move double-heading cock to "trailing" (4 o'clock) position. b. Place independent brake valve in release position and remove handle. c. Shut off control and generator field switches. Leave the fuel pump switch or fuel pump breaker on until fuel pump switch or fuel pump breaker are closed on opposite end making sure "PC" switch is reset, then open fuel pump switch or fuel pump breaker on end to be cut out. Lock control push buttons out in locomotives so equipped. d. Proceed to opposite cab, closing control switch and such other switches as necessary. e. Insert the independent brake valve handle and move independent brake valve to application position. f. Insert the automatic brake valve handle and move double-heading cock to "Lead" (6 o'clock) position and when line 10 is restored, place automatic brake valve in running position.

The locomotive is now ready for normal operation and control. In handling the cutting-in and cutting-out of the two control stations, pneumatic control switch ("PC") must be properly set on locomotives so equipped. If the pneumatic control switch is not properly set and the fuel pump control switches not handled as outlined above, will result in engine dying which is very harmful to the injectors.

OPERATION OF GYRATING AND OSCILLATING COMBINATION WHITE AND RED HEADLIGHT 

The gyrating or oscillating combination white and red head-light is designed to display either a stationary or gyrating white or red beam and is entirely independent of the standard headlight. For proper operation proceed as follows: 

(1) Close Headlight Generator Switch. 

a. To display a gyrating beam. 

1. Turn motor toggle switch to "ON" position. 

2. Move headlight switch to position of desired color beam. b. To display a stationary beam. 

1. Motor toggle switch must be in "OFF" position. 

2. Move headlight switch handle to position of desired color beam. 

3. Direct beam to position desired by operating the "control manual spring return" push button located on headlight switch. 

Note: It is not possible to display a stationary red beam on locomotives equipped with Pyle-National gyrating combination white and red head-light. (2) When moving headlight switch from "Emergency Red" position, the switch should be placed in "FULL" position until light comes on and then to "DIM" if required. (3) To shut off Headlight, open headlight generator switch. 

BATTERY CHARGING AMMETERS 

The battery charging ammeters indicate the proper functioning of the auxiliary generators. The battery charging rates can vary from only a few amperes to 150 amperes depending on the condition of the batteries. 

Observe the battery charging ammeters to see that they are indicating a charge when the diesel engines are running. 

PROPER USE OF DYNAMIC BRAKE ON DIESEL-ELECTRIC LOCOMOTIVES TO PREVENT DAMAGE TO ELECTRICAL EQUIPMENT 

Diesel-electric locomotives equipped with dynamic brakes have a meter in the cab which indicates traction motor current also a warning light which lights whenever the dynamic brake on any unit is overloaded.

In order to prevent damage to the electrical equipment, the dynamic brake will not be operated with the meter indicating in excess of the maximum allowable traction current. If for any reason the current becomes in excess of the allowable maximum, the dynamic braking lever will be immediately moved

to reduce the current to the normal operating range. 

In order to prevent damage to the electrical equipment, the dynamic braking lever will be immediately moved to reduce the current sufficiently to extinguish the warning light whenever it lights and the dynamic brake current must be controlled in such a manner thereafter as will prevent repeated lighting of the brake warning light.

In any case whichever occurs first, either the lighting of the brake warning light or indication of excessive traction motor current on the meter, the dynamic braking lever will be immediately moved to reduce the traction motor current sufficiently to correct the overload condition. 

The dynamic brake can be used at any speed. However, the independent brake should be kept fully released when using dynamic brake. At low speeds when dynamic brake becomes less effective, the dynamic brake may be released and apply independent brake simultaneously to prevent slack from running out. Care must be exercised to prevent wheel sliding. 

Any case where a Diesel engine is isolated due to ground relay picking up continually, it will be left isolated and that unit will not be used in dynamic braking.

When two or more Diesel locomotives are used in double heading or helper service on freight and passenger trains, the dynamic brake will be used on the lead locomotive or the locomotive in control of the train and the dynamic brake will not be used on the other locomotives regardless of their position in the train. The only exception is when a Diesel locomotive is double headed with a steam locomotive in the lead. In which case, the dynamic brake will be used on the 

Diesel locomotive if it is equipped with the dynamic brake interlock. 

Locomotives 42 to 47 inclusive and 306 to 314 inclusive are equipped with improved dynamic braking with a maximum current limit of 700 amperes. When the dynamic braking lever is moved from the "off" position, the engine speed increases to Run 3 and remains in Run 3 until the lever is moved back to the "off" position. These locomotives are also equipped with a wheel slide device to indicate wheel slide when using the dynamic brake. When one or more pair of wheels slide, the wheel slip indicator will light and sand will be applied automatically when the automatic sanding switch located on the control stand is closed. 

PROCEDURE TO STOP LOCOMOTIVES OTHER THAN STEAM IN THE EVENT OF LOSS OF MAIN RESERVOIR PRESSURE. 

Following is procedure to stop locomotives other than steam in the event of loss of main reservoir pressure when locomotive has separated from the train or its mating units. 

1. On locomotives equipped with dynamic brakes, proceed to apply dynamic brake as usual.

2. On locomotives not equipped with dynamic brakes or if dynamic brake fails to slow down locomotives: a. Move engine throttle to idle position. b. On locomotives with manual or automatic transition move transition lever to No. 1 position. c. Move reverser lever to opposite direction. d. Everyone aboard get set for severe retarding action. 

If should be noted that the action is more severe at lower speeds. e. Move throttle lever to position No. 1 only.

CAUTION Reversing traction motors to stop a locomotive otherwise out of control is the last resort when all other means have failed. Damage to the traction motors and the sliding of wheels is certain to result if this is necessary at any appreciable speeds. Under no circumstances should reversing traction motors be resorted to as an aid to air brakes unless an emergency arises making it necessary to do so. 

PROPER HANDLING OF BATTERY TRAINLINE CABLES AND SWITCHES ON DIESEL-ELECTRIC ROAD LOCOMOTIVES 

Diesel-electric road locomotives, Classes 16, 100, 200, 300 and 325 are equipped with plug-in receptacles and switches, similar to those used in passenger cars for trainlining batteries. Each unit has a receptacle located in the vestibule above the end doors, a switch located on the distribution panel marked "Battery Trainline" and one plug-in jumper cable is carried on the rear wall of each cab unit. 

When it is necessary to trainline the batteries, the plugin jumper cable should be plugged in the receptacle of the unit to be train-lined and in the receptacle on the adjoining unit. The jumper cable should be applied on top of the other cables, making sure it is all the way in the receptacle and the locking screw on the bottom of the receptacle tightened to prevent the jumper cable from pulling out. After the jumper cable has been applied it will be necessary to close the battery trainline switch on each distribution panel of the two units that are trainlined. Do not apply jumper cables with the battery trainline switches closed. 

When trainlining the batteries on passenger locomotives, the cab and booster units should be trainlined together when-

ever possible, rather than the two booster units due to the steam generators requiring considerable battery load. 

If it becomes necessary to trainline the complete locomotive or additional cables are required, the passenger car trainline cables may be used for this purpose. 

All units of Diesel locomotives, Classes 51 and 90 are equipped with permanent battery trainline cables and battery trainline switches. The battery trainline switches are marked "Trainline Battery Switch" and are located below the center of the engine control panel on Class 51 locomotives and on Class 90 locomotives they are located on the floor adjacent to the right of the air compressor. 

During normal operation, Diesel locomotives Classes 51 and 90 should be operated with the battery trainline switches closed at all times.

ARMATURE BEARING FAILURE ALARM INDICATIONS 

The failure of a traction motor armature bearing will result in one of two types of protective apparatus indications 

1. If motor and wheels are locked by a bearing failure, without accompanying electrical damage, the wheel slip relay will cause the wheel slip indicator to flash on and off. Reducing the throttle and applying sand will not overcome this type of wheel slip indication.

Generally, a defective bearing will not lock the wheels until the first time the locomotive is stopped after actual failure of the bearing. However, as soon as power is applied to start the locomotive, wheel slip light will be a warning that one pair of wheels is in distress, either slipping or sliding. 

2. Ground relay action will result if the bearing is damaged to the extent that the armature strikes the motor field pieces, or is displaced laterally far enough to damage the brush rigging. In some cases it will be possible to reset the ground relay after it's first operation, and operate the traction motor for a short time before the ground relay trips again. 

If wheel slip light indications continue after reducing the throttle and application of sand, locomotive should be stopped and observe all wheels for sliding while the locomotive is started.

A traction motor with bearing distress, which might cause repeated ground relay action, is more difficult to detect. 

In such instances the locomotive should be stopped and inspect all traction motors on the particular truck or unit for hot bearing by either smelling or feeling.

Under no condition of repeated wheel slip indication or

repeated ground relay action should locomotive unit be isolated and a locomotive continued in service without first making the above mentioned inspections. 

FREEZING WEATHER PRECAUTIONS 

In freezing weather, precautions must be taken to see that water in the locomotives does not freeze. Steam admission valves are provided on the following equipment and if not in use, steam should be supplied to prevent freezing. 

Steam Generators a. Heater coil Valves b. Water suction line valves c. Water tank valves.

Engine Cooling System a. Steam admission valves to engine cooling system b. Valves for steam supply to cab heaters

In freezing weather if heater facilities are not available all water must be drained from the locomotives. 

1. Engine cooling system and cab heaters. 

2. Steam Generators 

3. Steam Generator water tanks 

4. Toilet water tanks

Entire engine cooling system should be drained to prevent freezing and damage to equipment when necessary to shut down diesel engines in freezing weather. 

See cooling system section in each class of locomotive for detailed instructions for proper draining of the cooling system.

EXCESSIVE ENGINE TEMPERATURE 

The water temperature gauge may be used as a means of detecting an irregularity in the operation of the cooling system. 

High water temperature indicates: a. Radiator cooling fans not operating. b. Shutter controls not operating and shutter not open enough. c. Insufficient water in cooling system. d. Faulty circulation.

EMERGENCY FUEL CUT-OFF VALVE 

In the event of an emergency, a fuel cut-off valve is provided to cut-off the fuel supply. The cut-off valve is located in the feed line either underneath or near the fuel tank. 

On each side of the locomotive, near the center of the underframe, is a small box with a lift cover. Enclosed in this

box is a pull-ring or handle and when these are pulled, the flow of fuel on that unit is stopped. Similar pull-rings or handles are located in the car or car body so they can be pulled either from inside of the locomotive or from the outside when standing on the ground. 

When the emergency fuel cut-off valve is tripped, it must be manually reset to the open position by pulling up the valve stem and resetting the yoke which holds the valve open. The fuel cut-off valves which are located underneath the fuel tank are in a compartment with a slide cover. 

FUSE TEST SWITCH 

The fuse test switch is located in the low voltage cabinet or distribution panel and is connected in parallel with the fuse test blocks. To test a fuse, turn fuse test switch on to light lamp, place fuse to be tested across fuse test blocks and turn switch off. If lamp goes out, fuse is blown.

The lamp socket can also be used to check a lamp. 

AIR COMPRESSORS 

Each locomotive power plant drives an air compressor through a flexible coupling connected to the main generator or engine crankshaft. A few locomotives use a motor driven air compressor.

The air compressor has its own lube oil pump and lubricating system. Oil level in the crankcase can be checked by bayonet gauge.

Condensation collects in the bottom shell of the air compressor intercooler and should be drained as often as practicable. A drain valve is provided for this purpose. Operate this intercooler safety valve by hand when draining intercooler to be certain that it functions properly. 

If Air Pressure Does Not Build-Up: 

1. Check to see that angle cocks and main reservoir drain valves are in their proper positions. 

2. Blow out air filter and air compressor unloader. 

3. Check to see that air compressor is operating.

CONTROL AIR PRESSURE 

Control air pressure is maintained by a reducing valve in the line between the main air reservoir and the control air reservoir. 

If the control air pressure gauge should indicate main reservoir pressure, the reducing valve is probably stuck. Blowing out the filter and jarring the valve may correct this trouble.

Control air operates power contactors, reverser and other pneumatically operated electrical switch gear.

JOURNAL BOX HIGH TEMPERATURE INDICATION 

Each journal box is equipped with a "stench bomb". This device consists of a cylindrical container holding a liquid which when heated gives off an extremely pungent odor. The container is sealed with a bismuth solder plug. If for any reason the temperature inside the journal box exceeds 200°F, the plug melts instantly releasing the stench agent to the atmosphere. 

When the resultant odor is detected, the train should be stopped at once and the overheated bearing located. An examination to determine the cause of the overheating should be made and proper precautions taken before operating the equipment further.

PROPER HANDLING OF DOORS AND VENTILATION ON DIESEL-ELECTRIC ROAD LOCOMOTIVES 

On diesel-electric locomotives equipped with carbody filters, the engine room side doors should be closed at all times in order to allow only filtered air to enter in the engine room. 

The doors between each unit on all diesel-electric locomotives should be kept closed at all times. 

On diesel-electric passenger locomotives, which are not double-end locomotives, the rear door next to the train should be closed and locked with a coach key to prevent unauthorized persons from entering locomotive and the key should be removed from the lock.

The engine room side doors on diesel-electric locomotives not equipped with carbody filters should be closed when possible to keep dirt out of the engine room and such doors closed as will best meet the condition under which the locomotive is operating. When determining which doors should be closed, consideration should be given to direction of the wind, dust, condition of the road bed and passing trains. 

When locomotives are operated with the engine room side doors open, the safety bars or chains must be securely fastened in place when the locomotive is in motion. 

Diesel-electric locomotives equipped with roof ventilators for admitting air into the engine rooms should have the roof ventilators closed when it is raining to prevent water from entering engine rooms. These ventilators should also be closed when steam helpers are used on front end of the locomotives. 

Diesel-electric locomotives equipped with a door in the front of the nose must have this door closed and securely

fastened with the locking device when the locomotive is in motion.

INSTRUCTIONS FOR SETTING OF TRAIN STOP SWITCH ON DIESEL-ELECTRIC PASSENGER LOCOMOTIVES EQUIPPED WITH INTERMITTENT TRAIN STOP 

Locomotives 1 to 47, 80 to 88 and 300 to 349 inclusive

The old train control switch, located on the distribution panel in all cab units, is used for operation of the motor generator, except on diesel No.1, which has a main switch in cab immediately below the equipment box for operation of motor-generator set. Switch must always be in the "on" position in the cab unit from which the locomotive is being operated and in the "off" position in the trailing cab units if the locomotive is made up with more than one cab unit. 

Locomotives 50 to 90 Inclusive 

The switch for operating the motor generator set is located on the control stand in all cab units, except Diesel No.50, which has a switch located in the nose to right of door and near equipment box. Switches are marked and must always be in the "on" position in the cab unit from which the locomotive is being operated; and in the "off" position in the trailing cab unit if the locomotive is made up with more then one cab unit.

LOCATION OF EQUIPMENT BOX ON DIESEL-ELECTRIC ROAD LOCOMOTIVES 

Given below is the location of equipment boxes on Diesel electric road locomotives: 

Locomotive 1 Equipment box is located at the rear end of unit in the engine room, necessary to remove cap screw and cover plate. 

Engineman's tool box is carried in the cab. Spare ATC tube located in cab over high voltage cabinet. 

Locomotives 11 through 15 cab units 

Equipment boxes are attached to the right and left underside of the carbody located behind the front trucks on cab units. Engineman's tool box and spare ATC tube boxes are located in nose.

Locomotives 16 through 48, 80 through 88 and 200 through 349 Equipment boxes are located at the front end of each booster unit in the engineroon. Engineman's tools and spare

ATC tube boxes are carried in nose of unit.

Locomotive 50 Equipment box is attached to the right and left underside of the carbody located ahead of the rear truck on the cab units. Engineman's tool box and spare ATC tube box are located in nose. Locomotive 51 through 78 Equipment boxes are located at the rear end of each booster unit in the engine room. Engineman's tool box and spare ATC tube box is carried in nose of lead unit. Locomotive 90 Equipment box is carried in nose of unit. Engineman's tool box and spare ATC tube box is carried in nose of unit. 

Locomotive 100 through 199 and 400 through 430

Booster units have equipment boxes attached to the right and left underside of the carbody behind the rear trucks. Cab units operating without booster units have supplies carried in the nose. Engineman's tool box and spare ATC tube box on locomotives equipped with automatic train control are carried in the nose of the cab units. 

LOAD REGULATORS AND GOVERNORS 

Diesel locomotives are equipped with a load regulating device which works in conjunction with the engine governor. 

The purpose of the load regulator is to maintain a constant power output of the diesel engine for each throttle position. 

The load regulator allows the engine to determine the load which it can pull, based on fuel consumption. If the engine demands more fuel than a predetermined setting, the load regulator reduces the load on the engine by reducing the field excitation of the main generator. If the engine requires less fuel than the predetermined setting or balance point, the load regulator increases the load on the engine by increasing field excitation of the main generator. In this manner minor variations in excitation voltage, temperature changes in generator windings or locomotive speed do not cause overload or unloading of the engine.

The speeds of the diesel engines are controlled by a hydraulic flyball governor which is gear-driven from the engine or electric type governors. The governors are connected to the injector or injector fuel pump racks by necessary linkeage and opens or closes the fuel racks to inject more or less fuel according to the load on the engine and speed setting of the governors are controlled by the throttle through mechanical, pneumatic, electric electro-pneumatic or electrohydraulic connections.

BLOWING DOWN STEAM GENERATOR SEPARATORS 

The steam separator should be blown down for 10 to 15 seconds every 30 minutes. This can be done by pressing the separator blowdown button on the remote control panel being sure that the master switch is "on". Most locomotives are equipped with an automatic blowdown feature, therefore, not necessary for manual blowdown.

On locomotives with automatic separator blowdown the master switch on the remote control panel should be "off" on all cab units, when approaching and standing in a station to prevent automatic separator blowdown from operating. The master switch in operating cab should be moved to "on" after leaving the station.

GENERAL PRECAUTIONS 

1. Use only clean lintless rags in or around the locomotives as the use of waste is prohibited. 

2. Do not leave any loose cloth or rags lying in the engineroom. 

3. Smoking is not allowed in the engineroom. 

4. Use fire extinguishers instead of water, whenever possible, to put out any fire in or about the locomotive. 

Avoid over exposure to fire extinguishers fumes, particularly carbon tetrachloride. 

5. Know the location of all fire extinguishers and first aid kits which may be needed in case of emergency. 6. All concerned should familiarize themselves with the operation and location of emergency fuel cut-off rings. 

7. Air box covers should never be disturbed while an engine is operating. If an air box cover is blown off, stop engine immediately and do not run engine until cover is properly replaced. 

8. If necessary to go under the locomotive, make sure that the reverse lever is in "Neutral" (remove handle if possible) and brakes are set. 

9. Metal cased flashlights should never be used around electrical equipment. 

10. Do not move locomotive until main reservoir air pressure has been pumped up to 130 pounds. 

11. If the locomotive is to be left alone for any length of time, hand brakes must be set, blocking put by the wheels, throttle placed in "idle" and the reverse lever in "neutral" position.

12. Try the horn, bell ringer, sanders and make a test of the brake equipment before operating a locomotive. 

13. The safety grills on EMD 567 Type diesel engines should be fastened in place along the sides of the oil pan at all times while the engines are in operation. 

14. If dynamic brake grid blower motors are not operating, the dynamic brake will not be used on that unit. 

15. Cylinder test valves (Flashcocks) must be closed tight so that no blow comes from them while engine is running. 

16. If any unusual knocks or pounds are noted in the engine stop the engine immediately and do not operate the engine until the condition is corrected. 

17. When there are indications of malfunctioning and the engines contain more than the normal amount of fumes in the crankcase, the engine should be shut down and train handled to destination with the remaining units. 

The crankcase inspection covers should not be removed for any reason for at least 2 hours after the engine has been shut down also nobody should remain in the vicinity of the engine for at least 30 minutes. 

18. If smoke or odor of burning insulation is noted coming from below the floor level of the locomotive, stop the locomotive and investigate. If coming from within the body of the locomotive, isolate the engine and do not use engine until the trouble is corrected. 

19. When resetting the ground protective relay, the diesel engine must be isolated. In the event the ground protective relay repeatedly picks up, an inspection must be made of all traction motors on the particular truck or unit for hot bearings, and the engine should be isolated and not used for power or dynamic braking. 

20. When traction motor blowers are not operating, isolate the engine until condition is corrected. 

21. Diesel engines should not be operated when the water level in the radiator water tank is below the "Minimum water level mark". 

22. When changing any fuse, the switch in that circuit should be open. The auxiliary generator fuses should not be removed while the engine is running or the auxiliary generator switch is closed. 

23. Gang switch boxes containing the push button control switches located near the control station must be kept locked on all units so equipped, except the unit used to operate the locomotive. 

24. "Seals" used to secure the covers of speed recorders

in closed position should be in place, not broken or defective. Broken or defective seals should be reported to foreman in charge so that correction can be made before departure of the locomotive. 

TERMINOLOGY Main Generator 

The main generator is air cooled and directly connected to the diesel engine through a flexible coupling. The generator produces direct current for operation of the traction motors. Some generators have an alternator built into the engine end of the frame, which furnishes alternating current to drive the engine cooling water fans and the traction motor blowers. 

Traction Motor. 

The traction motor is a series wound D.C.motor that is cooled by forced air from the traction motor blowers. Traction motors supply power to the driving wheels through the armature spur gear and axle ring gear.

Traction Motor Blower 

The traction blower is either belt driven or motor driven centrifugal blower. Air from the blower is directed to the traction motor or motors through flexible couplings. Failure of a traction motor blower will result in serious damage to the traction motor as a result of overheating. 

Auxiliary Generator

The auxiliary generator is used for charging the locomotive storage batteries and to supply all the low voltage direct current power when the locomotive is operating. 

Exciter Generator - Alco Baldwin F.M. 

The exciter generator furnishes current for the main generator field excitation.

Amplidyne Exciter Alco 

Alco locomotives are equipped with amplidyne exciter which furnishes excitation for the main generator. 

Blower Generator 

Alco F.M. 

Furnishes power for the traction motor blowers.

Fan Generator 

F.M. 

The fan generator furnishes power to run the engine cooling water fan.

Motor Alternator 

Alco The motor alternator furnishes power for the transformer, that in turn supplies power for field circuit, field limit and current limit control. 

Dynamic Brake Blower Motor 

The dynamic brake blower motors are directly connected to dynamic brake grids. When the dynamic brake is being used, this energy is dissipated through the grids. Part of this energy is used to drive the brake blower motor which supplies cooling air for the braking grids. 

Cab Heaters and Defrosters

Cab heater and defroster fans are driven by electric motors and supply warm air for cab heat and windshield defrosting. The flow of the hot engine cooling water to the heater radiators provides the necessary heat. On some locomotives the defroster and heater fan are one and the same. The output of the heater fan can be controlled by varying fan motor speeds. 

Controller- or Control Stand 

The controller or control stand contains the operating levers for the locomotive. The throttle lever by means of mechanical or electrical connections to the engine governor, controls engine speed. The reverser through electrical contacts controls the direction of motion. The transition lever through electrical connections, controls the traction motor power circuits to obtain the desired locomotive tractive effort and speed.

Crankcase Exhauster Alco 

The crankcase exhauster is a small electric motor and fan, mounted on the engine for removing fumes from the crankcase of the engine and exhausting these fumes through a roof vent. 

Fuel Pump Motors 

The fuel pump motor is a D. C. motor that. drives the fuel pump supplying fuel to the injector pumps or unit injector, whichever, is used. On some locomotives the fuel pump motor also drives the governor oil pump and an A. C. generator.

Santa Fe 

E. M.D. LOCOMOTIVES CLASS 80 

SANTA FE PART II--SECTION 3

Class 80 passenger locomotives were rebuilt by Electro- 

Motive Division of General Motors Corporation. The units with the cab will be known as lead units, those without cabs as booster unit and are rated at 2000 H. P. per unit. 

Each unit has two 12-cylinder, 2 cycle, Model 567B Diesel engines which drives two 5GT544B1 generators. Four traction motors, and a Model CFK-4225 steam generator to furnish steam for heating or cooling the train (See part III Section 2 for description of steam generator). 

The engines have a speed range of 275 R.P.M. at idle to 

800 R.P.M. at full throttle. Their speed is controlled by an electro-hydraulic governor control which is operated by the main throttle. In this way all engines in the locomotive are controlled simultaneously. Each notch above Run 1 on the main throttle changes the speed of the Diesel engine. (For description of Engine see Part I Section 2) LOCOMOTIVE OPERATION 

All locomotives of this class must not be operated at a speed to exceed 100 miles per hour, and is equipped with a speed control which will set the brakes at 100 miles per hour. 

Control Station: 

The control station switches most frequently used are placed on a control panel adjacent to the control stand. 

The balance of the switches are located on the side of the electrical control cabinet on the left side of cab. 

These switches are not fuse protected. They are in reality circuit breakers. In the event of an overload the button snaps to the "OFF" position giving a visual indication of the circuit affected. 

The pneumatic control switch ("PC") is an air operated electric switch. The purpose of this switch is to reduce the power of the locomotive by bringing the speed of the engine to idle in the event of an emergency or penalty application of brakes. If the throttle is in the 5th or 6th notch when the "PC" switch is tripped the engine will stop. The locomotive is equipped with an indicating light which will show when the switch is tripped. To reset the switch the throttle must be returned to idle and the brake recovered. When this has been accomplished the "PC" switch will reset itself and the indicating light will go out.

PLATE 2300-1 CONTROL STATION

Engine Control and Instrument Panel: 

This panel is mounted on a frame which supports the engine cooling water tank near the governor end of the engine. 

Each power plant has its own engine control and instrument panel, on which are mounted the lube oil pressure and suction gauges and signal lights, along with the start, stop, fuel pump, and isolation switches. 

Starting Engines: 

A. In operating cab. 

1. Close all switches in electrical control cabinet. 

2. At control station close "control" and "fuel pump" switches. 

3. Check "PCS" open light. 

4. Be sure reverse lever is REMOVED from control stand. 

B. In engine room. 

1. Check main battery switch box located to the left of hand brake in steam generator compartment. 

2. Turn on fuel pump switch and check flow of fuel through sight glass on fuel filter nearest engine. 

3. See isolation switch in start position. 

4. Hold layshaft one quarter open. 

5. Press engine start button until engine starts. Do not hold button down more than 10 seconds at a time. 

6. After lubricating oil pressure builds up place isolation switch in run position, and idle engine until water temperature comes up to the green area on the gauge before working engine. 

7. Check ground relay light. 

8. Check starting contactors and interlocks.

To Move Locomotive: 

1. Make certain main reservoir air pressure is up to 130lbs. 

2. Release all hand brakes. 

3. Isolation switches on engine control panel must be in "Run" position. 

4. Close generator field switch at control station. 

5. Place reverse lever in "forward " or "reverse" position as required. 

6. Place foot on safety device pedal and release brakes. 

7. Open throttle as required.

Handling Train: 

During normal operation it should never be necessary to move the throttle or any of the controls hastily. Each move should be thought out carefully and made smoothly. Make sure and be positive that brakes throughout train are released. 

Starting a Train: 

The rate at which a train may be accelerated depends on the amount of power applied at the start. Therefore it is only possible to obtain a soft start by applying less power

This is accomplished It will be noticed that the locomotive does not respond as soon as the throttle is opened. In fact it may take a few seconds before the locomotive will start to move. This is due to the load regulator arm resting in the minimum field position.

Un- When starting a train, it may be necessary to open the throttle above the first notch to start the whole train. der these conditions, it is not damaging to the equipment to speed up the engines during acceleration at low locomotive speeds. 

The handling of the throttle depends on the weight and type of train being hauled and the grade conditions. In gen- eral it will be necessary to open the throttle as follows: 

Place throttle in Run 1 for 2 to 3 seconds then then Run 2 Run 3 2 " 3 11 2 "1 3 11 then 

Run 4 until train starts. 

If train does not start in 25 to 30 seconds shut off throttle and make sure brakes are fully released.

When slack is all taken and the whole train is moving proceed to other throttle positions as required.

When operating these locomotives with 1800 HP Class 1 

unit the throttle must not be advanced beyond run 7 until flashes on wheel slip light indicate the 1800 HP unit has made transition from series to parallel or a speed of 

30 M.P.H. has been reached, then throttle may be advanced to run 8 position. 

Close throttle with 2 to 3 seconds between each notch except in case of emergency, then close throttle without hesitation. 

When necessary to brake train with power still applied to the locomotive it must be remembered that for any given throttle position the draw bar pull rapidly increases as the train speed decreases. This pull might become great enough to part the train unless the throttle is reduced as the train speed drops. Since the pull of the locomotive is shown by the amperage on the load meter, a constant pull can be maintained on the train during a slow down, by keeping a steady indication on the load meter. This is done by reducing the throttle a notch whenever the emperage starts to increase. The throttle should be in idle before locomotive comes to a stop.

In order to avoid serious damage to the electrical equipment when locomotive is operating in parallel on heavy grades, it will be necessary to return to series at 24 M.P.H. 

When it is desired to negotiate heavy grades in series transition, it will be necessary to reduce throttle as train

accelerates to prevent train speed from exceeding 27 M.P.H. 

When operating in multiple with Class 1, an 1800 HP unit, shift the 2000 HP unit back to series at 24 MPH. and shift the 1800 HP unit back to series at 19 M.P.H. 

Load Indicating Meter: 

This meter is an accurate guide to the load on the locomotive. The dial of the meter is graduated in amperes, starting at 0 at the left and going to 1500 amperes at the extreme right of the scale. An instruction plate mounted below the meter gives the permissible short time ratings; these ratings are accumulative. In other words, it is permissable to operate at 800 amperes for one hour, then 850 amperes for 1/2 hour. After these ratings have been used it is necessary to reduce the load on the locomotive enough to bring the load indicating meter back to 750 amperes or less for at least 15 minutes.

The continuous and short time ratings, govern the operation whether the locomotive is being operated in series or parallel transition.

Backward transition (parallel to series) MUST be made when the locomotive speed decreases to the backward transition speed, except when slowing down for station stops. 

Forward and backward transition speeds are shown below:

Forward Transition Speed (Run 8) Backward Transition Speed

If the short time rating(s) have been exhausted or exceeded and/or the speed drops below 31 M.P.H., reduce the throttle to Run 7. By reducing the throttle, the speed of the train will slow down. If the speed drops below 24 M.P. H. (backward transition speed) close the throttle to idle and re-open to as high a throttle position as possible without exceeding the continuous rating on the load meter: As the grade decreases, advance the throttle without exceeding the continuous current rating. 

If the load indicating meter does not function, the continuous and short time ratings must be determined by locomotive speed when running with wide open throttle. 

31 miles per hour continuously. 

29 miles per hour for one hour. 

26 miles per hour for one half hour.

These ratings are accumulative and after these ratings have been used it is necessary to reduce the load on the locomotive enough to bring the speed to 31 M.P.H. or more for at least 15 minutes.

In general it is advisable to climb a hill with wide open throttle in order that time on hill be kept as short as possible.

Operation of  

Whenever any wheels slip, either one pair of wheels or both pair of wheels on the same truck or on any truck on the locomotive, the indicator on the instrument panel will light. 

To stop the slippage Ease Off On The Throttle One Or More Notches. After a few seconds throttle can be opened to desired position.

When there is a wheel slip indication, unless the rail conditions are extremely bad, it will probably be only one set of wheels that are slipping. 

It is therefore unnecessary to use sand except under the most extreme track conditions. Before applying sand, be sure to Reduce The Throttle Until The Wheel Slip Light Stops 

Flashing. It should be remembered that sand is injurious to the traction motors and associated apparatus. 

During transition from series to parallel the wheel slip relay will pick up, causing wheel slip light to flash.

This is not an indication that the wheels are slipping. 

The wheel slip relay will give wheel slip indications for series and parallel operation. 

Throttle Emergency Stop Button: 

This button is located on the end of the throttle handle. 

Its purpose is to stop all the engines in the locomotive in case of emergency.

It is operated by pressing the button and pushing the throttle handle to beyond the "IDLE" position.

Steam Locomotive Used As Helper: 

When steam helper service is provided for Diesel powered trains, the procedure necessary for the protection of the traction motors is Entirely Different From That Used Without Helper. 

As the train speed is reduced by the grade, the Diesel engine throttle Must Be Reduced and more of the work done by the steam helpers.

Diesel Locomotives Used As Helper: 

When operating multiple units having different shifting speeds from parallel back to series, such as 1800 HP. unit 1, at 24 M.P.H. shift the 2000 HP. unit back to series and if speed continued to decrease at 19 M.P.H. shift the 1800 HP. unit back to series.

Towing Locomotive: 

When preparing the locomotive for deadheading or yard movement where it will be towed by other power the reverse lever must be in neutral and the throttle in idle position. 

If the locomotive is to be towed in a train for any distance, the reversers should be locked in the neutral position with the locking pins provided for that purpose. 

When the locomotive is to be handled dead in train the dead engine cock on the control valve should be set for "DEAD", close double heading cock on the automatic brake valve pedestal, place Rotair valve handle in the "PASS" position, place automatic brake valve in running position and remove handles. The main battery switch and all control switches at the control station should be opened. Remove reverse lever from control stand. 

Isolating An Engine While Under Power: 

If it becomes necessary to take the engine "off the line" while the locomotive is operating under power, it should be done as follows: 

1. Reduce the speed of the engine to idle with the layshaft manual control lever or reduce the speed to idle at the control station so that the traction motor contactors will not interrupt the full motor current when isolation switch is moved to "start" position. 

2. Place the isolation switch in the "start" position. 

3. Push the engine stop button and hold in until engine stops

4. Turn off the fuel pump. 

5. If the engines will not stop by holding in "stop" button, hold down on the manual control lever until the engine stops. 

Placing Engine Back "On the Line": 

1. Close fuel pump switch. 

2. Place isolation switch in "start" position. 

3. Start engine in usual way. 

4. After lubricating oil pressure builds up, place isolation switch in "run" position. If throttle is above third position, hold down on the layshaft manual control lever or reduce main throttle to idle to allow engine to come up to speed gradually. 

Precaution during Locomotive Operation: 

1. Maintain engine cooling water temperature 1500 to 180° preferably 1650 whenever possible. Do Not Exceed 200º. 

Engines equipped with automatic shutter controls are adjusted to open the shutters at 1650 and if for any reason the automatic shutter controls become inoperative, the shutters should be operated manually. 

2. Lubricating oil pressure should be approximately 35 lb. at 800 R.P.M. If lubricating oil pressure drops below 

15 lb. (operating in Run 8 with hot oil) stop engine, and do not operate until the cause of low oil pressure is corrected. 

1. At control station: a. Close throttle to idle position. b. Place reverse lever in neutral position and remove handle. c. Open generator field switch. (Do not open control switch as it is impossible to shut down engines with stop button without the control circuit energized). 

2. At engine: 

a. Place isolation switch in "start" position. b. Push engine "stop" button and hold it until engine stops. c. Open fuel pump switch. d. Open cylinder test valves (flashcocks) on engine. 

NOTE: Engine will not stop with "stop" button unless control air pressure is up. 

3. At control station: a. Open control switch. b. Open fuel pump switch. 

4. At distribution panel: 

a. Open main battery switch. b. Open control switch.

TROUBLE SHOOTING If fuel Does Not Show in Fuel Return Sight Glass: 

1. If fuel pump does not run: a. Check other pumps in locomotive to see if they are running, If all fuel pumps are stopped, Check @ "PC" switch open light must not be burning. The control and fuel pump switches must be in the "ON" position. If only one fuel pump in a unit is sto stopped, be sure the switch on engine control panel is turned on. If locomotive consists of more than one unit and both fuel pumps in the same unit are stopped be sure switches on the engine control panels are on and control fuse in that unit is good. 

2. If motor is running but no fuel is being delivered: a. Check pump coupling and shaft. b. Check fuel supply and emergency fuel cut-off valve. c. Check air leaks in suction line due to broken pipe or loose connection. 

3. The battery may be too weak to turn engine over. Turn on engine room lights and note whether they become dim or go out when the "start" button is pressed. 

4. Check to see that both the starting contactors at the bottom of the low voltage cabinet go in.

If Engine Rotates But Does Not Fire When Start Button is Pressed and Injector Racks Are Held Open With Manual Lay shaft Lever: 

1. Check cylinder test valves at each cylinder, making sure that they are closed. 

2. Check injector linkage in event that it may be stuck in shut down position. 

3. Check to see that overspeed trip shaft is latched in "run" position. 

4. Check fuel pump and sight glass to make sure fuel is being delivered to engine.

If Locomotive Does Not Move When Throttle Is Opened: 

(Some of the items listed below would cause a loss of load on one engine which would cause slow acceleration). 

1. Check "PC" switch (light should not be burning). 

2. Check hand brakes in each unit to see that they are released. 

3. Repeat movement of brake valve to release all brake shoes. 

4. Check to see that generator field switch at control station is closed and fuse good. 

5. Check control air pressure and see that reversers are in proper position.

6. Check to see that isolation switches are in "run" position. 

7. Check ground relay and no power lights on engine control and instrument panel. 

8. Check 15 amp. battery field fuse and 60 amp. control fuse on the distribution panel. 

9. The battery field or shunt field contactors might be open or making poor contact. 

10. If starting contactors stick closed or wheel slip relay sticks open the generator of that power plant will not deliver power.

Loss of Load On An Engine: 

After an engine has operated several minutes in Run 8, the load indicator on the governor should read 7/16 in. and the throttle indicator shows No.8 position. If the load indicator reads 8 or 10 the engine is not loaded and the following should be checked. 

1. The motors across that generator are in series while all other power plants are in parallel. 

2. The battery field (BF) contactor is out. This may be caused by: a. Ground relay tripping or no voltage relay out. b. Starting contactors stuck shut. 

3. Generator field circuit breaker tripped. 

4. Dirty or poorly made contacts any place in the power or generator field circuits. 

5. Check 15 amp. battery field fuse in voltage cabinet.

If Battery Charging Ammeter (In Low Voltage Cabinet) Always 

Shows Zero: 

1. See that auxiliary generator switch is closed. 

2. Check all circuit breakers in circuit affected. a. 125 amp. auxiliary generator circuit breaker. b. 30 amp. auxiliary generator field circuit breaker.

If Locomotive Stops in Operation: 

If the locomotive suddenly becomes inoperative, check the following items: 

1. 60 amp. control fuses on the distribution panel. 

2. Control switch on control stand. 

3. Generator field switch on control stand. 

4. Fuel pump switch on control stand. 

5. See that the ground relays are set in normal operation. 

6. See that the "PC" switch is set (light should not be burning.)

7. Check to see that control air pressure is up. Should be 90 lb. 

8. Check 15 ampere battery field fuse in voltage cabinet. 

9. The fuel supply may have stopped. 

If Engine Fails To Stop: 

Stop engine with layshaft manual control lever if engine fails to stop when the throttle is placed in the shut down position. Check for binding linkage, inoperative governor control, faulty governors, or defective "D" valve. Also check the control switch and control fuse. 

SECTION 2 COOLING SYSTEM Description: 

Water is circulated through the engine radiators and around the oil coolers by two pumps mounted on the engine. 

Cooling air through the radiator is controlled by shutters and three electrically driven cooling fans. The operation of the fans and shutters is entirely automatic and under normal conditions will hold the temperature of the engine cooling water so that the gauge on the inlet of left water pump will read in the green area (120° - 190º).

In the event of excessive cooling water temperature the high temperature alarm switch will close, causing a red light to show on the engine affected and the alarm bells to ring in all units.

Operating water levels are stenciled on the water tank next to the water gauge glasses to indicate minimum and maximum water levels with engine running and stopped. The engine should never be operated with the water below the low water level. Progressive lowering of the water in the gauge glasses indicates a leak in the cooling system and should be reported. 

Filling The Cooling System: 

The system is filled either through the filler pipe located on the roof of the locomotive, or through the filler pipe at the center of the unit on either side. 

To Fill the system proceed as follows: 

1. Stop engine. 

2. Open filling level valve "G". 

3. Fill slowly until water runs out filling level pipe at valve "G". 

4. Close filling level valve "G".

If the cooling system of a hot engine has been drained, do not refill immediately with cold water. If this is done, the sudden change in temperature might crack or warp the cylinder liners and heads.

NEVER ADD COLD WATER TO A HOT ENGINE UNLESS HOT WATER IS NOT AVAILABLE AND THEN VERY SLOWLY WITH ENGINE IDLING: 

Engine High Temperature Alarm Switch: 

This is a thermal switch located on the engine water outlet manifold. If the water temperature exceeds 2000 F. this switch closes, operating the signal relay which lights the hot engine alarm lights and rings the alarm bells. 

Each engine has a completely independent cooling system so each engine must be drained separately. To drain engine open valve near floor at accessory end of engine. Open "G" valve on water tank and remove pipe plug from bottom of right hand water pump.

SECTION 3  

Oil under pressure is forced through the engine for lubrication and piston cooling by the duplex piston cooling and lube oil pump. Lube oil which falls into the oil pan is picked up by the scavenging oil pump and forced through the oil cooler and filters to the oil strainer housing where it is ready for recirculation by the oil pump. The excess returns to the oil pan where it is held until used. 

Oil Level:

The oil level may be checked with the engine running at any speed and should read between "low" and "full" on either bayonet gauge (one on each side of engine). When the engine is stopped the oil from the filter and cooler will drain back into the oil pan and the level on the dipstick should show "system charged." The mark "system uncharged" is used by maintenance forces when filling oil pan when new oil filter elements are installed.

Adding Oil To System: 

When oil is added to the system, it must be poured through the opening having the square cap on top of the strainer housing. Do not remove the round caps while the engine is running as hot oil under pressure will come from the openings.

When the engine is stopped, all the oil in the cooler core will drain into the Michiana filter and the strainer chamber and then overflow into the engine oil pan, which will bring the engine oil pan bayonet gauge reading to "system charged". This level is below the "system uncharged" level because some oil is trapped in the lube oil filter, oil lines and engine.

It 

Oil pressure at 800 RPM is normally 35 to 45 pounds. should not drop below 20 pounds. At idle the pressure should be at least 6 pounds. (In the event of dangerously low oil pressure the engines will automatically be stopped).

Low Oil Pressure Alarm: 

In case of low oil pressure or high vacuum. on the suction side of lube oil pump, the engine governor will stop the engine. The governor reset button will be "OUT", the alarm bell will ring in all units and the "LOW OIL" and "NP" lights will show on engine affected.

The governor reset button must be pushed in to extinguish the "LOW OIL" light and the isolation switch moved to "START" position to extinguish the "NP" light.

When engine is started and allowed to idle, the governor will again shut down the engine after a 40 second delay if the condition still exists. If the condition cannot be corrected, the engine should be isolated. 

The fuel system of all engines in the locomotive are independent of and identical to each other. A fuel tank is mounted under each unit of the locomotive. At the bottom of the tank in the suction line between tank and fuel pump is an emergency cut-off valve. The fuel pump draws fuel oil from the bottom of the tank through a suction line and suction filter. Fuel can only be drawn from the tank if the emergency fuel cut-off valve is open. After leaving the pump, fuel is pumped through the discharge and sintered bronze filters to the injectors. The injectors use only a part of the fuel, the surplus fuel oil lubricates and cools the internal mechanism of the injectors and then returns to the fuel tank through the 5 lb. relief valve.

Emergency Fuel Cut-Off Valve (See General Instructions all locomotives. 

Fuel Pump: 

The fuel pump assembly consists of an electric motor and a pump of the internal gear type. The gear pump is driven by the motor shaft through a flexible coupling. not leave fuel pump running unnecessarily.

Do 

Engine Room Ventilating System: 

Two ventilating fans in the roof of the locomotive (one on each power plant) supply air to the engineroom for engine scavenging, air compressors and main generator and traction motor cooling.

Air hoods are built onto the locomotive roof covering the ventilating fans, and extending over the No.1 engine cooling fans on each power plant. An open grill is over the 

No.1 cooling fans, allowing the fans to exhaust to atmosphere.

A movable damper is placed in each hood, between the ventilating fan and the cooling fan. 

Air ducts with movable dampers extend from the carbody side to the hood over the ventilating fan.

The dampers in the air ducts and in the hood over the ventilating fans are controlled by levers, one on the air duct on left side and at the front of engineroom. The lever controlling the dampers at the rear of locomotive are also on the left side.

With the lever in Summer position, the damper in the hood overhead between the ventilating fan and the No.1 cooling fan is closed. The dampers in the air ducts on each side of the locomotive are open. Outdoor air enters the air ducts, goes up to the hood, down through the ventilating fans and through the air filters into the engineroom With the lever in Winter position, the side dampers are closed, excluding outdoor air. The damper in the hood between the ventilating fan and No.1 cooling fan is open. The ventilatinf fan will now draw in some of the warm exhaust air from the No.1 cooling fan, forcing this warm air through the filters and into the engineroom.

Cooling Fan Motor Contactors 

AC1, AC2, AC3, AC4: 

These contactors connect the radiator cooling fans to the alternator of the main generator and are controlled through the thermostat switch. 

Electro Hydraulic Governor Control Solenoids AV, BV. CV, DV: 

Various combination of these solenoids control engine speed in accordance with throttle position.

Battery Charging Contactor BC: 

This contactor connects the auxiliary generator to the battery. The reverse current relay closes the battery charging contactor when the auxiliary generator voltage reaches a predetermined setting to charge the battery. 

Battery Field Contactor . BF: 

This contactor closes when the throttle is moved to Run 1 position, connecting the battery to the main generator battery field.

Cab Heaters: 

A cab heater is located on each side of cab and motor driven fans provide air circulation. The output of each heater can be varied by use of the cab' heater switches. The switches have four positions "OFF", 1, 2, 3, which provides three different motor speeds. Defrosters:

Each side of the cab is equipped with a defroster motor and fan which blows heated air on the inside of the front cab windows. Both motors are turned on and off by the one defroster switch located on the instrument panel. 

Engine High Temperature Switch ETS: 

When the water temperature exceeds the maximum allowable temperature, this switch closes, lights the hot engine alarm signal light and energized the signal relay, which rings the alarm bell. On some classes of locomotives the engine will return to idle and remain in idle as long as the hot engine alarm is on.

Engine Relay 

ER: 

This relay controls the current supply to the Electro- 

Hydraulic Governor Control Solenoids AV, BV and CV.

De-energizing this relay will stop the engine if the throttle is in Run 5 or Run 6 or bring the engine to idle in any other throttle position.

Fuel Pump Contactor 

FPC: 

All fuel pump contactors throughout the locomotive are controlled by the fuel pump switch at the control station. 

When this contactor is closed, the fuel pumps can be controlled individually by the switch mounted on the engine control and instrument panel. 

Forward Transition Voltage Relay 

FTR: 

This relay is a voltage operated relay which is open at all times, except during forward transition. When the main generator voltage reaches a predetermined setting, the relay picks up closing the contacts causing forward transition.

Ground Protective Relay 

GR: 

If a ground occurs in the high voltage system, this relay will open the battery field contactor and light the ground relay light, and by de-energizing the "ER" relay bring the engine to idle except when the throttle is in the 5th or 6th position and in this event the engines will stop. Should this occur isolate the engine and reset the relay by pressing in on the reset button on the cover. When the relay is tripped the white needle will point to the red indicator and to the yellow indicator when in normal position. If the relay repeatedly opens when the engine speed is increased the power plant MUST be isolated. 

Load Indicating Meter IM: 

This meter is an accurate guide to load on the locomotive as it shows amount of current furnished to the traction motors. Isolation Switch 

IS: 

The purpose of this switch is to open and close the control circuit of the unit as the occasion demands. When starting the Diesel engine this switch must be in the START position. 

After engine is running, move switch to RUN position. This will connect the control circuit of the unit to the control station. 

To isolate the engine, move this switch to START position. This will bring the engine to idle and cut off the power of this engine. 

Motor Field Shunting Contactor Magnet Valve M: 

This is an electro-pneumatic valve that operates the traction motor field shunting contactors. 

Traction Motor Field Shunting Contactors 

M1, M2: 

These contactors connect the shunting resistors across the traction motor fields. These contactors are closed in transition No.3.

No "AC" Voltage Relay NVR: 

This relay is connected across one phase of the AC alternator and should the AC voltage fail, the alarm bell rings and the No Power warning light (blue light) goes on. The engine speed will return to idle except when the throttle is in the 5th or 6th position and in this event the engine will stop. 

Overriding Solenoid ORS: 

This solenoid is incorporated in the governor. When this solenoid is energized it causes the pilot valve of the load regulator to move upward 1/8" which cause vane motor to move to minimum field. 

Parallel Contactors 

P1, P2, P3, P4: 

These contactors are closed to connect the main generator to the traction motors in parallel operation. 

Pneumatic Control Switch 

PCS: 

The purpose of this switch is to reduce the power of the locomotive by bringing the speed of the engines to idle when certain brake applications take place. This action takes place through the Pneumatic Control Relay (PCR).

Parallel Relay PR: 

This relay operates to close the parallel contactors; also in conjunction with the main generator it causes the battery field contactors to open during transition from 

No.1 to No.2. 

Reverse Current Relay RCR: 

This relay prevents the battery current from motoring the auxiliary generator by opening the battery charging contactor when the auxiliary generator voltage drops below the battery voltage.

Series Contactors S12, S34: 

These contactors are closed to connect the main generator to the traction motors in Series operation. 

Generator Shunt Field Contactor 

SH: 

This connects the main generator shunt field to the main generator armature. 

Signal Relay SR: 

This relay operates with the signal lights (Hot Engine, Boiler Off, Ground Relay, No Power and Low Oil Pressure) to ring the alarm bells throughout the locomotive.

ELECTRICAL CONTROL CABINET No.2 POWER PLANT

Starting Contactors - ST+, ST-: 

These contactors are used to connect the battery to the main generator for cranking the diesel engine. 

Thermostat Switch Assembly - TSA: 

This switch is operated by the temperature of the engine cooling water. As the temperature rises successive contacts close in the switch at 30 intervals. The contacts complete circuits which open the shutters and energize the cooling fan motor contactors one by one.

Wheel Slip Relays - WSR1, WSR2: 

Each relay has two normally open contacts. In the event of a pair of wheels slipping the coil connected to that motor will be energized. One of the contacts will light the wheel slip light and the other will de-energize the main generator battery field coil, thereby dropping out generator battery field. Power output will be reduced momentarily until slipping stops. 

Motor Control Circuit Explanation: 

Off to Series: 

1. Move reverser to forward or reverse position. 

2. Move throttle to No.1 position to energize control circuit. 

3. Reverser throws to desired position. 

4. Series contactor picks up through CD of Rev.,isolation switch, AB of PR and AB of Pl. 

5. Battery field picks up through-isolation switch, D and 

C of ST+ and ST-, AB of GPR, CD of PR, and AB of P2. 

6. Shunt field stays in through CD of Rev. and isolation switch. 

7. Move throttle to Run 8.

Transition Series to Parallel: 

1. Main Gen. voltage rises to setting of FTR picking up

FTR. 

2. Parallel relay picks up through CD of FTR, and CD of 

S12 and held in through EF of PR. 

3. CD of PR drops out (BF) battery field. 

4. AB of PR drops out Series. 

5. AB of 812 picks up P1 and P2.

Backward transition from Parallel to Series. 

1. FTR remains out when speed reaches point for transition it should be made at 24 miles per hour. 

2. Drop throttle to idle for 4 seconds. 

3. Parallel contactor, Parallel relay and Battery field contactors open. 

4. Return throttle to desired position and power circuit is again energized. 

5. Sequence of "Off" to "Series" is repeated.

SECTION 6 AIR SYSTEM Air Compressor: 

Each power plant is equipped with a Gardner Denver ABO air compressor, two stage, two cylinder, water cooled, and driven directly by the engine through a flexible coupling connected to the crankshaft.

The two cylinders are set at an angle of 200 above horizontal, on the crankcase which accomodates the compressor under the plumbing stack, yet it is accessable for maintenance. 

The cylinders and cylinder heads are cored for the circulation of water for cooling. This water is piped from the cooling system drain line on the engine. It circulates from the bottom of the low pressure cylinders up and out through the top of the high pressure cylinder and into the cooling water tank above the compressor.

The intercooler has been replaced by a manifold connecting the low and high pressure cylinders. A 55 pound relief valve is installed on the manifold. 

The lube oil system is the "direct feed" type. The piston type pump forces the oil through the drilled eccentric connecting rod and strap and into the drilled crankshaft. The passage in the crankshaft carry the oil to the connecting rod bearings and spray from these bearings lubricates both of the roller main bearings, the piston pin bushings and the cylinder walls.

The loading and unloading of each air compressor is controlled by a mechanical governor.

Draining Air Reservoirs: 

The air reservoirs are located in front of and behind the fuel tank underneath the carbody. In order to prevent moisture being carried into the air brake and control air system, these reservoirs should be drained at each crew change.

2000 H. P. DIESEL-ELECTRIC E. M.D. LOCOMOTIVE CLASS 11 

PART II - SECTION 4 

15 OED

These locomotives were built by the Electro-Motive Division of General Motors Corporation and consist of one or more units rated at 2000 H.P. each. The units with the cab are known as lead units, those without cab, booster units. Each unit has two 12 cylinder, Model 567 Series engines, two direct current generators, four traction motors, and a CFK-4225 steam generator to furnish steam for heating or cooling the train. 

(See Part III, Section 2, for description of steam generator). 

The engines (for description see Part I Section 2) have a speed range of 275 RPM at idle to 800 RPM at full throttle.

Their speed is controlled by an electro-pneumatic_governor control which is operated by the main throttle. In this way all engines in the locomotive are controlled simultaneously. Each notch above Run 1 on the main throttle changes the engine speed approximately 75 RPM.

Starting the engine is accomplished by pressing the engine start button located on the engine control and instrument panel. This switch energizes the starting contactors, closing the circuit from the batteries to the main generator. The main main generator, then operating as a motor, cranks the engine. 

LOCOMOTIVE OPERATION 

This class of locomotive must not be operated at a speed to exceed 100 MPH and is equipped with a speed control switch which will set the brakes at 100 MPH.

The control station contains the locomotive throttle and reverse lever. The control push-button box on the right of the controller has fused switches for: a. Control. b. Generator field. c.
