Control Station: 

The pneumatic control switch is an air operated electric switch located on the right side of the cab, below the window. 

The purpose of this switch is to reduce the power of the locomotive by bringing the speed of the engine to idle when a safety device pedal, automatic train stop, automatic train control or a speed control brake application occurs. If the throttle is in Run 5 or 6 when the switch operates, the engines will stop. Re-establishment of control to the locomotive is accomplished by resetting the manual reset button on the pneumatic control switch after the brake application has been released and the throttle placed in idle position. It will be necessary to start up all engines which have stopped. 

This switch is referred to as the "PC" switch. 

Starting Engines: 

1. At distribution panel: 

a. Be sure all fuses are in place. b. Close main battery switch. c. Close control switch. d. Close light switches as required. 

2. At control station: a. Put throttle in idle position. b. Close control push-button switch. c. Close fuel pump switch. 

3. At engine: 

a. Close fuel pump switch on engine instrument panel and watch for fuel in five-pound relief valve sight glass.

This will indicate that fuel is circulating through engine fuel oil system. b. See that isolation switch is in "start" position. c. Press in engine "start" button and hold in until engine starts. Do not hold button down for more than 10 seconds at a time. d. After lubricating oil pressure builds up, place isolation switch in "run" position. 

To Move Locomotive: 

1. Make certain main reservoir air pressure is up to 130 lb. 

2. Release all hand brakes. 

3. Isolation switches on engine control panel must be in "run" position. 

4. Close generator field switch at control station.

Handling Train: 

During normal operation it should never be necessary to move the throttle or any of the controls hastily.

Each move should be thought out carefully and made smoothly. Make sure and be positive that brakes throughout train are released. 

Starting A Train: 

The rate at which a train may be accelerated depends on the amount of power applied at the start. Therefore, it is only possible to obtain a soft start by applying less power in the first few throttle positions. This is accomplished by starting with a weak battery field. 

When starting a train with minimum battery field, it may be necessary to open the throttle above the first notch to start the whole train. Under these conditions, it is not damaging to the equipment to speed up the engines during acceleration at low locomotive speeds.

The handling of the throttle depends on the weight and type of train being hauled and the grade conditions. In gen eral it will be necessary to open the throttle as follows: 

Place the throttle in Run 1 for 2 to 3 seconds.

Then Then 

Run 2 for 2 to 3 seconds. 

Run 3 for 2 to 3 seconds. 

Run 4 until train starts. 

If train does not start in 25 to 30 seconds, shut off throttle and make sure brakes are fully released.

When slack is all taken and the whole train is moving proceed to other throttle positions as required.

When operating locomotives 11 and up with 1800 H.P. unit 1, the throttle must not be advanced beyond Run 7 position until flashes on wheel slip light indicate that the 1800 H. P. unit has made transition from series to parallel, or a speed of 30 MPH has been reached, then throttle may be advanced to Run 8 position. 

Close throttle with 2 to 3 seconds between each notch, except in case of emergency; then close without hesitation. 

When necessary to brake the train with power still

REVERSER CONDUCTORS VALVE GAUGE LIGHT CONTROL AIR BRAKE GAUGE 

SWITCHES HORN CORD 

THROTTLE BELL VALVE INDEPENDENT BRAKE VALVE STEAM GENERATOR WATER TANK PLATE 2400-4 CONTROL STATION-BOOSTER UNIT

supplied by the locomotive the throttle should be reduced to at least the sixth notch. It is very important that the locomotive brakes be released and held in the released position when the locomotive is supplying power. This must be observed. 

When preparing to stop with power applied to the locomotive and brakes applied to train, it is necessary to reduce the throttle as the train speed decreases. As the train slows down, the pulling power of the locomotive increases rapidly and might become great enough to part the train if the throttle is not reduced. The throttle should be in idle before the locomotive comes to a dead stop. 

In order to avoid serious damage to electrical equipment when locomotive is operating in parallel on heavy grades, it will be necessary to return to series at 24 MPH.

When it is desired to negotiate heavy grades with contactors in series position, it will be necessary to reduce throttle when train accelerates to prevent train speed from exceeding 27 MPH.

When operating multiple units having different shifting speeds from parallel back to series such as 1800 H.P. unit 1, with locomotives 11 to 15, inclusive, at 24 MPH shift the 2000 H. P. unit back to series and if speed continues to reduce at 19 MPH shift the 1800 H.P. (1) unit back to series.

Series to parallel transition takes place automatically at approximately 28 MPH with throttle wide open.

When operating in parallel if the speed drops to 24 MPH it is necessary to return from parallel to series by completely shutting off the throttle to idle. Leave throttle in idle position for 3 to 4 seconds to be sure change has been made back to series, then open the throttle to the desired position.

The purpose of making this transition from parallel back to series is to protect the generators and traction motors from excessive current.

In order to avoid excessive traction motor heating, it is important that the weight of train be kept down so that the speed is not reduced below the following values when running with wide open throttle. 

35 miles per hour continuously. 

30 miles per hour for one hour.

In general, it is advisable to climb a hill with wide open throttle in order that the time on the hill be kept as short as possible.

Operation of Wheels slip Indicator: 

Whenever any wheels slip, either one pair of wheels or

ELECTRO-PNEUMATIC ENGINE CONTROL AND GOVERNOR CONTROL INSTRUMENT PANEL 

AIR COMPRESSOR INTERCOOLER SCAVENGING OIL PUMP LUBRICATING OIL TANK PLATE 2400-5 RIGHT SIDE OF ENGINEROOM

both pairs of wheels on the same truck, or on any truck on the locomotive, the indicator on the instrument panel will light. To stop the slippage, ease off on the throttle one or more notches. After a few seconds throttle can be opened to desired position.

The lights will not burn continuously when wheels slip because the wheels will not slip continuously. The connections of the wheel slip relay to the power plant are such that the power is automatically cut off when the wheels slip. Therefore, the wheel will slip, then stop; slip again and stop about once a second. 

When there is a wheel slip indication, unless the rail conditions are extremely bad, it will probably be only one set of wheels that are slipping. 

It is therefore unnecessary to use sand except under the most extreme track conditions. Before applying sand, be sure to reduce the throttle until the wheel slip light stops flashing. It should be remembered that sand is injurious to the traction motors and associated apparatus. 

During transition from series to parallel the wheel slip relay will pick up, causing wheel slip light to flash.

This is not an indication that the wheels are slipping. 

The wheel slip relay will give wheel slip indications for series operation only.

Releasing Overspeed Control Application: 

When the train speed reaches 100 MPH a warning whistle will sound and in 5 seconds an automatic brake application will be initiated. Immediately after the application is initiated move the throttle to idle and place the automatic 'brake valve in lap position which will limit the application to a full service reduction. As soon as the speed has been reduced below the setting of the control, the brakes may be released. When the application is initiated air is removed from the "PC" switch, causing it to trip. Following the release, reset the "PC" switch by pulling the button out. 

The throttle may then be advanced in the usual manner. 

Throttle Emergency Stop Button: 

This button is located on the end of the throttle handle. 

Its purpose is to stop all the engines in the locomotive in case of an emergency.

It is operated by pressing the button and pushing the throttle handle to beyond the "idle" position.

Caution should be observed in ordinary handling of the throttle so that this button is not pressed in error when closing the throttle. If this is done, the engines will stop and cause a delay while restarting, as each engine must be started individually in the engineroom after the throttle has been brought back to "idle" position.

Diesel Locomotive Used As Helper: 

When operating multiple units haven different shifting speeds from parallel back to series-parallel such as 1800H. P. unit 1 with locomotives 11 to 15 inclusive, at 24 MPH shift the 2000 H. Towing Locomotive: 

When preparing the locomotive for deadheading or yard movement where it will be towed by other power, reverse lever must be in neutral and the throttle in idle position. If the locomotive is to be towed in a train any appreciable distance the reverser drum should also be locked in its neutral position, with locking pin provided for this purpose. 

When locomotive is to be handled dead in train the cutout cock on the distributing valve should be set for "dead" position. On distributing valves without the cut-out cock, the cap should be turned to dead engine position. Close douheading cock under automatic brake valve. The brake valves must be locked in running position and the distributing valve pop set at 25 lb. 

Care should be taken to see that the cooling system, steam generator and water tank are drained properly if there is any possibility of freezing. 

The main battery switch and all control switches at the control station should be opened. Place reverse lever in neutral position and remove reverse lever from control stand.

Precaution During Locomotive Operation: 

En- Maintain engine cooling water temperature 150° F to 180° F, Preferably 1650 Whenever Possible. Do Not Exceed 200° F.

Piston cooling oil pressure should be 20 to 30 lb. with engine operating in Run 8. If piston cooling oil pressure drops below 15 lb. (operating in Run 8 with hot oil) stop engine and do not operate until the cause of low oil pressure is corrected.

Loss of Load on An Engine: 

To detect a loss of load on an engine, look at the load indicator on the governor. After the engine and generator have had a chance to balance up (about 30 seconds) the load indicator should agree approximately with the indicator on the back of the electro-pneumatic governor control. If the indicator on the governor is low, it might show that: 

1. The motors across that generator are in series while all other power plants are in parallel. 

2. The "BF" and "SH" contactors are out. This may be caused by: a. Ground relay tripping. b. Starting contactors stuck shut. c. Wheel slip relay contacts dirty or open. 

3. Battery field fuse blow, this is the 80 amp. fuse in the low voltage cabinet. 

4. Dirty or poorly made contacts any place in the power or generator field circuits. In correcting any of these defects, it is important that the power plant being worked on is isolated. 

If Battery Charging Ammeter (In Low Voltage Cabinet) Always 

Shows Zero: 

1. See that auxiliary generator switch is closed. 

2. Check all fuses in circuit affected. a. 30 amp. auxiliary generator field fuse in the low voltage cabinet. b. 150 amp. battery charging fuse in the low voltage cabinet. 

3. A auxiliary generator drive belts might be loose or broken. 

In this case, or if replacing fuses is not corrective, the condition should be reported.

If Locomotive Stops in Operation: 

If the locomotive suddenly becomes inoperative, check the following fuses: 

1. 60 amp. control fuses on the distribution panel. 

2. 30 amp. control fuse opposite control switch at the control station. 

3. 15 amp. generator field fuse opposite generator field switch at the control station. 

4. 15 amp. fuel pump fuse opposite fuel pump switch at the control station.

Check the following: 

1. See that the ground relays are set in normal positions. 

2. See that the "PC" switch is set (pull out to reset). 

3. Check to see that control air pressure is up. should be 80 lb. 

4. A wire may have been jarred loose, or may be burned off. 

5. The fuel supply may have stopped. 

If Engine Fails To Stop: 

Stop engine with layshaft manual control lever if engine fails to stop when the throttle is placed in the shut-down position.

SECTION 2 COOLING SYSTEM Description: 

The cooling system of the two engines in a locomotive unit are absolutely independent, each must be filled separately.

Operation of Cooling System: 

Water is drawn from the water tank, by centrifugal water pumps located on the front end of the engine. 

These pumps circulate the water to the bottom of each cylinder liner and cylinder head out through the outlet manifold. From the engine, the water flows to two groups of radiator sections located under the roof of the locomotive body. Here the water

ALARM 

LIGHTS 

STEAM 

GAUGE SIGNAL RELAY 

("A" UNIT ONLY) ALARM RELAY LIGHT SWITCH 

BOILER POWER

CONTROL 

RELAYS BOILER 

ROTORY CONVERTER STEAM GAUGE CUTOFF VALVE PLATE 2400-12 STEAM GENERATOR AND ALARM RELAY PANEL

is cooled, returning through the oil cooler to the water tank. 

The water temperature dial gauge, located on the left front corner of the water tank, provides a means of checking the temperature of the water in the cooling system. 

Filling The Cooling System: 

The cooling water capacity is 190 gal. per engine with cooling system at "High level" and 160 gal. per engine at "Low level". 

The cooling system on No. 1 engine is filled from either side through one of the two filler pipes located back of the fuel tank under the edge of the body of the unit. The 

No. 2 engine cooling system is filled from eigher side through one of the two filler pipes located at the back end of the unit under the edge of the body of the unit. If the cooling systems are filled from the roof it is necessary to remove the cap from the filler pipe located over each water tank. Care must be taken when filling through this pipe to avoid putting water in the exhaust stacks.

Water sight glasses are provided on the water storage tanks and water level must be maintained above Minimum Water 

Level Mark (1/4 Glass). On No. 1 engine the water glass is located on the right side of the locomotive between the two engines, directly above the manhole cover in the bottom of the wind tunnel. This cover has a small inspection cover which must be raised up before the water glass can be seen. 

The No. 2 engine water glass is located directly above the traction motor blower on the end wall of the wind tunnel and is visible when facing the front of the unit. Progressive lowering of water in the gauge will indicate a leak in the cooling system. If water level drops below 1/4 Glass Shut 

Engine Down.

If the cooling system of a hot engine has been drained, do not refill immediately with cold water. Never add Cold 

Water to a Hot Engine unless hot water is not available and then very slowly, with engine idling. If this is done the sudden change in temperature might crack or warp the cylinder liners and heads.

Engine High Temperature Alarm Switch: 

This is a thermal switch located beside the engine control and instrument panel and connects with two thermal elements, one in each water outlet manifold of the engine. 

If the water temperature exceeds 2000 F this switch closes, operating the signal relay, which lights the hot engine alarm lights and rings the alarm bell.

Cab Temperature Control: 

The temperature of the cab in cold weather can be controlled by two valves, located in the cab, one under each

seat. There is also a valve in the steam line located under the cab floor.

SECTION 3  Description: 

The lubricating oil system of the engines in the locomotive are identical to and independent of each other. The oil is stored in the oil tank which has an overflow line to the oil pan.

This engine is classified as a wet sump type. 

Operation Of Low Oil Pressure Alarm Switch: 

A failure of the lubricating oil pressure will cause the alarm switch contacts to open. When these contacts open, the master "E" valve will be de-energized, bringing the engine to idle. The low oil signal light and alarm bell will ring. Should this occur, do not attempt to load the engine until the cause of the low oil pressure has been located and corrected. 

Lube Oil Suction Alarm Switch: 

The lube oil suction alarm switch is a vacuum switch connected to the suction side of the lube oil and piston cooling oil pumps, and electrically connected to the master "E" valve and low oil signal alarm circuits. The purpose of this switch is to reduce the engine speed and at the same time energize the low oil signal alarm circuit should the oil pump suction reach the value at which the switch is set to operate. Excessive oil pump suction is the result of clogged suction strainers.

Operation of this switch will most commonly occur at full engine speed as the pump suction increases with engine speed. The switch will then open the master "E" valve circuit and close the low oil signal alarm circuit. However, as the engine speed decreases, due to de-energizing the "E" valve, the pump suction will also decrease, allowing the suction switch to reset, thus re-energizing the "E" valve and opening the low oil signal alarm circuit. The engine will then speed up until the pump suction again becomes excessive, and the cycle will repeat. If this occurs, the 

Engine Should Be Shut Down and no attempt made to load it until cause of lube oil suction alarm is located and corrected.

The fuel system of all the engines in the locomotive are independent of and identical to each other. A fuel tank is mounted under each unit of the locomotive. At the bottom of the tank are two sumps, the tank sump and the supply sump, which are connected through an emergency fuel cut-off valve. 

The fuel pump draws fuel oil from the supply sump through a suction line and suction filter. Fuel can only be drawn from the supply sump if the emergency cut-off valve is open. After leaving the pump, fuel is pumped through the Duplex filter and sintered bronze filter to the injectors. The injectors use only part of the fuel pumped through them. The surplus fuel oil lubricates and cools the internal mechanism of the injectors and then returns to the fuel tank through the 5 lb. relief valve and sight glass.

The capacity of the fuel tanks is 1200 gal. per unit. 

Emergency Fuel Cut-Off Valve (See General Instructions all 

Locomotives, Fuel Pump: 

The fuel pump assembly consists of an electric motor and a pump of the rotor gear type. The gear pump is driven by the motor shaft through flexible couplings. Do not leave the fuel pump running unnecessarily.

Fuel Sight Glass And Relief Valve: 

The Fuel Sight Glass and relief valve assembly is located above the fuel pump. Its purpose is to indicate the condition of the various filters in the fuel system and the flow of fuel oil through the engine. The left relief valve is set at 60 lb., the center at 100 lb., and the right at 5 lb. 

The return fuel from the engine passes through the righthand sight glass and relief valve (5 lb.). A drop in the fuel level in this glass, or an empty glass, will indicate that the engine is not receiving its full supply of fuel. 

The left sight glass will show fuel by-passing when any of the filters on the pressure side of the fuel pump collect enough dirt to build up a back pressure of 60 lb. This fuel by-passes the filters and engine and returns to the tank. In an emergency the globe valve between the 60 lb. relief valve and the return pipe to the tank can be closed to force the fuel through the filters. This permits operation of the engine

until the maintenance point is reached.

This section deals with the description and function of electrical equipment on the locomotive. Reverser. 

Rev.:

Its The reverser is an air-operated reversing switch. purpose is to change the direction of flow of the current through the traction motor fields, thus reversing the rotation of the traction motors. The current does not reverse in the main generator. If the control air pressure is low (should be 80 pounds) or either of the magnet valves on top of the reverser is inoperative, the reverser will not operate properly. 

When the locomotive is to be towed in a train, it is advisable to lock the reverser drum in neutral. During normal operation a locking pin is screwed into the left hand side of the reverser. To lock the reverser drum in neutral, this pin should be removed and inserted in the hole on the opposite side. Turn the drum to its neutral position and the pin will engage with the hole in the shaft.

Engine Starting_Contactors ST+, ST-: 

The starting contactors are used to connect the battery to the main generator for starting the Diesel engines. These starting contactors are of the magnetic type. There are two for each power plant and located in the low voltage cabinet. 

The starting contactors close when the "start" switch button is pressed and isolation switch is in "start" position. Interlocks on these contactors open the generator field circuit when the contactors are operating. 

Battery Charging Contactor BC: 

This contactor connects the auxiliary generator to the battery. The reverse current relay closes the battery charging contactor when the auxiliary generator voltage is high enough to charge the battery. 

Battery Field Contactor BF: 

This contactor closes when throttle is moved to Run 1 position, connecting the battery to the main generator

battery field.

During transition the contactor is opened, reducing the generator output. 

Generator Shunt Field Contactor SH: 

This contactor connects the main generator shunt field to the main generator armature. As in the case of the battery field contactor, the generator shunt field contactor is opened during transition, reducing the generator output. Power Switches "P1" "P2" and "S": 

These contactors change the traction motor connections from series to parallel or vice versa. They operate on air pressure through magnet valves. 

Traction Motor Field Shunting Contactors "M1" and "M2":

These contactors connect the shunting resistors across the traction motor fields, decreasing the resistance of the circuit. Thus, more current can flow through the armature, increasing the torque of the motors, which in turn increases the speed of the locomotive.

Fuel Pump Contactor FPC: 

The fuel pump contactor is located behind the engine control and instrument panel of number one engine only. All fuel pump contactors throughout the locomotive are controlled by the fuel pump switch at the controller. This contactor provides a means of taking the current from the battery in the unit where the fuel pump motors are located, thus preventing overload on the leading unit's battery. When this contactor is closed, the fuel pumps can be controlled individually by a toggle switch mounted on the engine control and instrument panel.

Voltage Regulators: 

The voltage regulator maintains the auxiliary generator voltage at a constant of 74 volts throughout the operating speed range of the engine, regardless of the gravity of the battery. This keeps the battery charged and provides a constant source of excitation for the main generator during locomotive operation.

A defective voltage regulator will be evidenced by excessive fluctuations of the ammeter in the low voltage cabinet. 

Ground Protective Relay GR: 

If a ground occurs in the high voltage system, this

relay will open the battery and shunt field contactors and bring the engine to idle. Should this occur, isolate the engine and reset the relay by pushing in on the isulated button. Put the engine back on the line. If the relay repeatedly opens when the engine speed is increased, isolate that power plant. A ground in the low voltage system will also trip the relay when an engine is being started. (See 

Plate 2400-7).

Reverse Current Relay RCR: 

This relay prevents the battery current from "motoring" the auxiliary generator by opening the battery charging contactor when the auxiliary generator voltage drops below the battery voltage.

Time Delay VT: This relay delays the closing of "P1" contactor when transition occurs, giving the main generator voltage time to drop, thus preventing arcing of contacts. The time delay relay is mounted in the high voltage cabinet.

Wheel Slip Relay WSR: 

The relay is connected between the armature and fields of the traction motors. The relay has two normally open contacts. In the event of a pair of wheels slipping, the relay connected to that motor will pick up, one pair of contacts will light the wheel slip light on the instrument panel. 

The other pair of contacts will de-energize the shunt field operating coil, causing the shunt field and battery field contactors to open, and the main generator on the affected unit to cut off power until the slippage stops. This relay is connected so that it will operate in series position only. 

When wheels slip, it is necessary to reduce throttle until the wheels stop slipping and then re-apply the load by opening the throttle gradually. If necessary to use sand, it should be applied (sparingly) only after the wheels stop slipping.

Due to the fact that "P1" closes slightly before "P2", the relay will pick up causing a brief flash of the wheel slip light. This is an indication of transition from series to parallel. With several power plants going through transition the wheel slip light may flash several times.

Oil Pressure Relay OPR: 

This relay operates from the low oil pressure switch and

is located inside the engine control and instrument panel. 

It lights the low oil signal and opens the circuit to the master "E" valve. This in turn cuts off the air supply to the "A", "B", and "C" valve in the governor control thus bringing the engine to idle. If the throttle is in No.5 or

No. 6 position the "D" valve will be left energized thus stopping the engine.

Voltage (Transition) Relay V1: 

As the locomotive starts to move (motors in series) the generator voltage increases to approximately 980. At this voltage the relay "picks up" to put the motors in parallel. (This occurs at approximately 28 MPH). As the locomotive continues increasing in speed and the voltage again builds up to approximately 980, the relay operates (approximately 

70 MPH) shunting the motor fields. This drops the generator voltage again. If the locomotive continues increasing in speed the relay stays closed.

On the "downward" transition, as the speed decreases the relay "drops out", (700-710 volts) returning the motors to parallel (opening the shunting contactors). After a further decrease in speed, to bring the motors to their original or starting position (series), the throttle must be brought to idle position.

Isolation Switch: 

The isolation switch is located at the engine control and instrument panel. Its purpose is to open and close the control circuit of the unit as occasion demands. When starting the engine, this switch must be in the "start" position. 

After engine is running, move switch to "run" position. This will connect the control circuit of the engine to the control station. If it becomes necessary to isolate the unit, the isolation switch should be moved to the "start" position. 

This will bring the engine to idle and cut off the power of this engine.

Pneumatic Control Switch PC: 

The pneumatic control switch is an air-operated electric switch located on the right side of the cab, below the window. 

The purpose of this switch is to reduce the power of the locomotive by bringing the speed of the engines to idle when certain air-brake applications take place. The switch has a manual reset button which must be pulled out after the air brakes have been released and the throttle placed in idle position. This switch is referred to as the "PC" switch. 

Main Battery Switch: 

This switch isolates all circuits from the battery except

the external charging receptacle and the battery neutral wire. Opening the switch when the engine is running will not stop the engine. Switch should be opened when locomotive is left standing with engines shut down or when charging battery from an external source. 

Battery Charging Switch: 

One charging switch is used for each power plant. All battery charging equipment of one power plant (auxiliary generator, reverse current relay, battery charging contactor and voltage regulator) is isolated from the battery if its charging switch is opened. The generator battery field also feeds through this switch. Therefore, to isolate the auxiliary generator, remove the 150 ampere fuse in series with the auxiliary generator, Battery Load Switch: 

Locomotives 11, 11A, 15, 15A and 13A are equipped with battery load switch. The two lighting circuits of the locomotive are connected to this double-pole double-throw switch which is located on the distribution panel. If one circuit is more heavily loaded than the other (due to lamps burning out or a receptacle being used), reversing the switch periodically will equalize the drain on the battery. 

Start And Stop Switches: 

These switches are located on the engine control and instrument panel. They will operate only when the isolation switch is closed. The "start" switch operates the starting contactors. The "stop" switch energizes the "D" magnet valve in the electro-pneumatic governor control, stopping the engine. 

Fuel Pump Switch: 

This is a fused push-button switch located in the switch box at the right of the controller. The fuel pump contactors throughout the locomotive are controlled by this switch. When this switch is closed, each fuel pump can be individually controlled by a toggle switch located on the engine control and instrument panel.

Control And A.T.C. Knife Switches: 

This switch is located on the distribution panel and controls battery power to the relays, contactors, etc., in the control circuit. The other controls the battery power to the automatic train control equipment.

Engine Room Light Switches: 

The two (three way) switches operate independently of each other to turn the engineroom lights on or off. If the lights are on, either switch will turn them off and vice versa. 

Cab Light Switch: 

This switch turns on the two cab lights. It is located behind the seat on right side of cab. 

Hood Light Switch: 

This switch turns on the two hood lights. It is located to the right of the door to the hood. 

Engine High Temperature Alarm Switch TS: 

This is a thermal switch located at the engine control and instrument panel and connects with two thermal elements, one in each water outlet manifold of the engine. If the water temperature exceeds 200 degres F this switch closes, operating the signal relay, which lights the hot engine alarm lights and rings the alarm bells.

Battery: 

The locomotive storage battery has 32 cells and is rated at 426 ampere-hours (8 hour rating). Gravity should be between 1230 and 1250.

Battery Charging Resistor: 

This resistor is connected in series with the auxiliary generator. It prevents overloading of the auxiliary generator. 

Shunt Field Resistor: 

This resistor is connected in series with the generator shunt field. Its purpose is to regulate the shunt-field current. 

Shunt Field Discharge Resistor: 

This resistor is connected in series with the main generator shunt-field and is shorted out by the shunt field contactor. Its purpose is to reduce the high induced voltage when generator shunt field contactor opens.

Battery Field Discharge Resistor: 

This resistor shunts the main generator battery field to dissipate the high induced voltage when the circuit to the battery field is opened. 

Traction Motor Field Shunting Resistor: 

This resistor is connected across the traction motor fields when the shunt contactor closes. Thus, more current can flow through the traction motors, permitting increase in the speed of the locomotive. 

Wheel Slip Resistors: 

The 3500 ohm resistors are in series with the relay coils. The adjustable 700 ohm tube balances the inner coil to the outer coil.

Headlight Resistors: 

The resistor is in series with the headlight which is connected to the battery. 

Voltage Relay Resistors: 

The resistors are in series to drop the voltage across the relay. The adjustable resistor provides a means of setting the voltage at which the relay will operate. 

Load Regulator Resistors: 

These resistors are mounted under the load regulator and are in series with the generator battery field. 

Signal Relay Resistor: 

This resistor is mounted in the junction box with the signal relay and is in parallel with the relay coil to allow enough current to flow through the circuit to light the alarm lights.

High Voltage Cabinet: 

The high voltage cabinet contains the following equipments: (See Plate 2400-8)

Electro-Pneumatic Governor Control Magnet Valves A,B,C,D: 

This assembly consists of four magnet valves-designated as "A", "B", "C", and "D" valves. Various combinations of these valves control engine speed in accordance with throttle positions. 

Master ("E") Magnet Valve:

De- 

This magnet valve controls the air supply to the "A", "B", and "C" valves of the electro-pneumatic governor control. energizing this valve will stop the engine if throttle is in run 5 or run 6 or bring the engine to idle in any other throttle position.

FORWARD TRANSITION 

Before the locomotive will move, the following switches must be closed: Main battery switch, Main control switch, Control (Push button) switch, Generator field switch, and Isolation switch. The reversing handle and Reverser must be in "forward" or "reverse". The throttle must be in No. 1 position to complete the circuit to the control equipment. 

Pneumatic-Control switch must be set. 

1. Idle to series transition (Starting the locomotive): 

The a. Opening throttle completes electrical circuit to relays and contactors. b. S Contactor closes through Pl interlock. c. SH Contactor closes through ST+, ST-, GR and WSR. d. BF Contactor closes through SH interlock. 

2. Series to parallel transition (28 MPH approx.). a. Transition relay picks up. b. Holding coil is energized through V1 and P2 interlocks. c. Vl opening drops SH and BF contactors. d. Vl operating coil is thereby de-energized. e. Time delay relay closes through V1, S and BF interlocks closing VT. f. Pl contactor closes through V1, S and VT. g. Pl interlock holds Pl contactor closed.

h. S contactor is de-energized at Pl interlock. 

1. P2 contactor closes through Pl and S interlocks. 

J. Vl holding coil is de-energized by P2 interlock. k. SH and BF contactors closes by opening V1 interlock. 1. VT relay is de-energized by BF interlock opening. 

3. Parallel to shunt transition (approximately 75 MPH): a. Transition relay picks up. b. M1 and M2 contactors close through V1 and P2 interlocks. 

BACKWARD TRANSITION 

1. Shunt to parallel transition (approximately 58 MPH): a. Transition relay drops out. b. M1 and M2 contactors open. 

2. Parallel to series transition (accomplished by closing throttle to idle at 24 MPH): a. Throttle circuit opens, de-energizing P1, P2, BF and SH contactors.

SECTION 6 AIR SYSTEM Air Compressor: 

Each power plant in the locomotive is equipped with a type ADX air compressor which is driven by the diesel engine through a Falk coupling. The compressor has its own oil pump and lubricating system. Oil level in the crankcase can be checked on the bayonet gauge. 

Condensation collects in the bottom shell of the compressor intercooler and should be drained once at each crew change. A drain valve is provided for this purpose. 

The compressor is equipped with an unloader to maintain the main air reservoir pressure. This unloader is adjusted to cut in at 130 pounds pressure, and cut out at 140 pounds pressure. If it is suspected that the unloader is stuck, and blowing it out by means of the pet cock on the bottom does not help, the condition should be reported to the maintenance point for correction.

Draining Air Reservoir and Filter: 

The air reservoirs are located under the body and just ahead of the fuel tank of the unit. The filters are located by the distributing valves on both units. In order to prevent moisture being carried into the air brake and control air system, these reservoirs and filters should be drained at each crew change.

SECTION 7 MISCELLANEOUS EQUIPMENT Coupler Arrangement 

Locomotive 11: 

Diesel Locomotive Number 11 has a retractable coupler which is operated as follows: 

Open pilot doors and hook the doors to the pilot.

There is a small Facing the coupler, move coupler out into position and then move the coupler shank drawbar over to the left against the stop. Put drawbar lock latch in position (which is hinged) by raising it up and pull forward. hinged door to the left of the plate at the bottom which must be opened and the air and signal hose removed. rangement is ready for coupling with the helper. The ar- Should steam be required, the lower door at the front of the pilot should be removed and taken into the locomotive. 

This exposes the steam line which has a valve mounted behind the pilot and is operated by a handle from the right side which extends through the pilot. 

To retract the coupler, the operations are reversed. 

First the drawbar lock latch is raised and pushed back. Move coupler shank drawbar to the right as far as it will go and swing drawbar head to the left as far as it will go and closefront doors. Fold air and signal hose up in pocket and close door. 

Locomotives 12, 13, 14 and 15 (equipped with air operated retractable coupler)

This coupler arrangement places the pulling face of the coupler two inches in front of the pilot.

This arrangement consists of the buffer with coupler pocket. Draft gear, yoke and coupler are carried on the underside of the buffer, and this entire unit is moved in or out by air operated pistons.

Normally, there is a smooth front end without any

projection and if a "helper" is required the following procedure should be followed:

Open main air shut off valve behind right side of cab near floor in engineroom. Open pilot doors, fold anticlimber up and hook the doors to the pilot. 

The air and signal hoses should be moved out of the large pocket so that they are hanging down ready to attach to the "helper". Facing the coupler there is a 4-way cock on the left side of the coupler controlling air to pistons. 

Move handle of the 4-way cock to left and air will force coupler out. Put air valve on center after coupler has been forced out. Do Not Stand In Line With Coupler. 

When the stop on top of the buffer engages with the stop in the platform, the key slots in the buffer should match the key slots in the platform and track supporting the buffer. 

On top of the water tank in line with each of the two keys is a ratchet operated by a handle, and immediately below the handle is a rod extending. When this rod is pushed in, the ratchet is released, dropping the keys down into the slots until the top of the rack is almost flush with the ratchet casting. Should the key not drop fully down, about a dozen strokes with the handle will force the keys down in position, and the arrangement is ready for coupling with the "helper". 

Should steam be required, the lower door at the front of the pilot should be removed and taken into the locomotive. 

This exposes the steam line which has a valve mounted behind the pilot and is operated by a handle from the right side which extends through the pilot. 

Should the pocket be filled with ice and snow, there is a inch steam line branching out at the platform, entering into the rear end of the pocket with a inch opening for admitting steam.

To retract the coupler, the operations are reversed. 

First the keys are raised by working the ratchets on top of the water tank, and the ratchets are reversed by pulling out the handle. Each stroke with the handle raises the key one notch, and when the handle is in down position, the pawl at the top of the ratchet prevents the keys from dropping down in between strokes. The keys should not be lifted entirely out of the slots at the botton as otherwise they may not engage when it is desired to drop the keys.

With the keys in up position, the buffing unit is ready to be moved into the pocket. 

Move handle of the four way valve to the right and air will force the coupler in. After the coupler has been forced into the pocket, place four way valve on center or vertical position and close shut off valve in engineroom.

Bend air and signal hoses into the large pocket and close door. 

CAUTION: Be sure coupler keys are down in place before helper engine is coupled on. Do not raise coupler keys until helper engine is cut off.

Santa Fe 

DIESEL ELECTRIC E. M. D. LOCOMOTIVES 

CLASS 16 (16-36) (37 & UP) 

CLASS 300 CLASS 325 

SANTA FE PART II-SECTION 5

Description 

SECTION 1 

The passenger locomotive consists of units rated at 1500 hp. The units with the cab are called lead units, while the units without cabs are called the booster units. Each unit is equipped with a 16-567-B engine (See Part 1, Sec. 2) which drives the main generator and auxiliaries, as later described. 

The engine has a speed range of 275 r.p.m. to 800 r.p.m.

at full throttle. The speed is controlled by an electro-hydraulic governor. Through electrical connections to the throttle, the speeds of all engines are simultaneously controlled. 

The main generator consists of a D. C. generator, and A. C. generator (alternator) mounted on the same shaft, and driven by the engine through a flexible coupling. The alternator furnishes power for the traction motor blowers and engine cooling fans. The D. C. generator is connected to the traction motors, through power switches. The flow of current through the generator is always in the same direction, but reversing of the locomotive is accomplished by reversing the current in the traction motors.

LOCOMOTIVE OPERATION 

This class of locomotive must not be operated in excess of 100 m.p.h. Maximum speed for 325 class is 80 m.p.h. It is equipped with a speed control switch which will set the brakes if this speed is exceeded.

The control station contains the throttle lever, transition lever and reverse lever. Fuses are no longer contained in the control box but are in the distribution panel. The instrument panel contains the following indicators: Brake cylinder and brake pipe air gage, main reservoir and equalizing reservoir air gage, wheel slip light, transition meter and brake warning light. (Plate 2500-1)

The transition meter is an ammeter in the circuit of the No.4 traction motor and indicates the loading of that traction motor. If the lead unit is shut down or the No. 4 traction motor is cut out, the transition meter will be inoperative. 

The "PCS" open indicating light is located to the left and above the instrument panel. This light will burn only in the event of an emergency or penalty application of brakes. 

To get the light out, a good procedure is to move the throttle to idle position and then do what is required to recover the brake application.

If the brakes are applied in "Service", the brake valve should be placed in the "Lap" position and left there until light goes out. If there has been an emergency application, the brake valve must first be lapped and then put in the running position.

The purpose of the "PC" switch is to bring the engines to "Idle" when a safety device pedal, automatic train stop, automatic train control or a speed control brake application occurs. If the throttle is in run No.5 or No.6 the engines will be stopped but no bells with ring. The "PC" switch is located in the nose of the cab units. 

The sanding valve is located to the right of the right cab seat, adjacent to the window, and operates all sanders on the locomotive. 

The unit selector switch is located to the left of the instrument panel and in front of the control stand. It should be set to the number of units making up the locomotive. This switch is set by the maintenance forces and sealed. It should not be changed en-route.

The headlight switch is located above the window on the right side.

The speed recorder is hydraulically operated and is driven through a flexible shaft from the left No.1 truck of the lead unit. 

The hand brake is located in the engine room to the right of the air compressor. To set the hand brake, depress the foot pedal and turn the wheel until the brakes are set. To release the brakes, advance the wheel until foot pedal latch releases, let go of the wheel and keep clear of the wheel. On locomotives having the hand brake with friction lock, rotate wheel against friction lock to release brakes.

Engine Control and Instrument Panel: 

This panel is located in the engine room and mounted on the frame supporting the engine cooling water tank near the governor end of the engine. It contains the lube oil pressure and suction gages, fuel tank level gage, and start, stop, fuel pump and isolation switches. (Plate 2500-2)

Distribution Panel: 

This panel contains switches and fuses controlling battery, ammeter and fuse test light and test blocks are located here. (Plate 2500-16)

PLATE 2500-2 ENGINE CONTROL AND INSTRUMENT PANEL 1. Isolation Switch 2. Engine "Stop" Button 3. Fuel Pump Motor Switch 

4. Engine "Start" Button 

5. Lube Oil Pressure Gauge 6. Fuel Oil Gauge (Tank level) 

7. Lube Oil Suction Gauge

To Start Engines: 

1. Close all switches in distribution panel. 

2. At control station close "control" and "fuel pump" switches. 

3. Place independent brake in full application position. 

4. Check "PCS" light. 

5. Be sure reverse lever is REMOVED from control stand. 

6. Turn on fuel pump switch and check fuel flow in sight glass mounted on right front of engine. 

7. Check setting of overspeed trip. (Plate 2500-3) 

8. Check governor oil alarm trip button. 

9. Press engine start button until engine starts. Do not crank engine over 10 seconds, if the engine does not start by this time look for cause of trouble. Hold back on lay shaft to keep engine from speeding up too fast. 

10. Check oil pressure. 

11. Check ground relay. 

12. Check starting contactors and interlocks. 

13. Place isolation switch in "Run" position.

To Move Locomotive: 

1. Make certain main air reservoir is up to 130 p.s.i. 

2. Release all hand brakes. 

3. Close generator field switch. 

4. Put transition lever in No. 1 position. 

5. Move reverse lever to direction desired. 

6. Place foot on safety device pedal. 

7. Open throttle as required.

Operation of Transition Lever and Transition Indicator: 

These locomotives are equipped with automatic transi- To operate the locomotive in automatic transition, place the transition lever in the No. 1 position and leave it there. tion and will be operated in that manner.

TO SET 

PLATE 2500-3 OVERSPEED TRIP

The transition lever has an "off" position that sets up contacts for dynamic braking. As the transition lever is moved farther to the "B" position, the battery field contacts close exciting the fields of all main generators. The further the transition lever moves the more excitation is furnished the traction motors and, therefore, the greater braking effort. When the The transition lever is so designed that it can be moved only one transition position at a time. When moving from one position to another, lift lever upwards against the upper quadrant and then move it in the direction desired. lever strikes the end of the upper slot, lower lever to bottom quadrant (depressing bottom lug) and complete move to adjacent position.

The transition lever is mechanically interlocked with the throttle and can not be moves from transition No. 2 to 

No.3 with the throttle in Run 8. The throttle must be in the No.6 position or less before transition No. 2 to No.3 or No.3 to No. 2 can be made.

The transition meter in an accurate guide to the load and pulling force of the locomotive. Since the amperage is the same in all motors, each motor receives the amount of current shown on the meter. The dial of the meter is graduated into amperes from 0 at the left to 1500 amperes at the extreme right of the scale. (Plate 2500-4) 

On locomotives 37 and up 300 Class and 325 Class the ratings listed directly below the transition meter show the time it is permissible to operate at various stages of overload. These short time ratings are accumulative. In other words, it is permissible to operate at 865 amps for two hours, then at 900 amps. for one hour, then at 925 amps for 1/2 hour, then at 950 amps for 1/4 hour. After these ratings have been used it is necessary to reduce the load on the 10comotive enough to bring the transition meter back to 825 amps. or less for at least 15 minutes.

On locomotives 16 through 36, the short time ratings listed directly below the transition meter show the time it is permissible to operate at various stages of overload. 

These short time ratings are accumulative. In other words, it is permissable to operate at 750 amperes for one hour, then 775 amperes for hour, then 800 amperes for hour.

After these rating have been used it is necessary to reduce the load on the locomotive enough to bring the transition meter back to 700 amperes or less for at least 15 minutes. Automatic Transition: 

In automatic transition the transition lever is put in the No. 1 position and left there. Transition is made automatically with the throttle in Run 8 position. When train

In the event proper backward transition is not being made, the locomotive throttle can be reduced to idle and then advanced to the throttle position desired. In this way the proper transition position will be reached. Speeds at which transition should take place are shown in the section on manual transition. By comparing these speeds with transition meter action, it can be determined when the locomotive makes transition and whether or not transition is made at the correct speed. 

Manual Transition: 

If any unit of a multiple unit consist has to be controlled manually, the locomotive must be started with the transition lever in the No.1 position. The lever is then advanced to the 2nd, 3rd, and 4th positions consecutively, as the locomotive speed increases, according to the table shown below.

In the same manner, as the speed decreases, as on a grade, the transition lever must be returned from position to position as indicated in the table. Manual transition from position 2 to 3 or 3 to 2 cannot be made unless the throttle is placed in Run 6 or lower.

When controlling from a unit that is operating in automatic transition, movement of the transition lever does not affect transition in that unit but does effect transition in the manually operated units.

If at any time the transition meter does not function properly or the lead unit is isolated and there is no transsition meter indication, transition should be made by reference to locomotive speed.

PLATE 2500-5 ENGINE GOVERNOR

Whenever any wheels slip, either one or both pairs on the same truck or on any truck on the locomotive, the indicator on the instrument panel will light. To stop the slippage reduce the throttle one or more notches. After a few seconds the throttle can be returned to the desired position. 

The light will not burn continuously when the wheels slip because wheels do not slip continuously. The connections from the wheel slip relay to the power plant are such that the power is automatically reduced when the wheels slip. Therefore, the wheels will slip, then stop, slip again and stop about once a second. Unless rail conditions are extremely bad, it will probably be only one pair of wheels slipping, making it unnecessary to apply sand.

When it is necessary to apply sand, be sure to reduce the throttle until wheel slip light stops flashing before applying sand. Because sand is injurious to traction motors and associated apparatus, it should be used only when absolutely necessary. 

A steady glow of the wheel slip light indicates electrical trouble and should be immediately investigated. 

Air Braking With Power: 

When it is necessary to apply air brakes with full power still supplied by the locomotive, the throttle should be reduced to at least the sixth notch. It is imperative that the locomotive brakes be released and held in the release position when the locomotive is supplying power. 

When preparing to stop with power applied to the loconotive and brakes applied to the train, it is necessary to reduce the throttle as the speed of the train decreases. As the train slows down the pulling power of the locomotive rapidly increases and might become great enough to part the train. The throttle MUST be in idle before the train comes to a dead stop.

These instructions are not to interfere with operating instructions covering the use of dynamic or air brakes. 

Throttle Emergency Stop Button: 

This button is located on the end of the throttle lever. 

Its purpose is to stop all engines of the locomotive in case of emergency. It is operated by depressing the button and move the throttle to beyond the "idle" position.

Caution should be observed in normal operation not to press button by mistake when closing throttle. In the event engines are stopped in this manner, engines must be started

in the engine room after throttle is returned to idle.

Dynamic Brake: 

The dynamic brake is an electrical retarding device utilizing the traction motors and main generator to retard the train. It is not dependent on the air brake system and will function only when the engines are in operation and the train is moving.

The unit selector switch controls the field loop circuit and should be set for the number of units comprising the 10comotive and not changed. This will normally be done by the maintenance forces. When a booster unit is the trailing unit of the locomotive, the field loop jumper must be removed to complete the field loop circuit. The brake may be used at any speed, but is most effective at 28 MPH. 

To apply the dynamic brake, first place the throttle in the "Idle" position but leave the transition lever in the No.1 position for at least 10 seconds, then move the transition lever to the "off" position. After a five second wait, move the transition lever to the "B" position. Braking effort in this position will be sufficient to bunch slack in the train; however, at high train speeds care must be taken in applying the dynamic brake to avoid excessive slack action (run in) in the train. Pause 5 seconds in "B" position before moving to maximum braking.

The transition meter indicates the traction motor current in dynamic braking as well as when motoring. The dynamic brake can be loaded up to maximum amps as shown on rating plate. The pointer should not be allowed to go to the right of the triangle. In the event of overloading the dynamic brake, the dynamic brake warning light will come on.

By backing off on the transition lever the light will go out. If the dynamic brake will not maintain the desired train speed, the air brakes can be used in conjunction with the dynamic brake. Locomotive brakes must be kept release at all times when using the dynamic brake to avoid slid flat wheels. Some locomotives are equipped with the dynamic brake interlock which prevents application of locomotive brakes. 

In stopping the train when using dynamic brake, move the transition lever to the "off" position when the brakes fade out at low speed and make a light application of the locomotive brakes to avoid a severe run-out, complete the stop in the prescribed manner.

Difference in idling speeds of the engines will cause the dynamic brake warning light to come on before the pointer reaches the maximum amperage. At the first indication of overload (warning light or load meter) the brake must be reduced. Failure to reduce the overload will result in damage to the grids or traction motors.

Isolating Engine When Using Dynamic Brake: 

The dynamic brake can be isolated by putting the isolation switch in the "Start" position. FIRST, put the brake lever in the "Off" position, then move the isolation switch to "Start". DO NOT CHANGE THE UNIT SELECTOR. In an emer gency the dynamic brake on a unit can be isolated by stopping the engine in the unit by the manual lay shaft lever and then put the isolation switch in the "Start" position. 

When the lead unit is shut down and dynamic braking is being used, it will be necessary to train-line the batteries between the lead and the first booster unit. If this is not done, the lead unit batteries will be too weak to give adequate dynamic braking excitation. 

The isolation switch should not be closed to the "run" position while using dynamic brake. If this precaution is not observed, a heavy overload will be applied to the electrical equipment and possibly cause the wheels to slide. 

Operating With Steam Locomotive Helper: 

Steam locomotives often have tonnage ratings based on speeds lower than the continuous rating speed of the Diesel. 

It is, therefore, necessary to be careful that the Diesel locomotive does not pull more than its share of the tonnage and overheat the traction motors. Knowing the length of grade to be traversed, the time on the grade can be closely estimated. This time will set the minimum speed and maximum loading allowable. If the estimated time on grade is between 30 minutes and two hours, the transition meter must not be allowed to go higher than 865 amp. If the transition meter pointer does indicate excessive loading, reduce the throttle until the pointer comes within prescribed limits. In this way the steam locomotive will assume its share of the load.

If the transition meter is out of order the speed of the locomotive will indicate to you the traction motor loading. Do not operate at a speed below that indicated in the following table for time in excess of that specified: Class 16 (16-36)

It is always desirable to reach the top of a grade in the least possible time in order to avoid possible damage to the electrical equipment.

Towing Locomotive: 

When preparing the locomotive for dead heading or yard movement where it will be towed by other power, the transition lever must be placed in the No.1 position, throttle in "Idle" reverse lever in neutral and the generator field switch in "Off" position. All isolation switches must be in the "Start" position. If it is necessery to keep engines idling, the fuel pump and control switch has should be left in the closed position. 

Be sure the reverse lever is in neutral. If the locomotive is to be towed in a train for any distance, the reverser should be locked in the neutral position with the locking pins provided for that purpose. 

When booster units are cut out for any reason and are to be moved "dead in train", separated from lead units, or when the jumper cables have been removed between units, the cam switch of the booster unit must be placed in the "MOT" position or the wheels will pick up and slide. 

When the locomotive is to be handled dead in train the dead engine cock on the control valve should be set for "DEAD", close double heading cock on the automatic brake valve pedestal, place Rotair valve handle in the "PASS" position, place automatic brake valve in running position and independent brake valve in release position and remove handles.

Isolating An Engine While Under Power: 

If it becomes necessary to isolate an engine while under power, reduce the engine speed by the manual lay shaft until the engine speed is at "idle", then move the isolation switch to the "Start" position. If it is then desired to stop the engine, push the "Stop" button on the engine control panel until the engine stops. Turn off the fuel pump.

Placing Engine On the Line: 

1. Close fuel pump switch. 

2. Start engine in usual manner. 

3. After engine lube oil pressure builds up, place isolation switch in "Run" position. Hold back on manual lay shaft lever and allow engine to come up to speed gradually. 

4. If engine has been dead for some time, allow engine to idle until it has warmed up sufficiently to be operated under load.

Precautions During Locomotive Operation: 

Engine cooling water temperature should be 165° F., plus or minus 150 F. This temperature is maintained by proper operation of cooling radiator shutters and fans. All engines are equipped with automatic shutter and fan controls and are

adjusted to open shutters at 165° F. and start the fans as the temperature increases. Engine cooling water should reach 

125° F. before the engine is worked. Do not operate engines if water level is below the "Low" level on the radiator tank sight glass. 

Lube oil pressure should be 35 to 45 psi. with engine in Run 8 and hot oil. If main bearing pressure drops below 

20 psi. in Run 8, stop engine and do not operate until the cause of the low oil pressure is corrected. The oil level should always be between the "Low" and "High" marks on the bayonet gage when the engine is running. 

Charging ammeters may show 20 amp. to 150 amp. charge but should never show a discharge. Control air pressure should be 90 psi.

After the engine has operated several minutes in Run 8, the load indicator on the governor should show 6/16 in. and the throttle indicator No.8 position.

Placing the throttle in the "Stop" position will stop all engines of the locomotive. 

Stopping Engines in Preparation for Layover: 

At control station: 

1. Close throttle to idle. 

2. Place reverse lever in neutral and remove handle. 

Move transition lever to "Off" position. 3.

2. Push engine "Stop" button and hold it until engine stops. 3. 

Open fuel pump switch. If engine control air pressure is low, stop button will not stop engine.

2. Open fuel pump switch.

SECTION 2 COOLING SYSTEM Description 

The cooling system of each unit making up the locomotive is entirely separate from the other units. The systems are identical except for "lead" units, which have piping for cab heater units. The water level of the engines is below the radiators when engines are shut down. Specially treated water is used in cooling systems to offset the accumulation of scale and foreign matter, which contributes to overheating the engine. 

Filling Cooling System: 

The operating water levels are stenciled on the water tank adjacent to the water gage glasses. The engine should never be operated when the water is below the minimum level mark. 

The system is filled either through the filler pipe on the roof of the locomotive or through the filler pipes on either side at the rear of the units. With the engine stopped open the "G" valve on the opposite side of the tank from the gage glasses and slightly below the top gage cock. Fill slowly until water runs out of the filling level pipe, then close "G" valve. When adding cold water, apply very slowly with engine running in "idle".

1. Open drain valve at floor level beneath water pumps. 

Remove drain plug in bottom of each water pump. 2. 3. 

On cab units, open heater drain valve. This valve is located on the floor at left side of engine room next to the wall at lube oil tank filler.

Hot Engine Alarm Switch: 

The switch contacts are set to close at 208° F. and open at 1980 F. The closing of this switch by hot engine discharge water, lights the engine alarm light and rings the alarm bell. 

The hot engine will continue to operate until it is manually shut down or until failure occurs. 

Thermostat Switch (Verna therm): 

The thermostat switch is located in the water discharge line to the radiators. The switch governs the operation of the shutters through an air magnet valve and the fans through fan contactors located in the high voltage cabinet on Locomotives 16 to 21, and above the engine control panel on Locomotives 22 and up.

In the event of a hot engine alarm, where no other discrepancy is found in the cooling system, a check should be made to determine that radiator shutters and cooling fans are operating properly.

If the fans are not operating, inspect the fan contactors. It may be that the contactors have not closed but will operate if manually closed by lifting up the contactor armature.

SECTION 3  Description 

Oil under pressure is forced through the engine for lubrication and piston cooling. The lube oil falls into the crankcase pan and is picked up by the scavenging oil pump and forced through filters and cooler before it is recirculated by the oil pump.

Lube Oil Pressure: 

Lube oil pressure will be between 30 and 50 psi. in Run 8 when the engine is hot and should never go below 20 psi. idle, the pressure should run between 16 and 25 psi. 

Low Oil Pressure and High Suction:

At The low oil pressure and high suction alarm system is a built-in feature of the electro-hydraulic governor. In the event of low oil pressure or high suction, the governor will automatically shut the engine down to "idle" speed. When this occurs the button on the front of the governor (next to water tank) will be out. To reset button, push it in.

Possible Lubrication Troubles: 

(a) Absence of Oil in Strainer Chamber May be caused by an inoperative scavenging system or open drain valve. 

Failure of the scavenging system may be due to a loose oil connection, causing an air leak,faulty scavenging pump or clogged suction screen or low oil level. 

(b) Low Lube Oil Pressure 

May be due to a stuck relief valve or foreign material under valve holding valve open, broken oil lines, clogged suction strainers, excessive bearing wear, low viscosity, faulty pump or insufficient oil in strainer housing. (c) Failure of Oil Pump - May be due to sheared pump gear keys, broken housing or damaged gears.

SECTION 4 FUEL SYSTEM 

The fuel supply tank for each locomotive unit is located under the car body and is connected to the fuel manifold through pump, strainers, filters, etc. The fuel systems of all units are identical. The fuel tanks can be filled from either side of the locomotive, but watch fuel level gauge to prevent overflow. 

Emergency Fuel Cut-Off Valve (See General Section All Loco motives)

The electrical equipment is located in cabinets opening into the cab and engine room on lead units and at the generator end of the engine on booster units. The electrical cabinets house both high and low voltage equipment. to GD ele

Cooling Fan Motor Contactors 

AC1, AC2, AC3, AC4: 

These contactors connect the radiator cooling fans to the alternator of the main generator and are controlled through the thermostat switch. 

Electro Hydraulic Governor Control Solenoids - AV, BV, CV, DV: 

Various combinations of these solenoids control engine speed in accordance with throttle position. 

Dynamic Brake Contactors B, B1, B2, B3: 

These contactors connect the traction motor fields in series with each other and to the main generator during dynamic braking. 

Battery Charging Contactor BC: 

This contactor connects the auxiliary generator to the battery. The reverse current relay closes the battery charging contactor when the auxiliary generator voltage reaches à predetermined setting to charge the battery. 

Battery Field Contactor

BF: 

This contactor closes when the throttle is moved to Run 1 position, connecting the battery to the main generator battery field.

Dynamic Brake Relay BR: 

This relay is energized when the transition lever is in "off", "B" or succeeding braking positions and establishes the position of the cam switch for dynamic braking or motoring. 

Backward Transition Current Relay 

BTR: 

This relay is a current operated relay which is closed at all times except during backward transition. When the main generator current reaches a predetermined setting, the relay picks up causing backward transition.

Dynamic Brake Warning Relay 

BWR: 

Dynamic brake warning relay is a current operated relay, which will light the brake warning light indicating that the load on the dynamic brake is excessive. The light will remain on until the load is reduced to the continuous rating of the dynamic brake equipment.

Compressor Control Magnet Valve 

CC: 

This magnet valve admits main reservoir air to the unloader valves unloading the air compressor when energized. Deenergizing the magnet valve releases the unloaders and causes the air compressor to resume pumping.

PLATE 2500-8 ELECTRIC CONTROL EQUIPMENT

Compressor Control Switch 

CCS: 

The compressor control switch is actuated by main reservoir pressure and controls the loading and unloading of the air compressor in all units through the compressor control relays and control magnet valves. 

Compressor Control Relay CR: 

The compressor control relay energizes or de-energizes the control magnet valve causing the air compressor to load and unload according to the settings of the control switches. Cam Switch CS: When The cam switch is a power-braking transfer switch. in "Motor" position, it connects the traction motor armatures in series with their respective fields and disconnects the braking grids from the motor armatures. When in "tow" position, it disconnects the traction motor armatures from their fields and connects a braking grid across each 

Engine High Temperature Switch ETS: 

armature. 

When the water temperature exceeds the maximum allowable temperature, this switch closes, lights the hot engine alarm signal light and energizes the signal relay, which rings the alarm bell. On some classes of locomotives the engine will return to idle and remain in idle as long as the hot engine alarm is on.

Relay ER: 

This relay controls the current supply to the Electro- 

Hydraulic Governor Control solenoids AV, BV and CV. De-ener gizing this relay will stop the engine if the throttle is in 

Run 5 or Run 6 or bring the engine to idle in any other throttle position.

Field Loop Contactor FL: 

The field loop contactor is energized when the transition lever is moved to the "off" "B" and succeeding braking position. This operation places the dynamic brake control rheostat and resistors and the unit selector switch and its resistors in series with the field loop circuit. 

Fuel Pump Contactor FPC: 

All fuel pump contactors throughout the locomotive are controlled by the fuel pump switch at the control station. 

When this contactor is closed, the fuel pumps can be controlled individually by the switch mounted on the engine control and instrument panel.

Forward Transition Voltage Relay 

FTR: 

This relay is a voltage operated relay which is open at

Forward Transition Relay (for motor shunting) 

FTM: 

This is a voltage operated relay, which picks up at a predetermined voltage setting. When main generator voltage reaches this value relay picks up causing transition shift to No.3. 

Ground Protective Relay GR: 

If a ground occurs in the high voltage system, this relay will open the battery field and main generator shunt field contactors, and by de-energizing the "ER" relay bring the engine to idle except when the throttle is in the 5th or 6th position and in this event the engines will stop. Should this occur isolate the engine and reset the relay by pressing in on the reset button on the cover. When the relay is tripped the white needle will point to the red indicator and to the yellow indicator when in normal position. If the relay repeatedly opens when the engine speed is increased the power plant must be isolated. 

Load and Transition Indicating Meter 

IM: 

This meter is an accurate guide to load on the locomotive as it shows amount of current furnished to the traction motors. Isolation Switch 

IS: 

The purpose of this switch is to open and close the control circuit of the unit as the occasion demands. When starting the Diesel engine this switch must be in the START position. After engine is running move switch to RUN position. This will connect the control circuit of the unit to the control station. To isolate this unit, move this switch to START position. This will bring the engine to idle and cut off the power of this unit. 

Differential Relay (Auto transition) 

KP: 

This relay is actuated by BTR. When this relay is closed a circuit is established on the negative side of "M" contactor, MR, PM, TD and PR relays.

Motor Field Shunting Contactor Magnet Valve 

M: 

This is an electro-pneumatic valve that operates the traction motor field shunting contactors. 

Traction Motor Field Shunting Contactors 

M1, M2, M3, M4: 

These contactors connect the shunting resistors across the traction motor fields. These contactors are closed in transition No. 2 and No.4.

Parallel Relay

This relay prevents the battery current from motoring the auxiliary generator by opening the battery charging contactor when the auxiliary generator voltage drops below the battery voltage.

Signal Relay

This relay operates with the signal lights (Hot Engine, Boiler Off, and Low Oil Pressure) to ring the alarm bells throughout the locomotive.

Temperature Control Relay - TCR: 

This relay is actuated by the thermostat switch and serves the following purposes a. Open Shutters b. Establishes a holding circuit for A. C. motor contactors so that each fan remains running as long as TCR remains closed. 

Time Delay Relay 

TD: 

This relay establishes a circuit energizing PR relay, When FTR interlock CD is closed. It takes three to four seconds for this relay to close. 

Time Delay Backward Relay - TDB: 

This relay takes a minimum of 28 seconds to close. This relay prevents a backward step in automatic transition after forward step has been made, until 28 seconds have elapsed. 

Thermostat Switch Assembly 

TSA: 

This switch is operated by the temperature of the engine cooling water. As the temperature rises successive contacts close in the switch at 30 degree intervals. The contacts complete circuits which open the shutters and energize the cooling fan motor contactors one by one. 

Wheel Slip Relays 

WSR1, WSR2: 

When a pair of wheels slips, these relays pick up and light the wheel slip light and reduce the power output of the unit momentarily until slipping stops.

Wheel Slip Auxiliary Relay 

WSA: 

This relay is energized on later locomotives by WSR1 or WSR2 to light the wheel slip light and reduce power output of the unit affected.

Automatic Transition 

Assume the locomotive is ready for service and the transition lever is in the No. 1 position. When the throttle is opened to the No. 1 position, The S 14 and S 23 contactors close through the regular circuits. This makes No. 1 transition position. When the speed of the locomotive increases sufficiently,

A further decrease in speed will result in an increased current output of the main generator, which will reach the value necessary to trip the BTR. This opens the KP which breaks the PR and M circuits and the locomotive drops to No. 1 transition. As soon as the voltage builds up after transition, normal transition to No. 2 takes place. Automatic Transition 

Assume the locomotive is ready for service and the transition lever is in the No. 1 position. When the throttle is moved to the No. 1 position the S-14, S-23, SH and BF close and the cam switch goes to the motor position completing No. 1 transition. With the throttle in Run 8, the main generator voltage increases to a point that causes the FTR to pick up, this causes the "M" contactors to close, putting the locomotive in transition No. 2. A further build up of main generator voltage causes the FTR to again pick up, closing the PR and opening the SH and BF and energizing the ORS. the main generator voltage drops, the "M" contactors drop out, picking up VT. S14 opens and P1 and P4 closes, S23 opens and 

P2 and P3 closes, SH and BF closes thru P3 interlock, de-energizing ORS and VT relay. This completes transition No.3. As locomotive speed and generator voltage continue to increase, the FTR again picks up, causing the "M" contactors to close, making No.4 transition.

As Backward transition. With power circuits in transition 

No. 4 as voltage drops to a predetermined level, the FTR drops out which in turn causes the "M" contactors to drop out.

This sets up No. 3 transition. As the locomotive speed decreases, the generator amperage output increases to an extent sufficient to cause the BTP to pick up. Through the TDB and 

BTP relay backward transition from No. 3 to No. 1 occurs. If conditions are such that No. 2 transition is called for, transition forward will be made as required. Backward transition from No.2 to No.1 is brought about by the increase in generator amperage, causing the BTS to pick up, thus dropping out the "M" contactors. Automatic Transition

Further increase in generator voltage as a result of increased locomotive speed (in Run 8) will cause the FTP to pick up. Transition from No. 2 to No. 3 transition then takes place. A further increase in voltage will cause the FTM to again close, making No. 4 transition. 

Backward Transition. Diminishing generator voltage when in No. 4 transition causes the FTM relay to open, dropping the "M" contactors to make No. 3 transition. Further decrease in locomotive speed in this position increases the generator amperage, which causes the BTP relay to pick up. This initiates backward transition from No. 3 to No. 1. If the generator voltage in No. 1 is sufficient the FTM will pick up to make No. 2 transition. From this point, transition back to 

No. 1 will take place when the generator voltage decreases sufficiently to drop out the FTM. 

SECTION 6 AIR SYSTEM Air Compressor: 

Each power plant in the locomotive is equipped with a 

Gardner-Denver type WXE three-cylinder air compressor, rated at 178 cubic feet of air per minute at 800 RPM. It is driven by the shaft of the main generator through a flexible coupling. The compressor has its own oil pump and pressure lubricating system. Oil level in the crankcase can be checked on the bayonet type gauge. Lubricating oil pressure should be from 12 to 15 pounds at 800 RPM and not less than 5 pounds at idle (hot oil). Since each air compressor is directly connected to an engine and in operation at all times when the engine is running, an unloader is provided in the heads of both high and low pressure cylinders which cuts out the compressing action when actuated by air pressure. The unloader accomplishes this by blocking open the suction or intake valves of the high and low pressure cylinders. When the air operating the unloader is cut off, the unloader releases the suction valves and the compressor resumes pumping. 

The compressor consists of two low pressure cylinders and one high pressure cylinder. The pistons of all three cylinders are driven by a common crankshaft. The two low pressure cylinders are set at an angle to the vertical high pressure cylinder. Air from the low pressure cylinder goes to an intercooler, or radiator, to be cooled before entering the high pressure cylinder. The intercooler is provided with a

$23

4.
