SECTION 2 COOLING SYSTEM 

Description: 

The cooling water system is made up of two individually operated systems. One system is used for the purpose of cooling the lube oil, while the other system cools the Diesel engine. 

Water from the engine cooling system is used to heat the cab of the locomotive. Water is piped from the front of the engine to the cab heaters. 

A sketch of the cooling water system is shown on Plate 2800-5. 

Oil Cooling System: 

In this system the water is drawn from the tank on the right side of the locomotive through the pump and forced into the heat exchanger. After leaving the heat exchanger the water is branched into the two rear radiators. From the cooling radiators, the water drains back into its own individual tank.

Jacket Cooling System: 

Water is drawn from the left side of the locomotive, through the pump. From the pump, water is forced through the casing of the two exhaust elbows and into the engine. 

After circulating through the engine, the water flows out of the water header and into the water cooling radiators, and from there drains back into its own water tank. 

Filling System: 

A filler pipe for this system is located on each side of the unit above the front end of the rear truck. The end of the filler pipe is tapered to allow the water hose to be shoved over the end of the pipe. There is no sight glass at the filler connection so the water should be allowed to overflow through opposite filler pipe. The capacity of the system is 490 gallons. Add water to the cooling system slowly. 

While the cooling system should be operated with the expansion tank (roof) water glass showing 3/4 full, it is possible and safe to operate the engine with less water showing. In emergency it is possible and safe to operate the engine as long as the main supply tank sight glass, in the aisle of the engine room, shows not less than 1" below the top nut of that sight glass.

Draining System: 

Should an engine be shut down due to a failure, it will be necessary to drain the cooling system, if the unit is to be subjected to freezing weather, before it reaches its maintenance terminal. 

To drain the cooling water system, the following procedure should be followed: (1) Open drain valve on left side of the engine just ahead of the water tank. (2) Remove drain plugs from exhaust manifold water jackets at No. 10 cylinder and on underside of exhaust "L" water jacket. (3) Remove drain plugs on underside of oil cooler heads. (4) Remove drain plugs on underside of water inlet heater and on bottem of each water pump. (5) Remove drain plugs from cab heaters.

Engine Water Temperature Switch: WTS 

This switch is located in the control compartment of each unit and will close when the water temperature leaving the engine reaches 200° F., approximately. Closing of this switch causes the engine temperature relay (ETR) to pick up. which in turn establishes circuits to the signal bell and Hot Engine Light. It also causes the engine to shut down. This will be indicated in the cab by the low oil pressure alarm light.

Radiator Louver and Fan Control: 

The temperature of the engine cooling water and lube oil is controlled by volume of air passing through the radiators. This is done by varying the louver opening. The motor driven radiator fans vary in speed depending on the engine speed and contactor setting.

The operation of the louvers and fans is automatically controlled by lube oil activated louver operators which have bulbs in the engine water jacket line and lube oil line. The louvers start to open when the jacket water or lube oil discharged from the engine reaches 150° F. and are wide open when either liquid reaches 160° F.

When the louvers are approximately 50 per cent open, the slow speed fan contactor (W1) closes and fans operate at slow speed. When the louvers open 90 per cent, W1 opens and W2 and W3 close, changing the fan connections from series to parallel, causing them to operate at high speed.

The fan speed can also be controlled by SLOW or FAST switch located on the Engine Control Panel of each unit.

SECTION 3  

The lube oil system serves the dual purpose of supplying oil for piston cooling and lubricating the bearings and wearing parts of the engine. The pump draws oil from the engine crankcase and forces the major portion through the heat exchanger. The remaining portion is by-passed through the oil filter and is then returned to the engine oil header where it is circulated through the engine, returning to the engine crankcase. Plate 2800-6 and 2800-7.

Lube oil is also furnished to the radiator louver motors. This oil under pressure actuates the louvers through the temperature control mechanism. 

Protective Switches: 

The lube oil system is protected by an oil temperature switch and by two oil pressure switches OPS1 - OPS2. 

The engine lube oil temperature switch is located in the engine control panel. It is set to operature at 215° F. 

When it operates, the ETR is energized and the engine is shut down. 

The OPS1 is set to open when the oil pressure drops to 5 psi. and close when the oil pressure reaches 8 psi. 

OPS2 is set to open at 15 psi. and close at 18 psi. The The OPS1 closes a circuit to the governor clutch coil when the pressure is above 8 psi. and interrupts this circuit when the pressure falls to 5 psi. At the same time a circuit is completed lighting the Low Oil Pressure light, and ringing the alarm bell. 

The OPS2 is also in the governor clutch coil circuit and will act to shut the engine down in the event the oil pressure drops to 15 psi. This switch is interlocked with the governor relay (BVS) so that the engine will continue to operate with throttle in "Idle" or positions 1 to 4. However, if the oil pressure drops to 5 psi. the engine will shut down, as previously explained, regardless of the throttle position. 

SECTION 4 FUEL OIL SYSTEM 

Description: 

The fuel system is independent of fuel systems on other units. The fuel system employs a fuel supply tank, emergency fuel shut-off valve, filters, transfer pump and necessary fittings. The fuel transfer pump is a part of the motor driven alternator and pump set. The fuel pump motor can be

Operation: 

Fuel oil is drawn from the supply tank by the fuel transfer pump mounted on the motor alternator and pump set. 

From the tank, oil passes through a spring loaded emergency shut-off. A strainer is mounted in the supply line ahead of the pump, which removes particles that would be injurious to the pump. 

A pressure valve is located between the pump and the filter to by-pass fuel back to the tank in the event the filters get clogged. From the filter the fuel is delivered to the fuel manifold, from which the injector pumps are supplied. A relief valve located at the fuel return header maintains the pressure in the fuel manifold. valve is set at 15 psi.

This relief Fuel for steam generators comes from a separate line that is tapped into the main supply line above the fuel cutoff valve. 

Emergency Fuel Cut-Off Valve: 

(See General Section all locomotives) Fuel Booster Pump: 

The fuel booster-governor pump assembly consists of an electric motor driving a gear type fuel booster pump through a flexible coupling on one end of its shaft, and a gear type governor oil pump through another flexible coupling on the other end of its shaft. The brushes for the motor are located at the fuel pump end of the motor.

SECTION 5 ELECTRICAL SYSTEM 

The electrical equipment on this locomotive is very similar to the 51 class locomotives. The main generator, generators and amplydine exciter system are similar to the 51 class and are G. E. equipment. The load control system is also furnished by G. E. The tachometer generator is the same as used on the 51 class, with the exception that the overspeed control is omitted. The overspeed control is a mechanical device, as previously explained in the "Engine Section". 

The governor oil system is complete within itself, having a reservoir, strainer, pump, filter and low oil pressure switch. 

The system operates on 125 psi. oil pressure supplied by the double pump alternator set. One pump supplies fuel

Control Compartments: 

The control compartments are located at the generator end of the engine. On cab (lead) units the compartment forms the wall between the cab and the engine room; however, the doors into the compartment open into the engine room. See 

Plate 2800-9.

The compartment control panel is located on the control compartment just below doors on upper left when facing the compartment. Plate 2800-10.

Time Delay Relay (AR): 

Co-operating with field shunting control relay causes shunting one notch at a time. Sequence Relays (AVF-AVS-BVS-CVF-CVS-DVS): 

Commutates resistance in governor speed coil and current limit control circuits. 

Battery Contractor (B): 

This contactor connects the battery to the auxiliary generator. 

Braking Contactors (B1-B2): 

Connects traction motor armatures to braking resistors.

Blower Stopped Relay (BLR) : 

Energizes alarm circuit indicating lack of power.

Braking Interlocks (BR1-BR2): 

Sets up control circuits for dynamic brakes.

Brake Overload Relay (BWR) : 

Energizes warning light on a dynamic brake overload.

Motor Cut Out Switch (C.O.): 

This switch used to cut out traction motórs if it becomes necessary.

Automatic Transition Relays (CR1-CR2-CR3): 

These relays control the automatic transition shift from No.1 to 2, from No.2 to 3, from No.3 to 4.

Field Shunting Controller (CS): 

Shunts traction motor field for dynamic brake.

Ground Relay (GR): 

This relay removes power, idles engine, lights the warning light and rings the alarm whenever energized by a grounded condition.

Cranking Contactors (GS1-GS2): 

Connects the starting winding in the main generator to the batteries for starting diesel engine. 

Locomotive Overspeed Switch (LOS): 

Returns engine to idle and applies air brakes whenever maximum permissible speed is exceeded. 

Speed Relays (LS1-LS2-LS3): 

Control the automatic-transition relays CR1-CR2-CR3 respectively.

Field Shunting Contactors (M1-M2-M3-M4): 

Connects resistance across traction motor fields.

Oil Pressure Relay (OPR): 

Idles engine, lights warning light, rings alarm bell on low lube oil pressure or hot engine.

Low Lube Oil Pressure Switch (OPS1): 

Idles engine if lube oil pressure drops to 20 lbs. in 5, 6, 7 or 8th notch.

Low Lube Oil Pressure Switch (OPS2): 

Shuts engine down if lube oil pressure drops to 7 lbs. in any notch.

Engine Overspeed Switch (OS): 

Shuts engine down when engine RPM reaches 835.

Overspeed Magnet Valve (OSM): 

Cuts out dynamic braking and applies air brakes on locomotive when locomotive overspeeds. 

Parallel Motor Contactors (Pl-P2-P21-P22): 

Connects the traction motors across the main generator.

Pneumatic Control Switch (PCS): 

Pneumatic switch energizes DMR.

Exciter Field Rectifier (R1): 

Supplies DC for amplydine field Fl-F2.

Gen. Field Mod. Rectifier (R2): 

Supplies DC for generator field limit control.

Gen. Field Mod. Rectifier (R3): 

Supplies DC-for generator load limit control.

Reverse Current Relay (RC): 

Control battery charging contactor. 

Reverser (Rev.):

Controls the direction of the flow of current to the traction motors. 

Safety Relay (SAR): 

Opens governor clutch coil if speed coil circuit fails.

Shutter Magnet Valve (SMV): 

Controls air admission to shutter system.

Signal Relay (SR): 

Energizes alarm circuits.

Power Switch (SW): 

Connects power circuit for dynamic braking.

Series Motor Contactor (S1-S21): 

Connects No.1 and 3 motors in series S21 connects No. 2 

and 4 motors in series.

Transfer Relay (TR): 

Opens excitation circuits during transition.

Transformer (T1): 

Supplies power for field circuit, field limit and current limit control.

Traction Motor Blower Motors (TB1-TB2): 

Furnishes cooling air to traction motors.

Wheel Slip Relays (WS1-WS2-WS3): 

Energizes buzzer, lights warning light if wheels slip.

Voltage Control Relay (X): 

Regulates voltage output of auxiliary generator.

Operation of Relays and Contactors During Transition: 

1. Shift from Series-Parallel to Series Parallel Shunt a.

LS1 relay is picked up by the output of the axle generator. b. LS1 relay picks up CR1 relay. c. CR1 relay passes current through S21 and P2 interlocks to M1 and M4 field shunting contactor operating coils. d. Ml interlock closes to pick up M2 and M3.

2. Shift from Series Parallel Shunt to Parallel a.

LS2 relay is picked up by output of the axle generator. b. LS2 relay picks up CR2 relay. c. CR2 drops out CR1. d. CR1 opens, drops out M1 and M4. e. Ml interlock opens drops out M2 and M3. f. M2's interlock closes to pick up T.R. relay. g. TR'S contact opens to drop out EF and GF. h. GF's interlock closes to pick up Pl and P21. i. Pl's interlock closes to lock in Pl and P21. j. Pl and P21's interlocks open to drop out S1 and $21. k. S1 and S21's interlocks close to pick up P2 and P22. 

1. P2's interlock closes to lock in TR. m. P22's interlock closes to pick up EF and GF. 

3. Shift from Parallel to Parallel Shunt a. LS3 picks up from output of axle generator. b. LS3's contact picks up CR3. c. CR3's contact closes to feed through TR'S contact to pick up M1 and M4. d. Ml's interlock picks up M2 and M3. Backward Transition: 

1. From Parallel Shunt to parallel. a. LS 3 drops out due to decreased output of axle generator. This unshunts the motor fields. 

2. From Parallel to Series-Parallel Shunt a. LS2 drops out due to decrease in out put of axle generator causing a backward transition. 

3. From Series Parallel Shunt to Series Parallel a. LS1 drops out due to decrease in output of axle generator and unshunts the motor fields.

SECTION 6 AIR SYSTEM Description: 

Every road locomotive unit is equipped with a Westinghouse Type 3CDB three cylinder air cooled compound type air compressor. It is driven from the Diesel engine crankshaft through a flexible coupling. 

Draining Main Reservoirs: 

The main reservoirs should be drained every crew change to prevent moisture from reaching the air brake and control air systems. 

SECTION 7 MISCELLANEOUS 

The governor oil system consists of a reservoir strainer, pump, filter and low oil pressure switch. 

The system operates on 125 psi. oil pressure. A motor driven pump receives oil from the reservoir through a strainer and passes it through a filter to the governor at high pressure.

Santa Fe 

DIESEL-ELECTRIC FREIGHT LOCOMOTIVES CLASS 100 

PART II-SECTION 9 

Santa Fe 149

Note: See Supplement at rear of this section for additional information on Locomotives of this class used in Passenger service. 

The locomotive consists of units rated 1350 H.P. each. 

The units with the cab will be known as lead units, these without the cab as booster units. Each units has one 16-cylinder model 567 type diesel engine (See Section 2, Part 1), a direct current generator and four traction motors. From each power plant the power is distributed to the traction motors which are mounted on the trucks. The traction motors are geared to the axle through spur gears. The units are electrically independent of each other except for certain low voltage wiring and all of the units have batteries. 

The engines have a speed range of 275 R.P.M. at idle to 

800 R.P.M. at full throttle. Their speed is controlled by an electro- pneumatic governor control which is operated by the throttle. In this way all engines in the locomotive are controlled simultaneously. Each notch above run 1 on the throttle changes the engine speed approximately 75 R.P.M.

LOCOMOTIVE OPERATION 

This class of locomotive must not be operated at a speed to exceed 65 miles per hour and is equipped with a speed control which will set the brakes at 65 miles per hour.

The control station contains the locomotive throttle, transition lever and reverse lever. The control push-button box on the right of the controller has fused switches for:

The instrument panel contains the following indicators: 

Brake cylinder and brake pipe air gauge, application pipe and suppression pipe air gauge, main reservoir and equlizing reservoir air gauge and wheel slip light. The gauges are indirectly lighted for night operation.

The unit selector switch has four position and should be set corresponding to the number of units making up the locomotive. This switch will be set by the maintenance forces and sealed. The setting must not be changed enroute.

The headlight switch is located to the right of the controller on locomotives 105 and up. It has "Dim" "Off" and "Bright" positions.

The hand brake wheel is located in the cab of the lead unit and right rear of the booster unit. To set the brake, hold down the foot pedal and turn the wheel. To release the brake, advance the wheel enough to release the foot pedal latch and then let go the wheel.

The pneumatic-control switch is an air operated electric switch located on the right side of the cab, below the window. 

The purpose of this switch is to reduce the power of the locomotive by bringing the speed of the engines to idle, when an automatic train control, safety device pedal or a speed control brake application occurs. If the throttle is in run 5 or 6 when the switch operates, the engines will stop. Reestablishment of control to the locomotive is accomplished by resetting the manual reset button on the pneumatic control switch after the brake application has been released and the throttle placed in idle position. It will be necessary to start all engines which have stopped. This switch is referred to as the "PC" switch. 

Engine Control and Instrument Panel: 

This panel is mounted on the wall of the engine room at the front end of the engine. It contains the isolation switch, gauges, lights, relays and switches used in conjunction with the engine operation. (See Plate 2900-5.)

Distribution Panel: 

This panel contains switches and fuses controlling the battery circuits throughout the unit. The battery ammeter and

Starting Engines: 

1. At distribution panel: 

a. Be sure all fuses are in place. b. Close main battery switch. c. Close control switch. d. Close light switches as required. 

2. At control station: a. Put throttle in idle position. b. Close control push-button switch. c. Close fuel pump switch. d. Set "PC" switch. 

3. At Engine: 

a. Close fuel pump switch on engine instrument panel and watch for fuel in 5-pound relief valve sight glass. This will indicate that fuel is circulating through engine fuel oil system. b. See that isolation switch is in "start" position. c. Press in engine "start" button and hold in until engine starts. Do not hold button down for more than 10 seconds at a time. d. After lubricating oil pressure builds up, place isolation switch in "run" position.

To Move Locomotive: 

1. Isolation switches on engine control panel must be in "run" position. 

2. Close generator field switch on control stand. 

3. Transition lever must be in position No.1 

4. Place reverser lever in forward or reverse position as required. 

5. Place foot on safety device pedal and release brakes. 

6. See that "PC" switch is set. 

7. Open throttle as required.

Starting a Train: 

1. Before attempting to start, the brakes must all be released. 

2. Place transition lever in No.1 position. 

3. Place throttle in run 1 for 2 to 3 seconds. Then place throttle in run 2 for 2 to 3 seconds. Then place throttle in run 3 for 10 to 15 seconds. a. If train cannot be started in run 3 open throttle to run 4; as soon as locomotive moves notch the throttle back to run 3 until it is certain that all the slack is out. If locomotive stops, repeat as explained above. Make certain all brakes are

released before leaving throttle in run 4 for more than 10 seconds. b. If train keeps moving in run 3, leave the throttle in that position until all slack is out, then open throttle to the required notch, taking at least 4 to 5 seconds in each position. 

Operation of Transition Lever and Transition Indicator: 

The transition lever, located on the controller, has four positions to give four connections of the traction motors. 

The transition lever also has an "OFF" position and a "B" (braking) position on locomotives 105 and up while on locomotives 100 to 104 inclusive there is no "OFF" position and braking is controlled by an independent lever. 

The transition lever is mechanically interlocked with the throttle so that the lever can be moved from 1 to 2, 2 to 1, 3 to 4, or 4 to 3, in any throttle position. The lever cannot be moved from 2 to 3 or from 3 to 2 with the throttle in the 

7th. or 8th. position.

The transition indicator has four areas on its dial corresponding to the transition lever positions, and a red overload area. There are also two red triangles on the dial. The upper red triangle indicates the maximum continuous current of the traction motors with the transition lever in No.1 position, and the lower red triangle is used in conjunction with the dynamic brake. (See Plate 2900-6).

The red overload area is divided into four sections by vertical lines with each section numbered. These numbers indicate the overload rating at which the locomotive is operating. If the transition meter pointer reaches the area to the immediate left of any one of the numbers; i.e. 8,4,2 or 

1, it means the locomotive can be operated only for that many miles at that loading. If the pointer has not moved out of the overload area at the end of the specified distance, the load on the locomotive must be reduced a sufficient amount so that the pointer will swing to the left and out of the overload area for at least 15 minutes before again entering the overload area. 

When the transition meter is not functioning or the transition meter is not equipped with a dial indicating the distance in miles over which the locomotive may be operated, the distance in miles that the locomotive will be operated at wide open throttle at any time will be based on the speed of the locomotive as listed below: 

Locomotive Class 100 

Max. distance Min. speed

Gear Ratio 

62-15 in miles

MPH 8.0 2 9.6 10.7 11.6 Continuous 14.5

After any one of the above distances has been traversed at its corresponding speed, the load on the locomotive must be reduced a sufficient amount so that a speed of 14.5 MPH or greater is attained for at least 15 minutes before the speed again drops below 14.5 MPH with wide open throttle.

On any grade or series of grades it should be understood that the overload ratings are not to be accumulative, that is, the locomotive must not be operated in the 1 mile rating for a mile, then the 2 mile rating for 2 miles, then the 4 mile rating for 4 miles, and then the 8 mile rating for 8 miles or vice versa.

Increasing Speed: 

(Use "Increasing Speed" scale on transition indicator dial.) The locomotive should always be started with the transition lever in the No. 1 position. As the throttle is opened when starting a train from a standstill, the transition indicator pointer will swing to the right, indicating a high traction motor current. If the throttle is left in this position the pointer will gradually move to the left. The pointer will react in the same manner with each succeeding advance in throttle position. With the throttle in the run 8 position, as the pointer moves to the left and crosses the "Shift to 2" line, the transition lever should be moved to the No. 2 position. This procedure should be followed when the pointer crosses each succeeding shift line. The pointer should be in the numbered area corresponding to the position of the transition lever with the throttle in the run 8 position and in this area or to the left of it in any lower position of the throttle. 

Decreasing Speed: 

(Use "Decreasing Speed" scale on transition indicator dial.) As the train speed decreased due to a grade, the pointer will gradually move to the right. When the pointer crosses a shift point the transition lever MUST be moved to the position indicated. This should be done regardless of the throttle position.

The backward transition point at shift from 2 to 1 is located beyond the upper red triangle indicating the continuous rating, which indicates that it is allowable to operate continuously in No. 2 position beyond the red triangle. THIS 

IS NOT SO. If, when decelerating due to a grade, the indicator steadies out at some point between the upper red triangle and the shift to 1 point on the lower scale, then the transition lever must be moved to the No.1 position. 

When accelerating a heavy train from a stand-still, with the transition lever in run 1 position, as the throttle is advanced, the pointer of the transition indicator will move to the right of the red triangle, or continuous rating mark. 

This will do no harm as long as the train is accelerating. for as the train speed increases, the pointer will move to the left of the red triangle or into the safe operating zone.

PLATE 2900-6 TRANSITION INDICATOR

IF TRAIN SPEED BECOMES REDUCED ON A GRADE, (with throttle in run 8 position and transition lever in No.

1 position) TO 

THE POINT WHERE THE TRANSITION INDICATOR POINTER GOES TO THE RIGHT OF THE UPPER RED TRIANGLE, IT INDICATES THAT THE LOCO- MOTIVE IS OVERLOADED AND MUST BE OPERATED ACCORDING TO OUT- STANDING INSTRUCTIONS. 

The upper red triangle on the transition indicator dial indicates the maximum continuous current of the traction motors with the transition lever in No.1 position. If the tonnage is within the continuous rating limits of the locomotive, the pointer will not move to the right of this triangle except under the following conditions: 

1. When accelerating a train from a stand-still. 

2. Immediately following shift 1 to 2 when accelerating. 

3. Prior to shift 2 to 1 when decelerating.

No damage will result in failing to advance the transition lever with increasing locomotive speeds, but the electrical equipment will overload if the lever is not backed off at the point indicated by the transition indicator as the locomotive speed is decreasing. The transition lever should be in the No.1 position before the locomotive comes to a stop. Always move the lever one notch at a time. 

Manual Transition Speeds: 

If at any time the transition indicator does not function, it will be necessary to make transition and determine the continuous load by reference to locomotive speed. The following table shows the approximate speed in miles per hour at which transition should occur: 

Move transition lever from position 23432 to position 

Miles per hour 234321 19 47 23420 28 53 25 19 Transition should be made in accordance with reading of transition indicator and should never be made from speedometer reading, except when the transition fails to function. a. Transition indicator will not function at all when the lead unit is shut down. b. Transition indicator will not function properly when the engine in the lead unit is operating at reduced rating. 

Operation of  

Whenever any wheels slip, either one pair of wheels or both pairs of wheels on the same truck or on any truck on the locomotive, the indicator on the instrument panel in the cab

will light. To stop the slippage. ease off on the throttle one or more notches. After a few seconds throttle can be opened to desired position. 

The light will not burn continuously when wheels slip because the wheels will not slip continuously. The connections of the wheel slip relay to the power plant are such that the power is automatically reduced when the wheels slip. 

Therefore, the wheel will slip, then stop, slip again and stop, about once a second.

When there is a wheel slip indication, unless the rail conditions are extremely bad, it will probably be only one set of wheels that are slipping. 

It is therefore unnecessary to use sand except under the most extreme track conditions. Before applying sand, be sure to reduce the throttle until the wheel slip light stops flashing. It should be remembered that sand is injurious to the traction motors and associated apparatus. 

During transition from Series-Shunt to Parallel (position 

2 to 3) and from "B" to "off" position the wheel slip relay will pick up, causing wheel slip light to flash. This is not an indication that the wheels are slipping. 

The wheel slip relay will give wheel slip indication for both Series and Parallel operation on 100 Class locomotives. 

Air Braking With Power: 

When necessary to brake the train with power still supplied by the locomotive, the throttle should be reduced to at least the 6th. notch. It is very important that the locomotive brakes be released and held in the released position when the locomotive is supplying power. This must be observed. 

When preparing to stop with power applied to the locomotive and brakes applied to train, it is necessary to reduce the throttle as the train speed decreases. The transition lever must also be reduced as indicated by the transition indicator. As the train slows down, the pulling power of the locomotive increases rapidly and might become great enough to part the train if the throttle is not reduced. The throttle should be in Idle before the locomotive comes to a dead stop. 

Throttle Stop Button: 

This button is located on the end of the throttle handle. 

Its purpose is to stop all the engines in the locomotive. 

Each engine must be restarted individually after the throttle has been brought back to idle.

It is operated by pressing the button and pushing the throttle handle to beyond the "Idle" position.

Caution should be observed in ordinary handling of the throttle so that this button is not pressed in error when closing the throttle.

DYNAMIC BRAKE 

The dynamic brake is an electrical retarding device which utilizes the main generator and traction motors to hold back the train. The dynamic brake is in no way connected to the air brake system and is only effective when the diesel engines are running and the train is in motion. With the wheels turning, the traction motors act as generators and the power generated is dissipated through heavy resistors, called grids, located in the roof of the locomotive. 

Located next to the instrument panel at the control station is the unit selector switch. The seclector switch should be set before leaving the terminal to agree with the number of units making up the locomotive. When isolating an engine enroute this switch should not be changed. Under no circumstances should the switch be moved while using the dynamic brake. (Selector switch on locomotives 105 and up only).

On locomotives which are operating with a booster unit as the rear unit the field loop jumper must be removed from its receptacle at the rear end of the locomotive. When the receptacle is empty, a short-circuiting bar closes the loop circuit. The clamps on all loop circuit receptacles must be kept tight.

There are two types of dynamic brakes on the freight locomotives, the two position dynamic brake and the variable dynamic brake. Locomotives 100 to 104 inclusive have the two position dynamic brake and the locomotives 105 and up have the variable dynamic brake. 

Operation of the Variable Dynamic Brake: 

The brake may be used at speed up to 65 MPH and is most effective at a speed of 17 MPH. The brake may be applied or released at any speed; however, it must be done slowly to minimize slack action. It is not a stopping device and should be moved gradually to the "OFF" position and a full stop accomplished with the air brake.

To apply the brake, move the throttle to the Idle position and the transition lever to the No.1 position pausing for 10 seconds before moving the transition lever to the "OFF" position. This is desirable in order that the generator output will reduce sufficiently to make a smooth application of the brake possible. The greater the speed the greater the effect of the dynamic brake in the initial position. When it is certain that the slack is bunched, the lever may be moved in the counter-clockwise direction towards full brake to the desired braking. 

When operating a locomotive of the 105 class, which is equipped with "Variable" dynamic brake, with a unit of the 100 to 104 class inclusive which are equipped with the "twoposition" dynamic brake, the dynamic brake MUST NOT BE USED.

MAIN GENERATOR TRACTION MOTORS BATTERY PLATE 2900-8 LEAD UNIT BRAKING RESISTORS DYNAMIC BRAKING DIAGRAM- (LOCOMOTIVES 105 AND UP)

The transition indicator indicates the traction motor current when braking as well as when motoring. The lower red triangle indicates the maximum allowable motor current while braking and the pointer should not move to the right of this triangle. If the transition indicator is disregarded while braking, a brake warning light mounted above the transition indicator will flash at the danger point. If the lever is backed off, the light will go out but will come back on if the train speed increases. At the first indication of overload (warning light or loadmeter) the brake must be reduced. If it is impossible to maintain the desired train speed with the dynamic brake, the automatic air brakes should be applied on the train in conjunction with the dynamic brake, keeping locomotive independent air brakes released to avoid wheel slide. 

At speeds above 8 M.P.H. do not use dynamic brake and locomotive brake simultaneously. When locomotive speed descreased to 8 M.P.H. move transition lever toward the""off" position, making a light application with independent brake to prevent "run out" of locomotive, then place transition lever in "off" position and increase independent brake application as required.

USE OF THE LOCOMOTIVE AIR BRAKES AND DYNAMIC BRAKE AT SAME TIME OTHER THAN AS LISTED ABOVE WILL RESULT IN WHEEL SLIDING 

Difference in idling speed of the engines may cause the overload light to come on before the transition indicator pointer reaches the red triangle, but in any case the light must not be on. The light is an indication of an overload and operating with it on might damage the traction motors and braking grids.

Isolating An Engine While Using Dynamic Brake: 

If it should become necessary to isolate an engine while braking, it should be done by first stopping the engine, using the layshaft manual control lever, then move the isolation switch to the start position. 

Isolating an engine may become necessary due to failure of the braking equipment on that unit or failure of the diesel engine. This opens the braking contactors but leaves the main generator battery field connected in series with the other generator fields throughout the locomotive. Do not change the position of the unit selector switch. 

When the leading unit has its engine isolated, the transition indicator in this unit will not function. This is because the traction motors on this unit are not generating. Therefore, particular attention must be given to the brake warning light, as this will be the only guide to brake overload; however, care should be exercised and the braking lever operated to the left of that point at which the light comes on. 

The dynamic brake can be made inoperative on any unit by placing the isolation switch of that unit in the "start" position. However, except in case of an emergency, the

isolation switch should not be opened while the transition lever is advanced to the right beyond the "B" position. Isolating an engine while braking causes the motor load to be removed suddenly, which will cause a high inductive voltage to be built up and this might be great enough to break down the main contactor insulation. 

The isolation switch should not be closed to "run" position while the dynamic brake is in operation. If this precaution is not observed, the brake on this unit will be applied suddenly and will result in an overload to the electrical equipment and the possibility of sliding the wheels. The transition lever must be reduced to "B" before opening or closing an isolation switch. 

When the engine in the lead unit is shut down the breaking effort of the remaining units will be reduced also, unless the batteries are trainlined between the lead and the first booster unit.

Steam Locomotive Used As Helper: 

Steam locomotives used as helpers often have tonnage ratings based on speeds lower than the calculated tonnage rating speed of the diesel locomotive. As a result, the diesel locomotive will absorb more than its proportionate share of the load, since its tonnage rating is based on a higher speed, therefore, the diesel locomotive may be overloaded in endeavoring to gain its rated speed. 

When a steam locomotive helper is used over a helper grade, the locomotive should be allowed to operate at the short time overload rating consistent with length of the grade. For example; a steam helper is to be used over a grade 

7 miles long. The locomotive may be operated within the 8 mile rating on the transition meter or not below 11.6 MPH. 

If the train speed should drop below the short time overload rating speed, consistent with the length of grade, the the throttle should be reduced in order to keep the electrical load from being excessive. The following table shows the minimum allowable speed with the throttle in run 8 for the short time overload ratings.

Speed Rating Continuous Minimum speed 

M.P.H. 

14.5 11.6 10.7 9.6 2 1 8.0 

The transition meter indication is the principal guide and should never be allowed to show an overload above that allowed by the short time rating for the length of grade involved.

Diesel Locomotive Used As Helper: 

When using a 100 Class diesel as a helper locomotive for other locomotives, the same general instructions should be followed in that the helper locomotive should not be allowed to operate at an overload greater than the length of grade to be negotiated will allow: 

If the 100 Class locomotive is helping a 200 Class or 

2650 Class, or vice versa, it must be remembered that the continuous rating of the 200 Class or 2650 Class is 11 MPH which is in the 4 mile rating of the 100 Class locomotive. Therefore, the 100 Class locomotive should throttle back on grades requiring the continuous rating as the train speed reduced according to the following chart.

Throttle Position

11.0 to 14.5 

9.0 to 11.0 

6.5 to 9.0

If the grade to be negotiated is of a length that a short time overload rating is allowable, the correct short time rating can be used. The short time ratings as indicated by the pointer of the transition indicator are to be used. If the transition indicator is out-of-order use the speed recorder as a guide and do not allow the speed to drop below the minimum speed allowable as indicated in the chart under steam locomotive helper.

Controller Mechanical Interlock: 

The levers on the controller are mechanically interlocked so that: 1. The reverse lever can be operated with the transition lever in either No.1 or "OFF" position and throttle at idle. 

2. The reverse lever can be removed only with the transition lever in "OFF" position and the throttle at idle. 

3. The transition lever can be moved into dynamic braking only when the reverse lever is in forward position and the throttle at idle. 

4. The throttle cannot be operated when the transition lever is in "OFF" or dynamic braking position. 

5. The throttle can be moved to "Stop" with any position of transition or reverse levers. 

6. The transition lever cannot be moved from 2 to 3 or from 3 to 2 with throttle in the 7th. or 8th. position. 

Towing Locomotive (100 to 104 inclusive) (Without Cam Switch) When preparing the locomotive for deadheading or yard movement where it will be towed by other power, the transition lever must be in the No. 1 position. The throttle must be in "idle" position.

Be sure reverse lever is in neutral. If the locomotive is to be towed in a train any appreciable distance, the reverser drum should also be locked in its neutral position, with locking pin provided for this purpose. 

When locomotive is to be handled dead in train the cutout cock on the distributing valve should be set for "dead" position, brake valves locked in running position and the distributing valve pop set at 25 pounds. Close double heading cock on brake valve pedestal.

Towing Locomotive (105 Class) (With Cam Switch) Plate 2900-19 

When preparing the locomotive for deadheading or yard movement where it will be towed by other power, the transition lever must be moved to "OFF" position before the control button is pulled. This moves the camswitch into "TOW" position. 

If this is not done, the traction motors will generate and hold back the locomotive if towed in a direction opposite to the position of the reverser. 

Be sure reverse lever is in neutral. If the locomotive is to be towed in a train any appreciable distance, the reverser drum should also be locked in its neutral position with locking pin provided for this purpose. 

When booster units are cut out for any reason and are to be moved "dead" in train, separated from the lead units, or when the jumper cables are removed between the units, the camswitch of the booster units must be placed in the "TOW" position or the locomotive wheels will pick up and slide. On booster units close the "control" switch and follow by closing "TOW" switch. Allow these switches to remain to closed for a few seconds so that the camswitch will throw to "TOW" position and then open control and tow switch and lock them open. 

When locomotive is to be handled "dead" in train, the cutout cock on the distributing valve should be set for "dead" position,, brake valves locked in running position and the distributing valve pops set at 25 pounds. Close double heading cock on brake valve pedestal.

Towing Locomotive 

(105 Class units connected up behind units of locomotives 100 to 104 inclusive):

If units of the 105 class are connected up behind units of locomotives 100 to 104 inclusive, a special precaution must be observed to prevent wheel slide on the 105 class units. On booster of the 105 class locomotives, the "TOW" switch at the hostler control stand must be pushed in to throw the camswitch to "TOW" position in that unit, On lead units of the 105 class the transition lever must be in "OFF" position at the control station. After pushing "TOW" button in, or placing the transition lever in "OFF" position, an inspection of the camswitch, in the high voltage cabinet, will be necessary to make sure the camswitch has thrown to "TOW"

Isolating an Engine While Under Power: 

If it becomes necessary to take an engine "Off the line" while the locomotive is operating under power, it should be done as follows: 

1. Reduce the speed of the engine to idle with the layshaft manual control lever so that the traction motor contactors will not have to interrupt the full motor current. 

2. Place the isolation switch in the "start" position. 

3. Push the engine stop button. 

4. Turn off the fuel pump. 

5. If the engine will not stop by holding in "stop" button, hold up on the layshaft manual control until the engine stops. 

Placing Engine Back "On the Line": 

1. Close fuel pump switch. 

2. Place isolation switch in "start" position. 

3. Start engine in usual way. 

4. After lubricating oil pressure builds up, place isolation switch in "run" position. If throttle is above 

3rd position hold off on governor to injector linkage with layshaft manual control lever to allow engine to come up to speed gradually.

Precautions During Locomotive Operation: 

1. Maintain engine cooling water temperature 150° F. to 

180°F. preferably 165° F. whenever possible. Do not exceed 200° F. This is accomplished by proper operation of the fan clutches and shutters. Engines equipped with automatic shutter controls are adjusted to open the shutters at 1650 F and if for any reason the automatic shutter controls become inoperative, the shutters should be operated manually. Fan clutches should be engaged before locomotive is operated. Make sure fans are turning. 

2. Lubricating oil pressure should be approximately 28 pounds at 800 R.P.M. If main bearing pressure drops below 20 pounds when operating in run 8 with engine temperature 180° F. or lower, stop engine and do not operate until cause for low oil pressure is corrected. 

If main bearing pressure drops below 15 pounds, when operating in run 8 with engine temperature above 180°F. the engine should be stopped and not operated until the cause of the low oil pressure is corrected. 

3. Piston cooling oil pressure should be 20 to 30 pounds with engine operating in run 8. If piston cooling oil pressure drops below 15 pounds (operating in run 8

CONTROLLER HIGH VOLTAGE CABINET CONTROL SWITCHES LOAD REGULATOR PLATE 2900-10 CONTROL STATION-BOOSTER UNIT

with hot oil) stop the engine, and do not operate until the cause of low oil pressure is corrected. 

4. Control air pressure should be 80 pounds plus or minus 3 pounds. 

5. After engine has operated in run 8 for a few minutes, the load indicator at the governor should agree approximately with the indicator on the back of the electropneumatic governor control. 

6. With the engines running, the oil level should always be between the "LOW" and "HIGH" marks on the bayonet measuring gauge located in the crankcase. Engines equipped with sight glasses on the lubricating oil tank must have oil level maintained at least 2 inches above bottom of sight glass.

Stopping Engine: 

1. At control station: 

a. Close throttle to idle position. b. Place reverse lever in neutral position and remove handle. c. On locomotive 105 and up, put transition lever in "OFF" position. On locomotives 100 to 104 inclusive, put transition lever in No. 1 position. d. Open generator field switch. (Do not open control switch, as it is impossible to shut down engines with stop button without the control). 

2. At Engine: 

a. Place isolation switch in "start" position. b. Push engine "stop" button and hold it until engine stops. c. Open fuel pump switch. 

NOTE: Engine will not stop with "stop" button unless control air pressure is up. 

3. At control station: 

a. Open control switch. b. Open fuel pump switch. 

TROUBLE SHOOTING If fuel Does Not Show In Right-Hand (5-Pound) Sight Glass: 

1. If fuel pump does not run: a. Check all fuses in circuit. (1) Two 60-ampere control fuses on distribution panel. (2) Fuel pump fuses (15-ampere) in control stand.

(3) Fuel pump fuses (10-ampere) under engine control and instrument panel. b. Check switches in circuit. (2) "PC" switch. (3) Fuel pump switch at controller. (4) Fuel pump contactor on engine control and instrument panel. 

C. Check electric connector to fuel pump motor. 

2. If motor is running but no fuel is being delivered. a. Check pump coupling and shaft. b. Check fuel supply and emergency fuel cut-off valve. c. Move handle of sintered bronze filter to opposite direction to put other filter element in service. 

If Engine Does Not Rotate When Attempt Is Made to Start: 

1. Repeat customary sequence of operations in starting engine, making sure that all controls and switches are in the correct position. 

2. Check all fuses affecting starting circuit: a. Starting fuse. This is the 400-ampere fuse on the distribution panel. b. 60-ampere control fuse on the distribution panel. c. 30-ampere control fuses. These fuses are located opposite the control push button switches on the controller. 

3. The battery may be too weak to turn engine over. Turn on engineroom lights and note whether they become dim or go out when the "Start" button is pressed. 

4. Check to see that both starting contactors at the bottom of the low voltage cabinet go in.

If Engine Rotates but Does Not Fire When Start Button Is Pressed and Injector Racks Are Held Open Manually with Lay- Shaft Manual Control Lever: 

1. Check injector linkage in event that it may be stuck in shut down position. 

2. Check to see that overspeed trip shaft is latched in "run" position. 

3. Check to see that fuel pump is running and that there is fuel in the right hand sight glass.

If Locomotive Does Not Move When Throttle Is Opened; or Loco. 

stops in operation. (Some of the items below would cause a loss of load on one engine, which would cause slow acceleration.) 

1. Check "PC" switch. (Pull out button to reset.)

2. Check to see that generator field switch at control station is closed and fuse good. 

3. Check control air pressure. 

4. Check to see that ground protective relays are set in normal position. 

5. Check to see that isolation switches are in "run" position. 

6. Check all fuses: a. 60-ampere control fuses on the distribution panel. b. 30-ampere control fuses opposite the control switch on the controller. c. 80-ampere battery field fuse in the low voltage cabinet. d. 15-ampere generator field fuse at controller. 

7. If starting contactors stick shut or wheel slip relay sticks open, the generator of that power plant will not deliver power. 

Loss of Load on an Engine:

To detect a loss of load on an engine, look at the load indictor on the governor. After the engine and generator have had a chance to balance up (about 30 seconds) the load indicator should agree approximately with the indicator on the back of the electro-pneumatic governor control. If the indicator on the governor is low, it might show that: 

1. The "BF" or "SH" contactors are out. This may be caused by: a. Ground relay tripping. b. Starting contactor interlock open. c. Wheel slip relay stuck. 

2. Battery field fuse blown, this is the 80-ampere fuse in the low voltage cabinet.

If Battery Charging Ammeter (in Low Voltage Cabinet) Always 

Shows Zero: 

1. See that auxiliary generator switch is closed. 

2. Check all fuses in circuit affected: a. 30-ampere auxiliary generator field fuse in the low voltage cabinet. b. 150-ampere battery charging fuse in the low voltage cabinet. 

If Air Pressure Does Not Build Up: 

1. Check to see that angle cocks and main reservoir drain valves are in proper position. 

2. Blow out air compressor unloader.

If Engine Fails to Stop: 

Stop engine with the layshaft manual control lever if engine fails to stop when the throttle is placed in the shut down position.

COOLING SYSTEM Description: 

The cooling systems of the engines are similar in every respect except that the "Lead" units have cab heaters. The water level in the cooling system is below the radiators when the engine is shut down. 

Operation of Cooling System: 

Water is drawn from the water tank by centrifugal water pumps located on the front end of the engine. These pumps circulate the water to the bottom of each cylinder liner and cylinder head and out through the outlet manifold. From the engine the water flows to four groups of radiator sections located under the roof of the locomotive body. Here the water is cooled, returning through the oil cooler to the water tank. 

Filling the Cooling System: 

Fill cooling system through filler pipe along side of fuel tank or through filler pipe located on roof above water tank. Fill to the "G" valve level.

Engine High Temperature Alarm Switch: 

This is a thermal switch located beside the engine control and instrument panel and connects with two thermal elements, one in each water outlet manifold of the engine. If the water temperature exceeds 200° F, this switch closes, operating the signal relay, which lights the hot engine alarm light and rings the alarm bell.

To drain the engine cooling system, open drain valves "E", "G" and "H". 

To completely drain the water system in freezing weather, open valves as above, and remove drain plugs from cab heater lines and water pumps. The cab heater drain plugs are 14" pipe plugs located under the rear of the cab floor, to the right and left of the steps leading into the cab. Try all other valves for trapped water in the pipe lines. 

90 Degree Fan Drive: 

This drive unit is commonly called "speed increaser." A lubricating oil pump, sight glass, strainer and oil reservoir

Fan Clutch Operation: 

The cooling fan drives are equipped with friction clutches. These clutches are not to be used in place of the shutters for adjustment of the engine temperature, but should be engaged or disengaged according to outside temperatures. 

For winter operation, the fan clutches on the generator end of all units must be disengaged and the shutters closed. 

The engine temperature then can be controlled by the shutters and fans on the accessory end.

SECTION 3 LUBE OIL SYSTEM Low Oil Pressure Alarm Switch: 

A failure of the lubricating oil pressure will cause the alarm switch contacts to open. When these contacts open, the master "E" valve will be de-energized, bringing the engine to idle. The low oil signal will light and alarm bell will ring. Should this occur, do not attempt to load the engine until the cause of the low pressure has been located and corrected. 

Lube Oil Suction Alarm Switch: 

This is a vacuum switch connected to the suction side of the lube oil and piston cooling oil pumps, and electrically connected to the master "E" valve and the low oil signal alarm circuits. The purpose of this switch is to reduce the engine speed and to energize the low oil signal alarm circuit if the oil pump suction reaches the value at which the switch is set to operate. Excessive oil pump suction is the result of restricted suction strainers.

Operation of this switch will most commonly occur at full engine speed because the pump suction increases with engine speed. The switch will then open the master "E" valve circuit and close the low oil signal alarm circuit. However, as the engine speed decreases due to de-energizing the "B" valve, the pump suction will also decrease allowing the suction switch to reset, thus re-energizing the "E" valve and opening the low oil signal alarm circuit. The engine will then speed up until the pump suction again becomes excessive and the cycle will repeat. This cycling may cause the overspeed trip to operate. If the vacuum switch operates the engine should be shut down and no attempt made to load it until the cause of the lube oil suction alarm is located and corrected.

FUEL OIL SYSTEM Description: 

A fuel tank is mounted under each unit of the locomotive. 

At the bottom of the tank are two sumps, the tank sump and the supply sump, which are connected through an emergency fuel cut-off valve. The fuel pump draws fuel oil from the supply sump through a suction line and suction filter. Fuel can only be drawn from the supply sump if the emergency cutoff valve is open. After leaving the pump, fuel is pumped through the Ful-Flo filter and sintered bronze filter to the injectors. The injectors use only part of the fuel through them. The surplus fuel oil lubricates and cools the internal mechanism of the injectors and then returns to the fuel tank through the 5 pound relief valve and sight glass.

Emergency Fuel Cut-Off Valve: (See General Section all Loco motives).

Fuel Sight Glass and Relief Valve: 

The fuel sight glass and relief valve assembly is located above the fuel pump. Its purpose is to indicate the condition of the various filters in the fuel system and the flow of fuel oil through the engine. The left-hand relief valve is set at 

60 pounds, the center at 100 pounds and the right-hand at 5 pounds.

The return fuel from the engine passes through the righthand sight glass and relief valve (5 pounds). A drop in the fuel level in this glass, or an empty glass, will indicate that the engine is not receiving its full supply of fuel. 

The left-hand sight glass will show fuel by-passing when any of the filters on the pressure side of the fuel pump collect enough dirt to build up a back pressure of 60 pounds. 

This fuel by-passes the filter and engine and returns to the tank. In an emergency, the globe valve between the 60-pound relief valve and the return pipe to the tank can be closed to force the fuel through the filters. This permits operation of the engine until the maintenance point is reached. When fuel pressure reaches 100 pounds, the fuel will flow through the center sight glass and relief valve and return to the tank. 

ELECTRICAL EQUIPMENT 

This section deals with the electrical equipment and principally the location of the electrical equipment. 

Overload Relay (OLR): 

This is a through cable type relay that indicates an overload of the main generator by lighting the wheel slip light. 

In transition No.3 or 4 the OLR not only causes the light to come on but drops out the battery field contactor and holds it out. The throttle must be reduced to idle to release the over load relay. Shift the transition lever back to No. 2 transition.

In transition No.1 or 2, the OLR causes the light to light but does not unload the engine. Reduce the throttle until light goes out.

Low Oil Pressure Alarm Switch LOB, LOP and LOPS: 

The switch is connected to the engine lubricating oil system in such a manner that failure of the oil pressure will cause the switch contacts to open. When these contacts open, the master "E" valve will be de-energized, bringing the engine to idle. At the same time, the low oil alarm signal lamps will light and the alarm bell will ring. This alarm switch will not give low oil protection when isolation switch is in "start" position and engine at idle.

Electro Pneumatic Governor Control Magnet Valves 

CV, DV: AV, BV, Various combinations of these magnet valves control engine speed in accordance with throttle position. 

Dynamic Brake Contactors 

B1, B2, B3:

These contactors connect the traction motor fields in series with each other and to the main generator during dynamic braking. 

Battery Charging Contactor 

BC: 

This contactor connects the auxiliary generator to the battery. The reverse current relay closes the battery charging contactor when the auxiliary generator voltage reaches a predetermined setting to charge the battery. 

Battery Field Contactor 

BF: 

This contactor closes when the throttle is moved to Run 1 position, connecting the battery to the main generator battery field.

Dynamic Brake Warning Relay - BWR: 

Dynamic brake warning relay is a current operated relay, which will light the brake warning light indicating that the load on the dynamic brake is excessive. The light will remain on until the load is reduced to the continuous rating of the dynamic brake equipment.

Compressor Control Magnet Valve 

CC: 

This magnet valve admits main reservoir air to the uploader valves unloading the air compressor when energized. Deenergizing the magnet valve releases the unloaders and causes the air compressor to resume pumping.

Compressor Control Switch CCS: 

The compressor control switch is actuated by main reser-

voir pressure and controls the loading and unloading of the air compressor in all units through the compressor control relays and control magnet valves.

Compressor Control Relay - CR: 

The compressor control relay energizes or de-energises the control magnet valve causing the air compressor to load and unload according to the settings of the control switches. 

Cam Switch CS: 

The cam switch is a power-braking transfer switch. When in "Motor" position, it connects the traction motor armatures in series with their respective fields and disconnects the braking grids from the motor armatures. When in "tow" position, it disconnects the traction motor armatures from their fields and connects a braking grid across each armature.

Cam Switch Relay - CSR: 

This relay is energized when the transition lever is in "B" or succeeding braking positions and establishes the position of the battery field contactor in dynamic braking. 

Engine High Temperature Switch ETS: 

When the water temperature exceeds the maximum allowable temperature, this switch closes, lights the hot engine alarm signal light and energizes the signal relay, which rings the alarm bell. On some classes of locomotives the engine will return to idle and remain in idle as long as the hot engine alarm is on: 

Master E Magnet Valve 

F: 

This valve controls the air supply to the Electro-Pneumatic Governor Control valves AV, BV and CV. De-energizing this relay will stop the engine if the throttle is in Run 

5 or Run 6 or bring the engine to idle in any other throttle position.

Field Loop Contactor - FL: 

The field loop contactor is energized when the transition lever is moved to the "off" "B" and succeeding braking position. This operation places the dynamic brake control rheostat and resistors and the unit selector switch and its reresistors in series with field loop circuit. 

Fuel Pump Contactor - FPC: 

All fuel pump contactors throughout the locomotive are controlled by the fuel pump switch at the control station. 

When this contactor is closed, the fuel pumps can be controlled individually by the switch mounted on the engine control and instrument panel.

Ground Protective Relay - GR: 

If a ground occurs in the high voltage system, this relay will open the battery field and main generator shunt field contactors, and by de-energizing the "ER" relay bring the engine to idle except when the throttle is in the 5th or 6th position and in this event the engines will stop. Should this occur isolate the engine and reset the relay by pressing in on the reset button on the cover. When the relay is tripped the white needle will point to the red indicator and to the yellow indicator when in normal position. If the relay repeatedly opens when the engine speed is increased the power plant must be isolated. 

Load and Transition Indicating Meter 

IM: 

This meter is an accurate guide to load on the locomotive as it shows amount of current furnished to the traction motors. Isolation Switch IS: 

The purpose of this switch is to open and close the control circuit of the unit as the occasion demands. When starting the Diesel engine this switch must be in the START position. After engine is running move switch to RUN position. 

This will connect the control circuit of the unit to the control station. To isolate this unit, move this switch to START Position. This will bring the engine to idle and cut off the power of this unit. 

Motor Field Shunting Contactor Magnet Valve M: 

This is an electro-pneumatic valve that operates the traction motor field shunting contactors. 

Traction Motor Field Shunting Contactors 

M1, M2, M3, M4: 

These contactors connect the shunting resistors across the traction motor fields. These contactors are closed in transition No. 2 and 4. 

Cam Switch Magnet Valves 

MOT, TOW: 

Parallel Contactors P1, P2: 

These contactors are closed to connect the main generator to the traction motors in parallel operation. 

Pneumatic Control Switch PCS: 

The purpose of this switch is to reduce the power of the locomotive by bringing the speed of the engines to idle when certain brake applications take place.

Parallel Relay - PR: 

This relay operates to close the parallel contactors;

in conjunction with the main generator it causes shunt field and battery field contactors to open during transition from

Reverse Current Relay - RCR: 

This relay prevents the battery current from motoring the auxiliary generator by opening the battery charging contactor when the auxiliary generator voltage drops below the battery voltage. 

Series Contactors 

S: 

These contactors are closed to connect the main generator to the traction motors in Series Parallel operation. 

Generator Shunt Field Contactor 

SH: 

This relay connects the main generator shunt field to the main generator armature.

Signal Relay SR: 

This relay operates with the signal lights (Hot Engine, Boiler Off, and Low Oil Pressure) to ring the alarm bells throughout the locomotive. 

Starting Contactors ST plus, ST minus: 

These contactors are used to connect the battery to the main generator for cranking the diesel engine. 

Time Delay Relay 

VT: 

This relay establishes a circuit energizing PR relay, It takes three to four seconds for this relay to close. 

Wheel Slip Relays - WSR1, WSR2: 

Each relay has two normally open contacts. In the event of a pair of wheels slipping the coil connected to that motor will be energized. One of the contacts will light the wheel slip light and the other will de-energize the main generator shunt field coil, thereby dropping out generator shunt field and battery field. Power output will be reduced momentarily until slipping stops.

Brake Control Rheostat: 

Locomotive 105 and up have a brake control rheostat. The brake control rheostat is located in the bottom of the controller and operates between "B" and maximum braking positions of the braking lever to vary the resistance in series with the field loop circuit. This will vary the braking effort of the locomotive.

The distribution panel contains the following equipment:

Classification and Number Lights: 

The class lights are located on each side of the nose and are 64 volts-30 watts. The number lights are located on the side of the locomotive.

BACKWARD TRANSITION 

(Train speed decreasing)

On Locomotives 100 to 104 Inclusive: 

As train speed decreases due to a grade, the transition lever must be moved to prevent overload to the electrical equipment. 

1. Parallel Shunt to Parallel Transition.

The following operations occur when the transition lever is moved to No.3 position: a. "M" contactors open. 

2. Parallel to Series-Parallel Shunt Transition. 

When transition lever is moved to No. 2 position (throttle must be reduced to at least Run 6): 

a. Parallel relay (PR) opens. 

b. "SH" and "BF" contactors open. c. Time delay relay (VT) opens. d. "Pl" contactor opens. e. "S" contactor closes. f. "P2" contactor opens. g. "SH" and "BF" contactors close. h. "M" contactors close. 

3. Series-Parallel Shunt to Series-Parallel Transition. 

When transition lever is moved to No. 1 position: a. "M" contactors open. 

4. Series-Parallel to Idle Transition. 

When throttle is moved to idle position and transition lever to "off": a. "S" "SH" and "BF" contactors open. 

On Locomotive 105 and Up: 

As train speed decreases due to a grade, the transition lever must be moved in accordance with transition meter indication to prevent overload to the electrical equipment. 

1. Parallel Shunt to Parallel Transition: 

The following operations occur when the transition lever is moved to No. 3 position: a. "M" contactors open. 

2. Parallel to Series-Parallel Transition: 

When transition lever is moved to No. 2 position (throttle must be reduced to at least Run 6): 

a. Parallel relay (PR) opens. 

b. "SH" and "BF" contactors open. c. Time delay relay (VT) opens. d. "Pl" contactor opens. e. "S" contactor closes. f. "P2" contactor opens. g. "SH" and "BP" contactors close. h. "M" contactors close. 

3. Series Parallel Shunt to Series-Parallel Transition. 

When transition lever is moved to No.1 position: a. "M" contactors open. 

4. Series-Parallel to Idle Transition: 

When throttle is moved to idle position and transition lever to "off": a. "s", "SH", and "BF" contactors open.

SECTION 7 AIR SYSTEM Air Compressor: 

Each power plant in the locomotive is equipped with a type WXD or VXE air compressor which is driven by shaft of the main generator through a Falk coupling. The compressor has its own oil pump and lubricating system. Oil level in the crankcase can be checked on the bayonet gauge. 

The compressor is equipped with an unloader to maintain the main air reservoir pressure. This unloader is adjusted to cut in at 130 pounds pressure, and cut out at 140 pounds pressure. 

Draining Air System: 

The sump reservoir and type "H" filter are located in the "nose" of the "lead" unit and at the rear of the "booster" units. In order to prevent moisture from entering air system these sump reservoirs and filters should be drained at each crew change.

Modification of the air system has been made to provide more protection against "freeze-ups" during winter operation. 

The piping from the air compressor no longer goes directly to the air reservoir but is now piped below carbody floor to radiating pipes, and a cooling reservoir adjacent to the fuel tank. Excess moisture in the air is collected in this reservoir. The air is piped from this reservoir to the main air reservoir in the locomotives. This cooling reservoir adjacent to the fuel oil tank is equipped with a drain valve. 

On freight locomotives having a cut-out cock to regulate flow to cooling radiators, this valve should remain open at all times.

These locomotives have a CFK or OK type steam generator in each booster unit and is equipped with remote steam generator controls. (See Part 3 Section 2.) The fuel tank on each booster unit is used to carry water for the steam generator. The fuel tank on the lead unit contains 1330 gallons which supplies

BODY, CAB, TRUCK AND MISCELLANEOUS EQUIPMENT 

Hand Brake:

The hand wheel is located in the cab of the "lead" units and right rear of the "booster" units. To set brake, hold down the foot pedal and turn the wheel. To release the brake, advance the wheel enough to release the foot pedal and then let go and stay clear of the wheel. Before moving the locomotive, be sure the brake is completely released on all units. 

Journal Boxes: 

The speedometer drive journal box is mounted on the No.1 truck, No.1 axle at the left side.

Each journal box is equipped with a "stench bomb." This device consists of a cylindrical container holding a liquid which when heated gives off an extremely pungent and penetrating odor. The container is sealed with a bismuth solder plug. If for any reason the temperature inside the journal box exceeds 230 degrees F, the plug melts instantly releasing the stench agent to the atmosphere.

Santa Fe 

DIESEL-ELECTRIC E.M.D. LOCOMOTIVES 

CLASS 200 240 240 

PART II-SECTION 10 

Santa Fe

SECTION 1 

The freight locomotive consists of units rated at 1500 HP. The units with the cab are called lead units and those without cabs are called booster units. Each unit is equipped with a 16-567-B engine (See Part 1, Sec. 2) which drives the main generator auxiliaries as later described. 

The engines have a speed range of 275 R.P.M. to 800 

R.P.M. at full throttle. The speed is controlled through the control station by an electro-hydraulic governor. Through the electrical connections of the throttle, the speeds of all engines are simultaneously controlled. The main generator consists of a D.C. generator and A.C. generator (alternator) mounted on the same shaft and driven by the engine through a flexible coupling. The alternator furnishes power for the traction motor blowers and engine water cooling fans. The 

D.C. generator is connected to the traction motors through power switches. The flow of current through the generator is always in the same direction, but the reversing of the locomotive is accomplished by reversing the current in the traction motors. 

LOCOMOTIVE OPERATION 

This class locomotive must not be operated at a speed in excess of 65 M.P.H. It is equipped with a speed control switch which will set the brakes at 65 M.P.H. 

Control Station: (See Plate 3000-1) 

The control station contains the throttle lever, transition lever and reverse lever. The push button control box is located above the cab window on right side. Fuses are no longer contained in the control box but are in the distribution panel. The instrument panel contains the following indicators: Brake cylinder and brake pipe air gage, application pipe and suppression pipe air gage, main reservoir and equalizing reservoir air gage, wheel slip light and dynamic brake warning light and indicating meter.

The indicating meter is an ammeter in the armature circuit of the No. 4 traction motor and indicates the loading on that traction motor. If the lead unit is shut down or if the 

No. 4 traction motor is cut out, the indicating meter will be inoperative.

The "PCS" open indicating light is located to the left and above the instrument panel. This light will burn only in the event of an emergency or penalty application of brakes. 

To get the light to go out, a good procedure is to move the throttle to the idle position and then do what is required to get the light to go out.

If the brakes are applies in "Service", the brake valve should be placed in the "Lap" position and left there until the light goes out. If there has been an emergency application the brake valve must first be lapped and then moved to running position. There is no manual reset button for the PCS (pneumatic control switch). 

The purpose of the PCS is to bring the engines to "idle" when an automatic train control, automatic train stop, safety device pedal or speed control brake application occurs. If the throttle is in run No. 5 or No. 6, the engines will be stopped but no bells will ring. The "PCS" switch is located in the nose of the Diesel but has no manual control on it. 

The sanding valve is located to the right of the control station adjacent to the window and operates all sanders on the locomotive. 

The unit selector switch is located to the left of the instrument panel and in front of the control stand. It should be set to the number of units making up the locomotive. This switch will be set by the maintenance forces and sealed. The setting should not be changed en route. 

The headlight switch is located above the side window on the right side.

The speed recorder is hydraulically operated and is driven through a flexible drive shaft from the left No. 1 truck of the lead unit.

1. Place isolation switch in "Start" position. 

2. Push engine stop button and hold it in until engine stops. 

3. Place fuel pump switch in "off" position.

Operation of Transition Lever: 

200 class locomotives are equipped with automatic transition and will normally be operated in that manner. То ор- erate the locomotive in automatic transition, place the transition lever in the No. 1 position and leave it there. 

The transition lever also has an "off" position which sets up contacts for dynamic braking. As the lever is moved farther to the "B", the battery field contacts close, exciting the fields of all main generators. The farther the lever moves the more excitation and, therefore, increased braking. 

The transition lever is so designed that it may be moved in only one position at a time. When moving from one position to another, lift lever upwards against the upper quadrant and then move it in the direction desired. When the lever comes to the end of the upper slot, lower lever to bottom quadrant (depressing bottom lug) and complete move to adjacent position.
