PLATE 3000-4 LOAD INDICATING METER

The transition lever is mechanically interlocked with the throttle and cannot be moved from transition position 

No. 2 to No. 3 while the throttle is in No. 8 throttle position. The throttle must be in the No. 6 position or less before transition No. 2 to No. 3 can be made.

Load Indicating Meter: 

This meter is an accurate guide to the load and pulling force of the locomotive. (See Plate 3000-4) The dial of the meter is graduated into amperes from 0 at the left to 1500 amperes at the extreme right of the scale. 

A plate located directly below the load indicating meter shows the time it is permissible to operate at different stages of overload. These short time overload ratings are accumulative. In other words, it is allowable to operate at 865 amp. for two hours, then at 900 amp. for one hour, then at 925 amp. for one half hour and finally at 950 amp. for 15 minutes. After these ratings have been used up it will be necessary to reduce the load on the locomotive sufficiently to bring the transition meter reading back to 825 

amp. or less. DO NOT AGAIN EXCEED 825 AMPERES FOR FIFTEEN 

MINUTES. 

When starting a train, the pointer of the load indicating meter may go beyond the continuous rating (825 amperes). 

This is of no concern provided the pointer soon moves to the left of the continuous rating. However, if the pointer remains in the overload area or enters it on a grade, the rules governing the use of short time ratings will apply. Automatic Transition: 

In automatic transition the transition lever is placed in the No. 1 position and left there. Transition will be made automatically with the throttle in Run 8 position. When train speed is decreasing, the indicating meter should be watched for overload operation, but it should also be noted whether or not the locomotive has made proper backward transition. In the event proper backward transition has not been completed, the locomotive throttle can be reduced to idle and then advanced to the throttle position desired. In this vay, the proper transition position will be reached. Speeds at which transition should take place are shown below: 

No.1 to No. 2 at 19 M.P.H. 

No.2 to No.3 at 24.5 M.P.H. 

No.3 to No.4 at 52.0 M.P.H. 

No.4 to No.3 at 52.0 M.P.H. 

No.3 to No.2 at 24.5 M.P.H. 

No. 2 to No.1 at 19.0 M.P.H.

Whenever any wheels slip, either one or both pairs the same truck or on any truck on the locomotive, the indicator on the instrument panel will light. To stop the slippage reduce the throttle one or more notches. After a few seconds the throttle can be opened to the desired position. 

The light will not burn continuously when wheels slip because wheels do not slip continuously. The connections from the wheel slip relay to the power plant are such that the power is automatically reduced when the wheels slip. 

Therefore, the wheel will slip, then stop, slip again and stop about once a second. 

When there is a wheel slip indication, unless rail conditions are extremely bad, it will probably be only one pair of wheels that are slipping, so it is unnecessary to apply sand. 

When it is necessary to apply sand, be sure to reduce throttle until wheel slip light stops flashing. It should be remembered that sand is injurious to traction motors and associated apparatus.

A steady glow of the wheel slip light indicates electrical trouble and should be immediately investigated. 

Air Braking With Power: 

When it is necessary to apply air brakes with full power still supplied by the locomotive, the throttle should be reduced to at least the sixth notch. It is imperative that the locomotive brakes be released and held in the release position when the locomotive is supplying power. 

When preparing to stop with power applied to the locomotive and brakes applied to the train, it is necessary to reduce throttle as the train speed decreases. As the train slows down, the pulling power of the locomotive increases rapidly and might become great enough to part the train. The throttle should be in idle before the locomotive comes to a dead stop. 

These instructions are not to interfere with operating instructions covering the use of dynamic or air brakes. 

Throttle Emergency Stop Button: 

This button is located on the end of the throttle lever. 

Its purpose is to stop all engines of the locomotive in case of an emergency. It is operated by pushing the button and moving the throttle lever past idle.

Caution should be observed during ordinary operation not to press the button in error when shutting off the throttle. In the event engines are stopped in this manner, engines aust be started in the engine room after throttle is returned to idle. Dynamic Brake: 

The dynamic brake is an electrical retarding device utilizing the traction motors and main generator to retard the train. It is not dependent on the air brake system and will function only when the engines are in operation train is running. the The unit selector switch controls the field loop circuit and should be set for the number of units in the locomotive and not changed. This will normally be done by the maintenance forces. Also, when a booster unit is the trailing unit of the locomotive, the field loop jumper must be removed to complete the field loop circuit. The brake may be used at any speed, but is most effective at 17 M.P.H. 

To apply the dynamic brake, first place the throttle in the idle position and leave the transition lever in No.1 position for at least 10 seconds. Then move the transition lever to the "Off" position. After a 5 second wait, move the transition lever to the "B" position. Braking effort in this position will be sufficient to bunch slack in the train; however, at high train speed care must be taken in applying the dynamic brake to avoid excessive slack action. Leave in "B" position for 5 seconds before moving to maximum braking. 

The indicating meter indicates the traction motor current in dynamic braking as well as when motoring. The dynamic brake can be loaded up to the amperes shown on the rating plate. The pointer should not be allowed to move to the right of this rating. In the event of overloading the dynamic brake, the dynamic brake warning light will come on. By backing off on the transition lever, the light will go out. 

If the dynamic brake will not maintain the desired train speed, the air brakes can be used in conjunction with the dynamic brake. Locomotive brakes must be kept released at all times when the dynamic brake is on to avoid slid flat wheels. 

In stopping the train when using the dynamic brake, move the transition lever to the off position when the dynamic brake fades out at low speed and make a light application of the locomotive brakes to avoid a severe run-out of the locomotive. Complete the stop in the prescribed manner. 

Difference in idling speeds of the engines will cause the dynamic brake warning light to come on before the pointer reaches the maximum rating. At the first indication

of overload (warning light or load meter), the brake must be reduced. Failure to reduce the overload will result in damage to the traction motors or braking grids.

Isolating An Engine While Using Dynamic Brake: 

The dynamic brake can be isolated by putting the isolation switch in the "start" position. FIRST put the dynamic brake lever in the "off" position, then move the isolation switch to "start". Do Not Change The Unit Selector. 

In an emergency the dynamic brake on a unit can be isolated by stopping the engine with the manual lay shaft lever and then put the isolation switch in the "start" position. 

When the lead unit is shut down and dynamic braking is to be used, it will be necessary to train line the batteries between the lead and the first booster unit. If this is not done, the lead unit batteries will be too weak to give adequate dynamic braking. 

The isolation switch should not be closed to the "run" position while using the dynamic brake. If this precaution is not observed, a heavy overload will be applied to the electrical equipment and possibly cause the wheels to slide. 

Operating With Steam Locomotive Helper: 

Steam locomotives often have tonnage ratings based on speeds lower than the continuous rating speed of the Diesel. 

It is, therefore, necessary to be careful that the Diesel locomotive does not pull on more than its share of the tonnage and overheat the traction motors. Knowing the length of the grade to be traversed, the time on the grade can be closely estimated. This time will set the minimum speed and maximum loading allowable. If the estimated time on grade is between 30 minutes and one hour, the indicating meter indicated load must not be allowed to go higher than 900 amp. If the indicating meter pointer does indicate excessive loading, reduce throttle until the pointer comes within prescribed limits. In this way the steam locomotive will assume its share of the loading. 

If the indicating meter is out of order the speed of the locomotive will indicate to you the traction motor loading. Do not operate at a speed below the speeds indicated below for a longer period than indicated in the following table: 

Continuous Rating Speed - 11.0 M.P.H. 

TETT!!! 2 Hour 1 Hour 1/2 Hour 1/4 Hour 

10.2 M.P.H. 

9.0 M.P.H. 

8.5 M.P.H. 

8.0 M.P.H. 

If the train speed drops to 10 M.P.H., the locomotive can only be operated at this speed for a period not to exceed one hour; at which time the loading on the locomotive must be reduced to the extent that a speed of at least

11 M.P.H. must be maintained for a period of 15 minutes before the overload (short time) rating can again be used. 

These overload ratings are set up for the No. 1 transition position. If the short time ratings have been reached and the train speed is still below the continuous ratings, the throttle must be handled as shown below: Speed 11.0 & up. 

9.5 to 11.0. 

7.0 to 9.5

Towing Locomotive: 

Throttle Position 

When preparing the locomotive for dead heading or yard movement where it will be towed by other power, the transition lever must be placed in the No.1 position, throttle in "Idle", reverse lever in neutral and the generator field switch in "Off" position. All isolation switches must be in the "Start" position. If it is necessary to keep engines idling, the fuel pump and control switches must be in the closed position. 

Be sure the reverse lever is in neutral. If the locomotive is to be towed in a train for any distance, the reverser should be locked in its neutral position with the locking pins provided for that purpose. 

When booster units are cut out for any reason and are to be moved "dead in train", separated from lead units, or when the jumper cables have been removed between units, the cam switch of the booster unit must be placed in the "MOT. position or the wheels will pick up and slide. 11 When the locomotive is to be handled dead in train, the dead engine cock on the control valve should be set for "Dead" position, close double heading cock on the automatic brake valve pedestal, place Rotair valve handle in the "Pass" position, place automatic brake valve in running position, and independent brake valve in release position and remove handles.

Isolating An Engine While Under Power: 

If it becomes necessary to isolate an engine while under power, reduce the engine speed by the manual lay shaft until the engine speed is at idle and then move the isolation switch to the "start" position. If it is then desired to stop the engine, push "stop" button on the engine control panel until the engine stops and then turn off the fuel pump. 

Placing Engine "Back On Line": 

1. Close fuel pump switch. 

2. Place isolation switch in "start" position. 

3. Start engine in usual manner.

4. After engine lube oil pressure builds up, place isolation switch in "Run" position. Hold back on manual lay shaft control to allow engine to come up to speed gradually. 

5. If engine has been dead for some time, allow engine to idle until it has warmed up sufficiently to be operated under load.

Precautions During Locomotive Operation: 

Engine cooling water temperature should be 165 degrees plus or minus 15 degrees F. This temperature is maintained by proper operation of the radiator shutters. All engines are equipped with automatic fan and shutter controls and are adjusted to open the shutters at 165 degrees and start the fans as the temperature increases. Engine cooling water temperature should be allowed to reach 125 degrees F. before the engine is worked. 

Lubricating oil pressure should be approximately 35 to 40 lb. with engine in Run 8 and hot oil. If main bearing oil pressure drops below 20 lb. while operating in Run 8 with hot oil, stop engine and do not operate until cause of low oil pressure is corrected. The oil level should always be between the "Low" and "High" marks on the bayonet gage with the engine running. 

Do not operate engines if water level is below the "Low" level on radiator tank sight glass.

After an engine has operated several minutes in Run 8, the load indicator on the governor should read 6/16 in. and the throttle indicator shows No.8 position. (See Plate 3000-5)

Stopping Engines in Preparation for Layover: 

At control station: 

1. Close throttle to idle. 

2. Place reverse lever in neutral and remove handle. 

3. Move transition lever to "Off" position. 

4. Open generator field switch. Do not open control switch as this will make it impossible to shut down engines with "Stop" button. 

At engine: 

1. Place isolation switch in "Start" position. 

2. Push engine stop button and hold it until engine stops. 

3. Open fuel pump switch.

Note: If engine control air pressure is low stop button will not stop engine.

TROUBLE SHOOTING If Fuel Does Not Show in Fuel Return Glass: 

1. If fuel pump is not running: (a) Check all fuses in the circuit. (1) Control fuses on distribution panel. (2) Fuel pump fuses on distribution panel. (3) Fuel pump switch on engine instrument panel, also fuel pump fuse inside panel. (b) Check switches in circuit: (1) Fuel pump switch at control station. (2) "PCS" light. (3) Control knife switch. (4) Fuel pump switch on engine instrument panel. (c) Check electrical connector at fuel pump motor. 

2. If motor is running but no fuel is being delivered: (a) If fuel is flowing 60 lb. sight glass nothing can be done on road as filter is clogged. (b) Check fuel supply and emergency fuel cut-off valve. (c) Check pump coupling and shaft. (d) Check for air leaks in suction pipe due to broken pipes or loose connections. 

1. Repeat customary procedure, checking to make sure all controls and switches are in the correct position. 

2. Check all fuses affecting starting circuit. (a) Starting fuse and control fuse on distribution panel. 

3. Check to see if starting contactors come in. 

4. Check battery by turning on engine room lights, and press "Start" button. If lights dim out it is because batteries are weak.

If Engine Rotates but Does Not Fire: 

1. Check engine overspeed trip. 

2. Check fuel pump and sight glass to make sure fuel is being delivered to engine. 

3. Check injector linkage, it may be stuck in shut-down position.

If Locomotive Does Not Move when Throttle is Opened: 

1. Check "PC" switch (light above instrument panel). 

2. Check to see that generator field at control station is closed. 

3. Check to see that brakes are released. 

4. Check to see that ground protective relay is set and that isolation switch is in "Run" position. 

5. Check the following fuses: (See Plate 3000-6) (a) 60 and 30 amp. control fuses on distribution panel. (b) 15 amp. generator field fuse on distribution panel. (c) 80 amp. battery field fuse in electrical cabinet. 

If An Engine Does Not Load Up: 

1. Check ground protective relay. 

2. Starting contactor interlock open. 

3. Wheel slip relay stuck. 

4. If ground relay operates repeatedly, engine should be isolated until trouble is corrected, and Dynamic Brake 

Should Not Be Used.

30 amp. generator 

1. See that auxiliary generator switch is closed. 

2. Check all fuses in the circuit field fuse and battery charging fuse. 

If Locomotive Suddenly Becomes Inoperative: 

1. Check control fuses. 

2. Check generator field fuse. 

3. Check ground protective relays.

If Engine Fails to Stop: 

Stop engine with lay shaft manual control. Check for binding linkage, inoperative governor control, faulty governor or defective "D" valve. Also check control fuse.

Lack of Power: 

When the throttle has been advanced to Run 8, the pointer on the right hand scale (on the governor) should be on 8 and the pointer on the left hand scale should be at approximately 6. If the pointer on the right is below 

8, it indicates mechanical trouble. If the pointer on the left hand scale is at 9, 11 or 13, electrical trouble is indicated. (a) Check ground protective relay. (b) Check isolation switch. (c) Check fuel sight glass at engine, it should be full.

SECTION 2 COOLING SYSTEM 

The sys- The cooling system of each unit making up the locomotive is entirely separate from the other units. tems are identical except for the "lead" units, which have the cab heaters. The water level of the engines is below the radiators when engines are shut down. Specially treated water is used in cooling systems to retard corrosive action, etc.

Operation: 

Water is drawn by the engine driven pumps from the water tank and forced upwards through the engine flowing from the cylinder heads to the outlet header, and thence to the cooling radiators. From the cooling radiators, water flows down through the oil cooler into the water tank. 

Filling Cooling System: 

The operating water levels are stenciled on the water tank adjacent to the water gage glasses. The engine should never be operated when the water is below the minimum level mark. 

The system if filled either through the filler pipe on the roof of the locomotive or through the filler pipes on either side at the rear of the unit. With the engine stopped, open the filler level "G" valve on the opposite side of the tank from the gage glasses and slightly below the top gage cock. Fill slowly until water runs out of the filling level pipe, then close the "G" valve. When adding cold water, apply very slowly with engine idling.

1. Open drain valve at floor level beneath water pumps. 

2. Remove drain plug in bottom of each water pump. 

3. On cab units open heater drain valve. This valve is located on floor at left side of engine room next to the wall adjacent to the lube oil tank filler. 

Hot Engine Alarm Switch: 

The switch contacts are set to close at 208° F. and open at 1980 F. The closing of this switch by hot engine discharge water lights the hot engine alarm light and rings the alarm bell. The hot engine will continue to operate until it is manually shut down or until failure occurs. 

In the event of a hot engine alarm, where no other discrepancy is found: Plenty of cooling water, pumps operating satisfactorily and no restrictions in the cooling system, a check should be made to determine if the shutters and fans are operating properly. 

If the fans are not operating, inspect the fan contactors located above the engine control panel on the bulkhead. It may be that they have not operated but will operate if lifted up manually. 

SECTION 3  Description: 

Oil under pressure is forced through the engine for lubrication and piston cooling. Lube oil which falls into the pan is picked up by the scavenging oil pump and forced through filters and cooler to oil strainer housing for recirculation by the oil pump.

Lube Oil Pressure: 

Lube oil pressure will be between 30 and 50 lb. in Run 8 throttle position when the engine is hot and should never go below 20 lb. At idle the lube oil pressure should run between 

16 and 25 lb.' 

Low Oil Pressure and High Suction:

The low oil pressure and high suction alarm system is a built in feature of the electro-hydraulic governor. In the event of low oil pressure or high suction, the governor will

automatically shut the engine down to idle speed. When this occurs the button on the front of the governor (next to water tank) will be out. To reset, push this button in.

Possible Lubrication Troubles: 

(a) Absence of Oil in Strainer may be caused by an inoperative scavenging system or open drain valve. Failure of the scavenging system may be due to a broken or loose oil line connection, causing an air leak, faulty scavenging pump or clogged suction screen or low oil level. 

(b) Low Lube Oil Pressure 

may be due to stuck relief valve or foreign material under valve holding valve open, broken oil lines, clogged suction strainers, excessive bearing wear, low oil viscosity (dilution), faulty pump or insufficient oil in strainer housing. 

(b) Failure of Oil Pump due to sheared pump gear keys, broken housing or damaged gears. 

(d) Little or No Lube Oil Consumption water or fuel leaking into oil. this may be due to

SECTION 4 FUEL SYSTEM Description: 

The fuel supply tank for each locomotive unit is located under the car body and is connected to the fuel manifold through pump strainers, filters, etc. The fuel systems of all units are identical. The fuel tanks can be filled from either side of the locomotive, but watch fuel level gage to prevent overflow. el BET 

Emergency Fuel Cut-Off: (See Plate 3000-7) 

In the event of an emergency, a fuel cut-off valve is provided to cut-off supply of fuel to fuel pump. The valve is located in the feed line from the tank sump to the supply sump.

On each side of the locomotive attached to the underframe is a small red box with a lift cover. Enclosed is a pull ring operating the cut-off valve. A similar ring is located in the cab. The valve when closed must be reset by hand to the "Open" position.

PLATE 3000-7 EMERGENCY FUEL CUT-OFF VALVE

This section deals with the description and function of the electrical equipment on the locomotive. 

Cooling Fan Motor Contactors AC1, AC2, AC3, AC4: 

These contactors connect the radiator cooling fans to the alternator of the main generator and are controlled through the thermostat switch. 

Electro Hydraulic Governor Control Solenoids AV, BV, CV, DV: 

Various combinations of these solenoids control engine speed in accordance with throttle position.

Dynamic Brake Contactors B, B1, B2, B3: 

These contactors connect the traction motor fields in series with each other and to the main generator during dynamic braking. 

Battery Charging Contactor 

BC: 

This contactor connects the auxiliary generator to the battery. The reverse current relay closes the battery charging contactor when the auxiliary generator voltage reaches a predetermined setting to charge the battery. 

Battery Field Contactor 

BF: 

This contactor closes when the throttle is moved to Run 1 position, connecting the battery to the main generator battery field.

Dynamic Brake Relay - BR: 

This relay is energized when the transition lever is in "off", "B" or succeeding braking positions and establishes the position of the cam switch for dynamic braking or motoring. 

Backward Transition Current Relay 

BTR, BTP: 

This relay is a current operated relay which is closed at all times except during backward transition. When the main generator current reaches a predetermined setting, the relay picks up causing backward transition.

Dynamic Brake Warning Relay - BWR: 

Dynamic brake warning relay is a current operated relay, which will light the brake warning light indicating that the load on the dynamic brake is excessive. The light will remain

on until the load is reduced to the continuous rating of the dynamic brake equipment. 

Compressor Control Magnet Valve CC: 

This magnet valve admits main reservoir air to the unloader valves unloading the air compressor when energized. De-energizing the magnet valve releases the unloaders and causes the air compressor to resume pumping.

Compressor Control Switch 

CCS: 

The compressor control switch is actuated by main reservoir pressure and controls the loading and unloading of the air compressor in all units through the compressor control relays and control magnet valves.

Compressor Control Relay - CR: 

The compressor control relay energizes or de-energizes the control magnet valve causing the air compressor to load and unload according to the settings of the control switches. Cam Switch 

CS: When The cam switch is a power-braking transfer switch. in "Motor" position, it connects the traction motor armatures in series with their respective fields and disconnects the braking grids from the motor armatures. When in "tow" position, it disconnects the transition motor armatures from their fields and connects a braking grid across each armature. 

Engine High Temperature Switch - ETS: 

When the water temperature exceeds the maximum allowable temperature, this switch closes, lights the hot engine alarm signal light and energizes the signal relay, which rings the alarm bell. 

Relay ER: 

This relay controls the current supply to the Electro-hydraulic Governor Control solenoids AV, BV and CV. De-energizing this relay will stop the engine if the throttle is in Run 5 or Run 6 or bring the engine to idle in any other throttle position. 

Field Loop Contactor FL: 

The field loop contactor is energized when the transition lever is moved to the "off" "B" and succeeding braking position. This operation places the dynamic brake control rheostat and resistors and the unit selector switch and its resistors in series with the field loop circuit.

Fuel Pump Contactor 

FPC: 

All fuel pump contactors throughout the locomotive are controlled by the fuel pump switch at the control station. 

When this contactor is closed, the fuel pumps can be controlled individually by the switch mounted on the engine control and instrument panel.

Forward Transition Voltage Relay FTR: 

This relay is a voltage operated relay which is open at all times, except during forward transition. When the main generator voltage reaches a predetermined setting, the relay picks up closing the contacts causing forward transition. 

Forward Transition Relay (for motor shunting) FTM: 

This is a voltage operated relay, which picks up at a predetermined voltage setting. When main generator voltage reaches this value relay picks up causing transition shift to No.3.

Forward Transition Relay Parallel FTP: 

This relay is a voltage operated relay. When the main generator voltage reaches a predetermined setting, the relay picks up making forward transition to No.3.

Ground Protective Relay - GR: 

If a ground occurs in the high voltage system, this relay will open the battery field and main generator shunt field contactors, and by de-energizing the "ER" relay bring the engine to idíe except when the throttle is in the 5th or 6th position and in this event the engines will stop. Should this occur isolate the engine and reset the relay by pressing in on the reset button on the cover. When the relay is tripped the white needle will point to the red indicator and to the yellow indicator when in normal position. If the relay repeatedly opens when the engine speed is increased the power plant must be isolated. 

Load Indicating Meter 

IM: 

This meter is an accurate guide to load on the locomotive as it shows amount of current furnished to the traction motors. Isolation Switch 

IS: 

The purpose of this switch is to open and close the control circuit of the unit as the occasion demands. When starting the Diesel engine this switch must be in the START position. After engine is running move switch to RUN position. 

This will connect the control circuit of the unit to the control station. To isolate this unit, move this switch to START position. This will bring the engine to idle and cut off the power of this unit.

Motor Field Shunting Contactor Magnet Valve "M": 

This is an electro-pneumatic valve that operates the traction motor field shunting contactors. 

Traction Motor Field Shunting Contactors 

M1, M2, M3, M4: 

These contactors connect the shunting resistors across the traction motor fields. These contactors are closed in transition No. 2 and No.4. 

Cam Switch Magnet Valve for Power Position No "AC" Voltage Relay NVR: 

Mot.: 

This relay is connected across one phase of the AC alternator and should the AC voltage fail, the alarm bell rings and the alternator failure warning light (blue light) goes on. The engine speed will return to idle except when the throttle is in the 5th or 6th position and in this event the engines will stop. 

Overiding Solenoid ORS: 

This solenoid is incorporated in the governor. When this solenoid is energized it causes the pilot valve of the load regulator to move upward 1/8" which causes vane motor to move to minimum field. 

Parallel Contactors P1, P2, P3, P4: 

These contactors are closed to connect the main generator to the traction motors in parallel operation. 

Pneumatic Control Switch 

PCS: 

The purpose of this switch is to reduce the power of the locomotive by bringing the speed of the engines to idle when certain brake applications take place. 

Parallel Relay - PR: 

This relay operates to close the parallel contactors; also in conjunction with the main generator it causes shunt field and battery field contactors to open during transition from No.2 to No.3 and from No.3 to No.2. 

Reverse Current Relay RCR: 

This relay prevents the battery current from motoring the auxiliary generator by opening the battery charging contactor when the auxiliary generator voltage drops below the battery voltage.

Series Contactors - S14, S23: 

These contactors are closed to connect the main generator to the tranction motors in Series Parallel operation. 

Generator Shunt Field Contactor 

SH: 

This connects the main generator shunt field to the main generator armature. 

Signal Relay SR: 

This relay operates with the signal lights (Hot Engine, Boiler Off, and Low Oil Pressure) to ring the alarm bells throughout the locomotive. Starting Contactors 

ST+, ST-: 

These contactors are used to connect the battery to the main generator for cranking the diesel engine. 

Temperature Control Relay 

TCR: 

This relay is actuated by the thermostat switch and serves the following purposes a. Open Shutters b. Establishes a holding circuit for A.C. motor contactors so that each fan remains running as long as TCR remains closed. 

Time Delay Relay 

TD: 

This relay establishes a circuit energizing PR relay, when FTR interlock CD is closed. seconds for this relay to close. 

Time Delay Backward Relay - TDB: 

It takes three to four

This This relay takes a minimum of 28 seconds to close. relay prevents a backward step in automatic transition after forward step has been made, until 28 seconds have elapsed. 

Thermostat Switch Assembly TSA: 

This switch is operated by the temperature of the engine cooling water. As the temperature rises successive contacts close in the switch at 30 degree intervals. The contacts complete circuits which open the shutters and energize the cooling fan motor contactors one by one. 

Wheel Slip Relays - WSR1, WSR2, WSA: 

When wheel slippage occurs, these relays light the wheel slip light and reduces the main generator output momentarily until wheel slippage stops. On some locomotives this is accomplished by WSR1 and WSR2 relays energizing WSA relay.

Headlights: 

The locomotive has either a Pyle-National twin seal beam Headlight with 200 watt, 30 volt, units, or the standard 250 watt bulb in a 16 in. reflector.

Forward Transition - (Type B): 

After the engines have been started and instructions followed in preparation to move the locomotive, as previously described, place the transition lever in the No. 1 position and leave it there. Advance the throttle as needed. When the throttle is placed in the No. 1 position, the "S14", "S23", "BF" and "SH" close and the cam-switch goes to the motor position, completing No. 1 transition. As the throttle is advanced to Run 8, the locomotive speed increases. The voltage build-up of the main generator causes the FTR to pick up. This causes the "M" contactors to close, putting the locomotive in transition No. 2. A further built-up of the main generator voltage causes the FTR to pick up again, closing PR and opening SH and BF and energizing ORS. As the main generator voltage drops, the "M" contactors drop out picking up VT. S14 opens and Pl and P4 closes, S23 opens and P2 and P3 closes, SH and BF closes through P3 interlock, de-energizing_ORS and VT relay. This completes the power circuits for No.3 transition position. If the generator voltage continues to build up, the FTR will again pick up, causing the "M" contactors to close, making the No.4 transition position. Backward Transition 

(Type B): 

With power circuits in transition No. 4 position, as generator voltage drops to a predetermined value, the FTR re- 

Lay drops out, causing the "M" contactors to drop out. This sets up the No. 3 transition. As the locomotive speed decreases, the generator amperage output increases and causes the BTP relay to pick up. Through the TDB and BTP relay backward transition from the No. 3 to No. 1 position occurs. 

If conditions are such that No. 2 transition is called for, transition will progress forward as required. Backward transition from No. 2 to No. 1 is brought about by the increase in generator amperage causing the BTS relay to pick up, dropping out the "M" contactors.

Transition 

(Three Relay): 

After engines have been started and instructions followed in preparation to moving locomotive, place the transition lever in the No. 1 position and leave it there. When the throttle is moved to the No. 1 position, the series power contactors, shunt and battery field contactors will close. As the throttle is advanced and locomotive speed increases, the generator voltage builds up to a point where the FTM relay closes and the "M" contactors close, making

the No. 2 transition position. Further increase in generator voltage as a result of increased locomotive (traction motor) speed will cause the FTP to pick up. Transition from seriesparallel to parallel then takes place. As the voltage again increases, the FTM picks up, closing the "M" contactors, making the No. 4 transition position.

Backward Transition: (Three Relay) Diminishing generator voltage (decreasing locomotive speed) when in the No. 4 transition position causes the FTM relay to drop out, thus opening the "M" contactors to make

No. 3 transition position. Further decrease in locomotive speed in this position increases the generator amperage, which causes the BTP relay to pick up. This initiates backward transition from three to one. If the generator voltage in

No. 1 transition is sufficient, the FTM will pick up to make 

No. 2 transition. From this point transition back to the 

No. 1 position will take place when the generator voltage decreases sufficiently to drop out the FTM. 

SECTION 6 AIR SYSTEM Air Compressor: 

Each power plant in the locomotive is equipped with a 

Gardner-Denver type WXE three-cylinder air compressor, rated at 178 cubic feet of air per minute at 800 RPM. It is driven by the shaft of the main generator through a flexible coupling. The compressor has its own oil pump and pressure lubricating system. Oil level in the crankcase can be checked on the bayonet type gauge. Lubricating oil pressure should be from 12 to 15 pounds at 800 RPM and not less than 5 pounds at idle (hot oil). Since each air compressor is directly connected to an engine and in operation at all times when the engine is running, an unloader is provided in the heads of both high and low pressure cylinders which cuts out the compressing action when actuated by air pressure. The unloader accomplishes this by blocking open the suction or intake valves of the high and low pressure cylinders. When the air operating the unloader is cut off, the unloader releases the suction valves and the compressor resumes pumping.

The compressor consists of two low pressure cylinders and one high pressure cylinder. The pistons of all three cylinders are driven by a common crankshaft. The two low pressure cylinders are set at an angle to the vertical high pressure cylinder. Air from the low pressure cylinder goes to an intercooler, or radiator, to be cooled before entering the high pressure cylinder. The intercooler is provided

with a pressure gauge and safety valve. The gauge normally reads approximately 32 pounds when the compressor is loaded. 

The intercooler safety valve is set for 50 pounds. Any marked deviation of intercooler pressure from 32 pounds should be reported to maintenance for attention. 

Draining of Main Reservoir: 

The air system should be drained periodically to prevent moisture from being carried into the air brake and electric control air systems. The frequency of draining will depend on local conditions and can be determined by practice. It is recommended that draining be done at the time of each crew change.

Santa Fe 450 and 451 

44-TON SWITCHING LOCOMOTIVES 

Hob ФИ 450 

PART II-SECTION 11

GENERAL DESCRIPTION 

Locomotive 451 was manufactured by The Whitcomb Locomotive Co. and locomotive 450 by The Davenport Besler Corp. 

These locomotives have a top speed of 30 M.P.H. The cab is in center of the locomotive and two Caterpillar D-17000 diesel engines each rated 180 H.P. at 1000 R.P.H. are located at each end. Each diesel engine generator set drives two traction motors which are geared to the axles of each pair of wheels. 

The engines have a speed range from 400-420 at idle to 

1000-1020 R.P.M. at full throttle. Their speed is controlled by a mechanical lever from the throttle quadrant to the speeder shaft on each engine governor. Both engines are controlled simultaneously. 

Cranking the engine to start is accomplished by pressing the engine start button on the control station panel. 

Each engine is started separately. This switch button energizes the starting contactors, closing the circuit from the batteries to the main generator. The main generator, then operating as a motor, turns the engine. The flow of current through the generator is always in the same direction. Reversing the locomotive is accomplished by reversing the direction of the current in the traction motor fields. 

Control Station and Instrument Panel: 

The control station contains the throttle, 5 position reversing lever (which when removed from station voids all power movements) air brake valves, and a push button box with fused switches for: 

a. Control 30 amp. 

b. Cab light c. Gauge lights d. Front headlight 10 amp. e. Rear headlight 10 amp. f. Cab heaters 10 amp. g. Classification marker h. Two engine start buttons 

1. Engine stop button

Locomotive 450 has transfer fuel pump which operates off control button to pump fuel out of tank and deliver it to a small auxiliary fuel tank located on the side of the diesel engine. 

The instrument panel contains the following instruments: duplex air gauges for main and equalizing reservoir pressures, trainline and brake pipe pressure, water temperature thermometers, lubricating oil pressure gauges, battery charging ammeter, wheel slip light, and two tachometers.

The sander valve, windshield wiper, whistle cord and bell ringer are located to left of control station within easy reach.

Locomotive Operation: 

1. The control stand has two positions "Series" and "Parallel". Only one control lever is used for forward or reverse and for connecting traction motors to main generators. 

2. Transition from "Series" to "Parallel" is automatic when control lever is place in "Parallel" position and takes place at approximately 22 MILES per hour with full throttle. 

3. Backward transition is not automatic and if locomotive speed falls below 20 miles per hour after parallel transition has been made shut off throttle momentarily to restore "Series" connection of traction motors. 

4. Series to parallel transition can be forstalled by leaving the control lever in series position. 

5. For movements where locomotive speed will not exceed 

22 miles per hour, the locomotive should be operated with control lever in "series" position only to eliminate transitions to "Parallel". 

Operation in "Parallel" at speeds below 

22 M. P. H. will result in overheating of the main generators. 

6. Reverse lever must be thrown from "Forward to Re verse" or "Reverse to Forward" ONLY WHEN LOCOMOTIVE IS STAND- ING STILL WITH THROTTLE CLOSED TO IDLE POSITION. (Reverser should be operated while in motion only in emergency). 

7. Reduce throttle as much as practicable at railroad crossings to prevent burning of traction motor commutators. 

Vibration of motors at railroad crossings causes traction motor brushes to jump off commutators which creates an arc and burns commutator if throttle is not reduced.

Proper Operating Pressures and Temperatures: 

1. Fuel Oil. Caution (green) or Normal (white) on fuel indicators. 

2. Lubricating oil 20-45 pounds. If lubricating oil pressure drops below 15 LBS. PER SQ. IN. at full throttle

STOP ENGINE and do not operate until cause of trouble is corrected. 

3. Temperature cooling water 1600-1800. If cooling water temperature cannot be maintained at or below 190° F. 

STOP ENGINE and do not operate until cause of trouble is corrected. 

To Start Engine: 

1. Place controller handle in "OFF" and throttle handle in idle position. 

2. Lock throttle switch by placing lock pin crosswise in deep slot. IF

3. Close control push button switch. 

4. Open throttle approximately to half way position. 

5. Push in No. 1 and No. 2 engine start buttons respectively on locomotive 451 and No. 2 and No. 1 engine start buttons respectively on locomotive 450, ONE AT A TIME and hold in until engine fires. (NOTE: If engines are cold it will be necessary to crank them with the starter, holding the engine "Stop" rod out and having the compression release lever in the "Start" then "Half", then "Run" positions for a few minutes each to warm up the engine and facilitate its firing. 

Towing Locomotive: 

1. Stop engines and drain both radiating systems if severe cold weather. 

3. Close double heading cock under brake valve. 

4. Remove controller handle. 

5. Lock brake valves in running position. 

6. Cut in dead engine cock at distributing valve. 

7. Close shutters and cover exhaust stacks. 

8. Set pop valve on distributing valve at 25 lbs. 

To Stop Engine: 

1. Shut off throttle. 

2. Pull out and hold "Stop" rod on engine to be shut down. 

3. Pull out control button if finished with locomotive. 

4. If engine fails to stop as given above move the compression release lever to the "Start" position or pull the emergency fuel valve.

TROUBLE SHOOTING If Engine Does Not Rotate When Attempt to Start: 

1. If engines fail to turn overa. Examine throttle switch to determine if properly locked. b. Check 30 ampere control fuse in the control stand. 

2. If engines turn over but fail to firea. Examine throttle switch to determine if properly locked. b. Check to see that engine "Stop" rod in normal position. c. Inspect emergency stop valve in fuel line at top of fuel tank to determine if tripped. If so, it will be necessary to reset manually. d. Operate hand fuel oil pump mounted near the fuel pressure indicator while engine is being cranked.

e. Bleed off fuel pumps mounted on top of engine between cylinder banks to relieve any accumulation of air in fuel system. f. Check compression release lever.

If Engines Run But Locomotive Does Not Move: 

1. Check control air pressure. 

2. Check control fuse. 

3. Check throttle switch lock should be lengthwise. 

4. Check controller should be in either forward or reverse position. 

Battery Charging Indicator: 

An ammeter is provided to indicate battery charging. 

If batteries are not charging they must have attention quickly to prevent their becoming exhausted. 

If Battery Charging Ammeter Shows Zero: 

1. Push in No. 2 engine start button on locomotive 451 and No. 1 engine start button on locomotive 450.for an instant with throttle closed. 

2. Check 70 ampere charging fuse in small electrical cabinet. 

If in an emergency it is necessary to operate with No. 2 engine shut down on locomotive 451 push in large button at the control stand for an instant to reduce the load on the batteries. If No. 1 power plant is shut down on locomotive 

450, push in the auxiliary generator field cut out button for an instant. 

SECTION 2 COOLING SYSTEM 

Water is drawn from the bottom header of the radiator by two pumps, one on each side of the engine, and delivered through manifolds to the cylinder jackets then back to the radiator. In passing through the radiator, it is cooled by the air drawn over the radiator tubes by the engine fan. Specially treated water should be used in the cooling system. 

A thermostatic by-pass valve in the line from the engine to the radiator by-passes the radiator, and returns the water directly to the pump inlets until the water has reached a temperature of 1650 F. This enables the engine to warm up quickly after starting, and maintains an even temperature with varying loads.

A thermometer, located on the instrument panel at the control station, indicates the temperature of the cooling

water leaving the engine. The manual control of the water temperature by means of the radiator shutters and hatch cover opening. Best operation is obtained with tenperatures between 160 and 170° F. Excessive temperatures (ABOVE 1900 F.) or abrupt changes in temperature indicate trouble and if water temperature cannot be maintained at 1900 F. or less by easing off on throttle with radiator shutters and hatch covers wide open ENGINE SHOULD BE SHUT DOWN. 

To Fill Radiating System: 

1. Place hose over water filling nozzle under running board near each end of locomotive and fill until water overflows from fillup line on opposite side of locomotive. 

NOTE: The cooling system for each engine must be filled separately. 

To Drain Radiating System: 

1. Locomotive 451 a. Open pet cocks below both water pumps on each engine. 2. Locomotive 450. a. Open drain cocks below both water pumps on each engine. b. Remove 1/2 in. pipe plug on each side of each engine. c. Remove 1/4 in. pipe plug below each compression release lever. d. Remove two 1/8 in. pipe plugs from bottom of cab heater.

To Control Engine Cooling Water Temperatures: 

1. Operate shutter control levers mounted on top of each electrical cabinet.

SECTION 3  

The lubrication system supplies clean oil to bearings and other moving parts at all times when the engine is running. The pressure in this system is indicated by a gauge on the instrument panel at the control station. IF THE PRES- 

SURE DROPS BELOW 15 LBS. THE ENGINE SHOULD BE SHUT DOWN WITHOUT DELAY. 

The oil flows through the system in the following manner. Clean oil is drawn from the supply tank by the pressure pump and is forces to the oil cooler then to the engine oil filters on through the oil manifolds to the bearings and other parts of the engine. After it has lubricated the engine,

the oil drops down into the upper sump of the engine oil pan. 

The oil level should be maintained between the "LOW" and 

"FULL" marks (preferably ABOVE THE HALFWAY MARK) on the bayo net gauge in the engine oil pan while the engine is running or immediately after it has been shut down. 

NOTE: Oil level will raise after engine has been shut down a few minutes due to oil draining back into crankcase. 

To Check Level of Engine Lubricating Oil: 

Measure oil level in crankcase using bayonet gauge on compression release lever side of engine, while engine is running or immediately after it has been shut down. 

The gear driven fuel-transfer pump draws fuel from the fuel tank and supplies it under pressure to the fuel filters, which remove dirt and other foreign material. The fuel then passes to the manifold for the individual fuel-injection pumps one for each cylinder. These fuel-injection pumps measure the fuel according to engine load, and force the fuel through the fuel injection lines to the injection valves in the cylinder head. The injection valves spray the fuel into the precombustion chamber, where it is ignited and passes into the engine cylinder.

To Prime the Fuel System: 

Make sure the emergency fuel-shut off valve is open.

See that the fuel tank contains fuel and open the fuel filter vent valves. Unscrew the hand priming fuel-pump plunger, and operate with full and steady strokes. When the flow of fuel through the vents becomes continuous and contains no air bubbles, close the filter vents; then open each fuel-injection-pump vent in turn, continuing to operate the hand-priming pump until each vent discharges solid fuel. Open and close the vents several times in succession, to be sure that all of the air is bled from the system. 

After the system is primed, the hand-priming-pump plunger should be forced to the bottom of the pump cylinder and screwed into place. This closes the valve seat located in the bottom of the cylinder which prevents air from leaking by the piston into the fuel-injection system.

The low voltage equipment is located in the cab toward the front of locomotive (behind the No. 1 power plant). The battery charging equipment is operated from No. 2 power plant on locomotive 451 and No. 1 power plant on locomotive 450.

Shift from Parallel to Series. 

This must be done manually by reducing the throttle to idle-dropping out all contactors- or by returning transition lever to series position. 

Shift from series shunt to series is the same as shift from Parallel shunt to Parallel. 

SECTION 6 AIR SYSTEM Air Compressor: 

Each locomotive is equipped with two (2) Gardners-Denver 

ADS compressors 51 & 3x4 two cylinder two stage air cooled.

The compressors are driven by "y" belts from the engine crankshaft through the generator. Adjustment of the belt tension is provided for by moving these compressors on their bases so as to change the distance between the pulley centers.

The main reservoirs are located under the carbody directly beneath the cab. 

In order to prevent moisture from being carried into the air brake system and control air system. These reservoirs should be drained at the time of each crew change.

PART II-SECTION 12

This 44 ton-360 H.P. Diesel-electric switching locomo tive was built by General Electric Co. and has two four wheel swivel trucks and a cab with the control compartment in the center. 

Twin power plants, each consisting of a D 17000 Caterpillar diesel engine (See Part 1, Section 8) with a directconnected 250 volt electric generator supply power to drive two traction motors on the truck under the respective engine. 

Each motor is geared to a driving axle throught a double reduction gear.

An air compressor and auxiliary generator is belt driven from each diesel engine to provide compressed air and electricity used on the locomotive. The No. 1 engine is the front engine and the No. 2 engine is the rear engine.

SECTION 2 COOLING SYSTEM Description: 

Water is drawn from the bottom header of the radiator by two pumps, one on each side of the engine, and delivered through manifolds to the cylinder jackets then back to the radiator. In passing through the radiator, it is cooled by the air drawn over the radiator tubes by the engine fan. Specially treated water should be used in the cooling system. 

A thermostatic by-pass valve in the line from the engine to the radiator by-passes the radiator, and returns the water directly to the pump inlets until the water has reached a temperature of 1650 F. This enables the engine to warm up quickly after starting and maintains an even temperature with varying loads. 

A thermometer, located on the instrument panel at the control station, indicates the temperature of the cooling water leaving the engine. The cooling water temperature can be controlled by means of the radiator shutters and hatch cover opening. Best operation is obtained with temperatures between 1600 and 1700 F. Excessive temperatures (above 1900 F) or abrupt changes in temperature indicate trouble and if water temperature cannot be maintained at 1900 F. or less by easing off on throttle with radiator shutters and hatch covers wide open engine should be shut down.

To Fill Radiating System: 

1. Couple water hose to filling pipe located below running board near each end of locomotive (See Plate 2800-6) and fill until water runs out top gauge cock on radiator or overflows from fill up nozzle on opposite side of locomotive NOTE: The cooling system for each engine must be filled separately.

To Drain Radiating System:

3. Remove 1/4" pipe plug below each compression release lever. 

4. Remove drain plug in bottom of cab heater.

SECTION 3  Description: 

The lubrication system supplies clean oil to bearings and other moving parts at all times when the engine is running. 

The pressure in this system is indicated by a gauge on the instrument panel at the control station. If the pressure drops below 15 lbs. the engine should be shut down without delay.

The oil flows through the system in the following manner. 

Clean oil is drawn from the supply tank by the pressure pump and is forced to the oil cooler then to the engine oil filters on through the oil manifolds to the bearings and other parts of the engine. After it has lubricated the engine, the oil drops down into the upper sump of the engine oil pan. The oil level should be maintained between the "low" and "full" marks (preferably above the halfway mark) on the bayonet gauge in the engine oil pan while the engine is running or immediately after it has been shut down. 

NOTE: Oil level will raise after engine has been shut down a few minutes due to oil draining back into crankcase. 

The oil overflows from this sump into the lower sump where it is picked up by the scavenging pump and is delivered to the absorbent filter then to the supply tank completing the cycle.

The oil pan has two sumps the upper and lower. The upper sump acts as a settling basin to trap condensate and other foreign material. The lower, or main sump receives oil through an overflow pipe in the upper sump. In this way, most of the oil is shielded from the heat and blow by gases from the engine, retarding oil deterioration. 

Lube Oil Pressure: 

The supply and scavenging pumps are of the gear type. 

They form a single unit which mounts on the timing-gear housing and are gear driven. Engine oil pressure is automatically

SECTION 4 FUEL OIL SYSTEM 

The gear driven fuel-transfer pump draws fuel from the fuel tank and supplies it under pressure to the fuel filters, which remove dirt and other foreign material. The fuel then passes to the manifold for the individual fuel injection pumps one for each cylinder. These fuel-injection pumps measure the fuel according to engine load, and force the fuel through the fuel injection lines to the injection valve in the cylinder head. The injection valves spray the fuel into the precombustion chamber, where it is ignited and passes into the engine cylinder.

To Prime the Fuel System: 

Make sure the emergency fuel-shut off valve is open. See that the fuel tank contains fuel and open the fuel filter vent valves. Unscrew the hand priming fuel-pump plunger, and operate with full and steady strokes. When the flow of fuel through the vents becomes continuous and contains no air bubbles, close the filter vents; then open each fuel-injectionpump vent in turn, continuing to operate the hand-priming pump until each vent discharges solid fuel. Open and close the vents several times in succession, to be sure that all of the air is bled from the system.

After the system is primed, the hand-priming-pump plunger should be forced to the bottom of the pump cylinder and screwed into place. This closes the valve seat located in the bottom of the cylinder which prevents air from leaking by the piston into the fuel-injection system.

To Cut Out a Traction Motor: 

1. On locomotive 460 to 462 inclusive: 

To cut out a traction motor move respective cut-out switch located inside front electrical cabinet to "OFF" position. 

2. On locomotive 463 to 468 inclusive: 

(a) To cut out No. 1 or No. 2 traction motors move cutout switch located inside front electrical cabinet to "OFF" position. (b) To cut out No. 3 or No. 4 traction motors, move cut out switch located inside rear electrical cabinet to "OFF" position. 

3. If necessary to cut out both motors in same truck shut down engine above it and move exciter field switch for that power plant to "OFF" position. 

WARNING: Have throttle closed and master controller in "OFF" position when cutting out traction motors.

Avoid pulling heavy loads or operating with more than 1/2 throttle with one traction motor cutout. 

Operating Sequence of Relays and Contactors in Traction Motor Field Shunting Circuits: 

Full utilization of the available diesel-engine output and more power for traction at higher locomotive speeds is obtained by weakening the motor field. This is accomplished by paralleling the motor field with a resistor, and is called motor field shunting. The result is somewhat similar to overdrive in an automobile. 

Field-Shunting Operation Takes Place as Follows: 

When throttle is advances beyond the idling position:

1. Contact A on TC closes thus closing contactors P1, P2, P3 and P4. 

2. Contact B on TC, which is closed in the idling position, remains closed when contact A first closes. 

3. The armatures of relays CR and FS (FSR1 and FSR2 on locomo tives 460, 461 and 462) close. This opens one contact on 

CR and closes the other. This also opens the contacts on 

FS (FSR1 and FSR2 on locomotives 460, 461 and 462). 

4. Relay CR remains closed through its own closed contact. 

5. The currents in the motor circuits aid in keeping the armatures of relays CR and FS (FSR1 and FSR2 on locomotives 460, 461 and 462) closed through their series coils. 

When throttle is further advanced: 

1. Contact B on TC opens, thus de-energizing the shunt coil of relay FS (relays FSR1 and FSR2 on locomotives 

460, 461 and 462). 2. The armature of relay FS (relays FSR1 and FSR2 on locomotives 460, 461 and 462) remains closed through its series coil.

As the locomotive speed increases, and the traction motor current decreases: 1. Relay FS (relays FSR1 and FSR2 on locomotives 460, 461, and 462) drops out, because of decrease of current in the series coils, thus closing its contact. 

2. Field-shunting contactors close in the following sequence: M3, M4, M1 and M2 (M1, M3, M2 and M4 on locomotives 460, 461 and 462) causing field shunting on motors

No. 3, 4, 1 and 2 (No. 1, 3, 2 and 4 on locomotives 460, 461 and 462) respectively. 

3. The shunt coil of relay CR is de-energized, but the armature of CR is held closed by its own series coil. 

When maximum permissible locomotive speed is reached: 

1. Relay CR opens, because of the decrease in current through its series coil. 

2. Contactors M1, M2, M3 and M4 open, removing motor fieldshunting connections. 

3. To re-establish field-shunting connections, the speed of the locomotive must be reduced and the throttle shut off far enough to close contact B on switch TC. Field shunting can then be re-established by the sequence described above. 

If, with the field-shunting contactors closed, the locomotive load increases so that the traction current increases beyond a certain value: 

1. The armature of relay FS (FSR1 and FSR2 on locomotives 

460, 461 and 462) closes by the current in the series coils, thus opening the relay contact. 

2. Contactors M1, M2, M3 and M4 drop out, removing motor field-shunting connections. 

If field shunting is not obtained as above check for: 

1. Loose, open, short-circuited, or grounded wires and connections. 

2. Poor contacts on TC switch, cut out switches, relay and interlocks. 

3. With battery switch open, check contactors and relays by hand for free action.

SECTION 6 AIR SYSTEM 

Air Compressor: 

Each locomotive is equipped with two (2) Gardner-Denver 

ADS compressors 5 & 3x4 two cylinder two stage air cooled.

The compressors are driven by "V" belts from the engine crankshaft through the generator. Adjustment of the belt tension is provided for by moving these compressors on their bases so as to change the distance between the pulley centers.

The main reservoirs are located under the carbody directly beneath the cab.

In order to prevent moisture from being carried into the air brake system and control air system, these reservoirs should be drained at each crew change.

KEEP OFF KEEP OFFA PART II-SECTION 13

The 500 and 503 class Diesel-electric locomotive was manufactured by the Fairbanks, Morse & Company and is powered by a Model 38D8 1/8 6-cylinder opposed piston engine (See

Part I, Section 9). The engine has a speed range of 300 to 

800 r.p.m. which is controlled directly by the throttle through a pneumatic system.

The cab is located in the back of the locomotive and the hood extends toward the front. 

The Diesel engine is mounted in the locomotive with the generator in front of the cab and the accessory end of the engine toward the front of the locomotive. In front of the 

Diesel engine are located the traction motor blower, radiator cooling fan, water cooling radiator and oil cooling radiator. 

These locomotives have one 6-cylinder 1000-HP. or 1200-HP. engine, a direct current generator and four traction motors. 

From the power plant the power is distributed to the traction motors which are mounted on the trucks. The traction motors are geared to the axle through spur gears. (D503 and up are 1200 HP.) Starting the engine is accomplished by pressing the engine start button located on the control stand. This switch energizes the starting contactors, closing the circuit from the batteries to the main generator. The main generator, then operating as a motor, cranks the engine. 

The flow of current through the generator is always in the same direction. Reversing the locomotive is accomplished by reversing the direction of current in the traction motor fields. 

LOCOMOTIVE OPERATION Instrument and Control Panel: (See Plate 3300-1)

Starting Engine: 

1. In electrical cabinet: a. Be sure all fuses are in place. b. Close main battery switch. 

2. At control station: a. Turn shut down valve handle to "Run" position. 

b. Put Throttle-Reverse Lever In Neutral Position. 

c. Turn control switch to "on" position. d. Turn fuel pump switch to "on" position and watch for pressure on fuel pressure gauge at instrument panel. Pressure should be 5 to 10 lb. per square inch. e. Pull down low oil pressure release and hold down located above control panel. f: Press in engine "start" button and hold until engine starts. Do not hold button down for more than 10 seconds at a time. g. Release low lubricating oil pressure release after lube oil gauge registers pressure.

Note: Do Not Tie Or Block Out Lube Oil Pressure Release In Any Way. 

h. Check lubricating oil pressure.

This should be 20 to 30 lb. per square inch. Water temperature should be 125° F. before locomotive is worked. 

1. Shut off auxiliary fuel pump.

To Move Locomotive: 

1. Close exciter field switch at control station (arrow to right) or locomotive RUN switch. 

2. The throttle lever is designed to operate horizontally in two slots, with a connecting vertical slot which has a neutral position and locking device midway between top and bottom. The UPPER horizontal slot is for REVERSE operation, and the LOWER horizontal slot for FORWARD operation. There is no separate reverser..

Caution: In moving the locomotive do not pump the throttle handle back and forth as this will open and close the contactors and other electrical equipment which must make or break circuits each time the throttle is shut off or opened. A movement of the throttle handle of approximately 1/2 in. will close the control switch, thus closing the control circuit, allowing the power output of the generator to be delivered to the traction motors. 

Maximum Rating of Locomotive As Indicated By Ammeter At Control Panel: Locomotives 500-502 

1. Continuous Rating. 

2. Forty Minute Rating. 

3. Twenty Minute Rating 

4. Fifteen Minute Rating. 

5. Five Minute Rating 1040 amp. .1200 amp. 1350 amp. 1400 amp. .2000 amp. 

Maximum Rating of Locomotive As Indicated By Ammeter At Control Panel: 

Locomotives 503 & up. 

1. Continuous Rating. 

2. Forty Minute Rating. 

3. Twenty Minute Rating 

4. Ten Minute Rating. 1040 amp. 1100 amp. 1200 amp. 1300 amp. 

At the end of time limit for any value, notch back on the throttle to 1040 amp. or less. Do Not Again Exceed 

1040 Amp. for 15 Minutes.

Operation of "Power Control" Feature: 

1. To spot cars and case movement of cars, push in low speed button (bottom button). 

2. To nullify low power feature bring throttle to center and press the high power button before operating in power. 

3. For normal operation, push high power button (top button). 

4. High power operation will cut in automatically when throttle exceeds 2/3 position or engine exceeds 650 r.p.m. 

5. High or low power can be selected as desired, with the throttle in any position below 650 r.p.m. 

Operation of  

Whenever any pair of wheels slip on either truck the red indicator on the instrument panel will light. To stop the slippage, ease off on the throttle.

The light will not burn continuously when the wheels slip because the wheels will not slip continuously. The connections of the wheel slip relay to the power plant are such that power is automatically cut off to zero when the wheels slip. When the wheels stop slipping the power will go on at

full strength Unless Throttle Is Reduced. Unless throttle is reduced a Severe Strain on the motors, motor gears and generator will develop. The wheels will slip, then stop, slip again and stop about once a second if throttle is not reduced.

If necessary to apply sand reduce the throttle until the wheel slip light stops flashing. It should be remembered that sand is injurious to the traction motors and associated apparatus.

If Necessary to Move Dead in Train: 

1. Stop engine and drain radiating system if freezing weather will be encountered. 

3. Place throttle and reversing lever in center position and lock by screwing in on handle until tight. 

4. Close double-heading cock located below automatic brake valve. 

5. Open dead engine cock located to the front of the distributing valve on the right side of the locomotive under cab. 

6. Lock both brake valves in running position. 

7. Set relief valve on distributing valve at 25 lb. 

8. See that radiator shutters are closed.

Precautions During Locomotive Operation: 

1. Maintain engine cooling water temperature between 

1650 and 185° F. This is accomplished by operation of the automatic shutters and cooling fan. Do not 

Exceed 1950 F. If Water Temperature Exceeds 195° F. 

Shut Engine Down And Do Not Start Until Cause Of 

Trouble Is Corrected. 

2. Lubricating oil pressure should be approximately 

25 lb. at full throttle (800 r. p.m.). If main bearing pressure drops below 15 lb. (at 800 r.p.m. hot oil), Stop The Engine And Do Not Operate Until Con dition Is Corrected. 

3. The fuel pressure gauge should show a minimum pressure of 5 lb. 

4. Lubricating oil temperature should be 1600 to 1850 F.

If Lubricating Oil Temperature Exceeds 1950 F. Shut 

Engine Down And Do Not Operate Until Condition is Corrected.

Stopping Engine: 

1. At control station: a. Place throttle-reverse lever in neutral position. b. Open exciter field or locomotive "Run" switch. c. Open control switch. d. Move engine shut down lever to shut down position. 

2. In electrical cabinet: a. Open main battery switch.

TROUBLE SHOOTING If Fuel Pressure Does Not Show on Gauge in Cab (With Auxiliary Fuel Pump Running): 

1. If fuel pump does not run: a. Check 25 amp. control fuse in right lower corner of electrical cabinet. On locomotives 503 & up check control circuit breaker. 

2. If motor is running but no fuel is being delivered: a. Check pump couplings and shaft. b. Check fuel supply and emergency fuel cut-off valve. c. Check for air leaks in suction line due to broken pipe or loose connection.

1. Check 25 amp. control fuse in electrical cabinet.

On locomotives 503 & up check control circuit breaker. 

2. Battery may be too weak to turn engine over. 

3. Check to see that both starting contactors go in. 

4. Check to see that throttle is in "center" position.

If Engine Rotates But Does Not Fire When Start Button is 

Pressed: 

1. Check injector linkage in event that it may be stuck in shutdown position. 

2. Check to see that overspeed trip shaft is latched in "Reset" position.

If Locomotive Does Not Move When Throttle is Opened: 

1. Check hand brake. 

2. Repeat movement of brake valve to release brakes. 

3. Check to see that exciter field switch or locomotive 

Run switch is closed. 

4. Check control air pressure and see that reverser is in proper position. 

5. Check 25 amp. control fuse or control circuit breaker whichever is used. 

6. The exciter field contactor might be open or making poor contact. 

7. If starting contactors stick shut or wheel slip relay sticks open the generator will not deliver power. 

1. Check 100 amp. battery charging fuse (center fuse in group of three fuses) in right lower corner of electrical cabinet. Locomotives 503 & up use 125 amp. fuse. 

2. Auxiliary generator drive belts might be loose or broken.

NOTE: If batteries are not charging they Must Have

Attention Quickly to prevent exhaustion. 

If Radiator Fan Motor Does Not Rotate (When Top Shutter 

Opens) 

1. Check 200 amp. radiator fan fuse in right lower corner of electrical cabinet. 

2. See that radiator fan contactors are making good contact. 

3. See that belts to fan generator are not defective.

If Locomotive Stops in Operation: 

If locomotive suddenly becomes inoperative, immediately check the control fuse (25 amp.) in the electrical cabinet. 

A wire may have jarred loose or may be burned off. The fuel supply may have stopped. On 503 & up check control circuit breaker. 

SECTION 2 COOLING SYSTEM Operation of Cooling System: 

Water is drawn from the radiators by centrifugal water pumps located on the front end of the engine. These pumps circulate water through the exhaust manifold water jacket to the bottom of each cylinder liner up through the passages of the cylinder liner and out through the outlet manifold. From the engine, the water flows to two groups of radiator sections located on the side of the locomotive body near front of locomotive. Here the water is cooled returning to the engine. 

Filling Cooling System: 

The cooling system contains approximately 150 gal. and may be filled through the filler pipe located along the left side of the front truck or through the filler pipe on the expansion tank in engineroom. (Do not put water in the exhaust stacks). Fill the cooling system until water runs out of the overflow at the front of the fuel tank on the left side of locomotive or fill cooling system until water glass located inside hood on right side of water tank shows full. 

It is best to have the engine idling when system is being filled, to remove air pockets.

Add water very slowly if engine is hot and then only when engine is idling. Do not add cold water to a hot engine that has just been drained as this will crack and warp cylinder liners.

Specially treated water should be used in the cooling system to offset the accumulation of scale or foreign matter which contributes to pitting and overheating the engine. 

The engine should be operated with the water level above the minimum water level mark (2/3 Water Glass) and The Engine 

Should Not Be Operated With Water Not Showing In Sight Glass. 

To Drain The System: 

A common drain is installed. Lines from the jacket water, oil cooling water and the fuel oil drain systems are all connected to this common drain. 

The following must be accomplished in order to completely drain the cooling water system. 

1. Open valves V1 and V2, to drain the jacket water system. 

2. Open valves V3, V4 and V15 to drain the oil cooling water system. 

3. Remove the drain plugs, Pl, and P2 on the underside of the circulating water pumps to drain.

As a precautionary measure the plugs in the exhaust nozzles and manifolds can be removed for draining any water left in these passages. The plugs in the nozzles are numbered P3 and P4 (where two pipe fitting outlets are provided in each nozzle). The plugs in the manifolds (each side of the engine) are numbered P5, P6, P7 and P8.

Cooling Water Temperature Control: 

1. The cooling water temperature is controlled by automatic shutters and a motor driven fan which are set to hold the cooling water temperature between 1650 and 185° F. 

2. Three speeds, automatically controlled, are provided on the fan motor. 

3. If the cooling water temperature rises to 1950 F. with shutters open and fan operating at the highest speed, operate the locomotive at reduced throttle.

If Engine Temperature Exceeds 1950 F. Stop Engine And Do Not Operate Until Cause Of Excessive Temperature 

Is Corrected.

SECTION 3  Description: 

The The lubricating oil is stored in the engine crankcase which classifies the engine as a wet sump type. The system is a pressure system using a positive displacement pump. oil supply to this pump is drawn through a strainer in the bottom of the engine sump.

Oil may be added by removing cover on right side of engine and pouring required amount through strainer. With the engine idling, the oil level should always be between the "high" and "add oil" marks on the "engine running" side of the bayonet gauge in the crankcase. 

When starting a cold engine, allow it to idle for some time and see that pressure starts to build up almost immediately. The pressure should rise on a cold engine to about 40 lb. and will run approximately 20 to 30 lb. at full throttle (800 r.p.m.) with engine warm. At idle, pressure should be 

10 to 15 lb.

With normal operation, the lubricating oil pressure should not drop below 15 Lb. Per Sq. Inch with engine running at full throttle (800 r.p.m.).

If Oil Pressure Drops Below The Above Limits, Stop The Engine And Do Not Operate Until Cause Of Low Oil Pressure Is 

Corrected.

Low Oil Pressure Shut Down: 

If the lubricating oil pressure should drop below 5 lb. the engine will be shut down by a low oil pressure shut-down device. This is accomplished by a cylinder containing a piston attached through a linkage to the injector linkage. 

One side of the piston is subjected to engine oil pressure and the other side to mechanical spring pressure. When the force of the spring overcomes the force of the oil, the piston moves backward closing the throttle shaft to stop position. 

A fuel tank is mounted under the center of the unit with a capacity of 750 gal. At the bottom of the tank is a sump from which the fuel pump draws fuel oil through the suction line and suction filter. Fuel can only be drawn from the

The surplus fuel oil lubricates and cools the internal mechanism of the fuel pumps and then returns to the fuel tank through the 15 lb. relief valve.

Emergency Fuel Cut Off: 

On each side of the locomotive, attached to the underframe is a ring on the end of a cable running to the fuel cutoff valve. A similar ring is located in the cab. The valve can be closed and the fuel cut off by pulling any one of these three rings. If closed, the valve on back side of the fuel tank must be reset by hand to the "Open" position.

Fuel Level Gauge: 

Three direct reading sight level gauges are located on the right rear of the fuel tank. These gauges indicate level of fuel oil starting from the top of the tank. 

Fuel Pump: 

This main supply pump is built into and driven from the Diesel engine. It supplies all the fuel while the engine is running. Pressure will vary between 10 lb. at idle and 25 lb. at full throttle (800 r.p.m.). On locomotives 503 and up NO engine driven pump is provided.

Auxiliary Fuel Pump: 

Do The auxiliary fuel pump assembly consists of an electric motor and pump of the rotor gear type. The gear pump is driven by the motor shaft through a flexible coupling. not leave the fuel pump running unnecessarily as this pump should only be used while starting engine. Fuel pressure with this pump running is approximately 15 lb. 

Locomotives 503 & up have motor driven fuel pump running continuously. Minimum pressure allowed is 15 psi.

3. Exciter Field Contactor: (EF): 

Connects battery to main generator field circuit for excitation. 

4. Motor Cut Out Switches: 

These switches are in circuits to traction motor contactor operating coils and are used to isolate traction motors two at a time. Right knife switch cuts out No. 1 and 3 motors. Left knife switch cuts out 

No. 2 and 4 motors. Open switch to cut out motors desired. After cutting motors out, operate with HALF

THROTTLE to prevent excessive heating of other traction motors. 

5. Wheel Slip Relay (WSR) : 

Operates to drop load on engine and light red wheel slip light in event of wheel slippage. 

6. Field Shunting Relays (FS1, FS2): 

Voltage relays operating to provide automatic operation from series parallel to series-parallel-shunt in two successive steps as main generator loads change. 

7. Traction Motor Power Contactors (P1, P2): 

These contactors connect traction motors to the generator. 

8. Voltage Regulator: 

Controls the output of the auxiliary generator at 128 volts regardless of engine speed changes or battery voltage fluctuations. 

9. Battery Charging Contactor (A): 

Establishes circuit of auxiliary generator to battery charging equipment is controlled by reverse current relay. 

10. Reverse Current Relay (RCR): 

Protective relay for auxiliary generator it prevents battery current from flowing into auxiliary generator when the auxiliary generator voltage drops below battery voltage. 

11. Main Battery Switch: 

Connects batteries to all locomotive electrical circuits.

Battery Charging Contactor (BC): 

Connects the auxiliary generator to the battery and the low-voltage control circuits.

Blower Protective Relay (BL): 

If traction motor blower motor current fails, cut off power by de-energizing EF contactor.

Exciter Field Contactor (EF): 

Energizes exciter battery (4-pole) field controlling exciter and hence main generator output. 

Exciter Field Reduced Contactor (Used only with "Low Power" button: (EFR): 

When de-energized by pressing of low power button, opens to insert resistance in exciter battery (4-pole) field circuit. EPS (See TPS): 

Closes when main reservoir air pressure drops to 80 lbs., to energize TLV.

Locomotive Run Switch (LOCO RUN): 

Energizes propulsion control circuits.

Low Oil Switch (in Governor) (LOS): 

Shuts engine down if engine lubricating oil pressure falls too low for engine speed being maintained.
