3716. 

2. Engine Starting Contactors (G1, G2,) 

Establish circuit from batteries to main generator starting field for cranking engine. 

3. Exciter Field Contactor (EF) 

Connects battery to exciter generator field circuit for excitation. 

4. Motor Cut Out Switches. 

Switches in circuits to traction motor contactor operating coils used to isolate traction motors two at a time. Throw switch on motors to be cut out all the way down. After cutting motors out, operate with reduced throttle to prevent excessive heating of other traction motors. Do not open cut out switches until the throttle is in idle and reverse lever in center position. 

5. Generator Field Contactor (GF) 

Connects exciter generator to main generator shunt field. 

6. Wheel Slip Relay (WSR) 

Operates to reduce speed of engine and sound wheel slip buzzer in event of wheel slippage. 

7. Field Shunting Relays (FS1, FS2) (2207 to 2212 inclusive have FS3 in addition) Voltage relays operating to provide automatic field shunting from parallel to parallel shunt in two successive steps (three successive steps on 2207 to 2212 inclusive) as main generator loads change. 

8. Traction Motor Power Contactors (P1, P2) 

These contactors connect the traction motors to the generator. 9. Throttle Switch 

This switch, which is operated by pneumatic throttle control, cuts in the contactor and power switch circuits when changing from idling to running position. 

10. Voltage Regulator. 

Controls the output of the auxiliary generator at 128 volts regardless of engine speed changes or battery voltage fluctuations. 

11. Reverse Current Contactor (A) 

Establishes circuit of auxiliary generator to battery charging equipment-is controlled by reverse current relay. 

12. Reverse Current Relay 

Protective relay for auxiliary generator-it prevents battery current from flowing into auxiliary generator when the auxiliary generator voltage drops below battery voltage.

13. Main Battery Switch 

Connects batteries to all locomotive electrical circuits except cab and electrical equipment cabinet lights. 

14. Ammeter

Ammeter indicates battery charge or discharge. If ammeter shows discharge, condition should be corrected immediately as batteries will become exhausted and locomotive will stop. 

15. Field Shunting Contactors (M1, M2, M3, M4) (2207 to 

2212 inclusive have M5, M6 in addition). 

Connects motor field shunts across traction motor field,

SECTION 6 AIR SYSTEM Air Compressor: 

Each locomotive is equipped with a Westinghouse 3CD air compressor, driven from the main generator shaft extension, to furnish air for the air brake and control air systems. 

Main reservoir air pressure is maintained between 130 to 140 pounds per square inch by a mechanical governor. The control air pressure should be 70 pounds per square inch plus or minus 3 pounds.

Draining the Main Reservoirs: 

Each locomotive is equipped with two main air reservoirs, located underneath the carbody, between the trucks. 

In order to prevent moisture from carrying over into the brake and control air systems, these reservoirs should be drained at each crew change.

Santa Fe 

2260 CLASS AND 625 CLASS BALDWIN LOCOMOTIVES 

PART II-SECTION 18 

2260

GENERAL DESCRIPTION The 2260 class locomotives include Locomotives 2260 

through 2299 and Locomotive 2200. They are powered by 600 series, 6 cylinder, supercharged Diesel engines with direct connected generator. (See Part I, Section 11). These engines are rated at 1000 hp. 

The 625 class locomotives, numbered 625 through 633, are powered by 600 series normally aspirated engine rated at 750 hp. 

The engine and generator is contained within the hood extending in front of the cab of the locomotive with the generator end mounted towards the cab.. Engine speed is controlled by the throttle through levers, and the pneumatic actuator. The air compressor is driven through the generator armature shaft and the traction motor blower for the rear trucks is driven by "V" belt drive from the air compressor shaft. The radiator cooling fan and front truck blower is driven by the "V" belt drive from the engine crankshaft. 

LOCOMOTIVE OPERATION Control Station: 

The control station contains the throttle, reverser, push-button switches, air brake gauges and load meter. There is no transition lever. The traction motors are permanently connected in a parallel hook-up across the main generator. 

The traction motor field shunting is automatic as speed increases. For cab identification see Plate 3800-1.

The push button control box at the control station contains 5-15 amp. cartridge fuse protecting these circuits. In addition a 100 amp. Auxiliary Generator fuse protects the batteries and auxiliary generator. In addition, circuit breakers inserted in vital circuits protects them. 

Instrument Panel: 

The instrument panel on the control stand contains the following: 

1. Air brake gauges. 

2. Load ammeter. 

3. Push button control box. 

4. Throttle and reverse lever. 

Starting the Diesel Engines: 

1. Close main battery switch. 

2. Place throttle in idle position. 

3. Insert reverse lever and keep in "OFF" position.

4. Close control switch. This will also start fuel pump. Fuel pump pressure should build up to 25 psi. minimum. 

5. Press engine start button. If engine does not rotate, release button immediately. If engine does rotate, hold button in until the engine fires and lubricating oil pressure builds up to 20 psi. on gage. Releasing button before oil pressure builds up to 20 psi. will allow engine shut-down device to stop engine. DO NOT continue cranking over 10 seconds as continued cranking will run down the battery. 

6. Check lube oil pressure with engine running.

It should register between 20 psi. and 65 psi. with engine operating at rated speed and normal engine temperature. 

7. Close auxiliary generator switch at control station. 

The battery charging rate should range between 0 to 

28 amp. As the batteries approach full charge, the charging rate will decrease and approach 0. The anmeter should never show a discharge with the engine running. If this condition exists, report it to maintenance forces. 

8. Do not work locomotive until engine temperature reaches 120° F. 

To Move Locomotive; 

1. Make certain main reservoir air pressure is up. 

2. Release hand brake. 

3. Place reverse lever in either "forward" or "reverse" position as desired. 

4. Move throttle slowly from "idle" position until.current shows on load ammeter. When the meter registers current and train starts to move, advance throttle as needed to obtain desired acceleration. 

The throttle should be moved slowly to prevent wheel slip. If wheel slip should occur, the buzzer will sound and Diesel engine speed will automatically be decreased. If wheel slip should reoccur, reduce throttle until wheel slip stops.

Proper Handling of Controls: 

Care must be taken so as to move reverser ONLY when locomotive is not moving. Never move reverse lever while locomotive is in motion, as serious damage to the electrical equipment will result.

When starting a heavy or long cut of cars, open throttle slowly. Due to the high starting tractive effort of the locomotive, the throttle should be opened gradually to avoid rough handling or damage to the locomotive.

When making slow or short moves with the locomotive, avoid opening and closing the throttle excessively, as the power switches close and open with the throttle. This "make and break" of the circuit results in arcing of the power switches and will result in failure of the equipment.

Short Time Rating: 

It is permissible to operate with the load meter indicating various stages of overload as indicated below: 1060 amp. 

Continuously 1200 amp. 40 Minutes 1300 amp. 1400 amp. 

25 Minutes 15 Minutes 

1500 amp. 12 Minutes 10 Minutes 1600 amp. 

1800 amp. 7 Minutes 2000 amp. 5 Minutes At the end of time limit for any valve notch back on the throttle to 1060 amps. or less. Do Not Again Exceed 

1060 amps. For 15 Minutes. 

(Important: Overloading May Result In Serious Damage To The Generator And Traction Motors.) Precautions During Locomotive Operation: 

1. Maintain cooling water temperature betwwen 160° F. and 1700 F. This is done automatically by the automatic shutter control system which will open the shutters at 160° F. and close the shutter at 153° F. 

Engine temperature will, therefore, vary between 153° F. and 170° F. If the engine temperature reaches 

1800 F., inspect shutters to make sure they are open.

If water temperature reaches 1900 F., the locomotive should NOT BE WORKED until cause of high temperature is determined and corrected. Do not shut engine down with high temperature condition existing, let engine idle. 

2. Lubricating oil pressure should normally be 65 psi. at full throttle (625 R.P.M.). If lubricating oil pressure drops below 15 psi. (at 625 R.P.M. hot oil) stop the engine and do not operate until the condition is corrected. 

3. Turbo lube oil pressure should normally vary between 

20 and 30 psi. 

4. Rotate handle on metal edged lube oil strainer two full turns every four hours. 

5. The engine should be operated with the water level above the minimum water level mark, and the engine should be SHUT DOWN if water level drops below this level. 

6. With engine idling, the oil lever should always be between the "high" and "low" marks on the bayonet gauge in the right front corner of crankcase (preferably above half way mark).

Towing Locomotive. 

1. Stop engine, close shutters and drain cooling system if freezing weather prevails. 

2. Place reverser in neutral and remove handle. 

3. Open main, battery switch.

4.

Move reverser drum to neutral position and lock in place. 

5. Close double heading cock. 

6. Cut in dead engine cock. 

7. Set relief valve on distributing valve at 25 pounds. 

8. Lock both brake valves in running position. 

1. Let engine idle for at least 10 minutes. 2. Open the control switch. 

3. Open auxiliary generator switch. 

4. Open main battery switch. 

5. Set hand brake and release air brakes. 

6. Turn off all lights. 

7. Remove reverser handle. 

8. If locomotive will be exposed to freezing temperatures, take necessary precautions. 

TROUBLE SHOOTING Engine Does Not Rotate When Start Button is Pressed: 

1. Control switch open or fuse burned out. 

2. Battery switch open.

If Engine Rotates But Does Not Fire: 

1. Fuel tank empty. 

2. Emergency fuel cut-out valve is tripped. 

3. Overspeed stop has been tripped.

Reset. Reset. 

4. Releasing start button before oil pressure has built up to 20 psi. 

5. Low fuel oil pressure. 

6. Insufficient oil in governor.

Engine Does Not Respond to Throttle Setting: 

1. Low control air pressure: a. Low main reservoir air pressure. b. Control air cut-out cock closed. c. Control air pressure reducing valve sticking. 

Try tapping it lightly. 

2. Governor linkage disconnected.

Locomotive Does Not Move: 

1. Reverser not in forward or reverse position. 

2. Auxiliary generator switch open. 

3. Traction motor cut-out switches open. 

4. Hand brake still set. 

5. Pneumatic throttle switch open. 

6. Check 15 amp. control fuse at control station.

Battery Ammeter Shows Discharge (With Engine Running): 

1. Auxiliary generator switch open. 

2. Auxiliary generator fuse blown. (100 amp fuse). 

3. Poor contacts on battery contactor or reverse current relay. 

NOTE: If batteries are not charging they must have attention quickly to prevent exhaustion.

Engine Stops: 

1. Overspeed stop has tripped. Reset. Notify maintenance forces if it continues to trip. 

2. Low lube oil pressure, causing engine shut-down. device to operate. 

3. Fuel failure. 

Engine Overheats: 

1. Low water level. 

2. Faulty radiator shutter control. If shutters fail to open, operate manual control valve located on stiffening member just inside the left front hood door. 

3. Radiator fans not functioning properly. Check belt drive and belt tension. 

Air Pressure Too High or Too Low: 

1. Compressor governor cut-out cock closed. 

2. Compressor governor sticking. Try tapping it.

SECTION 2 COOLING SYSTEM Description: 

Water is circulated through the engine heat exchanger and cooling radiator by an engine driven water pump. The expansion tank, vented to the atmosphere through an overflow pipe, acts as a reservoir for maintaining a full system. 

The water level in the expansion tank must be maintained above the "low" mark on the gauge. 

Filling System: 

To fill the system add water through connection over right No. 1 truck, until water spills from overflow pipe.

If the engine runs hot due to low water level, idle the engine and add hot water, if possible. If it is necessary to use cold water, it should be added very slowly. 

Draining System: 

If the locomotive is to be left standing shut down and subjected to freezing weather. it will be necessary to drain the cooling system or use steam to prevent freezing. Attach steam line to drain pipe and open drain valve slightly to allow steam to enter system. To drain the system, open drain valve and also the drain cock on the underside of heat exchanger. See Plate 3800-2..

Automatic Temperature Control: 

The cooling water temperature is controlled by automatic shutters which open at 1600 F. and close at 1530 F. If the

temperature rises too high, investigate to determine cause of high engine temperature. Temperature should range from 

1530 to 170° F. If temperatures of 180° F. are experienced, investigate to make sure shutters are open. If not, they can be opened by the manual control valve located on a stiffening member just inside the left front hood door. If temperature rises to 1900 F., the locomotive should not be worked until the cause of high temperature is determined and temperature is reduced. 

SECTION 3  

The lube oil is drawn from the sump formed by the bed plate of the engine, through a suction strainer by the engine driven pump. 

The pump forces oil through an oil filter, a metal edged oil strainer and a heat exchanger. See Plate 3800-3. 

The oil is then delivered to the engine header, which distributes the oil to all parts of the engine requiring lubrication.

Oil may be added by removing cover on filler in crankcase and pouring required amount into the engine. With engine idling, the oil level should always be between the "high" and "low" marks on the bayonet gauge, preferably a above halfway mark.

 When starting a cold engine, allow it to idle for some time and see that pressure starts to build up almost immediately. 

The lubricating oil pressure gauge should normally indicate a pressure of 65 lbs at full throttle (625 R.P.M.) with engine warm. At idle, pressure should be at least 

15 lbs.

With normal operation, the lubricating oil pressure should not drop below 15 lbs with engine running full throttle (625 R.P.M.). 

If Oil Pressure Drops Below 15 lbs. With Full Throttle,

Stop the Engine Until Trouble Is Corrected. 

Lube Oil Pressure Switch: 

The lube oil pressure switch is a protective device which is kept closed by oil pressure, but opens when the lube oil pressure fails from loss of pressure. When this is closed, the shut-down valve SDV is energized, which is necessary to maintain the fuel supply to the engine. When the oil pressure drops, the switch opens and the SDV is de-energized and the engine stops.

SECTION 4 FUEL OIL SYSTEM 

The fuel tank is mounted under the locomotive and has a capacity of 726 gallons. The tank can be filled from either side of the locomotive. Fuel is drawn from the main supply tank, through the emergency cut-off valve and the suction strainers by the motor driven fuel supply pump. The pump then forces the fuel through the pressure filter to the header supplying the injection pumps. A pressure relief valve holds the fuel pressure in the header to 25 psi., bypassing excess fuel back to the supply tank. See Plate 3800-4 

Emergency Fuel Cut Off Valve: 

(See General Section all locomotives) Fuel Pump: 

The fuel oil supply pump is a rotor gear type pump driven by an electric motor through a flexible drive coupling. The fuel pump relief valve holds fuel pressure at 25 psi. 

A drop in this pressure indicates partially clogged fuel filters. 

Engine Shut-Down Device: 

An electrically controlled engine shut-down device protects the Diesel engine against low lube oil pressure. The device is operated by fuel oil pressure and automatically moves the control shaft to the stop position when the protective device functions. 

A three-way magnetic valve is supplied with fuel oil from the fuel supply pump. The valve is energized when the lubricating oil pressure is over 15 psi. The switches in the control circuit are closed at such times. When starting the engine the lube oil pressure switch closes at 19 psi. 

When energized, oil flows through the valve to the shutdown cylinder, releasing the fuel control shaft and allowing it to act according to the demands of the governor. When lube oil pressure falls below 15 psi., the valve is de-energized and oil is released from the shut-down cylinder. A spring in the cylinder then forces the piston and fuel control shaft to the stop position.

When the engine start button is held in, these protective switches are by-passed so that the engine may start. See 

Plate 3800-4.

ELECTRICAL EQUIPMENT CABINET

SECTION 5 ELECTRICAL SYSTEM Electrical Control Cabinet: 

This cabinet is located in the front of the locomotive cab and includes the following: See Plate 3800-5. 

1. Reverse Current Contactor (A): 

Establishes circuit of auxiliary generator to battery charging equipment and controlled by reverse current relay. 

2. Auxiliary Generator Fuse: 

Protects auxiliary generator in case of overload.

3. Voltage Regulator: 

Controls the output of the auxiliary generator at 74 Volts regardless of engine speed changes or battery voltage fluctuations. 

4. Field Shunt Relays (FS1, FS2): 

Voltage relays operating to provide automatic field shunting from parallel to parallel shunt in two successive steps. 

5. Reverse Current Relay (RC): 

Protective relay for auxiliary generator as it prevents battery current from flowing into auxiliary generator when the auxiliary generator voltage drops below battery voltage. 

6. Exciter Field Contactor (EF): 

Connects battery to exciter generator field circuit for excitation. 

7. Wheel Slip Relay (WS1, WS2): 

Operates to reduce speed of diesel engine and sound wheel slip buzzer in event of wheel slippage. 

8. Wheel Slip Valve: 

A contact of the wheel slip relay energizes the wheel slip valve which reduces the speed of the engine during wheel slippage. 

9. Throttle Switch: 

This switch which is operated by pneumatic throttle control, cuts in the contactor and power switch circuits when changing from idling to running position. 

10. Pneumatic Field Switch: 

Operates to cut out soft start feature when throttle is approximately 1/4 open. 

11. Battery Switch: 

Connects batteries to all locomotive electrical circuits except cab and electrical cabinet lights. 

12. Field Shunting Contacters (M1, M2, M3, M4): 

Connects motor field shunts across traction motor fields. 

13. Reverser (REV): 

Changes direction of flow of current through traction motor fields to change direction of locomotive movement. 

14. Ground Relay (GR): 

Class 2260 locomotives are not equipped with ground relays.

15. Engine Starting Contactors (G1, G2): 

Establish circuit from batteries to main generator starting field for cranking engine. 

16. Traction Motor Power Contactors (P1, P2): 

These contactors connect the traction motors to the generator. 

17. Motor Cut Out Switches: 

Switches in circuits to traction motor contactor operating coils used to isolate traction motors two at a time. Throw switch on motors to be cut out all the way down.

After cutting motors out, operate with reduced throttle to prevent excessive heating of other traction motors. DO NOT OPEN CUT OUT SWITCHES UNTIL THE THROTTLE IS IN IDLE AND REVERSE LEVER IN CENTER POSITION: 

SECTION 6 AIR SYSTEM Air Compressor: 

Each locomotive is equipped with a Gardner-Denver WXO air compressor, driven from the main generator shaft extension, to furnish air for the air brake and control air systems. 

Main reservoir air pressure is maintained between 130 to 140 pounds per square inch by a mechanical governor. The control air pressure should be 70 pounds per square inch plus or minus 3 pounds.

Draining the Main Reservoirs: 

Each locomotive has two main air reservoirs located under the car body between the trucks. 

In order to prevent moisture from being carried over into the air brake and control air systems, these reservoirs should be drained at the time of each crew change.

2300 and 2310 CLASS 600 and 1000 H. P. ALCO 

PART II-SECTION 19

The 2300 class of Diesel-electric locomotives comprises locomotives 2300, 2301, 2302 and the 2310 class comprises locomotives 2310 to 2321, inclusive. They were manufactured by the American Locomotive Company and are powered by an Alco 6-cylinder, 4-cycle Diesel engine. (See Part I, Section 6). 

The cab is located in the back of the locomotive and the hood extends toward the front. The left and right sides of the locomotive are to the left and right when sitting in the cab and facing front.

The Diesel engine is mounted in the locomotive with the generator in front and the accessory end of the engine toward the rear of the locomotive. In front of the cab are located the oil cooling radiator, air compressor, traction motor blower and radiator cooling fan.

These locomotives have one 6-cylinder supercharged 1000 hp. engine (2300, 2301 and 2302 are rated at 600 hp. and have no supercharger), a direct current generator and four traction motors. From the power plant the power is distributed to the traction motors which are mounted on the trucks. The traction motors are geared to the axle through spur gears. The engine has a speed range of 340 to 740 r.p.m. (350 to 700 r.p.m. on locomotives 2300, 2301 and 2302) which is controlled directly by the main throttle through a system of levers and rods. 

Starting the engine is accomplished by pressing the engine start button located at the control station. This switch energizes the starting contactors, closing the circuit from the batteries to the main generator. The main generator, then operating as a motor, cranks the engine. 

LOCOMOTIVE OPERATION Control Station: 

The control station contains the locomotive throttle for controlling engine speed and selective reversing controller for reversing locomotive direction and controlling transition manually. This selective reversing control ler has seven positions: Series, Series-Parallel and Re duced-Field for each direction of movement and Off. Limitation to Series-Parallel or to Series connection of traction motors may be made by keeping the controller handle in the desired position. The control push button box on the left of the controller has fused switches for:

To Move Locomotive: 

1. Close air compressor control switch and make certain main reservoir pressure is up. 

2. Release hand brake. 

3. Close traction motor blower switch on control push button box in cab. 

4. Turn controller handle from Off to one of its three positions in the direction of movement desired. These positions are: a. Series low speeds. 

b. Series-parallel 

intermediate speeds. c. Reduced-field high speeds. 

5. Pull back on throttle handle. Movement of the throttle handle should be steady and gradual enough to move load without slipping the wheels: Caution: In moving the locomotive do not pump the throttle handle back and forth as this will open and close the contactors and other electrical equipment which must make or break circuits each time the throttle is shut off or opened. A movement of the throttle handle of approximately 1/2 in. will close the control switch on the throttle shaft, thus closing the control circuit, allowing the power output of the generator to be delivered to the traction motors. 

This term applies to the changing of the traction motor electrical connections from series to series-parallel to reduced field. This is done to obtain the desired tractive effort and speed within the voltage operating limit of the generator. 

To obtain maximum tractive effort from the motors when the locomotive is at low speed, they are connected in series. 

As their speed increases, the generator voltage increases until the transition relay closes, operating the traction motor contactors, connecting the motors in series-parallel if reversing controller handle is in series-parallel position. 

This reduces the generator voltage. 

When train speed decreases to 5 m.p.h. on 2300, 2301, 2302 and 7 m.p.h. on 2310 to 2321, inclusive, due to an increase in grade (if no throttle change is made) the generator current will rise to a point where it will be necessary to shift to series manually in order to prevent generator overload. The throttle must then be reduced to quarter speed or less and the selective-reversing controller handle moved to "series" which places the motors back in their series position. The throttle then can be moved to the desired position.

Engine Overspeed Trip: 

(See Part I, Section 6). 

Towing Locomotive: 

1. Stop engine and drain radiating system. 

2. Pull main battery switch. 

3. Place controller in neutral position. 

4. Close doubling heading cock under brake valve. 

5. Cut in dead engine cock at distributing valve. 

6. Set relief valve on distributing valve at 25 pounds. 

7. Lock both brake valves in running position. 

Precautions During Locomotive Operation: 

1. Maintain engine cooling water temperature at 160° F. plus or minus 100 F. by controlling speed of radi ator fan. If Engine Cooling Water Temperature Cannot be Maintained at or Below 1870 F.

by Reducing Throttle 

Shut Engine Down and Do Not Operate Until Cause of Trouble is Corrected. 

2. Lubricating oil pressure should be approximately 

35 lb. If Lubricating Oil Pressure Drops Below 20 Pounds Stop the Engine and Do Not Operate Until Con dition is Corrected.

1. At control station: a. Close throttle to idle position. b. Place selective reversing controller in off position and remove handle. c. Move handle of 3 position valve to "stop" position and after engine stops return handle to starting position. d. Open control switch. e. Open fuel pump switch. f. Open main battery switch. 

TROUBLE SHOOTING If Fuel Pressure Does Not Show on Gauge in Cab: 

1. If fuel pump does not run. a. Check all fuses in circuit. (1) 60 amp. fuse in electrical cabinet. (2) Fuel pump fuse (10 amp.) in control stand. b. Check fuel pump switch at controller. 

2. If motor is running but no fuel is being delivered: a. Check pump couplings and shaft. b. Check fuel supply and emergency fuel cut-off valve. c. Check for air leaks in suction line due to broken pipe or loose connection.

If Engine Rotates But Does Not Fire When Start Button is 

Pressed: 

1. Check cylinder test plugs at each cylinder, making sure that they are closed. 

2. Check fuel pump linkage in event that it may be stuck in shutdown position. 

3. Check to see that overspeed trip shaft (marked Reset

Handle) is latched in position for running with "Emergency Stop Handle" (on the governor end of the fuel pump drive casing) set in the vertical position. 

4. Check fuel pressure.

If Locomotive Does Not Move When Throttle Is Opened: 

1. Check hand brake. 

2. Repeat movement of brake valve to release brakes. 

3. Check to see that control switch at control station is closed and fuse good. 

4. Check control air pressure and see that reverser is in proper position. 

5. Check 10 amp. control fuse opposite control switch on controller. 

6. The generator or exciter field contactors might be open or making poor contact. 

7. If starting contactor sticks shut the generator will not deliver power.

If Locomotive Stops in Operation: 

If locomotive suddenly becomes inoperative, immediately check the control fuse in the box on the controller. A wire may have jarred loose or may be burned off. The fuel supply may have stopped.

SECTION 2 COOLING SYSTEM Description: 

This Water is drawn from the radiators by a centrifugal water pump located on the left back corner of the engine. pump circulates water to the cylinder block then up through the cored passages of the cylinder head and out through the outlet manifolds. From the engine, the water flows to two

groups of radiator sections located on the side of the locomotive body. Here the water is cooled returning to the water pump. 

Filling Cooling System: 

The cooling system may be filled through the filler pipe located along the right and left sides of the fuel tank. (Do not put water in the exhaust stack.) Fill the cooling system until water runs out the filler pipe. It is best to have the engine idling when system is being filled, to remove air pockets. 

Add water very slowly if engine is hot and then only when engine is idling. Do not add cold water to a hot engine that has just been drained as this will crack and warp cylinder heads and liners.

Specially treated water should be used in the cooling system to offset the accumulation of scale or foreign matter which contributes to pitting and overheating the engine. 

The water level should be maintained above the minimum water level mark (3/4 Water Glass) And The Engine Should Not Be Operated With Water Below This Level. 

Checking Circulation: 

The water temperature thermometer located on the engine instrument panel in cab may be used as a means of detecting an irregularity in the operation of the cooling system. High water temperatures indicate: a. Electric motor drive cooling fan not working properly. b. Insufficient water in system. c. Faulty circulation.

If Cooling Water Temperature Cannot Be Maintained At Or Below 1870 F. By Reducing Throttle Stop Engine And Do Not Operate Until Cause of Trouble Is Corrected. 

If the water is to be drained, open drain valve near No. 1 cylinder. Fans: 

The cooling fan is located in the compartment in back of the engine compartment and is driven by an electric motor. 

The speeds of the fan motor are controlled by three push button switches on control push button box in cab marked Rad. fan Slow, Rad. fan - Medium and Rad. fan Fast. Speed of motor is determined by cooling air necessary to hold engine water temperature between 1500 F. and 170° F. The air enters the radiator on the side and goes up through the wind tunnel to the roof.

Cab Temperature Control: 

The cab heater is operated with electrical resistors.

SECTION 3  Description: 

The lubricating oil is stored in the engine sump which classifies the engine as a wet sump type. The system is a pressure system using a positive displacement pump. The oil supply to this pump is drawn from the bottom of the sump. 

Oil may be added by removing crankcase cover and pouring required amount into the engine. With the engine idling, the oil level should always be between the "high" and "low" marks on the bayonet gauge in the right center of crankcase (preferable above halfway mark).

When starting a cold engine, allow it to idle for some time and see that pressure starts to build up almost immediately. 

The pressure should rise on a cold engine to about 50 lb. and will run approximately 35 lb. with engine warm. If lubricating oil pressure drops below 20 Lb. Per Sq. In., Stop The

Engine And Do Not Operate Until Condition Is Corrected. 

Low Oil Pressure Shut Down: 

The starting and stopping of the engine is controlled by a three-way control valve in the cab through a pilot valve mounted on the lubricating oil pump end of the engine. The engine is stopped by admitting air to a piston in the governor which is held in the "Run" position by a combined spring and hydraulic pressure. This causes the governor to force the fuel pump racks back into the "Stop" position. As the governor is actuated by the governor oil pressure, the fuel pumps are automatically pulled into the "Stop" position as soon as the governor stops turning.

When starting the Diesel engine, the lever in the cab is placed in the "Start" position. This shuts off all air to the valve on the engine and allows the oil pressure to force the piston up, closing the valve seat at the top. The lever in the cab is then placed in the "Run" position, allowing the air to flow to the top of the valve on the engine. Then, If 

There Is A Loss Of Oil Pressure, The Air At The Top Of The Pilot Valve On The Engine Will Force The Piston Down Opening A Port And Allowing The Air To Shut The Engine Down.

A fuel tank is mounted under the cab floor with a capacity of 625 gal. (400 gal. on 2300 class). At the bottom of the tank is a sump from which the fuel pump draws fuel oil through the suction line and suction filter. Fuel can only be drawn from the supply sump if the emergency cut-off valve is open. After leaving the pump, fuel is pumped through the filters to the fuel pumps. The fuel pumps use only a part of the fuel pumped to them.

Emergency Fuel Cut Off Valve: 

(See General Section all locomotives). 

Fuel Pump: 

The fuel pump assembly consists of an electric motor and pump of the rotor gear type. The gear pump is driven by the motor shaft through a flexible coupling. Do not leave the fuel pump running unnecessarily.

 The Electrical Cabinet In Front Of Cab Includes The Following: 

1. Reverser 

(Rev). Changes direction of flow of current through traction motor fields to change direction of locomotive movement. 

2. Engine Starting Contactor (GS1-GS2). 

Establishes circuit from batteries to main generator starting field for cranking Diesel engines. 

3. Exciter Field Contactor (EF). Connects battery to exciter generator field circuit for excitation.

4. Motor Cut-Out Switches (MCO1, MCO2). Switches in cir cuits to traction motor contactor operating coils used to isolate traction motors two at a time. Throw switch on motors to be cut out all the way over. 

After cutting motors out, Operate With One-Half Throttle, to prevent excessive heating of other traction motors. Do not open cut out switches until the throtle is in idle and reverse lever in center position. 

5. Generator Field Contactor (GF). Connects exciter generator to main generator shunt field.

6. Transition Relay (V). A voltage relay operating to provide automatic transition from series to seriesparallel to series-parallel-shunt as main generator loads change. 

7. Traction Motor Power Contactors (S1, S2, SP1, SP2): 

These contactors change traction motor to generator connections from series to series-parallel or vice versa. 

8. Voltage Regulator. Controls the output of the aux iliary generator at 128 volts regardless of engine speed changes or battery voltage fluctuations. 

9. Battery Charging Contactor (B). Establishes circuit of auxiliary generator to battery charging equipment is controlled by reverse current relay. 

10. Reverse Current Relay (RCR). Protective relay for auxiliary generator it prevents battery current 

from flowing into auxiliary generator when the auxiliary generator voltage drops below battery voltage. 

11. Main Battery Switch. Connects batteries to all locomotives electrical circuits. 

12. Battery Ammeter. Ammeter indicates battery charge or discharge. If ammeter shows discharge, condition should be corrected immediately as batteries will become exhausted and locomotive will stop. 

13. Load Ammeter. 

The load ammeter is located at the control station and the scale is divided into two colored sec tions as follows:

15. Field Shunting Contactors (M1, M2). Connects resistors in parallel with motor fields for higher locomotive speeds.

OPERATION OF RELAYS AND CONTACTORS DURING TRANSITIONS BETWEEN 

Series. Series-parallel Series-parallel-shunt 

Low Speeds

Intermediate Speeds .High Speeds

Series to Series-parallel (approximately 7 m.p.h.) 

1. Transition Relay V closes due to main generator voltage reaching 810 volts. 

2. CR1 relay closes through contact on V relay. 

3. GF contactor opens through contact on CR1 relay. 

4. V relay opens due to drop in main generator voltage. 5. SP1 contactor closes through contact on GF. 

6. S2 opens through interlock on SP1. 

7. SP2 contactor closes through interlock on S2. 8. GF contactor closes. 

Series-parallel to Series-parallel-shunt (approximately 19 m. p. h.) 

1. V relay closed by main generator voltage at 810 volts. 

2. M1 and M2 contactors closed by contact on V relay.

Series-parallel-shunt to Series-parallel (approximately 15 m. p. h.) 

1. V relay opens as main generator voltage drops to 575 volts. 

2. M1 and M2 contactors open through contact on V relay.

Series-parallel to Series (approximately 7 m.p.h.) 

1. CR1 relay opens by moving selective-reversingcontroller handle to Series position. 

2. SP1 and SP2 contactors open when CR1 relay opens. 3. GF contactor opens. 

4. S2 contactor closes through SP1 interlock. 

5. GF contactor closes through S2 interlock.

SECTION 6 AIR SYSTEM Air Compressor: 

D2301 and 2302 are each equipped with one Westinghouse 

D4P Air Compressor D2300 and 2310 to 2321 incl. are each equipped with a General Electric CP26 motor driven air compressor, to supply air for the air brake and control air systems. 

Each locomotive has two main air reservoirs located beneath the carbody, between the trucks. 

In order to prevent moisture from being carried over into the air brake and control air systems, these reservoirs should be drained at time of each crew change. 

Main reservoir's air pressure is maintained between 130 to 140 pounds per square inch and the control air pressure should be 70 pounds per square inch plus or minus 3 pounds.

PART II-SECTION 20

The 2303 class of Diesel-electric locomotive comprises locomotives 2303 and 2304, and the 2322 class comprises locomotives 2322-2391-1500 up. They were manufactured by 

The American Locomotive Company and are powered by an Alco 

6-cylinder 4-cycle Diesel engine. (See Part 1, Section 6). 

The 2303 class have no super-charger and are rated at 660 hp.; 

2322 class have a super-charger and are rated at 1000 hp. The 

2394 class locomotives (2394-2399) are much the same but have steam generators in the rear end of the locomotive. See 

Part 111, Section 2. 

The cab is located in the back of the locomotive and the hood extends toward the front. The left and right sides of the locomotive are to the left and right when sitting in the cab and facing front.

The Diesel engine is mounted in the locomotive with the generator in front of the cab and the front (or lube oil pump) end of the engine toward the front of the locomotive. 

In front of the Diesel engine are located the oil cooling radiator, traction motor blower and radiator cooling fan. 

The air compressor is located behind the main generator. 

These locomotives have one 6-cylinder engine, a direct current generator and four、traction motors. From the power plant the power is distributed to the traction motors which are mounted on the trucks. The traction motors are geared to the axle through spur gears. The engine has a speed range of 

275 to 740 r.p.m. which is controlled by the main throttle through a system of levers and rods. 

Starting the engine is accomplished by pressing the eagine start button located at the control station. This switch energizes the starting contactors, closing the circuit from the batteries to the main generator. The main generator, then operating as a motor, cranks the engine. 

LOCOMOTIVE OPERATION Control Station: 

The control station contains the locomotive throttle and selective-reversing controller for reversing locomotive direction and controlling transition manually. This_selectivereversing controller has seven positions: Series, Series 

Parallel and Reduced-Field for each direction of movement, and Off. Limitation to Series-Parallel or to Series connec tion of traction motors may be made by keeping the controller handle in the desired position.

Starting Engine:

5. Pull back on throttle handle. Movement of the throttle handle should be steady and gradual enough to move load without slipping the wheels: Caution: In moving the locomotive do not pump the throttle handle back and forth as this will open and close the contactors and other electrical equipment which must make or break circuits each time the throttle is shut off or opened. A movement of the throttle handle of approximately 1/2 in. will close the control switch on the throttle shaft, thus closing the control circuit, allowing the power output of the generator to be delivered to the traction motors. 

Operating Selector and Throttle Handles: 

The current limit relay lights the generator overload light when the generator current reaches 900 amperes and 700 amperes on 2303 class. If in Series-Parallel The Throttle 

Should Then Be Closed to the One-Quarter Position or Less and 

Push the Selective-Reversing Controller Handle to "Series".

The traction motors are then in series instead of series-parallel.

Operation of  

The wheel slip buzzer indicates wheel slippage. When the wheel slip buzzer works, sand should be applied After the Throttle has Been Reduced and the buzzer has stopped. 

Proper Handling of Controls: 

1. The controller has three positions. "Series", "Series-Parallel" and "Reduced-Field". Only one control lever is used for forward or reverse and for connecting traction motors to main generator. 

2. Transition of main contactors from "Series" to "Series-Parallel" to "Shunt" is automatic upward when control lever is placed in "Reduced-Field" position (third notch from off position) and automatic backward from "Reduced-Field" to "Series-Parallel". 

3. Transition of main contactors from "Series" to "Series-Parallel" is automatic upward when control lever is placed in "Series-Parallel" position (second notch from off position). 

4. Since transition from "Series-Parallel" to "Series" is not automatic it is necessary to close throttle to idle position or reduce to about 1/4 throttle and shift manually from "Series-Parallel" to "Series". 

Proper time to shift from "Series-Parallel" to "Series" is indicated by burning of overload light

SECTION 2 COOLING SYSTEM Description: 

The engine and radiators are full at all times and the water is circulated through the system by a centrifugal pump driven from the fuel pump drive gear on the left back corner of the engine. The water pump pulls water from the radiators into the block of the engine where it surrounds the cylinder liners and circulates up through the inner passages in the heads and passes into the outlet manifold which connects to the radiators. An expansion tank is provided behind the radiators and a connecting line from the outlet manifold feeds into the tank. On locomotives with turbo-chargers water is piped from the top of the water pump to the turbo-charger. The water cools the turbo-charger and returns to the radiators. 

The expansion tank, which is near the roof in the front of the engine compartment, is provided with a water glass to indicate level of water in tank. The water should be maintained above the minimum water level mark (3/4 Full) on this glass at all times. If Water Goes Below This Mark Shut Engine Down.

Fill Cooling System: 

The cooling system contains approximately 240 gal. (220 gal. on 2303 class) and may be filled through the filler pipe located along the left side of the locomotive near back side of front truck or through the filler pipe extension on the

Cab Temperature Control: 

The cab heater operates on hot water from the engine cooling system and is equipped with a motor driven fan. 

SECTION 3  Description: 

The lubricating oil is stored in the engine sump which classifies the engine as a wet sump type. The system is a pressure system using a positive displacement pump. The oil supply to this pump passes through screens in the bottom of the sump. 

Oil may be added by removing cover on left front corner of crankcase and pouring required amount into the engine. 

With the engine idling, the oil level should always be between the "High" and "Low" marks on the bayonet gauge in the left center of crankcase (Preferably Above Halfway Mark): 

When starting a cold engine, allow it to idle for some time and see that pressure starts to build up almost immediately. 

The pressure should rise on a cold engine to about 50 lb. and will run approximately 35 Lb. with engine warm. If the lubricating oil pressure drops below 20 lb. Per Square Inch

Stop The Engine and Do Not Operate Until Condition is Cor rected.

Low Oil Pressure Shut Down: 

If the lubricating oil pressure should fail the engine will be stopped by a low oil pressure shut-down switch which opens when the lubricating oil pressure drops below 20 Lb. 

Per Square Inch. When this switch opens it de-energizes the solenoid on the engine governor allowing the spring on the solenoid armature to raise. This releases the pressure that the armature has been holding downward on the governor dump valve. Governor oil pressure under this valve then raises it and allows any oil below the power piston to pass to the top of the power piston and then to the governor oil-sump tank. 

With the pressure equalized on the top and the bottom of the governor power piston, this power piston is forced downward by its heavy spring and, through levers, it pulls the high pressure injection-pump control racks inward toward the engine. These racks will than shut off fuel to the nozzles and the Diesel engine will stop. 

A fuel tank is mounted under the cab floor with a capacity of 635 gal. At the bottom of the tank is a sump from which the fuel pump draws fuel oil through the suction line and suction filter. Fuel can only be drawn from the supply sump if the emergency cut-off valve is open. After leaving the pump, fuel is pumped through filters to the fuel pumps.

The fuel pumps use only a part of the fuel pumped to them. 

Emergency Fuel Cut Off: Valve: (See General Section All Loco motives)

Fuel Pump: 

The fuel pump assembly consists of an electric motor and pump of the rotor gear type. It is located in right back corner of electrical cabinet. The gear pump is driven by the motor shaft through a flexible coupling. Do not leave the fuel pump running unnecessarily.

The Electrical Cabinet in Front of Cab Includes the following: 

1. Reverser (RKV) 

Changes direction of flow of current through traction motor fields to change direction of locomotive movement. 

2. Engine Starting Contactors (GS1, GS2) 

Establish circuit from batteries to main generator starting field for cranking engine. 

3. Exciter Field Contactor (EF) 

Connects battery to exciter generator.

field circuit for excitation. 

4. Generator Field Contactor (GF)

Connects exciter generator to main generator shunt field. 

5. Ground Relay (GR) 

Protective relay to drop load on engine if ground occurs in high voltage system. If this relay picks up, bring the engine to idle and reset the relay by raising latch on top of relay. If the relay repeatedly opens when engine speed is increased the locomotive should not be operated. 

6. Wheel Slip Relay (WS1, WS2) 

Operates to sound wheel slip buzzer in event of wheel slippage. 

7. Transition Relay (V) 

A voltage relay operating to provide automatic transition from series to series-parallel to shunt as main generator loads change. 

8. Overload Relay (CL) 

Protective relay for main generator. It operates to light generator overload indicator when load on main generator is excessive so that backward transition may be made or decrease throttle to reduce generator output to within limits of generator. 

9. Traction Motor Power Contactors (SP1, S, SP2) 

These contactors change traction motor to generator connections from series to series-parallel or vice versa. 

10. Control Relay (CR1) 

Used in connection with transition relay to provide automatic transition from series to series parallel. 

11. Voltage Regulator 

Controls the output of the auxiliary generator at a

constant voltage regardless of engine speed changes or battery voltage fluctuations. 

12. Battery Charging Contactor (BC) 

Establishes circuit of auxiliary generator to battery charging equipment-is controlled by reverse current relay. 

13. Reverse Current Relay (RCR) 

Protective relay. for auxiliary generator-it prevents battery current from flowing into auxiliary generator when the auxiliary generator voltage drops below battery voltage. 

14. Main Battery Switch 

Connects batteries to all locomotive electrical circuits. 

15. Field Shunting Contactors (M1, M2)

Connects resistors in parallel with motor fields for higher locomotive speeds.

SECTION 6 AIR SECTION Air Compressor: 

Each locomotive is equipped with one Westinghouse 3 CD air compressor to furnish air for the air brake and control air systems.

Draining Main Air Reservoirs: 

Each locomotive is equipped with two main air reservoirs located underneath the carbody between the trucks. 

In order to prevent moisture from being carried over into the air brake and control air systems, these reservoirs should be drained at time of each crew change. 

Main reservoir air pressure is maintained between 130 to 140 pounds per square inch and the control air pressure should be 70 pounds per square inch plus or minus 3 pounds.

PART II-SECTION 21 

A.T.& S.F 2400

The 2400 class locomotive comprises locomotives 2400 

to 2402 inclusive and were manufactured by the Electro-Motive 

Division of General Motors Corp., powered by 12-201A engine. 

The cab is located in the back of the locomotive and the hood extends toward the front. The left and right sides of the locomotive are to the left and right when sitting in the cab and facing front.

The engine is mounted in the locomotive with the blower end of the engine in front of the cab and the generator end toward the front of the locomotive. The radiators, air conpressor and cooling fans are located in front of generator. 

The engine has a speed range of 275 to 750 R.P.M. which is directly controlled by the main throttle through a system of rods.

LOCOMOTIVE OPERATION Control Station:

Instrument Panels: 

1. The instrument panel in front of controller contains the following indicators: a. Duplex air gauge indicating brake cylinder pressure. b. Duplex air gauge indicating main reservoir and equalizing reservoir pressure. c. Engine cooling water temperature gauge. d. Wheel slip light.

2. The engine instrument panel located to the left of the shutter control lever. a. Tachometer. b. Fuel pressure gauge. c. Lubricating oil pressure gauge. d. Control air pressure gauge. e. Engine shut down rod. 

Starting Engine; 

1. In low voltage cabinet: a. Be sure all fuses are in place. b. Close main battery switch. 

2. At control station: a. Put reverse lever in center position. b. Put throttle in idle position. c. Close control push button switch. d. Close fuel pump switch and watch for pressure on gauge at engine instrument panel. Return pressure should be 5 to 10 pounds per square inc e. Press in engine "start" button and hold until engine starts. Do not hold button in for more than 10 seconds at a time. f. Check lubricating oil pressure. This should be 25 to 40 pounds per square inch. Water temperature should be 1250 F. before locomotive is worked. 

To Move Locomotive, 

1. Make certain main reservoir pressure is up. 

2. Release hand brake. 

3. Close exciter field switch at control station. 

4. Place reverse lever in forward or reverse position. 

5. Pull back on throttle handle. Movement of the throttle handle should be steady and gradual enough to move load without slipping the wheels. Caution: In moving the locomotive do not pump the throttle handle back and forth as this will open and close the contactors and other electrical equipment which must make or break circuits each time the throttle is shut off or opened. A movement of the throttle handle of approximately 1/2" will close the throttle switch on the control shaft, thus closing the control circuit, allowing the power output of generator to be delivered to the traction motors. 

Operation of  

Whenever any pair of wheels slip on either truck the indicator on the instrument panel will light. To stop the slippage, ease off on the throttle.

If necessary to apply sand reduce the throttle until the wheel slip light stops flashing. It should be remembered that sand is injurious to the traction motors and associated apparatus.

During transition from series to series-parallel the wheel slip relay will pick up causing wheel slip light to flash. This is not an indication the wheels are slipping. 

The wheel slip relay will give wheel slip indication for series connection only.

Towing Locomotive: 

1. Stop engine and drain cooling system if freezing weather will be encountered. 

3. Place reverser handle in "off" position and remove handle. 

4. Cut in dead engine cock located below cab floor. 

5. Close double heading cock located below automatic brake valve. 

6. Place handle of automatic and independent brake valves in running position. 

7. Set relief valve on distributing valve at 25 lbs. 

8. See that radiator shutters are closed.

Precautions During Locomotive Operation: 

1. Maintain engine cooling water temperature 150° to 180° preferably 1650 whenever possible. DO NOT EXCEED 2000.

This is accomplished by proper manipulation of shutter control handle in the cab. If engine cooling water temperature can not be maintained AT OR BELOW 2000 by reducing the throttle STOP ENGINE and do not operate until cause of excessive temperature is corrected. 

2. Lubricating oil pressure should be approximately 28 pounds at FULL THROTTLE (750 R.P.M.). If main bearing pressure drops below 15 POUNDS (at 750 R.P.M. hot oil) 

stop engine and DO NOT OPERATE ENGINE UNTIL CONDITION 

IS CORRECTED. 

1. At control station: a. Close throttle to idle position. b. Place reverse lever in neutral position and remove handle. c. Open exciter field switch. d. Pull out on engine shut down rod and hold until engine stops. e. Open control switch. f. Open fuel pump switch. 

2. At engine: 

a. Open cylinder test valves.

TROUBLE SHOOTING 

If Fuel Pressure does not Show on Gauge in Cab: 

1. If fuel pump does not run: a. Check all fuses in circuit. 

1. 70 amp. fuse in low voltage cabinet. 

2. Fuel pump fuse (15 amp.) in control stand. b. Check fuel pump switch at controller. c. Check electric connector at fuel pump motor. 

2. If motor is running but no fuel is being delivered: a. Check pump couplings and shaft. b. Check fuel supply and emergency fuel cut-off valve. c. Check for air leaks in suction line due to broken pipe or loose connection. d. Turn handle on fuel purolator several turns. Purolator is located above fuel pump. e. Place duplex fuel filter handle in vertical position on filter at fuel pump and filter at engine. 

If Engine Does Not Rotate When Attempt Is Made to Start: 

1. Check to see that main battery switch is closed, reverse lever in neutral, throttle closed and control switch in. 

2. Check all fuses affecting starting circuit. a. Starting fuse. This is the 400 amp. fuse in the low voltage cabinet. b. 70 amp. fuse in low voltage cabinet. c. 15 amp. control fuse. This fuse is opposite the control push button switch on the controller. d. 15 amp. starting fuse. This fuse is opposite the engine start push button switch on the controller. 

3. Battery may be too weak to turn engine over. 

4. Check to see that both starting contactors go in.

If Engine Rotates But Does Not Fire When Start Button is 

Pressed: 

1. Check cylinder test valves at each cylinder making sure that they are closed. 

2. Check injector linkage in event that it may be stuck in shut down position. 

3. Check to see that reset on overspeed governor is latched down. 

4. Check fuel pressure and supply. 

5. Check to see that engine shut-down rod is in extreme forward position.

If Locomotive Does Not Move When Throttle is Opened: 

1. Check hand brake. 

2. Repeat movement of brake valve to release brake. 

3. Check to see that exciter field switch at control station is closed. 

4. Check control air pressure and see that reverser is in proper position.

5. Check to see that ground relay is set in normal position. 

6. Check all fuses. a. 70 amp. control fuse in low voltage cabinet. b. 15 amp. control fuse opposite control switch on controller. c. 15 amp. exciter field fuse at control stand. 

7. The generator or exciter field contactors might be open or making poor contact.

1. Check all fuses in circuit affected. a. 30 amp. auxiliary generator field fuse in low voltage cabinet. 

2. Auxiliary generator drive belts might be loose or broken. In this case the condition should be reported. 

3. 100 amp. auxiliary generator fuse.

If Locomotive Stops in Operation: 

If locomotive suddenly becomes inoperative, immediately check the control fuse (70 ampere) in the low voltage cabinet, then check the control fuse in the box on the controller. A wire may have jarred loose or may be burned off. The fuel supply may have stopped. 

If Engine Fails to Stop (by pulling engine shut-down rod): 

1. Pull governor control linkage on the engine to "OFF" position manually, holding until engine stops, or 

2. Pull up on overspeed governor trip, or 

3. Shut off fuel pump by opening fuel pump switch, or 

4. Trip emergency fuel valve, located beside control stand and on each side of locomotive near center. 

SECTION 2 COOLING SYSTEM Description: 

Water is drawn from the oil cooler by a centrifugal water pump located on the front end of the engine. This pump circulates water to the bottom of each cylinder liner up through the cored passages of the cylinder liner and cylinder head and out through the outlet manifolds. From the engine the water flows to two groups of radiator sections located under the roof of the locomotive body. Here the water is cooled returning through the oil cooler to the water pump. 

Filling Cooling System: 

The cooling system is filled through the filler pipe along left side over rear truck or through filler pipe extension on roof. (Do not put water in the exhaust stacks.)

Fill system until water runs over top. It is best to have engine idling when filling to remove air pockets. Keep winter level valve closed unless engine is shut down and steam is being used to keep engine from freezing. Add water very slowly if engine is hot and then only when engine is idling. Do not add cold water to a hot engine that has just been drained. This will crack and warp cylinder heads and liners.

Specially treated water should be used in the cooling system to offset the accumulation of scale and foreign matter which contributes to pitting and overheating the engine. 

Checking Circulation: 

The water temperature gauge located on the engine instrument panel in cab may be used as a means of detecting an irregularity in the operation of the cooling system. High water temperatures indicates: a. Shutters not open wide enough. b. Insufficient water in System. c. Faulty circulation. d. Cooling fans not operating.

There is no danger of overheating due to insufficient water if the water is not allowed to go below the minimum level mark on the water tank. This minimum level mark provides that the water glass on the water tank NEVER BE LESS 

THAN 3/4 FULL AND THE ENGINE SHOULD NOT BE OPERATED WITH WATER LEVEL BELOW THAT POINT: 

If water is to be drained open drain valve at oil cooler, remove plugs from exhaust elbows and open petcock on bottom of water pump. If steam is used, winter level valve should be left open so water will not build up in radiators.

SECTION 3  Description: 

The lubricating oil is stored in the lube oil supply tank, instead of the engine sump which classifies the engine as a dry sump type. The system is a pressure system using a positive displacement pump. The oil supply to this pump is drawn through strainers in the bottom of the supply tank then through a suction pipe to the oil pump.

A scavenging oil pump is used to draw oil from the

engine sump through a strainer, pump it through the oil filters to the oil cooler and return it to the supply tank. 

Oil may be added by removing cover on supply tank and pouring required amount through strainer basket. With the engine idling, the oil level should always be between the "high" and "low" marks (preferably above the halfway mark) on the bayonet gauge in the supply tank. 

 When starting a cold engine, allow it to idle for some time and see that pressure starts to build up almost immediately. 

The pressure should rise on a cold engine to about 

40 LBS. AND will run approximately 28 LBS. AT FULL THROTTLE 

(750 R.P.M.) with engine warm. At idle, pressure should be at least 15 POUNDS.

With normal operation, the lubricating oil pressure should not drop below 15 LBS. PER SQUARE INCH with engine running FULL THROTTLE (750 R.P.M.). 

IF OIL PRESSURE DROPS BELOW 15 LBS. PER SQ. IN. STOP THE ENGINE AND DO NOT OPERATE ENGINE UNTIL CONDITION IS 

CORRECTED. 

Low Oil Pressure Shut Down: 

If the lubricating oil pressure should fail the engine will be returned to idle by a low oil pressure shut-down device, and opening the throttle will have no effect on engine speed. This is accomplished by a cylinder containing a piston shaft. One side of the piston is subjected to engine oil pressure and the other side mechanical spring pressure. When the force of the spring overcomes the force of the oil, the piston moves backward closing the throttle shaft to idle position. A spring loaded rod in the throttle linkage permits this action regardless of the position of the trottle. The throttle starts to close at 18 lbs. and when the pressure drops to 9 lbs. the engine speed is reduced to idle. 

A fuel tank is mounted under the center of the locomotive. The fuel pump draws fuel from the sump of this tank through the suction line and suction filter. Fuel can only be drawn from the supply sump if the emergency cut-off valve is open. Leaving the fuel pump fuel is pumped through the duplex Nugent and sintered bronze filters to the injectors.

The injectors use only a part of the fuel pumped through them. The surplus fuel oil lubricates and cools the internal mechanism of the injectors and then returns to the fuel tank through the 5 pound relief valve.

Emergency Fuel Cut Off Valve (See General Sect. all Loco motives)

Fuel Pump: 

The fuel pump assembly consists of an electric motor and a pump of the rotor gear type. This pump is a pressure pump. The gear pump is driven by the motor shaft through a flexible coupling. Do not leave the fuel pump running unnecessarily. 

 Traction Motor Field Shunting Contactors: (M1-M2) 

These contactors connect the shunting resistors across the traction motor fields when the generator voltage, in series-parallel motor connection rises high enough to operate transition relay (TR2).

Voltage Regulator: 

The voltage regulator maintains the auxiliary generator voltage at a constant of 74 VOLTS. This keeps the battery charged and provides a constant source of low voltage power. 

A defective voltage regulator will be evidenced by excessive fluctuation of the ammeter on outside of low voltage cabinet.

Reverse Current Relay: (RCR) 

This relay prevents the battery current from "motoring" the auxiliary generator by opening the battery charging contactor when the auxiliary generator voltage drops below the battery voltage.

Ground Protective Relay: (GR) 

If a ground occurs in the high voltage system, this relay will open the exciter field circuit and unload the engine. The engine will run up to speed but no work is done.

Should this occur, return the engine to idle and reset the If relay by tripping the latch on the bottom of the relay. the relay repeatedly opens when the engine speed is increased do not operate locomotive. The relay is located in the right bottom corner of the high voltage cabinet. A ground in the low voltage system will also trip the relay when an engine is being started.

Wheel Slip Relay: (WSR) 

The relay has two normally open contacts and two normally closed contacts. In the event a pair of wheels slip the relay picks up. One pair of contacts will light the wheel slip light on the instrument panel. The other pair of contacts will de-energize the generator field operating coil cutting off power on the main generator until the slipping stops.

Main Battery Switch: 

This switch isolates all circuits from the battery. 

Switch should be opened when locomotive is left standing with engines shut down.

Motor Cut Out Switches (MC01, MCO2): 

These switches are in the circuits to traction motor contactor operating coils and are used to isolate traction motors two at a time. Throw switch on motors to the cut out all the way up. After cutting motors out, OPERATE WITH 

HALF THROTTLE OR LESS TO PREVENT EXCESSIVE HEATING OF OTHER TRACTION MOTORS. DO NOT OPEN CUT OUT SWITCHES UNTIL THE THROTTLE IS IN IDLE AND REVERSE LEVER IN CENTER POSITION. 

Transition Relays: (TR1, TR2) 

These relays are operated by generator voltage to automatically control transition from series to series-parallel to series-parallel-shunt positions. 

Exciter Field Contactor: (EF) 

This contactor connects exciter field to battery to energize exciter generator field. It is located in the right top corner of the low voltage cabinet.

Generator Field Contactor: (GF) 

This contactor connects the main generator shunt field to the exciter. It opens during transition from series to series-parallel to reduce generator output and prevent arcing of contactors. It is located in the lower left corner of the high voltage cabinet.

Traction Motor Power Contactors (P1, S, P2) 

These contactors change traction motor to generator connections from series to series parallel or vice versa.

2. M1 and M2 open through contact on TR2 relay.

Series-parallel to Series (manually at approximately 3 M.P.H.) 

1. Throttle moved to idle. 

2. P1 and P2 contactors open through contacts in control stand. 

3. GF contactor opens through contact in control stand. 

4. S and GF contactors close as throttle is opened, through contact in control stand.

SECTION 6 AIR SYSTEM Air Compressor: 

Each locomotive is equipped with a Gardner Denver WXE air compressor, to furnish air for the air brake and control air systems.

Draining the Main Reservoirs: 

Each locomotive has two main air reservoirs located underneath the carbody between the trucks. 

In order to prevent moisture from being carried over into the air brake and control air systems, these reservoirs should be drained at time of each crew change. 

Main reservoir air pressure is maintained between 130 to 140 pounds per square inch and the control air pressure should be 80 pounds per square inch plus or minus 3 pounds.

Santa Fe 2403, 2418 AND 2420 CLASS 1000 & 1200 H. P. E. M.D. an S. F 2403 

PART II-SECTION 22

These classes of diesel-electric locomotive comprises locomotives 2403 thru 2438 manufactured by the Electro-Motive 

Division of General Motors Corporation and are powered by 12567 type engines (See Part 1, Section 2). 

The cab is located in the back of the locomotive and the hood extends toward the front. The left and right sides of the locomotive are to the left and right when sitting in the cab and facing front.

The diesel engine is mounted in the locomotive with the generator in front of the cab and the accessory end of the engine toward the front of the locomotive. In front of the diesel engine are located the oil cooler, air compressor, traotion motor blower and radiator cooling fans. (See Plate 4200-5)

The engine has a speed range of 275 to 800 RPM which is controlled directly by the main throttle through a system of levers and rods.

These locomotives have one 12 cylinder 1000 horsepower engine, a direct current generator and four traction motors. 

From the power plant the power is distributed to the traction motors which are mounted on the trucks. The traction motors are geared to the axle through spur gears. (See Plate 4200-3)

Starting the engine is accomplished by pressing the engine start button located on the engineer's control stand. 

This switch energizes the starting contactors, closing the circuit from the batteries to the main generator. The main generator, then operating as a motor, cranks the engine.

On right cab wall is light control box with fused push buttons for: (See Plate 4200-1) a. Front headlight b. Rear headlight c. Number lights dim 15 amp.

Starting Engine: 

1. In low voltage cabinet: a. Be sure all fuses are in place. b. Close main battery switch. c. Close control switch. 

2. At control station: 

a. PUT REVERSE LEVER IN CENTER POSITION. 

b. Put throttle in idle position. c. Close control push button switch. d. Close fuel pump switch and watch for pressure on fuel return pressure gauge at engine instrument panel. Return pressure should be 5 to 10 pounds per square inch. e. Press in engine "start" button and hold until engine starts. Do not hold button down for more than 10 seconds at a time. f. Check lubricating oil pressure. This should be 

35 to 45 pounds per square inch. Water temperature should be 1250 F. before locomotive is worked. 

To Move Locomotive: 

1. Make certain main reservoir pressure is up. 

2. Release hand brake. 

3. Close generator field switch on controller. 

4. Place reverse lever in forward or reverse position as required. 

5. Pull back on throttle handle. Movement of the throttle handle should be steady and gradual enough to move load without slipping the wheels: Caution: In moving the locomotive do not pump the throttle handle back and forth as this will open and close the contactors and other electrical equipment which must make or break circuits each time the throttle is shut off or opened. A movement of the throttle handle of approximately 1/2" will close the control circuit, allowing the power output of the generator to be delivered to the traction motors. 

Operating Reversing and Throttle Handles: 

To eliminate arcing of the series contactor and consequently to eliminate the burning of the arc chutes, the

control circuit is wired so that the series contactor closes when the reversing handle is thrown and the generator field contactors close when the throttle is opened. Because the reverser drum turns when the reversing handle is thrown, ex treme care should be taken NOT TO MOVE THE REVERSING HANDLE UNTIL THE LOCOMOTIVE COMES TO A COMPLETE STOP: i.e., if the locomotive is moving forward and the throttle is closed, throwing the reversing handle to the reverse position would cause the reverser fingers to arc on the drum. Carelessness in handling the reversing handle will cause the reverser drum to become badly beaded.

The current limit relay lights the wheel slip light when the generator current reaches 1550 amperes. If in SERIE- 

PARALLEL the throttle should be closed to ONE-QUARTER POSI- TION OR LESS and push the Selective Transition Switch to 

"Series". The traction motors are then in series instead of series-parallel. 

Operation of Selective Transition Switch: (See Plate 4200-4) 

The selective transition switch serves two purposes. It is to be used as a forestalling switch to prevent series-toparallel transition taking place, or may be used as a backward transition switch to get from parallel back into series without the necessity of shutting the throttle completely off. 

Where the locomotive is being used in yard switching service, or when the operating conditions are such that frequent backward transition would be required, the switch should be used as a forestalling switch, thus keeping the traction motors connected in series.

Where the locomotive is being used in fast, light switching or transfer work and will be operating at speeds above 9 M.P.H. much of the time, the switch should be kept in "automatic" position and merely be used when necessary to make backward transition. 

The switch, which is of the push-button type, is mounted on the control cabinet or in the control stand. With the button pulled out, forward transition takes place automatically when the locomotive speed reaches approximately 9 M.P.H. With the button pushed in, the traction motor connections are limited to series. 

To go from the series position to the automatic position at ANY time, simply pull the button out. If the speed of the locomotive is less than about 9 M.P.H., nothing will happen until the proper speed is reached, at which time normal transition will take place immediately. 

The backward transition operation should be made as follows: If the locomotive speed drops below 8 M.P.H. as indicated by continuous burning of the wheel slip light when in parallel, return to series by reducing to QUARTER THROTTLE OR 

LESS, pushing the button to the series position and then

PLATE 4200-3 RIGHT SIDE OF LOCOMOTIVE

opening the throttle to the desired position. Speeds above 9-10 M.P.H. in series will have no detrimental effect on the equipment. However, at approximately 

10 M.P.H. the generator start to unload so that at speeds above this, in series, the engine will not be developing its full horsepower. Therefore, if at any time there is to be much work done at speeds ranging above 9 M.P.H. it is a better and more efficient use of power to be in parallel. 

Operation of  

Whenever any pair of wheels slip on either truck the indicator on the instrument panel will light. To stop the slippage ease off on the throttle.

The light will not burn continuously when wheels slip because the wheels will not slip continuously. The connections of the wheel slip relay to the power plant are such that power is automatically cut off to zero when the wheels slip. When the wheels stop slipping the power will go on at full strength UNLESS THROTTLE IS REDUCED. Unless throttle is reduced a SEVERE STRAIN on the motors, motor gears and generator will develop. The wheels will slip, then stop. slip again and stop about once a second if throttle is not reduced.

If necessary to apply sand reduce- the throttle until the wheel slip light stops flashing. It should be remembered that sand is injurious to the traction motors and associated apparatus.

During transition from series to parallel the wheel slip relay will pick up causing wheel slip light to flash. This is not an indication the wheels are slipping. 

The wheel slip relay will give wheel slip indication for series connection only.

Towing Locomotive: 

1. Stop engine and drain cooling system if freezing weather will be encountered. 

3. Place reverser handle in "off" position and remove handle. 

4. Lock reverser in neutral position with special locking bolt. 

5. Cut in dead engine cock located below cab floor. 

6. Close double heading cock located below automatic brake valve. 

7. Place handle of automatic and independent brake valves in running position. 

8. Set relief valve on distributing valve at 25 lbs. 

9. See that radiator shutters are closed.

Precautions During Locomotive Operation: 

1. Maintain engine cooling water temperature at 1650 F. plus or minus 150. This is accomplished by proper manipulation of the shutter control handle in cab.

TROUBLE SHOOTING If fuel Pressure Does Not Show on Gauge in Cab: 

1. If fuel pump does not run. a. Check all fuses in circuit. (1) 60 amp. fuse in low voltage cabinet. (2) Fuel pump fuse (15 amp.) in control stand. b. Check fuel pump switch at controller. c. Check electric connector at fuel pump motor. 

2. If motor is running but no fuel is being delivered: a. Check pump couplings and shaft. b. Check fuel supply and emergency fuel cut-off valve. c. Check for air leaks in suction line due to broken pipe or loose connection. d. Place handle on duplex fuel filter in opposite direction. 

If Engine Does Not Rotate When Attempt Is Made to Start: 

1. Check all. fuses affecting starting circuit. a. Starting fuse. This is the 400 Amp. fuse in the low voltage cabinet. b. 60 amp. control fuse in the low voltage cabinet. c. 30 amp. control fuse. This fuse is opposite the control push button switch on the controller. d. 15 amp. starting fuse. This fuse is opposite the engine start push button switch on the controller. 

2. Battery may be too weak to turn engine over. 

3. Check to see that both starting contactors go in.

If Engine Rotates But Does Not Fire When Start Button Is 

Pressed: 

1. Check cylinder test valves at each cylinder, making súre that they are closed. 

2. Check injector linkage in event that it may be stuck in shut-down position. 

3. Check to see that overspeed trip shaft is latched in "Run" position. 

4. Check to see if engine shut down lever is in "Run" position. 

If Locomotive Does Not Move When Throttle Is Opened: 

1. Check hand brake. 

2. Repeat movement of brake valve to release brakes. 

3. Check to see that generator field switch at control station is closed and fuse good. 

4. Check control air pressure and see that reverser is in proper position. 

5. Check to see that ground protective relay is set in normal position. 

6. Check all fuses. a. 60 ampere control fuse in low voltage cabinet. b. 30 ampere control fuse opposite control switch on controller.

If Locomotive Stops in Operation: 

If locomotive suddenly becomes inoperative. immediately check the control fuse (60 amperes) in the low voltage cabinet. then check the control fuse in the box on the controller. A wire may have jarred loose or may be burned off. The fuel supply may have stopped.

SECTION 2 COOLING SYSTEM Description: 

These pumps

Water is drawn from the water tank by centrifugal water pumps located on the front end of the engine. circulate water to the bottom of each cylinder liner up through the cored passages of the cylinder liner and cylinder head and out through the outlet manifolds. From the engine, the water flows to two groups of radiator sections located under the roof of the locomotive body. Here the water is cooled returning to the water tanks (See Plate 4200-6).

Filling Cooling System: 

The cooling system contains approximately 200 gallons and may be filled through the filler pipe located along the left side of the fuel tank or through the filler pipe extention on the roof. (Do not put water in the exhaust stacks.) Fill the cooling system until water runs out the filler pipe. It is best to have the engine idling when system is being filled to remove air pockets.
