CONNECTION

Add water very slowly if engine is hot and then only when engine is idling. Do not add cold water to a hot engine that has just been drained as this will crack and wrap cylinder heads and liners.

Specially treated water should be used in the cooling system to offset the accumulation of scale or foreign matter which contributes to putting and overheating the engine. 

The engine should be operated with the winter level valve closed. The water level should be maintained above the minimum water level mark (3/4 water glass) and the engine should not be operated with water below this level. 

If the water is to be drained, open drain valve near No. 7 cylinder. In addition to the drain valve are drain plugs in the water pumps and the pipe between the water pumps and the water inlet manifolds. 

Checking Circulation: 

The water temperature dials (one for each bank of cylinders) located on the engine instrument panel in cab and the water temperature on the engine in the engine room may be used as a means of detecting an irregularity in the operation of the cooling system.

If engine cooling water temperature cannot be maintained 

AT OR BELOW 190° F. by reducing throttle SHUT ENGINE DOWN AND DO NOT OPERATE UNTIL TROUBLE IS CORRECTED. 

Cab Temperature Control: 

The cab heater operates on hot water from the engine cooling system and is equipped with a motor driven fan. 

SECTION 3  Description: 

The lubricating oil is stored in the lube oil supply tank, which has an overflow line to the oil pan. The oil level should be maintained, with engine idling, at the bottom of the overflow line. This engine is classified as a wet sump type. The system is a pressure system using two positive displacement pumps combined in a single unit. One pump delivers oil from the pressure lubricating system, the other for piston cooling. The oil supply to these pumps is

drawn through strainers in the bottom of the supply tank then through suction pipes to each individual pump (See Plate 4200-6). 

Oil may be added by removing cover on supply tank and pouring required amount through strainer basket. With the engine idling, the oil level should always be between the "high" and "low" marks on the bayonet gauge in the engine oil pan (preferably at the half-way mark). 

When starting a cold engine, allow it to idle for some time and see that pressure starts to build up almost immediately. 

The pressure should rise on a cold engine to about 40 

lbs. and will run approximately 28 POUNDS at FULL THROTTLE 

(800 RPM) with engine warm. At idle, pressure should be at least 15 POUNDS. 

With normal operation, the lubricating oil pressure should not drop below 20 LBS. PER SQUARE INCH with engine running 800 RPM (full throttle). 

If oil pressure drops below the above limits, STOP THE

ENGINE AND DO NOT OPERATE UNTIL CONDITION IS CORRECTED. 

Piston Cooling Oil Pressure: 

As the piston cooling oil system has no relief valve, pressure is dependent upon oil temperature, viscosity and engine speed. Average pressure will be 20 to 30 POUNDS PER

SQUARE INCH AT FULL THROTTLE (800 RPM) and 4 TO 8 POUNDS at IDLE with WARM ENGINE. 

If pressure drops below 15 POUNDS PER SQUARE INCH at 800 RPM, stop engine and DO NOT OPERATE UNTIL CONDITION IS 

CORRECTED. 

Low Oil Pressure Shut Down: 

If the lubricating oil pressure or the piston cooling oil pressure should fail the engine will be returned to idle speed by a low oil pressure shut-down device, and opening the throttle will have no effect on engine speed. This is accomplished by a cylinder containing a piston attached through a linkage to the throttle shaft. One side of the piston is subjected to engine oil pressure and the other side to mechanical spring pressure. When the force of the spring overcomes the force of the oil, the piston moves backward closing the throttle shaft to idle position. A spring loaded rod in the throttle linkage permits this action regardless of the position of the throttle.

If the piston cooling oil pressure fails, the oil switch mounted on the oil pump will close, energizing the oil magnet brings the engine to idle. 

A fuel tank is mounted under the center of the unit with a capacity of 600 gallons. At the bottom of the tank is a sump from which the fuel pump draws fuel oil through the suction line and suction filter. Fuel can only be drawn from the supply sump if the emergency cut-off valve is open. After leaving the pump, fuel flows through the duplex Nugent filter and sintered bronze filter to the injectors. The injectors use only a part of the fuel pumped through them. The surplus fuel oil lubricates and cools the internal mechanism of the injectors and then returns to the fuel tank through the 5 pound relief valve (See Plate 4200-6).

Emergency Fuel Cut Off Valve: 

(See General Section All Locomotives). 

Fuel Pump: 

The fuel pump assembly consists of an electric motor and pump of the rotor gear type. The gear pump is driven by the motor shaft through a flexible coupling. Do not leave the fuel pump running unnecessarily. 

SECTION 5 ELECTRICAL SYSTEM 

The high voltage cabinet in front center of cab includes the following: (See Plate 4200-7) 

1. Reverser (F-R on respective coils) 

Changes direction of flow of current through traction motor fields to change direction of locomotive movement.

Operates to provide a momentary delay during transition so as to prevent arcing of the contactors. 

3. Engine Starting Contactors (ST ST ) 

Establish circuit from batteries to main generator starting field for cranking Diesel engine. 

4. Battery Field Contactor (BF) 

Connects battery to main generator battery field circuit for excitation. 

5. Motor Cut Out Switches (MCO1 MC02) 

Switches in circuits to traction motor contactor operating coils used to isolate traction motors two at a time. 

Throw switch on motors to be cut out all the way down and place selective transition switch in automatic position. After cutting motors out, operate with ONE-HALF throttle or less to prevent excessive heating of other traction motors. DO NOT OPEN CUT OUT SWITCHES UNTIL THE THROTTLE IS IN IDLE AND REVERSE LEVER IN CENTER POSITION. 

6. Generator Shunt Field Contactor (GF)

Connects the battery to main generator shunt field. 

7. Ground Relay (GR) and cut out switch. 

Protective relay to drop load on engine if ground occurs in high voltage system. If this relay picks up, bring the engine to idle and reset the relay by pushing in on the insulated button. If the relay repeatedly opens when engine speed is increased the locomotive should not be operated. 

8. Wheel Slip Relay (WSR)

Operates to drop load on engine and light wheel slip light in event of wheel slippage. 

9. Transition Relay (V1) 

A voltage relay operating to provide automatic transition from series to parallel as main generator loads change. 

10. Current Limit Relay (CLR) 

Protective relay for main generator. It operates to light wheel slip light when load on main generator is excessive so that transition may be made or decrease throttle to reduce generator output to within limits of generator. 

11. Traction Motor Power Contactors (S1 P1 P2) 

These contactors change traction motor to generator connections from series to series-parallel or vice versa. 

Low Voltage Equipment: 

The low voltage cabinet located under the left seat box includes: (See Plate 4200-8) 

1. Voltage Regulator 

Controls the output of the auxiliary generator at a constant voltage regardless of engine speed changes or battery voltage fluctuations. 

2. Battery Charging Contactor (BC) 

Establishes circuit of auxiliary generator to battery charging equipment is controlled by reverse current relay. 

3. Reverse Current Relay (RCR) 

Protective relay for auxiliary generator it prevents

Each unit is equipped with two main air reservoirs located underneath the carbody between the trucks. 

In order to prevent moisture from entering the air brake and control air systems, these reservoirs should be drained at each crew change.

Main reservoir air pressure is maintained between 130 to 140 pounds per square inch and the control air pressure should be 80 pounds per square inch plus or minus 3 pounds. 

SUPPLEMENT FOR CLASS 2418 & 2420 Wheel Slip Action: 

In the event of wheel slip action during the application of power, the wheel slip relay picks up and stops slipping by initiating the action that: 

1. Lights the wheel slip light. 

2. Opens the main generator battery field circuit. 

3. Inserts a choking resistor in series with the main generator shunt field circuit. 

4. Causes the load regulator to move toward the minimum field position. 

Ground Relay (GR) 

If ground relay trips, the white needle in the relay will point to a red dot. points to a yellow dot.

In the normal position the needle 

Lube Oil Button on the Governor: 

In case of low oil pressure or high suction the governor will shut the engine down. A push button on the front of the governor will move out approximate 1/2" exposing a red band on the button. The engine cannot be restarted until the button has been reset by being pushed back into the governor housing. 

Electro Hydraulic Governor Control: 

The throttle control at the control station actuates four sets of electrical contacts, which energize four solenoids (A, B, C, D) in the electro-hydraulič governor control assembly on each engine. These solenoids when energized act on a triangular plate which operates the speed control pilot valve. As the speed control pilot valve raises or lowers, oil flows to or from the speeder spring piston, increasing or decreasing the pressure on the speeder spring to increase or decrease the engine speed.

Engine Relay (ER): 

The ER relay is used on locomotives with electro-hydraulic governor control, and is normally energized when the isolation switch is in RÚN position and the fuel pump switch is closed.

CLASS 2418 ONLY: 

These locomotives are Electro-Motive Built Model TR4- 2400 HP. Transfer locomotives. 

A transfer locomotive consists of two units permanently coupled together. Only one cab is included on such locomotives and that is on the lead unit. The "A" unit does not have a cab.

Hump Control: 

Each operating cab is equipped with a toggle switch to cut in or cut out the hump control. Also there are two push button switches for increasing and decreasing the battery field voltage. When hump control is being used the load regulator brush arm is positioned by the two push button switches and remains in that particular position until moved thus giving battery field exication control while leaving the throttle in full or Run 8 position.

2600 CLASS BALDWIN LOCOMOTIVE

PART II-SECTION 24

SECTION 1 

GENERAL DESCRIPTION The 2600 class locomotives include Locomotives 2600 

through 2606. They are powered by two 600 series, 6 cylinder, supercharged engines, rated at 1000 HP. each, built by Baldwin 

Locomotive Works. 

One The cab is located in the center of the locomotive. engine and generator is located to the front and rear of the cab with the generator end towards the cab. The engine has a speed range of from 315 to 625 R.P.M. Engine speed is controlled through a pneumatic throttle. Locomotives 2600, 2603, 2604, 2605 and 2606 are equipped with two control stations each is equipped with complete sets of controls necessary for locomotive operation. 

Locomotives 2601 and 2602 are equipped with a "Hump Control" which is a manually controlled variable resistor that allows manual control of the generator field excitation. The engine speed can be kept constant and speed of the locomotive and train controlled by varying the generator field excitation, which in turn varies the horsepower output of the locomotive. 

It is desirable and most efficient to operate engines at full throttle (625 R.P.M.) when using the hump control. 

Locomotives 2600,2603,2604 and 2605 are equipped with dynamic brake. This dynamic brake is not to be used in controlling train speeds, but is built to be used as a holding brake for the locomotive when it is being operated as a light engine. 

There is an air compressor driven by each engine through the generator armature shaft. The two traction motor blowers are y" belt driven from the engine and furnishes cooling air for the traction motors in direct proportion to the loading (engine speed). Traction motor blowers on Locomotive 2600 are motor driven. 

LOCOMOTIVE OPERATION Control Station: 

The control station is equipped with all necessary controls for engine operation. These controls are throttle, reverse lever, air brake gauge and brake valves, push-button control station, load meter, etc. On Locomotives 2600, 2603, 2604, 2605 and 2606 dual controls are furnished. (See

Plate 4400-1). 

Locomotives 2601 and 2602 are equipped with one set of controls only. (See Plate 4400-2).

The throttle controls the engine speed through a pneumatic actuator operating on the speed control shaft of the engine governor. In addition, the engine throttle is used in 3 3. Bet

1. Place No. 1 and No. 2 throttles in off positions. 

place both reversers in off position.

Also 

2. Unlock control push button station at No. 1 position. 

3. Close control push button switch. 

4. Check auxiliary generator switch. 

Should be "OFF".

6. Close Fuel Pump Switch. Fuel pressure should come up to 25 PSI. 

7. Close Engine Stop Switch. 

8. Press engine start switch.

This should crank the engine. 

Continue cranking until the engine starts and the oil pressure builds up to 20 psi. If the start button is released before oil pressure builds up to 20 psi. the oil pressure switch will shut engine down. 

CAUTION: If engine fails to start within 10 sec., release start button and determine cause for not starting before attempting to start again. 

9. After engine starts, place isolation switch in the "ON" position.

To Move Locomotive: 

1. Make certain main reservoir pressure is up. 

2. Release hand brake. 

3. Insert reverse lever and move to position required for desired direction. 

4. Move throttle slowly away from "OFF" position to first notch and hold until ammeter shows current is flowing to traction motors, then advance throttle as required. 

5. Throttle should be advanced gradually using 20 to 25 seconds to reach full throttle. 

6. The wheel slip buzzer will sound if wheel slip occurs and the speed of the engine powering that truck will be reduced until the wheel slip stops. Engine will return to former speed automatically. If slipping continues, reduce throttle setting. 

7. Two steps of field shunting occur as speed of the locomotive increases. These changes in field shunting occur at approximately 12 M.P.H. and 18 M.P.H.

Load Meter Ratings: 

Maximum rating of the locomotive is indicated by load ammeter at control stand:

Continuous Rating 40 Minute Rating 20 Minute Rating 10 Minute Rating 6 Minute Rating 4 Minute Rating 

900 amperes 1000 amperes 1100 amperes 1300 amperes 1500 amperes 1800 amperes After any one of the above time limit periods is reached, back off on the throttle until the load indicator drops to or below 900 amp. Do not again exceed 900 amperes for 15 minutes or longer. 

Operation of  

When one pair of traction wheels slip, a buzzer will sound and the power of the engine involved will be reduced momentarily by a relay system and then increased to the original load. If the buzzer sounds again the throttle should be retarded to prevent further slippage.

It is in no way connected with the air brake system. The dynamic brake is effective only when the No. 1 engine is running and on the line and the locomotive is in motion. In the event of traction motor difficulty on the No. 1 truck, DO NOT use the dynamic brake until the difficulty has been corrected. a. The dynamic brake may be used at speeds up to 55 M.P.H.

It is not a stopping brake so it should be disengaged between 8 and 5 M.P.H. and air used to complete stop. b. To apply the brake, move throttle to "OFF" position.

Move reverse lever to the braking position. Wait 10 seconds before advancing throttle from idle position. 

After 10 seconds wait, throttle may be advanced according to the safe braking effort. c. A brake warning light on the instrument panel will light, indicating motor current has reached the maximum allowable and throttle must be as reduced to KEEP 

WARNING LIGHT OFF. 

d. DO NOT USE locomotive brakes while using dynamic brake. 

e. To isolate No. 1 engine when using dynamic brake, return throttle to idle, reverse lever to correct power position and then place isolation switch in OFF position. 

To Move Locomotive Dead in Train: 

1. Both engines must be shut down. In freezing weather the cooling system must be drained to prevent damage from freezing. 

2.

Move reverser to OFF position and turn all locomotive lights off. 

3. Open main battery switch. 

4. Lock both brake valves in running position.

Close double- heading cock on brake pedestals. Open dead engine cock located over distributing valve under cab. 

5. Close control air shut-off valve at control air reducing valve under cab. 

6. Be sure all radiator shutters and doors are closed. 

7. Set relief valve on distributing valve at 25 pounds. 

Precautions During Locomotive Operation: 

1. Maintain engine cooling water temperature between 1500 F. and 160° F. This is done automatically by the Grad-U- 

Stat controls which cause the shutters to open and fans to operate at low speed when the water temperature reaches 154° F. Fans operate at medium speed at 156° F. and high speed at 158° F. When temperature reduces to 

1510 F. the fans stop and shutters close. If automatic shutter controls fail to operate, place fan and shutter control switch in the "ON" position. This will open shutters and run fans at intermediate speed. Temperature can then be controlled by manual manipulation of shutters. If water temperature becomes excessive, a hot water control switch set at 200° F. will shut the engine

Stopping Engines in Preparation for Layover: 

1. Let engine idle for a few minutes. 

2. Move isolation switches to OFF position. 

3. Open auxiliary generator switches. 

4. Open engine stop switches. 

5. Open fuel pump switches. 

6. Open main battery switches. 

7. Set hand brake. 

8. Remove reverser handle. 

9. If locomotive will be exposed to freezing conditions, take necessary precautions.

To Change Control Stations on Dual Control Locomotives: 

1. At brake pedestal and control stand being cut-out: a. Make full service reduction with automatic brake valve, then place valve in lap position and lock with locking pin. b. Close double-heading cock. c. Place independent brake valve in running position. d. Remove reverser lever and lock out control push button switch lever. 

2. Proceed to opposite side of the cab to brake pedestal and control stand being cut in. a. Place independent brake valve in full application position. b. Open double-heading cock. c. Place automatic brake valve in running position.

Engine Fails to Start: 

TROUBLE SHOOTING 

1. Engine does not turn over check battery switch. a. Check Items 1 through 8 under "Starting Diesel Engines". b. Starting contactors may be blocked or stuck open. c. Check 15 amp. control fuse in control stand. 

2. Engine turns over but does not start, or starts but does not continue to run. DO NOT exhaust batteries by continued effort to start engines but locate trouble first. a. Check overspeed trip mechanism. Reset by inserting bar or prying down. b. Check fuel pressure. 

Should be 25 psi. c. Emergency fuel cut-off may be tripped. d. Lube oil pressure below 19 psi. when start button released. e. Insufficient oil supply in governor.

Engine Does Not Respond to Throttle Setting: 

1. Isolation switch is not "ON". 

2. Starting contactor stuck in "closed" position. 

3. Control air pressure low. Should be 70 psi. 

4. Power cut-out switch open. 

5. Check 15 amp. control fuse. 

6. Ground relay tripped on locomotives so equipped. 

7. Throttle magnet valve not operating properly.

If Locomotive Does Not Move: 

1. Reverser lever not in proper position. 

2. Auxiliary generator switch open. Must be closed. 

3. Power knockout switch open. This switch is located in the control stand and opens on an emergency application of brakes, and closes automatically when the brake pipe pressure builds up to 35 psi. 

4. Check ground relay on locomotives so equipped. 

5. Power cut-out switches open.

If Engine Stops: 

1.

Low lube oil pressure causing engine shut-down valve to operate. 

2. Engine overspeed stop has tripped. Reset as per instructions. 

3. Fuel failure. Emergency fuel cut-off valve tripped or fuel pump switch (breaker type tripped). 

4. Hot water switch has tripped.

If Fuel Oil Pressure is Low: 

1. Fuel oil tank empty. 

2. Emergency fuel cut-off valve tripped. 

3. Stuck relief valves. 

Try tapping them. 

4. Clogged suction strainers or pressure filters. 

5. Faulty pump.

If Lubricating Oil Pressure is Low: 

1. Lube oil is hot or oil level is too low. 

2. Clogged pressure strainer. turns in clockwise direction. 

Turn handle at least two 

3. Sticking relief valves. Try tapping them lightly.

If Engine Overheats: 

1. Water supply low. 

2. Automatic shutter control not functioning properly. Operate manually if required. 

3. Radiator fans not functioning properly. Check fan fuses in Electrical Equipment Cabinet. 

4. Diesel engine overloaded.

If Battery Ammeter Shows Discharge (Engine Running): 

1. Auxiliary generator switch open. 

2. Auxiliary generator fuse blown. (150 amp.) 

3. Poor contacts on battery contactor or reverse current relay. 

4. Battery parallel light has gone out (Locomotive 2600). a. Starting contactor stuck in closed position. b. Battery series contactor stuck closed. c. Poor contact on normally closed contactors at engine start button. d. Battery protective relay picked up. Reset. 

5. If batteries are not charging, give them immediate attention to prevent their being exhausted. 

SECTION 2 COOLING WATER SYSTEM 

Description: 

The water in the cooling system is circulated by an engine driven water pump. The water flows through the engine cooling radiators, heat exchanger and water pump in that order. The expansion tank, which acts as a reservoir for maintaining the cooling water system at full level, is vented to the atmosphere through an overflow vent line. 

Filling Water System: 

The cooling system can be filled either through the filler cap located on the cab roof over radiator section or

Water level gauges are 10cated on radiator bulkheads. Water should normally be added to system when cooling water is cold. If engine is warm (above 120° F) either add warm water or, if cold water is used, add cold water very slowly. Engine cooling water should be chemically treated. 

Temperature Control: 

Engine cooling water temperature is controlled between the temperatures of 151° F. and 1580 F. The shutters open and fans run at various speeds between these two temperatures. 

Above 1580 F. the shutters are open and fans are running at full speed. Below 1510 F. the shutters are closed and the fans are not running. Since no further fan or shutter action is possible to cool engine water, the only other control is for hot engine shut-down. This device operates through the fuel shut-down device previously explained. It will shut the engine down at 2000 F. Temperature control air pressure should be 17 psi. (approximately).

When automatic temperature control does not function properly the temperature control mechanism may be operated 

Place fan and shutter switch located on engine control panel in "ON" position. Shutters will open and fans will run at intermediate speed. Should water temperature drop too low in this position, shutters can be partially closed by operating shutter control handle manually. 

To Drain the Cooling System: 

It is necessary to drain each engine separately as the two cooling systems are independent of each other. To drain the engine and radiators open the two (2) drain and fill valves located water pump end of each engine. 

SECTION 3  Description: 

The lube oil system for each engine is independent of the other and is of the wet sump type. The oil is drawn from the sump by the engine driven gear type pump, chain driven from the crank shaft. The oil is drawn through a suction strainer to the pump and forced by pump pressure through the lube oil filter, metal edged strainer and heat exchanger to the engine.

Filling Lube Oil System: 

The oil level in the engine crankcase sump should be checked daily. To fill the crankcase, pour proper type oil into the filler pipe and fill until oil level reaches full mark on the bayonet gage. Never let oil level drop below "Low" mark on bayonet gage.

Lube Oil Pressure Switch: 

A safety device incorporated in the fuel system will shut the engine down if lube oil pressure drops to approximately 17 psi. This device consists of a pressure operated switch that is closed when under pressure. When the pressure on it drops to the minimum, the switch opens, interrupting flow of electricity to the shut-down solenoid, causing it to close and, therefore, stop fuel flow to shut down cylinder. 

The shut down cylinder then moves the injector pump control shaft to the shut down position, stopping the engine. A diagram of the system is shown in the section 4 on "FUEL OIL SYSTEM" 

The fuel tank is mounted under the locomotive and can be filled from either side. A sight glass located adjacent to the filler connection indicates the level of fuel in the tank. 

Fuel is drawn from the main supply tank, through the emergency cut-off valve and suction strainer by the motor driven fuel pump. The pump then forces the fuel oil through the pressure filler to the header supplying the fuel injection pumps.

A relief valve at the end of the header holds fuel pressure to 25 psi., by-passing excess fuel back to the supply tank.

Emergency Fuel Cut-Off Valve (See General Section All Locomo tives)

Shut-Down Valve: (Plate 4400-8) 

This valve is electrically energized and must be energized to allow fuel to flow to engine. The fuel pressure from fuel flowing through the stop valve to the shut-down unit "C" allows the fuel injection pumps to operate. In the event of high engine temperature or low oil pressure this valve is de-energized shutting off the flow of fuel to the shut-down unit, which allows the piston in the shut-down unit to move control shaft to the stop position.

SECTION ELECTRICAL SYSTEM 

Alarm Relay (SG): 

Rings warning bell if engine stops or blowers stop. 

Fan Motor Field Contactors (F1, F2, F3, F4, F5, F6).

Used to increase and decrease radiator fan speed and maintain correct water temperature. Generator Field Contactor (GF1, GF2): 

Connects the exciter to the main field of the main generator. 

Reverse Current Contactor (A1, A2): 

Contactor is closed by voltage coils and contacts in RCR. This contactor is opened by means of another coil which measures the current in the circuit between the auxiliary generator and battery. The coil opens contactor when the reverse current reaches a magnitude of about 20 to 25 amperes. 

Traction Motor Field Shunt Contactors (M1, M2, M3, M4, 

M5, M6, M7, M8):

These contactors when closed shunts part of the current around the traction motor fields, allowing tractor motor armatures to increase speed.

Radiator Fan Relays (R1, R2): 

These relays are set to pick up and open the field shunting circuit on both the fan and blower motors, preventing excessive fan speed.

Power Switch (P1, P2): 

Traction motor power switch, electro-pneumatically operated. Connects the traction motors to the generator. Reverser (REV) The reversers control the direction of rotation of the traction motors, by reversing the direction of current through the field windings.

Power Control Contactor (FR1, FR2): 

When the engine is idling or the generator isolated, current is provided through this relay for GF and EF in order to maintain power supply to the fan motors. 

Exciter field Contactor (EF1, EF2, EF3): 

These contactor connects the carbonstat load regulator and the exciter 4 pole field to the auxiliary generator for excitation of the main generator. 

Blower Motor Contactors (B1, B2, B3, B4, B5):

Regulates the blower speed by controlling the field shunting in the blower motor circuit. 

Power Cut-out Switch: 

When opened, completely disconnects the fields and power circuits of the unit. The fans and blowers will also stop. Battery Switch A manually operated double pole single throw knife switch, disconnects the battery from all auxiliary circuits except a few lights. Starting Contactors (G1, G2, G3, G4): 

When start button is held in these contactors close, the current from the battery then flows to the main generator which now acts as a motor and cranks the diesel engine. 

Reverse Current Relay (RC1, RC2): 

Connects the auxiliary generator to the battery through contactors when the generator voltage is sufficiently high to charge the battery, and protects against a reverse flow of current from the battery, when the generator voltage is too low, by opening the circuits. 

Voltage Regulator (VR) 

Holds the auxiliary generator voltage constant over the normal operating speed range of the diesel engine. 

Wheel Slip Relay (WS1 & WS2) 

The relay is connected to an intermediate point in the

circuit for each set of traction motors in such a manner that the difference in voltage drop through the motors when one motor is slipping, causes a current to flow through the relay. This reduces pressure to governor (through TV) thus reducing engine speed.

Throttle Unloading Valve (TV1, TV2): 

This valve reduces pneumatic pressure to the governor actuator, (during wheel slip) resulting in decreasing the engine speed until wheel slipping stops. 

Traction Motor Field Shunt Relays (FS1 - FS2) 

These relays pick up to give two steps of field shunting when armature current drops to a preset value. They control 

M1, M2, M3, M4, M5, M6, M7, M8.

ELECTRICAL CONTROL CABINETS 

The electrical control cabinets for each engine are located in the cab of the locomotive adjacent to the engine. 

The power plants of the two engines are entirely separate. 

Each power plant furnishes electrical energy for traction motors mounted directly under it. The No. 1 engine generator through the electrical controls is permanently hooked in series with the three traction motors on the No. 1 truck. 

Operation of Relays and Contactors During Transition: 

As the locomotive speed increases, two steps of automatic field shunting occur. Relays FS1 and FS2 successively close contactors M1 and M2 then contactors M3 and M4 (M5 to M8 on unit 2), which decreases the traction motor fields when armature current drops to a preset value. This occurs at speeds of approximately 12 and 18 miles per hour. 

There is a sudden increase of about 200 amperes on the load ammeter as the locomotive accelerates past these speeds. 

These relays, contactors and field shunting resistors are mounted in the equipment cabinet. 

SECTION 6 AIR SYSTEM Air Compressor: 

Each locomotive is equipped with 2 Westinghouse 3CD air compressors to furnish air for the air brake and control air systems.

REAR ELECTRICAL CABINET LOCO. 2601 AND UP

Draining Main Air Reservoirs: 

Each locomotive is equipped with two main air reservoirs, located underneath the carbody. One in front and one behind the fuel tank. 

In order to prevent moisture from being carried over into the air brake and control air systems, these reservoirs should be drained at each crew change. 

Main reservoir air pressure is maintained between 130 to 140 pounds per square inch and the control air pressure should be 70 pounds per square inch plus or minus 3 pounds.

Santa Fe 

DIESEL ELECTRIC 

CLASS 2650 & S 

PART II-SECTION 26

The 2650 class locomotive as built by the Electro-Motive Division of General Motors Corp. is classified as a general purpose locomotive suitable for use as local passenger and local freight power. The locomotive is powered by a 16 cylinder 

Model 567-B engine. The power plant and engine in general are the same as used on the 200 class freight power. The engine, generator and traction motors are the same. The cooling fans are similarly mounted in the roof and the traction motor blowers are the same as the 200 class locomotives. The locomotive is equipped with automatic transition. The engine uses the PG governor, which is an Electro-hydraulic governor similar to the SI governor.

LOCOMOTIVE OPERATION 

This locomotive must not be operated in excess of 65 M.P. H. The overspeed control switch will cause a warning whistle to blow at 65 M.P.H. but brakes will not be applied.

The control station contains the necessary controls for operation of the locomotive. See Plate 4600-1.

The transition meter shown at the control station is an ammeter in the traction motor circuit and indicates the amperage flowing in each traction motor. This meter is calibrated in amperes from 0 to 1500 amp. but shows no short time ratings. The short time ratings are posted on a metal tag secured to body of the meter. The only marking on some meter is a red diamond at 600 amp. which is the maximum allowable load in dynamic braking.

The locomotive is equipped with a "PC" switch of the automatic reset type. This switch is tripped by any penalty or emergency application of the brakes. When the "PC" trips, the engine returns to idle. In the event the throttle is in No.5 or No.6 position, the engine will stop. To reset the "PC" switch, return the throttle to "Idle" and "Recover" air brake application.

The controls for stopping and starting the engine are located in the left side of the high voltage cabinet as viewed when standing in the cab of the locomotive facing the high voltage cabinet. The failure warning lights and other gages pertinent to locomotive operation are located above the high voltage cabinet on the rear wall of the locomotive cab. See Plate 4600-3

The hand brake lever is located at the rear of the locomotive on the running board on the right side. It is mounted on the locomotive car body.

Starting Engines: 

1. Close all switches in distribution panel. 

2. At control station close "control" and "fuel pump" circuit breakers. 

3. Place independent brake in full "application" position. 

4. Check "PC" light. 

5. BE SURE REVERSE LEVER IS REMOVED FROM CONTROL STAND. 

6. Close fuel pump switch at distribution panel and check fuel flow through sight glass on the fuel filter nearest engine (mounted on right fron corner of engine). 

7. Check setting of overspeed trip (pull to set). 

8. Check governor oil alarm trip button. 

9. At engine start panel, check alarm circuits by momentarily placing isolation switch in "RUN" position. Bell should ring and blue light should light. 

10. Start engine by pressing START button until engine starts. Do not hold button down for more than 10 seconds. 

11. Check oil pressure, starting contactor interlocks, and ground relay for proper setting. 

12. Idle engine until water temperature raises to 1250 F. before working engine. 

13. Place isolation switch in RUN position. 

To Move Locomotive: 

1. Make certain main air reservoir pressure is up to 130 to 140 p.s.i. 

2. See that air brake equipment is properly set. 

3. Release hand brakes. 

4. Close generator field circuit breakers. 

5. Move reverser to direction desired. 

6. Place Road Service Switch in proper position on locomotives so equipped. 

7. Open throttle as required, pausing 2 seconds between notches until train starts. It should not be necessary to go beyond Run 5 to start a train. 

8. Manipulate throttle as needed to regulate train speed. 

9. When the locomotive is to be reversed be SURE LOCOMOTIVE 

IS FULLY STOPPED before reverser is thrown. 

To Stop Train: 

In stopping the train care must be taken to shut the throttle fully OFF. If the throttle is not shut off, the traction motors will be damaged due to the flow of current into motors being concentrated at one spot. Use air brakes as required.

Operation of Transition: 

Some locomotives have no transition lever but all are equipped with a special type of automatic transition. This transition incorporates the use of a "teaser" circuit when starting. After the train is in motion and the load regulator passes the "12 o'clock" position, the "teaser" circuit is cut out. This puts the locomotive traction motors in

LOW OIL TRIP BUTTON 

PUSH IN TO SET · 

WOODWARD ELECTRO HYDRAULIC SPEED CONTROL 

FUEL SPEED 17 15 13 11 9 7 5 3 4 6 8 

PLATE 4600-2 GOVERNOR

series-parallel connections. As locomotive speed increases, the load regulator moves toward maximum field. When the maximum field position is reached the load regulator arm trips a switch which causes the power contactors to put the traction motors in a parallel connection. This occurs at approximately 

20 M.P.H. Backward transition is made when the main generator amperage becomes excessive. 

A transition forestalling switch located at the control station panel can be used to forestall an undesired forward transition. When the switch is "UP" forward transition will take place normally. When forward transition is not desired, the switch lever is placed "DOWN". Traction motor connections will stay in series-parallel regardless of locomotive speed, generator voltage or position of the load regulator. 

Use of Transition Meter in Train Operation: 

The transition (load indicating) meter is an ammeter calibrated from 0 to 1500 amp. There is no marking on some of the meter except a red triangle at 600 amp. for maximum dynamic braking. However, some locomotives are equipped with dynamic brakes. 

A metal tag fixed to the meter case shows the allowable continuous and short time ratings: Continuous Rating 2 Hour 

825 amps. - 865 amps. 11 1 11 1/2" 11 - 900 amps. 11 - 925 amps. 1/4" - 950 amps. 

These ratings are accumulative; that is, the locomotive may be operated for two hours at 865 amps., then 1 hour at 

900 amps., then 1/2 hour at 925 amps. and finally 1/4 hour at 950 amps. If the locomotive exceeds 950 amps., the locomotive is overloaded and must be stopped. The load must be reduced sufficiently to allow the locomotive to operate at 825 amps. or less for at least 15 minutes before the short time ratings can again be used. 

In case the load indicating meter is out of order, it will be necessary to operate the locomotive according to the speed indicator. The speeds equal to the continuous and short time ratings are shown below:

Continuous Rating Speed 11.0 M.P.H. 

Hour Rating Speed 

10.2 M.P.H. 1/2 

Hour Rating Speed Hour Rating Speed 

9.0 M.P.H. 

8.5 M.P.H. 1/2 

Hour Rating Speed 

8.0 M.P.H. 

In the event the train speed drops to 9.5 N.P.H., the locomotive can only be operated for 1 hour at this speed. 

Whenever the speed drops below one listed above, the locomotive is operating in the next lower short time rating. If the locomotive speed drops below 8.0 M.P.H., the locomotive is overloaded and must be stopped. The load must be reduced sufficiently to allow the locomotive to operate at the continuous speed of 11 M.P.H. or above for at least 15 minutes before the short time ratings can again be used.

Road Service Switch: 

The road service switch is a toggle switch located on the right side of the controller. This switch has two positions SWITCHING (up) and ROAD (down). The switch is not mechanically interlocked with the control levers and allows the selection of either a fast or slow start, depending on the type of service being performed.

In the ROAD position, normal modified maximum field starting is provided which assures a slow smooth start. 

With the switch in the SWITCHING position a faster start is provided by use of the "teaser" circuit, which allows a more fully control of the loading of the main generator by the throttle position.

In multiple unit operation the road service switches in all units should be placed in the same position to assure that all units start uniformally.

Mechanical Interlocks On The Controller: 

that: 

The levers on the control stand are interlocked so 1. The reverse lever can be operated only with the transition lever in either No.1 of OFF position and the throttle at IDLE. 

2. The reverse lever can be removed from the control stand only with the transition lever in OFF and the throttle at IDLE; this locks against movement of the levers. 

3. The throttle can be moved to STOP with any position of transition or reverse levers. With the throttle in STOP the reverse lever cannot be moved. 

4. The transition lever cannot be moved from position 2 to 3 or 3 to 2 unless the throttle is in Run 6 or lower. 

5. The throttle cannot be opened if the transition lever is in OFF.

1. Forward Transition Switch (FTS) 2. Load Regulator Shunting Switch (LRS) 3. Actuating Fingers 

A,B,C 4. Load regulator arm, minimum field (4 o'clock) position, LRS interlocks CD closed, energizing LRC. 

This partially completes the throttle controlled "Teaser" circuit in the generator battery field circuit. The open AB interlock prevents the generator shunt field contactor from closing. 

PLATE 4600-4 LOAD REGULATOR ARM 

MINIMUM POSITION

Load regulator arm "1" in mid (12 o'clock) position. "B" finger has tripped LRS, opening CD interlock which de-energizes LRC, cutting out "Teaser" circuit. AB interlock closes, allowing generator shunt field to close. 

Battery field excitation of main generator normal. 

PLATE 4600-5 LOAD REGULATOR ARM MID-POSITION

Load regulator arm "1" in maximum field (8 o'clock) position. "C" finger has tripped FTS, closing FTS interlock CD which energizes parallel relay PR, causing forward transition to take place from series-parallel to full parallel. 

As the load regulator arm moves away from maximum field, "C" finger also moves away from FTS, allowing FTS to assume its normal position. PR relay stays energized through its holding circuit, and the traction motors stay in parallel. 

The motors remain in parallel until generator amperage has increased to the pickup value of the backward transition relay BTR. This action de-energizes PR, opening power contactors 

P1-P2-P3 and P4. The series contactors S13-S24 or S14-S23 

close, changing the motor connections back to series-parallel normal battery field, closing the throttle to idle will also change the motor connections back to series-parallel, normal battery field.

The "Teaser" circuit remains cut out until the load regulator arm gets back to minimum field (4 o'clock) position, when "A" finger trips LRS back to effect this change. 

PLATE 4600-6 LOAD REGULATOR ARM 

MAXIMUM FIELD POSITION

Whenever any pair of wheels slip on the locomotive, the 

Wheel Slip light in the cab will light. To stop wheel slippage reduce the throttle one or more notches. After a few seconds the throttle can be advanced to the desired position. 

If the locomotive is equipped with dynamic braking this light will serve as both the wheel slip light and the dynamic brake warning light.

Since wheels do not slip continuously, the wheel slip light will not burn continuously. The connections from the wheel slip relay to the power plants are such that the power is automatically reduced when the wheels slip. For this reason wheel slips are usually intermittent. Unless rail conditions are extremely bad, it will not be necessary to apply sand. 

When applying sand be sure that wheels are not slipping at the time sand is being applied. Due to the fact that sand is injurious to traction motors and electrical equipment, use sand only when necessary. 

Air Braking With Power: 

When it is necessary to apply air brakes with full power supplied by the locomotive, the throttle must be reduced to the sixth notch. It is imperative that the locomotive brakes be released and held in the release position when the locomotive is supplying power. 

When preparing to stop with power applied to the locomotive and brakes applied to the train, it is necessary to reduce the throttle as the speed of the train decreases. The throttle must be in idle before the train comes to a dead stop.

These instructions are not to interfere with operating instructions covering the use of dynamic or air brakes. 

Throttle Emergency Stop Button: 

This button is located on the end of the throttle lever. 

Its purpose is to stop the engine in case of emergency. It is operated by depressing the button on the end of the throttle handle and push the throttle back past "Idle" position.

In the event engine (or engines when operating multiple units) is stopped in this manner, return the throttle to idle and start engine (or engines) in the prescribed manner. Dynamic Brake: 

Some locomotives are provided with dynamic brake equipment permitting a portion of the power developed by the

The independent brake must be kept fully released at all times when the dynamic brake is in use. As the speed decreases below 10 miles per hour the dynamic brake becomes less effective. When the speed further decreases it is permissable to completely release the dynamic brake by placing the transition lever in No.1 position and apply the independent brake simultaneously to prevent the slack from running out. 

Whenever desirable the automatic brake may be used in conjunction with the dynamic brake provided the locomotive brakes is KEPT FULLY RELEASED.

Operating as Helper Locomotive: 

When operating as a helper locomotive, especially when helping a steam engine, care must be taken not to allow the helper engine to assume more than its share of the load. 

Knowing the length of the grade to be traversed, the time on the grade can be closely estimated. This time will set the minimum speed and maximum loading allowable. If the estimated time on the grade is above 2 hours, the transition meter should not be allowed to go higher than 825 amp. If the transition meter pointer does indicate excessive loading, reduce the throttle until the pointer comes within the continuous rating for at least 15 minutes before the overload (short time) rating can again be used.

Should the load indicating meter be out of order, it will be necessary to handle the throttle according to locomotive speed shown below:

Continuous Speed Rating: 

8 Throttle Position 

11 "I 11 

11.0 M.P.H. and up 

9.5 to 11.0 M.P.H. 

7.0 to 9.5 M.P.H. 

4.5 to 7.0 M.P.H.

Therefore, according to the above table, the throttle must be reduced as the speed of the train decreases, when using the locomotive in helper service with steam locomotives. 

Towing Locomotive: 

When towing the locomotive in yard movements with the engines running place the reverse lever in the neutral position and, throttle in "Idle". 

1. If locomotive is to be towed dead in train any appreciable distance, the reverser switch must be placed in neutral and locked in that position. To lock reverser, first center reverser and then remove locking pin from

left side of reverser and insert pin in hole in right side side of reverser, pushing pin all the way through the shaft and screw it into place. 

2. On air brake equipment, handle according to instructions contained in Manual 2501 Standard covering type of equipment on locomotive. 

Isolating an Engine While Under Power: 

(Multiple Unit Operation): 

If it becomes necessary to take an engine "off the line" while the locomotive is operating under power (multiple unit operation), it should be done as follows: 

1. Pull manual lay shaft lever shut and hold until engine stops. 

2. When bell starts ringing place isolation switch in "start" position. 

3. Turn fuel pump switch "Off" on distribution panel.

Starting and Placing Engine On the Line While Under Power (Multiple Unit Operation): 

1. Start engine in usual manner. 

2. After lube oil pressure builds up and engine temperature rises to at least 1250 F., place isolation switch in "Run" position.

Precautions During Locomotive Operation: 

1. Engine cooling water temperature should be maintained at 1650 F. plus or minus 150 F. This temperature is automatically maintained by the automatic shutter controls. However, the No. 1 shutters are manually operated from the cab of the locomotive. The No. 1 fan can be controlled by the summer-winter cut-out switch. 

In warm weather this switch should be "ON" and the shutters open to obtain proper cooling. 

2. Lube oil pressure should be 35 to 45 p.s.i. with engine in Run 8 and oil hot. If main bearing pressure drops below 20 p.s.i. in Run 8, stop engine and do not operate until cause of low oil pressure is corrected. 

3. Do not operate engines if water level is not showing in the water tank sight glass. 

4. The oil level should always be between the "Low" and "High" marks. 

5. The battery charging ammeter may, show various degrees of charging but should never show discharge. 

6. After the locomotive has operated several minutes in 

Run 8, the load indicator on the governor should show 

6/16 in. and the throttle indicator should show No. 8 position. 

7. Control air pressure should be 90 p.s.i.

In Cab of Locomotive: 

1. Close throttle to idle. 

2. Place reverse lever in neutral and remove handle. 

3. Open generator field switch. 

4. Place isolation switch in "Start" position. 

5. Push engine stop button and hold it until engine stops. 

6. Open fuel pump switch on distribution panel. 

7. Open control and fuel pump circuit breakers at controller. 

8. Open main battery switch. 

9. Open control knife switch. 

10. If the engine is being shut down during freezing weather, precautions must be taken to prevent cooling water from freezing or the water must be drained. 

11. Set hand brake.

Multiple Unit Operation: 

The GP7 locomotive was basically designed for single unit operation, but can be arranged for multiple unit operation. When properly equipped, the GP7 may operate in any position with other GP7, FT, F3 or F7 units. 

However, before attempting multiple unit operation among the various models there are four important items to consider, which are as follows: 

1. Units having 6 BL or 6 SL brake equipment cannot be operated in multiple with units having 24 RL brake equipment, and vice versa. 

2. Unless all units are to be operated in automatic transition, a method of making manual transition at the proper time must be available. 

3. The proper power plant jumper cables must be available (i.e. 27:27 or 27:21, etc.). 

4. If the units of a multiple consist are of different gear ratios and/or different continuous ratings, the locomotive should not be operated so that the unit geared for the HIGHEST minimum continuous speed is overloaded by being operated below that speed, or short time rating; nor must the locomotive be permitted to operate at a speed in excess of that for the unit having the LOWEST maximum permissible speed. 

If all units of a locomotive consist are not equipped for automatic transition, transition must be made manually at the proper time (according to the speeds for the particular type of unit that is being controlled manually).

Operation of Headlights: 

The headlight circuits are so designed that complete headlight control can be maintained even during multiple

unit operation.

The headlight switch is the standard fiveposition switch used on switch locomotives. This switch has an "Off" position and both front and rear "Bright" and "Medium" positions. In addition the control panel in the cab contains the usual class number and headlight switches, both front and rear. 

The switch controlling the headlight multiple unit circuit is located directly in front of the controller and just below the front window. This switch is located in a green box. The switch has four positions labeled as follows: "single unit", "controlling unit coupled at No. 2 end", "controlling unit coupled at No.1 end" and "controlled from another unit".

To operate headlights when locomotive is used as a single unit: 

1. Turn multiple unit control switch to "single unit". 

2. Place headlight switches on control panel to "On" position 

3. Control headlights as desired by five-position switch mounted on control stand. 

To operate headlights when locomotive is used in multiple unit operation: 

1. Turn multiple unit control in operating cab to "controlling unit coupled at No.1 end" or "controlling unit coupled at No. 2 end", whichever applies. The No.1 end of the GP-7 is the short end which is considered the front of the locomotive as determined by sitting at the controller. The No.2 end is the long end or rear of locomotive and is adjacent to the locomotive engine room. 

2. (a) Place multiple unit headlight control switch in all trailing cabs, except last cab, in "Single Unit" position. (b) Place multiple unit headlight control switch in last cab to "Controlled from Another Unit". 

3. Place headlight switches on control panel in operating cab to "On" position. 

4. The headlights can now be controlled in the normal manner by the five-position switch mounted on the control stand. The front headlight of the operating unit and the trailing headlight of the last unit will light, depending on the position of the five-position switch. 

TROUBLE SHOOTING If Fuel Does Not Show In Fuel Return Sight Glass: 

1. If fuel pump is not running: a. Check all fuses or circuit breakers in the circuit. (1) Fuel pump circuit breaker on distribution panel. (2) Fuel pump circuit breaker on control station panel.

b. Check switches in circuit. (1) Check "PC" switch (light). 

2.

If fuel pump motor is running but no fuel is being delivered: a. If fuel is flowing in 45 lb. sight glass, nothing can be done on the road as fuel filters are clogged. b. Check fuel supply and emergency fuel cut-off valve. d Check for air leaks in suction pipes due to broken or loose connections. 

If engine Does Not Rotate When Attempt is Made to Start: 

1. Repeat customary procedure, checking to make sure all control switches are in the correct position. 

2. Check all fuses and circuit breakers in starting circuit. a. 400 amp. starting fuse in electrical cabinet. 

3. Check to see if starting contactors come in.

control 4. Check batteries by turning on engine room lights and pressing "Start" button, If lights dim out, it is because batteries are weak. 

If Engine Rotates But Does Not Fire: 

1. Check engine overspeed trip. 

2. Check fuel pump and sight glass to make sure fuel is being delivered to engine. 

3. Check injector linkage, it may be stuck in shut-down position, shutting off the supply on fuel. 

If Locomotive Does Not Move When Throttle is Opened: 

1. Check "PC" switch (light). 

2. See that generator field circuit breaker at engine control stand is closed. 

3. Check hand and air brakes for full release. 

4. Check ground relay and reset if necessary. switch should be in "run" position. 

5. Check following fuses: 

(1) 80 amp. battery field fuse.

Isolation (2) Auxiliary generator field circuit breaker. (3) Control circuit breaker. 

To Reset Circuit Breakers: 

When a circuit breaker has been tripped, move switch to "off" position and then move to "on" position. 

If Engine Does Not Load Up: 

1. Check ground relay. 

2. Starting contactor interlock open. 

3. Wheel slip relay stuck.

4. Repeated ground relay operation indicates engine should not be operated until trouble is found and corrected. 

If Battery Charging Ammeter Shows Zero: 

1. Auxiliary generator field circuit breaker must be ON. 

2. Battery charging contactor must be closed. 

3. 150 or 250 amp. battery charging fuse must be good. 

4. Auxiliary generator cut-out switch must be closed. 

If Locomotive Suddenly Becomes Inoperative: 

1. Check control circuit breaker. 

2. Check generator field circuit breaker. 

3. Check ground relay.

If Engine Fails to Stop: 

Failure to

Stop engine with manual layshaft control: stop by use of "Stop" button may be due to the control circuit breaker being tripped or a faulty governor.

If Cooling Fan Motors Fail to Operate: 

1. Check AC contactors to make sure they are making.

If not, they may, at times be lifted up manually. 

SECTION 2 COOLING SYSTEM Description: 

Water is circulated through the engine radiators and oil coolers by means of two engine driven water pumps. 

Cooling air through the radiators is controlled by the automatic shutter and fan controls. 

The No. 1 shutter is manually controlled from the cab of the locomotive. These shutters must be opened in warm weather in order that the engine will receive proper cooling. The No. 1 fan can also be controlled either automatically or cut out with the switch for the No. 1 cooling fan located on the rear wall of the cab. In winter operation when the No. 1 fan is not needed for cooling, it may be turned off. 

To operate the cab heater system the No. must be closed and the No. 1 fan must be off. 1 shutters This allows

air to be drawn through the No. 1 radiators and forced through the cab heater system. 

Operating water levels are stenciled on the water tank next to the water gauge glass to indicate minimum and maximum levels. The engine should never be operated without water showing in the gauge glass. 

The system is filled either through the filler pipe located on the roof or through the filler pipe located at the rear of the unit on either side. 

To Fill Cooling System: 

1. Stop engine. 2. Open "G" valve. 

3.

Fill slowly until water runs out of filling level pipe at "G" valve. 

4. Close "G" valve.

Take following additional steps in filling dry or nearly dry engine: 

5. Start engine and run several minutes to eliminate any air pockets. 

6. Shut down engine, open "G" valve and wait several minutes. 

7. Add water until it overflows filling level pipe. 

8. Close "G" valve.

CAUTION: 

1. DO NOT attempt to fill a very HOT engine as damage will be done to it. 

2. Do not attempt to fill cooling system through drain pipe. 

To Drain Cooling System: 

The engine water drain valve will drain the entire engine cooling system EXCEPT the water pump on the right bank. 

A pipe plug in the bottom of this water pump scroll MUST be removed. 

SECTION 3  

Description: 

Oil under pressure is forced through the engine for lubrication and piston cooling. The lube oil drains into the oil pan where it is picked up by the scavenging pump and forced through the oil filters and cooler to the oil strainer housing, ready for recirculation by the oil pump. 

Excess oil drains into the crankcase. 

The oil level may be checked with the engine running. and should read between the "low" and "high" marks on the

Oil may be added to the system through the strainer housing when the engine is STOPPED. Fill the system to the "system charged" mark on the bayonet gage.

Oil Pressure: 

Oil pressure at 800 R. P.M. is normally 35 to 40 p.s.i. 

It should not drop below 20 lb. (p.s.i.). At idle the pressure should be above 6 p.s.i. If oil pressure becomes dangerously low the engine will stop. In this case the bell will ring and yellow and blue light will light.

Low oil pressure or high suction will cause the governor trip button to come out. This must be reset before engine can be started and alarm bell stopped, etc. 

The fuel in each unit is circulated through the injectors by an electrically driven fuel pump. Failure of the pump to operate, closed emergency fuel cut-off valve, or a clogged strainer can cause fuel failure.

The fuel tank may be filled from either side of the locomotive. The sight glass adjacent to filler opening 

The fuel gage aids in filling the tank without overflowing. is the air operated push-button type located on the front wall of the cab.

Emergency Fuel Cut-Off Valve (See General Section All 

Locomotives) 

As has been previously stated, the electrical system for the GP-7 is essentially the same as the 200 class freight locomotives. The principal exception is the load regulator "teaser circuit", which is designed to give a more instantaneous response from locomotive on starting.

1. "G" Valve 

2. Cooling Water Level Sight Glass 3. Oil Cooler Housing 4. Load Regulator 5. Air Compressor Governor 6. Traction Motor Blower and Motor PLATE 4600-9 PLUMBING STACK (RIGHT SIDE)

Traction Motor Cut-Out Switches: 

Some locomotives are equipped with traction motor cut-out switches. These switches are located in the electrical control cabinet above the engine control panel. 

However, they are reached through the engine room door into the electrical control cabinet. These switches are single pole, single throw, heavy duty switches.

GENERATOR VOLTAGE TO THE TRACTION MOTORS. 

THEY CARRY FULL 

To cut out a traction motor, stop the locomotive, isolate the engine and proceed to open the desired traction motor cut-out switch. Be sure it is fully open. ONLY ONE 

TRACTION MOTOR MAY BE CUT OUT AT ANY TIME. 

When any traction motor is cut out, the traction motor series contactors and the parallel contactor for the motor being cut out are held open. The remaining three traction motors are held in a permanent parallel hook-up. The dynamic brake is also cut out on locomotives so equipped. 

With the traction motors in parallel, the locomotive should NOT BE ALLOWED TO OPERATE BELOW THE CONTINUOUS RAT- 

ING. This is to protect the main generator from damaging overload. With a traction motor cut out, the BTP is so connected that the over-riding solenoid (ORS) of the governor is energized whenever excessive main generator current is experienced. This will UNLOAD the engine. Therefore, care must be taken to prevent unloading in this manner, as it will contribute to rough handling and other train handling difficulties. There is NO wheel slip protection when a traction motor is cut out. So care must be taken to make sure no wheels are sliding, and particularly that the wheels connected to the traction motor that is cut out turns freely. 

Battery Field Fuse: 

There is an 80 amp. fuse in the battery field circuit of the main generator. Should this fuse be blown, no power will be developed. The battery field fuse is located in the electrical control cabinet below the engine start panel. 

Battery Charging Fuse: 

The locomotives equipped with steam generators carry 250 amp. battery charging fuses. These locomotives are equipped with 18 KW auxiliary generators. 

The locomotives which are not equipped with a steam generator, carry a 150 amp. battery charging fuse. These locomotives are equipped with a 10 KW auxiliary generator. 

Electrical Control Cabinet: 

The electrical control cabinet, located in the rear wall of the cab, houses the engine control panel, distribution

Teaser Circuit Contactors 

A, B, C: 

These contactors connect resistors into the battery field circuit. They operate simultaneously with the electrohydraulic governor control solenoids.

Cooling Fan Motor Contactors 

AC1, AC2, AC3, AC4: These contactors connect the radiator cooling fans to the alternator of the main generator and are controlled through the thermostat switch. 

Electro Hydraulic Governor Control Solenoids AV, BV, CV, DV: 

Various combinations of these solenoids control engine speed in accordance with throttle position.

Dynamic Brake Contactors - B, B1, B2, B3: 

These contactors connect the traction motor fields in series with each other and to the main generator during dynamic braking. 

Battery Charging Contactor 

BC: 

This contactor connects the auxiliary generator to the battery. The reverse current relay closes the battery charging contactor when the auxiliary generator voltage reaches a predetermined setting to charge the battery. 

Battery Field Contactor 

BF: 

This contactor closes when the throttle is moved to Run 1 position, connecting the battery to the main generator battery field. 

Backward Transition Current Relay 

BTR: 

This relay is a current operated relay which is closed at all times except during backward transition. When the main generator current reaches a predetermined setting, the relay picks up causing backward transition.

Dynamic Brake Warning Relay 

BWR: 

Dynamic brake warning relay is a current operated relay, which will light the wheel slip light indicating that the load on the dynamic brake is excessive. The light will remain on until the load is reduced to the continuous rating of the

Cut Out Relay COR1, COR2: 

These relays are energized by the traction motor cut out switches to make the electrical changes necessary when traction motors are cut out.

Cut Out Switches 

CO1, CO2, CO3, CO4: 

These switches are opened to cut out traction motors as 

Open CO1 to cut out No.1 traction motor.

Open CO2 to cut out No. 2 traction motor. 

Open CO3 to cut out No. 3 traction motor. 

Open C04 to cut out No. 4 traction motor.

Do not operate traction motor cut out switches unless the isolation switch is in the "START" position. Cam Switch 

CS: When The cam switch is a power-braking transfer switch. in "MOT." position, it connects the traction motor armatures in series with their respective fields and disconnects the braking grids from the motor armatures. When in "TOW" position, it disconnects the traction motor armatures from their fields and connects a braking grid across each armature.

Engine High Temperature Switch 

ETS: 

When the water temperature exceeds the maximum allowable temperature, this switch closes, lights the hot engine alarm signal light and energizes the signal relay, which rings the alarm bell. On some classes of locomotives the engine will return to idle and remain in idle as long as the hot engine alarm is on.

Engine Relay ER: 

This relay controls the current supply to the Electro- 

Hydraulic Governor Control solenoids AV, BV and CV. De-ener gizing this relay will stop the engine if the throttle is in 

Run 5 or Run 6 or bring the engine to idle in any other throttle position.

Field Loop Contactor FL: 

The field loop contactor is energized when the transition lever is moved to the "OFF" "B" and succeeding_braking position This operation places the dynamic brake control rheostat and registors and the unit selector switch and its resistors in series with the field loop circuit. 

Fuel Pump Contactor 

FPC: 

All fuel pump contactors throughout the locomotive are

controlled by the fuel pump switch at the control station. 

When this contactor is closed, the fuel pump can be controlled individually by the switch mounted on the distribution panel. 

Forward Transition Switch 

FTS: 

Actuated by arm on load regulator causes forward transition from series-parallel to full parallel. 

Ground Protective Relay 

GR: 

If a ground occurs in the high voltage system, this relay will open the main generator shunt field and battery field contactors, and by de-energizing the "ER" relay bring the engine to idle except when the throttle is in the 5th or 6th position and in this event the engines will stop. Should this occur isolate the engine and reset the relay by pressing in on the reset button on the cover. When the relay is tripped the white needle will point to the red indicator and to the yellow indicator when in normal position. If the relay repeatedly opens when the engine speed is increased the power plant MUST be isolated until the condition is corrected. 

Load and Transition Indicating Meter IM: 

This meter is an accurate guide to load on the Locomotive as it shows amount of current furnished to the traction motors. Isolation Switch 

IS: 

The purpose of this switch is to open and close the control circuit of the unit as the occasion demands. When starting the Diesel engine this switch must be in the START position. After engine is running move switch to RUN position. This will connect the control circuit of the unit to the control station. To isolate this unit, move the switch to START position. This will bring the engine to idle and cut off the power of this unit. 

Load Regulator Control Relay LRC: 

This relay cuts in and cuts out the throttle controlled Teaser circuit. 

Load Regulator Shunting Switch LRS: 

This switch completes circuit to energize or de-energize LRC: 

Cam Switch Magnet Valve for Power Position No "AC" Voltage Relay 

NVR: 

MOT.: 

This relay is connected across one phrase of the AC alternator and should the AC voltage fail, the alarm bell rings and the alternator failure warning light (blue light) goes on. 

The engine speed will return to idle except when the throttle is in the 5th or 6th position and in this event the engine will stop.

Overiding Solenoid 

ORS: 

This solenoid is incorporated in the governor. When this solenoid is energized it causes the pilot valve of the load regulator to move upward 1/8" which causes vane motor to move to minimum field. 

Parallel Contactors P1, P2, P3, P4:

These contactors are closed to connect the main generator to the traction motors in parallel operation.

Pneumatic Control Switch - PCS: 

The purpose of this switch is to reduce the power of the locomotive by bringing the speed of the engines to idle when certain brake applications take place.

Parallel Relay PR: 

This relay operates to close the parallel contactors; also in conjunction with the main generator it causes shunt field and battery field contactors to open during transition from No. 2 to No.3 and from No.3 to No.2. 

Reverse Current Relay RCR: 

This relay prevents the battery current from motoring the auxiliary generator by opening the battery charging contactor when the auxiliary generator voltage drops below the battery voltage.

Series Contactors S14, S23 or S13, S24: 

The contactors are closed to connect the main generator to the traction motors in Series Parallel operation. 

Generator Shunt Field Contactor 

SH: 

This connects the main generator shunt field to the main generator armature.

Signal Relay SR: 

This relay operates with the signal lights (Hot Engine, Boiler Off, and Low Oil Pressure) to ring the alarm bells throughout the locomotive.

Starting Contactors ST+, ST-: 

These contactors are used to connect the battery to the main generator for cranking the diesel engine.

Time Delay Relay VT: 

This relay establishes a circuit energizing PR relay, It takes three to four seconds for this relay to close.

Wheel Slip Relays WSR1, WSR2, WSA: 

When wheel slippage occurs, these relays light the wheel slip light and reduce the main generator output momentarily until wheel slippage stops On some locomotives this is accomplished by WSR1 and WSR2 relays energizing WSA relay. 

SECTION 6 AIR SYSTEM Air Compressor: 

Each locomotive is equipped with a Gardner-Denver Air Compressor to supply air for the air brake and control air system. 

Draining Main Air Reservoirs: 

Each locomotive is equipped with two main air reservoirs, located underneath the carbody, between the trucks. 

Main reservoirs on locomotives 2650 to 2654 are located on the roof over engine rooms. 

In order to prevent moisture from being carried over into the air brake and control air systems, these reservoirs should be drained at each crew change.

Main reservoir air pressure is maintained between 130 to 140 pounds per square inch and the control air pressure should be 90 pounds per square inch, plus or minus 3 pounds. 

SUPPLEMENT FOR LOCOMOTIVES 2788 TO 2792 INCLUSIVE: 

Locomotives 2788 to 2792 inclusive are two unit 3000 HP locomotives consisting of a lead and booster unit of the "Cow and Calf" type. Only one operating cab is included on these locomotives and that is on the lead units. 

The continuous and short time rating, top speed and the operation of these locomotives are the same as the other locomotives of the 2650 Class. 

The lead units are powered by a 16 cylinder Model 567B engine and the booster units are powered by a 16 cylinder Model 567C engine. Each unit being provided with a local control panel which permits isolation of either power plant

without affecting the operation of the other power plant. 

This panel also contains the engine "START" and "STOP" buttons.

The engine on the booster units cannot be started or will not run when disconnected from the lead units as the booster units receive their controls through the jumper from the lead units.

These locomotives are equipped with improved dynamic braking with a maximum current limit of 700 amperes. When the dynamic braking lever is moved from the "OFF" position, the engine speed increases to Run 3 and remains in Run 3 until the lever is moved back to the "OFF" position. These locomotives are also equipped with a wheel slide device to indicate wheel slide when using the dynamic brake. When one or more pair of wheels slide, the wheel slip indicator will light and sand will be applied automatically when the automatic sanding switch located on the panel at the control stand is closed.

The transition and "teaser" circuit on the lead units are the same as other units of the 2650 Class. The booster units are equipped with a FTR (Forward Transition Relay) to make transition on main generator voltage after the load regulator arm has reached the maximum field (8 o'clock) position to close the FTS switch. In starting, the ". "teaser" circuit is in use at all times on the booster units and cannot be cut out manually. This "teaser" circuit cuts out automatically when the throttle is advanced to Run 3 and above, or when the load regulator arm reaches mid (12 o'clock) position to open the LRS switch. 

The alarm bell and alarm signal lights are trainlined between the two units to give simultaneous audible and visual alarm indications in case of low oil, hot engine and ground relay action.

Santa Fe 

DIESEL ELECTRIC FAIRBANKS-MORSE LOCOMOTIVES CLASS 3000 

3019 2319 

PART II-SECTION 27 

0-2019

The 3000 class locomotive is built by Fairbanks-Morse and is classified as a general purpose locomotive suitable The for use in local, freight, and passenger services. locomotive is powered by an 8-cylinder Model 3808 1/8 diesel engine, a Westinghouse Type 472A main generator, and Westing house Type 370 traction motors. The locomotive is equipped with automatic shunting, as no transition takes place other than shunting. The engine uses the PG governor, which is an electro-hydraulic governor. 

LOCOMOTIVE OPERATION 

This locomotive must not be operated in excess of 

65 M.P.H.

The control station position contains the necessary controls for operation of the locomotive. (See Plate 4700-2)

The control for stopping and starting the engine are also located at the control station. 

The hand brake is located inside the cab behind the cab heater.

Starting Engines: 

1. Throttle handle idle. 

2. Selector handle off. 

3. Reverser handle neutral. 

4. Main Battery switch on. 

5. Isolation switch start. 

6. Control breaker on. 

7. Fuel pump breaker on. 

8. Fuel pump switch on. 

9. Locomotive switch off. 

10. Traction motor cut out switches closed. 

11. Engine overspeed trip set. 

12. Governor low oil pressure button set.

To Move Locomotive: 

1. Snap ON Locomotive Run switch. 

2. Release independent brakes. 

3. Open throttle as required.

Use of Load Indicating Meter in Transition: 

Traction motors are permanently connected in series parallel and hence require no transition, but do use automatic field shunting in four steps. Shunting differs from transition in that no power circuits are completely opened.

The allowable continuous and short time ratings as shown on the load indicating meter are as follows: 

Continuous Rating 1020 amps. 

30 Minute Rating 

10 Minute Rating 1100 amps. 1150 amps. 

After any one of the These ratings are not accumulative. short time ratings has been used or the load meter pointer goes above 1150 amps., the locomotive is overloaded and must be stopped. The load must be reduced sufficiently to allow the locomotive to operate at 1020 amps. or less for at least 

15 minutes before the short time ratings can again be used. 

In case the load indicating meter is out of order, it will be necessary to operate the locomotive according to the speed indicator. The speeds equal to the continuous and short time ratings are shown below: 

Continuous Rating 9.2 MPH 

30 Minute Rating 8.1 MPH 

10 Minute Rating 

7.6 MPH 

After any one of the short time ratings has been used or the speed drops below 7.6 MPH, the locomotive is overloaded and must be stopped. The load must be reduced sufficiently to allow the locomotive to operate at the continuous speed of 9.2 MPH or above for at least 15 minutes before the short time ratings can again be used.

To stop wheel slip- 

Wheel Slip light in the cab will light.
